# Daily Summary: 2025-09-17

### Knowledge Graph Tokenization for Behavior-Aware Generative Next POI Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12350v1)
- **Authors**: Ke Sun, Mayi Xu
- **Abstract**: Generative paradigm, especially powered by Large Language Models (LLMs), has emerged as a new solution to the next point-of-interest (POI) recommendation. Pioneering studies usually adopt a two-stage pipeline, starting with a tokenizer converting POIs into discrete identifiers that can be processed by LLMs, followed by POI behavior prediction tasks to instruction-tune LLM for next POI recommendation. Despite of remarkable progress, they still face two limitations: (1) existing tokenizers struggle to encode heterogeneous signals in the recommendation data, suffering from information loss issue, and (2) previous instruction-tuning tasks only focus on users' POI visit behavior while ignore other behavior types, resulting in insufficient understanding of mobility. To address these limitations, we propose KGTB (Knowledge Graph Tokenization for Behavior-aware generative next POI recommendation). Specifically, KGTB organizes the recommendation data in a knowledge graph (KG) format, of which the structure can seamlessly preserve the heterogeneous information. Then, a KG-based tokenizer is developed to quantize each node into an individual structural ID. This process is supervised by the KG's structure, thus reducing the loss of heterogeneous information. Using generated IDs, KGTB proposes multi-behavior learning that introduces multiple behavior-specific prediction tasks for LLM fine-tuning, e.g., POI, category, and region visit behaviors. Learning on these behavior tasks provides LLMs with comprehensive insights on the target POI visit behavior. Experiments on four real-world city datasets demonstrate the superior performance of KGTB.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Knowledge Graph Tokenization for Behavior-Aware Generative Next POI Recommendation" proposes KGTB, a novel method to enhance generative next Point-of-Interest (POI) recommendation systems that leverage Large Language Models (LLMs).  The approach addresses two limitations of existing generative methods: information loss during the tokenization of POIs and insufficient understanding of user mobility. KGTB constructs a knowledge graph (KG) to represent POI recommendation data, preserving heterogeneous information.  A KG-based tokenizer then generates "structural IDs" (StruIDs) for each node in the KG, encoding structural information. Finally, a multi-behavior learning strategy incorporates behavior-specific prediction tasks (POI, category, region) during LLM fine-tuning to improve mobility understanding. Experimental results on real-world datasets demonstrate that KGTB outperforms existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates several novel contributions:
    *   **KG-based Tokenization:**  The key novelty is the use of a knowledge graph and a KG-based tokenizer (RGCN + quantization supervised by KG reconstruction) to create StruIDs.  This is a significant departure from simpler approaches of assigning random IDs or quantizing POI features directly. This is a clever way of incorporating relational information to enrich the discrete token representation.
    *   **Multi-Behavior Learning:** The addition of category and region prediction tasks during fine-tuning is a valuable extension. It's intuitive that modeling these related behaviors would improve understanding of user mobility. The authors provide empirical validation of these tasks.
    *   **Application of Graph Tokenizer:** First attempt to explore potential of graph tokenizers for generative POI recommendation is claimed, this is likely a novel contribution within the POI recommendation domain, although graph tokenization is not novel in itself.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a relevant and important problem. Generative POI recommendation is a burgeoning area, and improving the tokenization step to retain more information is crucial. The multi-behavior learning strategy also contributes to addressing the "mobility understanding" gap. The significant performance gains reported across multiple datasets suggest that KGTB offers a tangible improvement over existing methods. The experiments related to cold-start POIs and out-of-domain data is particularly valuable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the KGTB framework.
    *   The experimental setup is thorough, with a good selection of datasets and baselines.
    *   The ablation study is comprehensive and helps isolate the impact of each component of KGTB.
    *   The analysis of cold-start and out-of-domain performance provides important insights into the robustness and generalization capabilities of KGTB.
    *   The evaluation of model efficiency comparing computational cost demonstrates the effectiveness.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper acknowledges a choice of GPT-2 over Llama3-8B due to low latency requirement. There may be a tradeoff with latency when implementing KGTB.
    *   While KG is constructed, and the edge types are listed, the graph density/sparsity isn't quantified, which could provide additional insight.
    *   While there is performance gain compared to GNPR-SID (a SID based tokenization), it is unclear how the KG tokenizer compares against recent advances in Graph tokenization techniques in other domain of applications (e.g., in NLP).

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in generative POI recommendation. The KG-based tokenization approach could be adopted and extended by other researchers. The multi-behavior learning strategy also offers a valuable framework for improving mobility understanding in LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of POI recommendation.  The novel approach to tokenization and the incorporation of multi-behavior learning are significant improvements over existing methods. The rigorous experiments validate the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed KGTB framework. The paper is clearly written, well-organized, and provides valuable insights. However, the novelty is somewhat limited by the potential adaptation of graph tokenization techniques.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Diffusion-Based Generation and Imputation of Driving Scenarios from Limited Vehicle CAN Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12375v1)
- **Authors**: Julian Ripper, Ousama Esbel, Rafael Fietzek, Max Mühlhäuser, Thomas Kreutz
- **Abstract**: Training deep learning methods on small time series datasets that also include corrupted samples is challenging. Diffusion models have shown to be effective to generate realistic and synthetic data, and correct corrupted samples through imputation. In this context, this paper focuses on generating synthetic yet realistic samples of automotive time series data. We show that denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs) can effectively solve this task by applying them to a challenging vehicle CAN-dataset with long-term data and a limited number of samples. Therefore, we propose a hybrid generative approach that combines autoregressive and non-autoregressive techniques. We evaluate our approach with two recently proposed DDPM architectures for time series generation, for which we propose several improvements. To evaluate the generated samples, we propose three metrics that quantify physical correctness and test track adherence. Our best model is able to outperform even the training data in terms of physical correctness, while showing plausible driving behavior. Finally, we use our best model to successfully impute physically implausible regions in the training data, thereby improving the data quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of generating and imputing time series data, specifically vehicle CAN data, when only a limited number of samples are available. It proposes a hybrid generative approach using denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs). The approach combines autoregressive (AR) and non-autoregressive techniques: sequentially generating windows in an AR manner, while using a non-AR setting to generate each window. The authors evaluate this approach using two recent DDPM architectures for time series generation, LongConv and SSSD$4, proposing improvements to these architectures. They introduce three new metrics to evaluate generated samples based on physical correctness and test track adherence. The paper also explores the imputation of physically implausible regions in the data using the best performing model, comparing a naive approach with an adapted RePaint technique. The key findings indicate that DDPMs can effectively generate and impute vehicle CAN data even with limited training data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:

    *   Applying DDPMs to the specific domain of vehicle CAN data generation and imputation. While DDPMs have been used for time series data in other domains (e.g., healthcare), their application to automotive CAN data is relatively underexplored.
    *   The hybrid AR/non-AR generative approach for CAN data, designed to overcome the limitations of small datasets and long time series.
    *   The proposal of three domain-specific metrics to evaluate the physical correctness and driving behavior of generated CAN data. These metrics are specifically tailored to the context of vehicle dynamics and track adherence, and it gives a more complete and practical evaluation of generated data.
    *   Modification of the SSSD architecture through the replacement of S4 layers with Mamba layers to achieve better performance.
    *   Evaluation of different imputation schedules for correcting physically implausible data regions.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper has the potential to significantly impact the automotive industry by enabling the generation of synthetic CAN data for various applications like motion planning, control system development, and sensor data augmentation.
    *   It addresses the practical challenge of limited availability of real-world CAN data, which can hinder the training of deep learning models for autonomous driving and other automotive applications.
    *   The introduced evaluation metrics provide a standardized way to assess the quality of generated CAN data, promoting comparability between different generative models in the field.
    *   The imputation experiments demonstrate the potential of DDPMs for data cleaning and error correction in real-world CAN datasets, further improving data quality for downstream tasks.
    *   The exploration of the Mamba architecture within the SSSD framework shows an understanding of recent trends in sequence modeling and the possibility of improving performance through architectural change.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed approach and experimental setup.
    *   The evaluation is thorough and includes both quantitative metrics and qualitative analysis (visualizations).
    *   The domain-specific evaluation metrics enhance the credibility and practical relevance of the results.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of different components of the proposed approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The dataset used is confidential, which limits the reproducibility and external validation of the results. While the paper provides enough detail, the proprietary nature of the data could make it more difficult for other researchers to directly compare their work to the results presented in this paper.
    *   The comparison to state-of-the-art time series generation methods could be more extensive, especially including more AR baselines.
    *   The improvements to the SSSD$4 architecture could be more thoroughly explained and justified.
    *   While the results are promising, the paper does not demonstrate the impact of the generated data on a downstream task, such as motion planning or control. Showing how these synthetic data improve performance in real-world applications would strongly increase the paper's impact.

*   **Overall Score:**

    Given the paper's novelty in applying DDPMs to vehicle CAN data with domain-specific improvements and evaluation, its potential impact on the automotive industry, and its thorough experimental evaluation, but acknowledging the limitations regarding dataset confidentiality and potential for more extensive comparisons, I would assign it a score of **7.5**.

Score: 7.5

### LLM-as-a-Judge: Rapid Evaluation of Legal Document Recommendation for Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12382v1)
- **Authors**: Anu Pradhan, Alexandra Ortan, Apurv Verma, Madhavan Seshadri
- **Abstract**: The evaluation bottleneck in recommendation systems has become particularly acute with the rise of Generative AI, where traditional metrics fall short of capturing nuanced quality dimensions that matter in specialized domains like legal research. Can we trust Large Language Models to serve as reliable judges of their own kind? This paper investigates LLM-as-a-Judge as a principled approach to evaluating Retrieval-Augmented Generation systems in legal contexts, where the stakes of recommendation quality are exceptionally high. We tackle two fundamental questions that determine practical viability: which inter-rater reliability metrics best capture the alignment between LLM and human assessments, and how do we conduct statistically sound comparisons between competing systems? Through systematic experimentation, we discover that traditional agreement metrics like Krippendorff's alpha can be misleading in the skewed distributions typical of AI system evaluations. Instead, Gwet's AC2 and rank correlation coefficients emerge as more robust indicators for judge selection, while the Wilcoxon Signed-Rank Test with Benjamini-Hochberg corrections provides the statistical rigor needed for reliable system comparisons. Our findings suggest a path toward scalable, cost-effective evaluation that maintains the precision demanded by legal applications, transforming what was once a human-intensive bottleneck into an automated, yet statistically principled, evaluation framework.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) as judges to evaluate Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems in the context of legal document recommendation. It addresses two main research questions: (1) How to effectively evaluate and select LLM judges using inter-rater reliability (IRR) metrics, and (2) Which statistical methods are best for comparing RAG systems evaluated by LLM judges, considering multiple hypothesis testing corrections.  The study finds that traditional IRR metrics like Krippendorff's alpha can be misleading in skewed data distributions, and that Gwet's AC2 and rank correlation coefficients are more robust. It also advocates for using the Wilcoxon Signed-Rank Test with Benjamini-Hochberg corrections for statistically sound system comparisons. The paper's core argument is that a principled, statistically rigorous approach is necessary to transform the human-intensive evaluation bottleneck in legal RAG into an automated framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Relevance:** The problem addressed is highly relevant and timely. The evaluation bottleneck in RAG systems, especially within specialized domains like law, is a significant challenge. Automating this process using LLMs as judges could drastically reduce the cost and time associated with evaluation.

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the research questions and articulates the challenges of using LLMs as judges, including the potential for biases and inconsistencies.

*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper explores a range of IRR metrics and statistical methods in a systematic way, making it valuable for other researchers and practitioners.  The inclusion of multiple hypothesis testing corrections is a crucial aspect of ensuring the validity of system comparisons.

*   **Domain-Specific Focus:** The focus on legal RAG systems adds a layer of complexity and importance due to the high stakes of accuracy and reliability in legal applications.

*   **Actionable Recommendations:** The paper provides specific, actionable recommendations for selecting LLM judges (favoring Gwet's AC2 in skewed distributions, using rank correlations for ordering consistency) and comparing systems (using WSRT with B-H corrections).

*   **Well-Structured and Written:** The paper is clearly written, well-organized, and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Metrics/Methods:** While the *application* of these metrics and statistical tests to LLM-based RAG evaluation is valuable, the metrics and tests themselves are not new. The paper essentially advocates for using appropriate existing statistical techniques.  The novelty resides more in highlighting the pitfalls of using inappropriate techniques.

*   **Dataset Scale:** The dataset size of 117 anonymized legal user queries, while realistic and practically representative, may be considered relatively small, potentially limiting the generalizability of the findings.

*   **Lack of Comparative LLM Judge Assessment Detail:** The paper provides some comparative analysis between different LLMs as judges (GPT40, Llama2, etc.), but the detailed experimental methodology regarding the selection and training (if any) of these LLMs for judge roles could be more explicit.  How were these LLMs prompted/instructed to perform the evaluation tasks?  This is vital for reproducibility.

*   **Generality:** While the focus on legal RAG systems is a strength, it also limits the generality of the findings. It's unclear whether the recommendations for IRR metrics and statistical tests would apply equally well to other domains or RAG tasks.

**Significance:**

The paper is significant because it provides a concrete framework for evaluating LLMs as judges in specialized domains. It highlights the importance of choosing appropriate statistical tools and provides practical guidance for navigating the challenges of automated RAG evaluation.  It has the potential to accelerate the development and deployment of reliable RAG systems in the legal domain, where accuracy and trustworthiness are paramount. The focus on statistically sound methodologies will push the community towards more reproducible research.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**. The paper makes a valuable contribution by adapting and applying existing statistical best practices to the novel problem of using LLMs to evaluate RAG systems in a high-stakes domain. While the individual components (IRR metrics, statistical tests) are not groundbreakingly new, the way the paper synthesizes them, reveals their relative suitability in a specific LLM evaluation context, and provides actionable recommendations increases its contribution. A stronger case for higher novelty would be made with a substantially larger dataset demonstrating the generalizability of the findings across diverse query types and LLM models.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring Distributed Vector Databases Performance on HPC Platforms: A Study with Qdrant
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12384v1)
- **Authors**: Seth Ockerman, Amal Gueroudji, Song Young Oh, Robert Underwood, Nicholas Chia, Kyle Chard, Robert Ross, Shivaram Venkataraman
- **Abstract**: Vector databases have rapidly grown in popularity, enabling efficient similarity search over data such as text, images, and video. They now play a central role in modern AI workflows, aiding large language models by grounding model outputs in external literature through retrieval-augmented generation. Despite their importance, little is known about the performance characteristics of vector databases in high-performance computing (HPC) systems that drive large-scale science. This work presents an empirical study of distributed vector database performance on the Polaris supercomputer in the Argonne Leadership Computing Facility. We construct a realistic biological-text workload from BV-BRC and generate embeddings from the peS2o corpus using Qwen3-Embedding-4B. We select Qdrant to evaluate insertion, index construction, and query latency with up to 32 workers. Informed by practical lessons from our experience, this work takes a first step toward characterizing vector database performance on HPC platforms to guide future research and optimization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents an initial performance evaluation of the distributed vector database Qdrant on the Polaris supercomputer, focusing on a biology-related workflow.  The authors investigate embedding generation (using Qwen3-Embedding-4B on the pes2o corpus), data insertion, index building, and query performance using up to 32 Qdrant workers. The paper provides empirical results regarding the performance bottlenecks and scaling characteristics of Qdrant on an HPC system and makes recommendations for future optimizations and studies. The authors also make their dataset and query workload publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **First Look at HPC Vector Databases:** The paper's primary strength lies in being an early investigation into the performance of vector databases specifically within an HPC environment. Most existing research focuses on cloud or single-machine setups. This fills a gap in understanding how these databases behave in systems with high-performance interconnects, parallel filesystems, and other HPC-specific features.
    *   **Realistic Workload:** The use of a biological text-based workload based on BV-BRC and pes2o adds realism and relevance to the study. This sets it apart from purely synthetic benchmarks.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The authors provide practical insights and recommendations based on their deployment experience, like the limitations of asyncio for data insertion and the observation that index building easily saturates CPU resources on a node. These observations are valuable for practitioners deploying vector databases on HPC systems.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The publication of the embedding dataset and query workload promotes reproducibility and enables other researchers to build upon their work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study is limited to a single vector database (Qdrant) and a specific HPC system (Polaris).  Generalizability to other databases or architectures is not guaranteed.
    *   **Shallow Analysis:** While the paper identifies bottlenecks, the analysis of their root causes is sometimes high-level. For example, the discussion of network bandwidth limitations during data insertion could benefit from more detailed profiling.
    *   **CPU Focus:** The paper primarily focuses on CPU-based performance. While understandable given the system configuration, the absence of GPU-based indexing and querying is a significant omission, as many vector databases leverage GPUs for acceleration. The comment that work will explore GPU-indexing helps, but the lack of this comparison is still noteworthy.
    *   **Lack of Comparative Benchmarking:** The work lacks a comparative analysis against other vector databases on the same HPC platform. This would provide a stronger basis for understanding Qdrant's strengths and weaknesses.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The novelty is moderate. The core contribution is applying existing tools to a new (HPC) environment, but this application provides important insights that were previously unexplored. While the individual components are not revolutionary, their combination in this context is valuable.
    *   The significance is also moderate. The paper provides valuable initial insights for HPC users looking to integrate vector databases into their workflows. However, its impact is limited by its scope and the need for further research to validate and generalize the findings. The study can inform the design of HPC systems and vector database optimizations tailored for scientific workloads.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper warrants a **Score: 6**.

Here's the rationale: The paper makes a valuable and timely contribution by exploring vector database performance on HPC systems. It identifies important practical challenges and makes useful recommendations for those deploying these databases in HPC environments. The strengths of a realistic workload and practical insights are quite commendable.

However, the limitations of scope (single vector database, single HPC system, CPU focus) and the lack of comparative benchmarking temper its impact. The novelty is incremental rather than breakthrough. Follow-on work building on this foundation will be critical to demonstrating long-term impact and generalizability. While a promising start, it needs further breadth and depth to fully realize its potential.

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Evaluating Large Language Models for Functional and Maintainable Code in Industrial Settings: A Case Study at ASML
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12395v1)
- **Authors**: Yash Mundhra, Max Valk, Maliheh Izadi
- **Abstract**: Large language models have shown impressive performance in various domains, including code generation across diverse open-source domains. However, their applicability in proprietary industrial settings, where domain-specific constraints and code interdependencies are prevalent, remains largely unexplored. We present a case study conducted in collaboration with the leveling department at ASML to investigate the performance of LLMs in generating functional, maintainable code within a closed, highly specialized software environment. We developed an evaluation framework tailored to ASML's proprietary codebase and introduced a new benchmark. Additionally, we proposed a new evaluation metric, build@k, to assess whether LLM-generated code successfully compiles and integrates within real industrial repositories. We investigate various prompting techniques, compare the performance of generic and code-specific LLMs, and examine the impact of model size on code generation capabilities, using both match-based and execution-based metrics. The findings reveal that prompting techniques and model size have a significant impact on output quality, with few-shot and chain-of-thought prompting yielding the highest build success rates. The difference in performance between the code-specific LLMs and generic LLMs was less pronounced and varied substantially across different model families.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Evaluating Large Language Models for Functional and Maintainable Code in Industrial Settings: A Case Study at ASML":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the applicability of Large Language Models (LLMs) for code generation in a proprietary, domain-specific industrial setting at ASML.  It evaluates different prompting techniques (zero-shot, few-shot, chain-of-thought), compares generic and code-specific LLMs, and examines the impact of model size on the generated code's functionality, maintainability, and buildability within ASML's complex codebase. The authors introduce a new metric, `build@k`, to assess the successful integration of LLM-generated code into a real industrial repository. The study finds that few-shot and chain-of-thought prompting are significantly better than zero-shot, code-specific LLMs generally outperform generic ones (although results vary across model families), and larger models tend to perform better up to a certain point (around 14B parameters).

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a critical gap in the LLM literature: evaluating their performance in realistic, proprietary industrial settings. While much research focuses on open-source code and standardized benchmarks, this study tackles the challenges posed by domain-specific constraints, legacy code, and tight interdependencies that are common in industrial software systems. This focus on real-world applicability is a major strength.

Here's a breakdown of strengths and weaknesses:

**Strengths:**

*   **Real-world Relevance:** The study is conducted in collaboration with ASML, a major player in the semiconductor industry, making the results highly relevant to industrial practitioners.
*   **Novel Metric:** The introduction of the `build@k` metric is significant.  It's a much more practical and relevant measure of usefulness compared to traditional similarity-based metrics (BLEU, etc.) because it directly assesses whether the generated code can be integrated into the existing codebase and pass the build process. This is a key contribution.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper systematically investigates various factors, including prompting techniques, model types, and model sizes, using a combination of match-based, execution-based, and human-evaluated metrics.
*   **Clearly Defined Research Questions:** The research questions are well-defined and addressed in a structured manner.
*   **Reproducibility Considerations:** While the proprietary nature of the ASML codebase limits direct reproducibility, the paper clearly outlines the experimental setup, data preparation, and evaluation procedures, making it easier for other researchers to adapt the methodology to their own industrial settings.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Unit Test Coverage:**  The relatively low unit test coverage (only 27% of garages had associated tests) is a significant limitation. This means that the evaluation of functional correctness is incomplete and relies more heavily on the `build@k` metric, which only indicates integration success, not necessarily complete correctness. The paper mentions this as a limitation but it impacts the strength of conclusions about generated code functionality.
*   **Specificity of ASML Domain:** While the focus on ASML provides real-world relevance, it also limits the generalizability of the findings to other industrial domains. The unique characteristics of ASML's codebase and software architecture might influence the performance of LLMs. However, that’s clearly specified in the title as a case study.
*   **Manual Review Limited:** The number of cases for manual inspection should ideally be increased for more solid claims.
*   **Lack of Comparison to Alternatives:** The paper doesn't compare the performance of LLMs against alternative code generation techniques (e.g., rule-based systems, traditional program synthesis methods).  This would have provided a stronger baseline for evaluating the effectiveness of LLMs.
*   **Limited Exploration of Fine-tuning:** While code-specific LLMs are compared to general LLMs, the paper doesn't explore the potential of fine-tuning LLMs on ASML's internal code, which could have further improved performance. The authors did clearly indicate why they didn’t do this and noted they use parameter efficient training in future works.

**Significance:**

Despite its limitations, the paper is significant because it provides valuable empirical evidence on the challenges and opportunities of using LLMs in real-world industrial code generation. The introduction of the `build@k` metric and the systematic evaluation framework are particularly valuable contributions that can guide future research and practice in this area. The findings highlight the importance of prompting strategies, model specialization, and model size in achieving functional and maintainable code in complex industrial environments. It directly addresses a space not extensively explored (domain-specific in proprietary codebases).

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper earns a score of 7 because it makes a solid contribution to the field by addressing a practically important problem and introducing a relevant metric. It is well-written, the experiments are clearly described, and the results are thoroughly analyzed.

However, the score is not higher due to the limitations in unit test coverage, the specificity of the ASML domain (limiting generalizability), and the lack of comparison to non-LLM alternatives, and a smaller manual inspection of generated outputs. While the authors acknowledge these limitations, they do impact the strength of the conclusions and the overall impact of the paper. The paper is novel, significant, but several limitations need to be overcome for a stronger contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt Commons: Collective Prompting as Governance for Urban AI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12415v1)
- **Authors**: Rashid Mushkani
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are entering urban governance, yet their outputs are highly sensitive to prompts that carry value judgments. We propose Prompt Commons - a versioned, community-maintained repository of prompts with governance metadata, licensing, and moderation - to steer model behaviour toward pluralism. Using a Montreal dataset (443 human prompts; 3,317 after augmentation), we pilot three governance states (open, curated, veto-enabled). On a contested policy benchmark, a single-author prompt yields 24 percent neutral outcomes; commons-governed prompts raise neutrality to 48-52 percent while retaining decisiveness where appropriate. In a synthetic incident log, a veto-enabled regime reduces time-to-remediation for harmful outputs from 30.5 +/- 8.9 hours (open) to 5.6 +/- 1.5 hours. We outline licensing (CC BY/BY-SA for prompts with optional OpenRAIL-style restrictions for artefacts), auditable moderation, and safeguards against dominance capture. Prompt governance offers a practical lever for cities to align AI with local values and accountability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Prompt Commons: Collective Prompting as Governance for Urban AI" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes "Prompt Commons," a community-maintained repository of prompts with governance metadata, licensing, and moderation, to manage the behavior of LLMs used in urban governance toward pluralism.  The authors pilot three governance states (open, curated, veto-enabled) using a Montreal dataset of human prompts. Their results suggest that commons-governed prompts can raise neutrality in model outputs and reduce time-to-remediation for harmful outputs in a synthetic incident log. The paper also outlines licensing, auditable moderation, and safeguards against dominance capture.  The core argument is that prompt governance offers a practical way for cities to align AI with local values and accountability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of treating prompts as first-class governance objects is relatively novel. While prior work addresses dataset governance and post-training alignment, the explicit focus on prompts *and* their governance within a *civic* context is a distinguishing feature.  Existing work on "prompt engineering" typically focuses on optimizing model performance rather than democratic governance. The implementation of Ostrom's principles to prompt governance is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in the responsible AI deployment landscape for cities. With LLMs becoming increasingly integrated into urban planning and management, the sensitivity of model outputs to prompts poses a risk of embedding biases or prioritizing certain values over others. Providing a practical mechanism for aligning AI with local values and promoting accountability is highly significant.  Furthermore, the paper contributes to the growing body of literature emphasizing local innovation and governance capacity for responsible AI in cities.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practicality:** The Prompt Commons concept provides a concrete, actionable framework for governing LLMs in urban settings.
    *   **Empirical grounding:** The pilot study with the Montreal dataset offers initial evidence supporting the effectiveness of the proposed governance mechanisms.  The use of a contested policy benchmark and a synthetic incident log is a sensible approach.
    *   **Governance framework:** The paper outlines a well-reasoned governance framework, including licensing, moderation, and safeguards against dominance capture, drawing upon existing research in commons governance and open-source practice.
    *   **Explicit attention to limitations and future work:** the authors frankly address limitations of their evaluation, and identify key areas for future research, which adds to the paper's credibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The pilot study is limited to a single city (Montreal) and a specific set of urban scenes. Generalizability to other contexts may be limited.
    *   **Small Sample Size:** While the authors augment the original prompt set, the initial number of human-authored prompts (443) could still be considered small. Further studies with larger and more diverse datasets are needed.
    *   **Reliance on single LLM:** Cross-model replication is needed to assert if the results are model agnostic.
    *   **Subjectivity of Evaluation:** The use of community reviewers and Likert scales to assess subgroup satisfaction introduces a degree of subjectivity. While this is inherent in value-based evaluation, the potential for bias should be acknowledged.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development and deployment of LLMs in urban governance. It could inspire further research on prompt governance and lead to the creation of practical tools and platforms for cities to manage AI in a responsible and equitable manner. By demonstrating how Ostrom's principles can be applied to a novel technological domain, the paper can also contribute to the broader field of digital commons governance.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    While the paper has limitations in scope and sample size, the novelty and significance of the Prompt Commons concept, combined with the practical governance framework and empirical grounding, make it a worthwhile contribution. It opens up an important avenue for research and offers a concrete approach to addressing the governance challenges posed by LLMs in urban settings.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CY

### Understanding Prompt Management in GitHub Repositories: A Call for Best Practices
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12421v1)
- **Authors**: Hao Li, Hicham Masri, Filipe R. Cogo, Abdul Ali Bangash, Bram Adams, Ahmed E. Hassan
- **Abstract**: The rapid adoption of foundation models (e.g., large language models) has given rise to promptware, i.e., software built using natural language prompts. Effective management of prompts, such as organization and quality assurance, is essential yet challenging. In this study, we perform an empirical analysis of 24,800 open-source prompts from 92 GitHub repositories to investigate prompt management practices and quality attributes. Our findings reveal critical challenges such as considerable inconsistencies in prompt formatting, substantial internal and external prompt duplication, and frequent readability and spelling issues. Based on these findings, we provide actionable recommendations for developers to enhance the usability and maintainability of open-source prompts within the rapidly evolving promptware ecosystem.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Understanding Prompt Management in GitHub Repositories: A Call for Best Practices" empirically investigates how developers manage prompts in open-source GitHub repositories, a key element in the emerging area of "promptware." It analyzes 24,800 prompts from 92 repositories, revealing issues such as inconsistencies in formatting, significant duplication (both internal and external), and readability/spelling problems.  Based on these findings, the paper provides recommendations for improving the usability and maintainability of prompts, including standardizing formats, improving discoverability via categorization, mitigating duplication, and integrating automated quality assessment tools into repository workflows. The study identifies three categories of GitHub Prompt repositories: Prompt Collection, Prompt Application and Prompt Courseware. The most popular prompt storage format is Markdown, with a mixed preference for either single or multi-prompt files. 23.9% of prompt repositores had duplication issues. 80% of the analysed prompts are difficult to read and half the analyzed GitHub prompts have spelling errors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper addresses a relevant and timely problem. The rise of promptware as a software paradigm built on natural language prompts necessitates understanding how prompts are being managed. While prior work exists on prompts embedded in source code, this paper's focus on GitHub repositories specifically designed for prompt storage and reuse makes it relatively novel. The analysis of prompt quality attributes like readability and spelling within the GitHub context is also a valuable contribution.

* **Significance:** The study highlights critical challenges in prompt management, which is crucial for the long-term success and trustworthiness of promptware. The findings on formatting inconsistencies, duplication, and readability issues have practical implications for developers building and maintaining prompt-based applications. The recommendations provide a concrete path toward improved prompt management practices, which can enhance the usability, maintainability, and overall quality of promptware.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Empirical Analysis:** The large dataset of 24,800 prompts from 92 repositories provides a solid empirical foundation for the findings.
    * **Actionable Recommendations:** The paper goes beyond simply identifying problems and offers concrete and actionable recommendations for developers.
    * **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper examines prompt storage formats, organizational practices, duplication issues, and prompt quality attributes, providing a holistic view of the prompt management landscape.
    * **Relevant Category definition:** The three category definitions provides a nice way to define how these repositories are used.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Generalizability:** The study is limited to open-source GitHub repositories. The findings may not be directly generalizable to commercial or closed-source promptware development.
    * **Subjectivity in Readability Measurement:** While the paper uses a standard readability metric (Flesch Reading Ease), readability assessments are inherently subjective. The interpretation of the FRE scores and their implications for prompt usability could benefit from further qualitative analysis and user studies.
    * **Duplication Detection Method:** The methodology for detecting duplication focuses on identifying exact duplicates. The paper should discuss approaches that identify prompts with similar meanings, as this is a more common real-world challenge.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence prompt management practices within the promptware development community.  By raising awareness of the challenges and providing actionable recommendations, it can contribute to the development of better tools, standards, and workflows for prompt management. This can ultimately lead to more reliable, usable, and maintainable promptware applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the emerging field of promptware. It addresses a timely and relevant problem, provides a comprehensive empirical analysis, and offers actionable recommendations. However, the limitations regarding generalizability and potential subjectivity in readability assessment prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution.

### Small Models, Big Results: Achieving Superior Intent Extraction through Decomposition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12423v1)
- **Authors**: Danielle Cohen, Yoni Halpern, Noam Kahlon, Joel Oren, Omri Berkovitch, Sapir Caduri, Ido Dagan, Anatoly Efros
- **Abstract**: Understanding user intents from UI interaction trajectories remains a challenging, yet crucial, frontier in intelligent agent development. While massive, datacenter-based, multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) possess greater capacity to handle the complexities of such sequences, smaller models which can run on-device to provide a privacy-preserving, low-cost, and low-latency user experience, struggle with accurate intent inference. We address these limitations by introducing a novel decomposed approach: first, we perform structured interaction summarization, capturing key information from each user action. Second, we perform intent extraction using a fine-tuned model operating on the aggregated summaries. This method improves intent understanding in resource-constrained models, even surpassing the base performance of large MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, focusing on novelty, significance, and providing a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a two-stage approach to improve user intent extraction from UI interaction trajectories, especially for small, resource-constrained models. The first stage performs structured summarization of each atomic interaction, capturing key screen context and user action details. The second stage then uses a fine-tuned model to extract the overall user intent from the aggregated summaries. The authors demonstrate that this decomposed approach outperforms both smaller models and large MLLMs (in some settings) on public UI automation datasets, while addressing limitations like noisy data and large context windows. The work highlights specific design components, presents an evaluation, and provides a detailed error analysis to guide future improvements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the specific decomposition strategy for intent extraction, particularly its suitability for small models and on-device operation. While multi-stage summarization and intent extraction have been explored previously, this particular combination and the focus on structured summaries appears to be a contribution. The emphasis on improving smaller model performance specifically for UI-based intent extraction is also a differentiator.

*   **Significance:**  The potential impact is significant, especially if on-device intent extraction can be achieved effectively. This leads to preserving privacy, reducing latency, and reducing energy consumption, while supporting the use of intelligent agents in mobile and web environments. The comparative benchmark demonstrates that by adopting this method, the gap between large and small MLLMs can be significantly shortened. This is particularly important because large MLLMs are not always feasible, especially for on-device inference. The modularity of the architecture and the detailed analysis facilitate iterative improvements.
  There are areas that could be improved further. The paper could offer more insights on the limitations. For instance, it acknowledges several discrepancies between the dataset and real-world user behavior and also points out its limitation to Android and web environments. But what does this mean for generalization beyond these conditions?
  Also, from the data presented in Section 6.1, it can be seen that Gemini-1.5-Pro CoT is better than Gemini Flash 8B Decomposed-FT on Android Control data (0.634 vs 0.630). This could be better clarified to emphasize the cases when Decomposed-FT is advantageous.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined two-stage approach with specific design choices.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on multiple datasets.
    *   Detailed ablation studies to analyze the impact of different components.
    *   Interesting error analysis and propagation analysis.
    *   Computational cost and latency analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on existing UI automation datasets, which may not fully capture the complexity of real-world user behavior, remains a challenge. More emphasis could be placed on addressing these data limitations.
    *   The generalization of results to environments beyond Android and web is limited. The paper mentions this in the limitations section, but a more thorough discussion of potential challenges in other environments could be beneficial.
    *   While the authors did a good job with the cost analysis, it would be useful to investigate the trade-offs between complexity, performance and cost.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's approach and findings can influence the development of more efficient and privacy-preserving intelligent agents for mobile and web applications. The detailed analysis can also inform future research directions in UI intent extraction and related tasks.

**Justification for Score:**
Overall, the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of UI intent extraction. Although the evaluation uses existing datasets and can be strengthened with better generalization capability, the decomposition strategy and specific focus on small models are original and effective. The findings and analytical results offer practical insights for future research and development.

### Building Coding Agents via Entropy-Enhanced Multi-Turn Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12434v1)
- **Authors**: Jiahao Yu, Zelei Cheng, Xian Wu, Xinyu Xing
- **Abstract**: Software engineering presents complex, multi-step challenges for Large Language Models (LLMs), requiring reasoning over large codebases and coordinated tool use. The difficulty of these tasks is exemplified by benchmarks like SWE-bench, where current LLMs still struggle to resolve real-world issues. A promising approach to enhance performance is test-time scaling (TTS), but its gains are heavily dependent on the diversity of model outputs. While standard alignment methods such as Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and Kahneman-Tversky Optimization (KTO) are effective at aligning model outputs with human preferences, this process can come at the cost of reduced diversity, limiting the effectiveness of TTS. Additionally, existing preference optimization algorithms are typically designed for single-turn tasks and do not fully address the complexities of multi-turn reasoning and tool integration required for interactive coding agents. To bridge this gap, we introduce \sys, an entropy-enhanced framework that adapts existing preference optimization algorithms to the multi-turn, tool-assisted setting. \sys augments the preference objective to explicitly preserve policy entropy and generalizes learning to optimize over multi-turn interactions rather than single-turn responses. We validate \sys by fine-tuning a diverse suite of models from different families and sizes (up to 106B parameters). To maximize performance gains from TTS, we further propose a hybrid best-trajectory selection scheme combining a learned verifier model with model free approaches. On the \swebench leaderboard, our approach establishes new state-of-the-art results among open-weight models. A 30B parameter model trained with \sys ranks 1st on \lite and 4th on \verified on the open-weight leaderboard, surpassed only by models with over 10x more parameters(\eg$>$350B).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ENTROPO, a novel entropy-enhanced framework for building more effective coding agents using Large Language Models (LLMs).  It addresses the problem of diversity collapse during fine-tuning using preference optimization methods like DPO and KTO, which can hinder the effectiveness of test-time scaling (TTS). ENTROPO augments existing preference optimization algorithms with an explicit entropy regularization term, promoting policy diversity throughout multi-turn interactions required for complex software engineering tasks. The paper also proposes a hybrid best-trajectory selection scheme, combining a learned verifier model with model-free approaches to improve sampling effectiveness during TTS.  Empirical results on the SWE-bench leaderboard demonstrate that ENTROPO-trained models achieve state-of-the-art performance among open-weight models, highlighting the importance of preserving diversity for effective TTS.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the explicit incorporation of entropy regularization into preference optimization *for multi-turn, tool-assisted coding tasks*. While entropy regularization has been explored in single-turn settings, adapting it to the complexities of interactive coding agents is a significant contribution. The theoretical analysis of the close-form optimal policy further strengthens the approach. The hybrid trajectory selection method also adds value, by building on prior verification works while using the diversity from the entropy regularization for improved outcomes.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is twofold. First, it addresses a critical bottleneck in improving coding agent performance: the tendency for models to converge on a narrow set of solutions, limiting the benefits of TTS. Second, the empirical results clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of ENTROPO in overcoming this limitation, leading to substantial performance gains on challenging benchmarks like SWE-bench.  The fact that a 30B parameter model can rival or surpass models with >350B parameters underscores the importance of the proposed approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of diversity collapse and its implications for coding agent performance.
    *   **Sound Methodology:**  The ENTROPO framework is well-motivated and grounded in theoretical analysis.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The results on SWE-bench are compelling and demonstrate the practical benefits of ENTROPO. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper evaluates ENTROPO across a diverse set of models and benchmarks.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Releasing code, models, and datasets greatly enhances the reproducibility and impact of the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Existing Scaffolding:**  While building upon existing tooling is a practical choice, it could limit the generalizability of the approach to different coding environments or task formulations. It should be acknowledged to what extent the benefit comes from the framework vs the existing scaffolding.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Online RL:**  The paper briefly mentions the potential for extending ENTROPO to an online RL setting. A more detailed discussion of the challenges and opportunities associated with this extension would be valuable.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Entropy regularization adds computational complexity, especially in a multi-turn setting. While the paper demonstrates performance benefits, it doesn't explicitly quantify the added computational cost.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of more powerful and robust LLM-based coding agents.  By addressing the diversity collapse problem, ENTROPO paves the way for more effective exploration of the solution space, leading to better generalization and performance on real-world software engineering tasks.  The results are important for practitioners aiming to improve coding LLMs in general.

*   **Justification of Score:** I'm assigning a score of 8.  The paper addresses a significant problem, presents a novel and well-validated solution, and demonstrates strong empirical results. The weaknesses are relatively minor and do not detract significantly from the overall contribution. It's a solid, impactful paper that advances the state of the art in building coding agents, justifying the score.

### MedFact: Benchmarking the Fact-Checking Capabilities of Large Language Models on Chinese Medical Texts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12440v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayi He, Yangmin Huang, Qianyun Du, Xiangying Zhou, Zhiyang He, Jiaxue Hu, Xiaodong Tao, Lixian Lai
- **Abstract**: The increasing deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs) in healthcare necessitates a rigorous evaluation of their factual reliability. However, existing benchmarks are often limited by narrow domains of data, failing to capture the complexity of real-world medical information. To address this critical gap, we introduce MedFact, a new and challenging benchmark for Chinese medical fact-checking. MedFact comprises 2,116 expert-annotated instances curated from diverse real-world texts, spanning 13 medical specialties, 8 fine-grained error types, 4 writing styles, and multiple difficulty levels. Its construction employs a hybrid AI-human framework where iterative expert feedback refines an AI-driven, multi-criteria filtering process, ensuring both high data quality and difficulty. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 20 leading LLMs, benchmarking their performance on veracity classification and error localization against a human expert baseline. Our results reveal that while models can often determine if a text contains an error, precisely localizing it remains a substantial challenge, with even top-performing models falling short of human performance. Furthermore, our analysis uncovers a frequent ``over-criticism'' phenomenon, a tendency for models to misidentify correct information as erroneous, which is exacerbated by advanced reasoning techniques such as multi-agent collaboration and inference-time scaling. By highlighting these critical challenges for deploying LLMs in medical applications, MedFact provides a robust resource to drive the development of more factually reliable and medically aware models.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary, evaluation, and score for the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper "MedFact: Benchmarking the Fact-Checking Capabilities of Large Language Models on Chinese Medical Texts" introduces a new benchmark dataset, MedFact, specifically designed for evaluating the fact-checking abilities of large language models (LLMs) when dealing with Chinese medical texts. The dataset contains 2,116 instances of expertly annotated medical texts, covering 13 medical specialties, 8 fine-grained error types, and varying writing styles and difficulty levels. The authors rigorously constructed MedFact using a hybrid AI-human pipeline to ensure high data quality, diversity, and minimal contamination from LLM training data. They then benchmarked 20 leading LLMs on MedFact, assessing their performance in veracity classification and error localization. The results showed a significant performance gap between LLMs and human experts, highlighting challenges such as precise error localization and a tendency for models to "over-criticize" correct information. The paper analyzes the impact of advanced reasoning strategies on LLM performance and concludes that improvements in medical knowledge are critical for safe LLM deployment in medical applications.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of the MedFact dataset. While other medical benchmarks exist, MedFact distinguishes itself by focusing on Chinese medical texts, covering a broad range of real-world medical content, and employing a construction methodology designed to reduce data contamination. The rigorous pipeline, combining AI-driven filtering with expert human annotation, is a key strength. However, the paper does not present radically new techniques in the LLM evaluation space.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is substantial, stemming from the growing need to ensure the factual reliability of LLMs in healthcare. Medical misinformation can have severe consequences, making accurate fact-checking crucial. MedFact addresses a critical gap by providing a challenging and realistic benchmark for assessing LLM performance in this domain. The paper's findings on the limitations of current LLMs, particularly the difficulty with error localization and the "over-criticism" phenomenon, are valuable insights for guiding future research. These findings highlight the need for improving the medical domain knowledge of LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The MedFact dataset is well-defined, diverse, and rigorously constructed.
    *   The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of multiple LLMs.
    *   The analysis of model behavior, including the "over-criticism" phenomenon, is insightful.
    *   The construction process minimizes the risk of training data contamination.
    *   The paper highlights a practical need for verifiable and factually consistent LLMs in healthcare.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the dataset is novel, the evaluation methodology largely relies on standard techniques in the LLM space (zero-shot, CoT prompting). This is not necessarily a weakness, as establishing a solid baseline is important, but a more innovative evaluation framework could have further enhanced the paper's impact.
    *   The "over-criticism" observation is intriguing, but a deeper dive into the reasons behind this phenomenon (e.g., through error analysis and detailed case studies) would strengthen the argument.
    *   The paper primarily focuses on evaluating existing models and doesn't propose specific solutions for improving fact-checking in LLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:** MedFact will likely become a widely used benchmark for assessing LLMs in the Chinese medical domain. It provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners aiming to develop more reliable and medically aware LLMs. The findings will also encourage further exploration of techniques for improving error localization and mitigating the "over-criticism" phenomenon. By clearly demonstrating current limitations, the paper sets a direction for future research efforts.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contribution to the field. MedFact is a valuable and well-constructed dataset addressing a significant real-world problem. The comprehensive evaluation and insightful analysis are notable strengths. However, the relatively standard evaluation methodology and lack of proposed solutions prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. While not a groundbreaking contribution, the paper is an essential step towards reliable LLMs in healthcare and should serve as a critical resource for future research and development.
