### From Legacy Fortran to Portable Kokkos:An Autonomous Agentic AI Workflow
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12443v1)
- **Authors**: Sparsh Gupta, Kamalavasan Kamalakkannan, Maxim Moraru, Galen Shipman, Patrick Diehl
- **Abstract**: Scientific applications continue to rely on legacy Fortran codebases originally developed for homogeneous, CPU-based systems. As High-Performance Computing (HPC) shifts toward heterogeneous GPU-accelerated architectures, many accelerators lack native Fortran bindings, creating an urgent need to modernize legacy codes for portability. Frameworks like Kokkos provide performance portability and a single-source C++ abstraction, but manual Fortran-to-Kokkos porting demands significant expertise and time. Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in source-to-source code generation, yet their use in fully autonomous workflows for translating and optimizing parallel code remains largely unexplored, especially for performance portability across diverse hardware. This paper presents an agentic AI workflow where specialized LLM "agents" collaborate to translate, validate, compile, run, test, debug, and optimize Fortran kernels into portable Kokkos C++ programs. Results show the pipeline modernizes a range of benchmark kernels, producing performance-portable Kokkos codes across hardware partitions. Paid OpenAI models such as GPT-5 and o4-mini-high executed the workflow for only a few U.S. dollars, generating optimized codes that surpassed Fortran baselines, whereas open-source models like Llama4-Maverick often failed to yield functional codes. This work demonstrates the feasibility of agentic AI for Fortran-to-Kokkos transformation and offers a pathway for autonomously modernizing legacy scientific applications to run portably and efficiently on diverse supercomputers. It further highlights the potential of LLM-driven agentic systems to perform structured, domain-specific reasoning tasks in scientific and systems-oriented applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an agentic AI workflow designed to automatically translate and optimize legacy Fortran code into portable Kokkos C++ code for heterogeneous HPC systems. This workflow utilizes specialized LLM agents that collaborate to handle code translation, validation, compilation, execution, debugging, and optimization based on hardware profiler feedback. The authors evaluated the workflow on benchmark kernels from the NAS Parallel Benchmarks and OpenBLAS, demonstrating that it can produce functionally correct and performance-portable Kokkos implementations. They tested both proprietary (GPT-5, o4-mini-high) and open-source (Llama4-Maverick) LLMs, finding that the proprietary models were generally more successful in completing the workflow, often exceeding the performance of the original Fortran code at a cost of just a few dollars in API usage.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its **holistic, fully automated agentic AI workflow** for Fortran-to-Kokkos transformation, incorporating not just translation but also validation, testing, debugging, and performance optimization.  While individual components, such as LLM-based code translation, have been explored, the integration of these aspects within a single autonomous workflow targeted towards HPC portability is a significant step forward. The use of LLM-driven agents that actively learn from build failures, runtime errors, and functionality mismatches also adds to the novelty.  The integration of hardware profiler feedback into the optimization loop is a particularly strong element.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in HPC - the modernization of legacy Fortran code for increasingly heterogeneous architectures. By automating this process, the authors offer a potential path toward significantly reducing the time, expertise, and cost associated with migrating scientific applications to modern supercomputers. This could have a profound impact on fields that rely on legacy Fortran code, allowing them to leverage the performance of modern GPUs and other accelerators more easily. The economic viability, shown by the low cost of using OpenAI models to achieve substantial improvements, is another significant aspect.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Workflow:** The workflow addresses all key aspects of code modernization, from translation to performance tuning.
    *   **Agentic Approach:** The use of specialized agents allows for effective delegation of tasks and iterative refinement of the code.
    *   **Performance Results:** The demonstration of performance improvements over the original Fortran code is compelling.
    *   **Economic Feasibility:** The low cost of using proprietary LLMs makes the approach practical.
    *   **Clear Evaluation:**  The authors provide a good level of detail in their experimental setup, results, and ablation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Benchmark Suite:** While the benchmark kernels are well-known, they represent a relatively small set of HPC applications.  The claim of performance portability needs more extensive validation.
    *   **Functionality Testing Specificity:** The functionality testing framework is currently specific to the benchmark kernels used and needs to be generalized for broader applicability. This requires further validation that this framework can properly validate more complex codes.
    *   **Reliance on Proprietary Models:** While cost-effective in their experiments, the dependence on proprietary LLMs raises concerns about long-term accessibility and reproducibility. The open-source model evaluated (Llama4-Maverick) exhibited limitations, highlighting the current gap in performance. While there is mention of scalability, there is a lack of analysis of the overall scalability.

*   **Potential Impact:**  If the workflow can be generalized and scaled, it could significantly accelerate scientific discovery by lowering barriers to code modernization. It also offers a promising direction for applying agentic AI to complex engineering tasks in HPC and other domains. The workflow’s success may also encourage open-source LLM development teams to improve the performance on code-related tasks.

*   **Justification:** The paper presents a significant advance in the application of AI to HPC code modernization. The fully automated workflow, incorporating translation, testing, debugging, and optimization, represents a substantial contribution. The demonstration of performance improvements and economic feasibility further strengthens its impact. However, the limitations in benchmark suite, the specificity of the functionality testing and dependence on proprietary models prevent a score above 8.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### PromptSculptor: Multi-Agent Based Text-to-Image Prompt Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12446v1)
- **Authors**: Dawei Xiang, Wenyan Xu, Kexin Chu, Zixu Shen, Tianqi Ding, Wei Zhang
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of generative AI has democratized access to powerful tools such as Text-to-Image models. However, to generate high-quality images, users must still craft detailed prompts specifying scene, style, and context-often through multiple rounds of refinement. We propose PromptSculptor, a novel multi-agent framework that automates this iterative prompt optimization process. Our system decomposes the task into four specialized agents that work collaboratively to transform a short, vague user prompt into a comprehensive, refined prompt. By leveraging Chain-of-Thought reasoning, our framework effectively infers hidden context and enriches scene and background details. To iteratively refine the prompt, a self-evaluation agent aligns the modified prompt with the original input, while a feedback-tuning agent incorporates user feedback for further refinement. Experimental results demonstrate that PromptSculptor significantly enhances output quality and reduces the number of iterations needed for user satisfaction. Moreover, its model-agnostic design allows seamless integration with various T2I models, paving the way for industrial applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "PromptSculptor: Multi-Agent Based Text-to-Image Prompt Optimization" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PromptSculptor, a novel multi-agent system (MAS) designed to automate the iterative process of optimizing text prompts for Text-to-Image (T2I) models. The system decomposes the task into four specialized agents: Intent Inference Agent, Scene and Style Agent, Feedback and Tuning Agent, and Self-Evaluation Agent. These agents collaboratively transform a short, vague user prompt into a comprehensive, refined prompt. The system uses Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning to infer hidden context and enrich scene details. A self-evaluation agent aligns the modified prompt with the original input, and a feedback-tuning agent incorporates user feedback for further refinement. Experimental results demonstrate improved output quality, reduced iterations for user satisfaction, and model-agnostic integration with various T2I models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its multi-agent approach to prompt optimization. While prompt engineering and optimization have been explored before, the decomposition of the task into specialized agents working in a collaborative manner is a relatively novel approach. The integration of CoT reasoning for intent inference and the self-evaluation agent based on a Vision-Language Model (VLM) provide additional unique components. The idea of incorporating an evaluation of the generated image's *semantic* alignment using BLIP-2 to determine if the prompt needs further refinement is also a good idea.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from its potential to lower the barrier to entry for new AI users. By automating prompt engineering, PromptSculptor could make T2I models more accessible and user-friendly. The model-agnostic design allows for widespread application across different T2I models, further enhancing its practical value.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The multi-agent architecture provides a clear and well-defined structure for the prompt optimization process.
    *   The use of CoT reasoning enhances the system's ability to understand user intent and enrich scene details.
    *   The self-evaluation agent and feedback-tuning agent create an iterative refinement loop that improves output quality.
    *   Experimental results demonstrate improvements over baseline methods in terms of output quality and number of iterations.
    * The ablation study shows that the self-evaluation agent does improve the quality of the generated prompts.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on GPT-4 (or a similar LLM) for the agents' functionality means the system's performance is inherently limited by the capabilities and biases of the underlying LLM.
    *   While model-agnostic in terms of T2I models, it does rely on access to large language models and the CLIP and BLIP-2 models for evaluation, limiting its potential portability or use in resource constrained environments.
    *   The evaluation, while including human preference scores, could benefit from more rigorous and diverse quantitative metrics, especially focusing on the subjective quality aspects of images. It would have been good to get human raters who were not part of the design process to evaluate the system.
    *   The human eval is vague regarding the specifics about *how* the users were recruited or what demographics they represented. This reduces the reliability of the eval since its hard to discern whether biases were inadvertently introduced.
    * The paper could be improved by discussing the limitations of the agent, especially considering edge-case scenarios and the potentially negative impact from using LLMs (for example, biases).
*   **Potential Influence:**  If successful in real-world applications, PromptSculptor could significantly impact how users interact with T2I models and other generative AI tools. It could empower non-experts to create high-quality images and streamline the prompt engineering workflow for professionals. The multi-agent design could inspire similar approaches to other complex AI tasks.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable and novel contribution to the field of prompt engineering. The multi-agent approach is well-structured and shows promising results. However, the dependence on powerful LLMs and the somewhat limited evaluation prevent a higher score. While the core idea is strong, further work is needed to address the identified weaknesses and fully demonstrate its broad applicability and robustness.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.MA
- **Score**: 7/10

### Redefining Website Fingerprinting Attacks With Multiagent LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12462v1)
- **Authors**: Chuxu Song, Dheekshith Dev Manohar Mekala, Hao Wang, Richard Martin
- **Abstract**: Website Fingerprinting (WFP) uses deep learning models to classify encrypted network traffic to infer visited websites. While historically effective, prior methods fail to generalize to modern web environments. Single-page applications (SPAs) eliminate the paradigm of websites as sets of discrete pages, undermining page-based classification, and traffic from scripted browsers lacks the behavioral richness seen in real user sessions. Our study reveals that users exhibit highly diverse behaviors even on the same website, producing traffic patterns that vary significantly across individuals. This behavioral entropy makes WFP a harder problem than previously assumed and highlights the need for larger, more diverse, and representative datasets to achieve robust performance. To address this, we propose a new paradigm: we drop session-boundaries in favor of contiguous traffic segments and develop a scalable data generation pipeline using large language models (LLM) agents. These multi-agent systems coordinate decision-making and browser interaction to simulate realistic, persona-driven browsing behavior at 3--5x lower cost than human collection. We evaluate nine state-of-the-art WFP models on traffic from 20 modern websites browsed by 30 real users, and compare training performance across human, scripted, and LLM-generated datasets. All models achieve under 10\% accuracy when trained on scripted traffic and tested on human data. In contrast, LLM-generated traffic boosts accuracy into the 80\% range, demonstrating strong generalization to real-world traces. Our findings indicate that for modern WFP, model performance is increasingly bottlenecked by data quality, and that scalable, semantically grounded synthetic traffic is essential for capturing the complexity of real user behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Redefining Website Fingerprinting Attacks with Multi-Agent LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges of website fingerprinting (WFP) attacks in modern web environments dominated by single-page applications (SPAs) and diverse user behaviors. Traditional WFP methods, relying on page-based classification and scripted browser traffic, fail to generalize to these environments. The authors demonstrate that user behavior introduces significant entropy, making WFP harder than previously assumed. To overcome these limitations, they propose a new paradigm: dropping session boundaries in favor of contiguous traffic segments and developing a scalable data generation pipeline using LLM agents. These agents simulate realistic, persona-driven browsing behavior at a lower cost than human collection. The authors evaluate state-of-the-art WFP models on traffic from modern websites, comparing training performance across human, scripted, and LLM-generated datasets. Results show that LLM-generated traffic significantly improves accuracy, demonstrating strong generalization to real-world traces. The paper concludes that data quality, driven by realistic user behavior modeling, is crucial for modern WFP, and that scalable, semantically grounded synthetic traffic is essential.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key areas:

    *   **Problem Framing:** Explicitly identifying and framing the challenges posed by modern web architectures (SPAs, dynamic content) and user behavior to WFP attacks is crucial.  While prior works have noted the shift, this paper deeply investigates and quantifies the performance drop of existing methods.
    *   **Data Generation using LLMs:** The use of LLM agents to simulate realistic browsing behavior for WFP is a novel and impactful contribution. The approach effectively balances realism, controllability, and scalability in data generation.
    *   **Evaluation Methodology:** The rigorous evaluation methodology, involving real user data, scripted data, and LLM-generated data, allows for a thorough comparison of different training/testing regimes. The Leave-One-User evaluation is particularly important.
    *   **Finding & Insight:**  The key insight that model performance is increasingly bottlenecked by data quality rather than model architecture, and that LLM data can close this gap, is extremely valuable.

*   **Significance:** The work has significant implications for the field of WFP:

    *   **Revisiting Assumptions:** The paper challenges the traditional assumptions of WFP research, highlighting the limitations of page-based classification and scripted traffic.
    *   **Improved Generalization:**  The proposed LLM-based data generation pipeline significantly improves the generalization of WFP models to real-world scenarios. This is crucial for the practical applicability of WFP techniques.
    *   **New Directions for Research:** The work opens new avenues for WFP research, focusing on more realistic data generation, personalized simulation, and targeted fingerprinting.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong empirical validation with real-world data.
    *   Well-defined problem and clearly articulated contributions.
    *   Rigorous evaluation methodology and insightful analysis.
    *   Addresses a critical gap in the WFP literature by focusing on data quality.
    *   Provides a scalable and cost-effective alternative to human data collection.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Reliance on Commercial LLM APIs: The framework depends on commercial LLM APIs, which might introduce limitations in terms of cost, availability, and control.  Exploring open-source LLM integration could strengthen the work.
    *   Limited Scale of LLM-Sim Dataset:  The reduced dataset size of LLM-Sim compared to Scripted Browsers Dataset due to API constraints is a weakness, although the results still demonstrate LLM-Sim's superiority in quality. More work in lowering the costs to generate a much bigger dataset would be important.
    *   Limited Scope of Web Interaction:  The agent interaction is still relatively simple, focusing primarily on scrolling, clicking, and typing.  Incorporating more sophisticated interactions (e.g., form filling, complex media interactions) could further improve realism.

* **Justification for Score:**
    The paper presents substantial novelty through its problem framing, LLM-based data generation approach, and evaluation methodology. The significance is high, as it fundamentally challenges existing assumptions and offers a practical solution to improve WFP model generalization. While the limitations of the use of the LLM APIs are noted in the evaluation, the demonstrated improvement in comparison to scripted traffic gives confidence that it is a worthwhile avenue to continue to improve upon. Overall, the paper presents a strong, well-executed, and impactful contribution to the field.

### Reasoning Models Can be Accurately Pruned Via Chain-of-Thought Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12464v1)
- **Authors**: Ryan Lucas, Kayhan Behdin, Zhipeng Wang, Qingquan Song, Shao Tang, Rahul Mazumder
- **Abstract**: Reasoning language models such as DeepSeek-R1 produce long chain-of-thought traces during inference time which make them costly to deploy at scale. We show that using compression techniques such as neural network pruning produces greater performance loss than in typical language modeling tasks, and in some cases can make the model slower since they cause the model to produce more thinking tokens but with worse performance. We show that this is partly due to the fact that standard LLM pruning methods often focus on input reconstruction, whereas reasoning is a decode-dominated task. We introduce a simple, drop-in fix: during pruning we jointly reconstruct activations from the input and the model's on-policy chain-of-thought traces. This "Reasoning-Aware Compression" (RAC) integrates seamlessly into existing pruning workflows such as SparseGPT, and boosts their performance significantly. Code reproducing the results in the paper can be found at: https://github.com/RyanLucas3/RAC
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of compressing large language models (LLMs) used for reasoning tasks.  It observes that standard compression techniques like pruning, when applied to reasoning LLMs, can disproportionately degrade accuracy and even increase inference time. The core argument is that existing pruning methods primarily focus on reconstructing input activations, while reasoning tasks are decode-dominated, with a significant portion of activations arising from the model's own generated "chain-of-thought" (CoT). To address this, the paper proposes "Reasoning-Aware Compression" (RAC), a simple modification to existing pruning workflows. RAC augments the calibration data used for pruning with activations derived from the model's own on-policy CoT traces, simulating the decoding process. The paper demonstrates that RAC significantly improves accuracy and stabilizes CoT generation compared to standard pruning methods, especially at high sparsity levels, while also reducing the tendency for pruned models to produce excessively long and unreliable reasoning traces.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of incorporating on-policy CoT activations into the pruning calibration data is novel and directly addresses a crucial observation: reasoning LLMs are decode-dominated. The observation itself, while perhaps intuitive in retrospect, is explicitly articulated and validated through experiments. The RAC method is simple to implement, seamlessly integrating into existing pruning algorithms like SparseGPT. The idea of adapting the calibration distribution to better match the operational workload is an insight that is broadly applicable.

*   **Significance:** The paper's contribution is significant because it offers a practical solution to a real-world problem: deploying reasoning LLMs at scale.  The increasing computational cost of these models is a major barrier to their wider adoption. Improving the effectiveness of compression techniques directly addresses this barrier. The experimental results, particularly the accuracy improvements at higher sparsity levels, are compelling. Further, the observation that standard pruning can increase inference time by leading to longer, less reliable reasoning traces is an important finding. By stabilizing CoT generation, RAC offers a pathway to more efficient and reliable reasoning with compressed models. The throughput analysis also highlights the benefits of combining RAC with other compression techniques like quantization.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-defined problem statement.
    *   Simple yet effective proposed solution.
    *   Strong empirical validation across different model sizes and reasoning tasks.
    *   Insightful analysis of the causes of performance degradation with standard pruning.
    *   Easy to implement and integrate with existing workflows.
    *   Provides a pathway to improving runtime overhead due to long and unreliable CoTs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The increase in calibration cost due to generating CoT traces, especially with large decode budgets, is acknowledged but not explored deeply. Trade-offs between the length of the calibration traces and performance improvements are not examined. A more detailed runtime analysis comparing RAC and standard pruning methods would also be welcome.
    *   The method is primarily evaluated with unstructured pruning. While a section of the ablation study explores Structured Pruning and Quantization, broader explorations of structured pruning methodologies are lacking.
    *   The scope of experimentation, while strong, could be expanded to include a wider range of reasoning tasks and model architectures.
    *   The paper states that the increased inference runtime due to long CoTs is somewhat addressed. Deeper exploration as to why pruning increases CoT length when a smaller model should (arguably) output faster and shorter CoTs might yield further refinement to the RAC method.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper has the potential to significantly influence the way LLMs are compressed for reasoning tasks. The RAC approach could become a standard component in compression pipelines.
    *   The insights about the importance of matching the calibration distribution to the operational workload could inspire new research in other areas of LLM compression and optimization.
    *   The paper could encourage further research into developing compression techniques that explicitly optimize for inference time, rather than just model size.
    *   The ideas could lead to improvements in other tasks where calibration data can be modified and adapted for specific tasks.

Overall, the paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM compression. The RAC method is simple, effective, and addresses a real-world problem in a practical way. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses.

### Empowering Clinical Trial Design through AI: A Randomized Evaluation of PowerGPT
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12471v1)
- **Authors**: Yiwen Lu, Lu Li, Dazheng Zhang, Xinyao Jian, Tingyin Wang, Siqi Chen, Yuqing Lei, Jiayi Tong, Zhaohan Xi, Haitao Chu, Chongliang Luo, Alexis Ogdie, Brian Athey, Alparslan Turan, Michael Abramoff, Joseph C Cappelleri, Hua Xu, Yun Lu, Jesse Berlin, Daniel I. Sessler, David A. Asch, Xiaoqian Jiang, Yong Chen
- **Abstract**: Sample size calculations for power analysis are critical for clinical research and trial design, yet their complexity and reliance on statistical expertise create barriers for many researchers. We introduce PowerGPT, an AI-powered system integrating large language models (LLMs) with statistical engines to automate test selection and sample size estimation in trial design. In a randomized trial to evaluate its effectiveness, PowerGPT significantly improved task completion rates (99.3% vs. 88.9% for test selection, 99.3% vs. 77.8% for sample size calculation) and accuracy (94.1% vs. 55.4% in sample size estimation, p < 0.001), while reducing average completion time (4.0 vs. 9.3 minutes, p < 0.001). These gains were consistent across various statistical tests and benefited both statisticians and non-statisticians as well as bridging expertise gaps. Already under deployment across multiple institutions, PowerGPT represents a scalable AI-driven approach that enhances accessibility, efficiency, and accuracy in statistical power analysis for clinical research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Empowering Clinical Trial Design through AI: A Randomized Evaluation of PowerGPT":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PowerGPT, an AI-powered system that integrates large language models (LLMs) with statistical engines to automate statistical test selection and sample size estimation for clinical trial design. The authors conducted a randomized controlled trial (RCT) comparing PowerGPT's performance to traditional methods (textbooks, statistical software, online searches) in two institutions (UPenn and UTHealth).  The RCT assessed task completion rates, accuracy, and time spent on tasks. The results indicate that PowerGPT significantly improved task completion rates, accuracy, and reduced completion time compared to traditional methods.  These improvements were consistent across different statistical tests and benefited both statisticians and non-statisticians. The authors conclude that PowerGPT is a scalable AI-driven approach that enhances accessibility, efficiency, and accuracy in statistical power analysis for clinical research. The system is openly accessible and freely available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly addresses a real-world problem: the difficulty and time involved in performing power analyses for clinical trial design, particularly for non-statisticians.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The RCT design is a significant strength. It provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of PowerGPT compared to traditional methods. The use of a balanced and stratified randomization approach adds to the rigor. The statistical analysis methods are appropriate for the data collected.
*   **Quantifiable Results:** The paper presents clear and quantifiable results showing significant improvements in completion rate, accuracy, and time reduction. The statistical significance is high (p < 0.001).
*   **Practical Impact:** The development of a freely available tool (PowerGPT) with demonstrable benefits has significant practical implications for clinical researchers, potentially leading to more efficient and reliable trial design. The deployment and piloting at multiple institutions further strengthens this point.
*   **Stratified Analysis:** The stratified analysis based on the statistical expertise level is valuable, showing how PowerGPT bridges the expertise gap.
*   **Detailed description of the PowerGPT architecture and the prompt engineering process:** The paper describes how prompts are structured to ensure that the LLM accurately calls external APIs for computational functions. The inclusion of Github link allows future researchers to reproduce the work.
*   **Careful attention to security, privacy, scalability and concurrency:** The authors described the security measures that were adopted to make PowerGPT HIPAA-compliant and designed to handle a large number of queries.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Statistical Tests:** While the paper covers a range of commonly used statistical tests, it acknowledges that the generalizability to more nuanced or domain-specific power analyses remains to be explored. This is a key limitation.
*   **Focus on a Specific Implementation:** The evaluation focuses specifically on PowerGPT. While the results are positive, it's unclear how easily these findings would generalize to other similar AI-assisted statistical tools.
*   **Lack of Long-Term Usability Data:** The RCT provides a snapshot of performance, but it doesn't address long-term usability, potential biases in using the tool, or how users' trust in AI-assisted statistical decision-making evolves over time. This is an important area for future research.
*   **Limited assessment of bias or errors in edge cases:** the study mentions that the PowerGPT underwent validity checking, there is no mention of edge case assessment or how to detect and eliminate the errors.
*   **Limited comparison to prior LLM-based approaches**: The paper mentions some limitations of prior LLM-based approaches, it does not directly compare PowerGPT performance to a similar LLM tool.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The integration of LLMs with statistical engines for automated power analysis is a novel approach. While there have been previous attempts to use AI in statistical analysis, the rigorously evaluated demonstration of improved performance metrics (completion rate, accuracy, and time) in a controlled trial setting is a significant contribution. PowerGPT makes sophisticated analyses accessible and bridges expertise gaps. The practical implications for clinical research are substantial.

**Justification for Score:**

PowerGPT represents a significant step forward in automating and democratizing statistical power analysis. The RCT provides compelling evidence for its effectiveness. However, the limitations related to the scope of statistical tests, generalizability beyond PowerGPT, and lack of long-term usability data warrant a slightly lower score.

### FunAudio-ASR Technical Report
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12508v1)
- **Authors**: Keyu An, Yanni Chen, Chong Deng, Changfeng Gao, Zhifu Gao, Bo Gong, Xiangang Li, Yabin Li, Xiang Lv, Yunjie Ji, Yiheng Jiang, Bin Ma, Haoneng Luo, Chongjia Ni, Zexu Pan, Yiping Peng, Zhendong Peng, Peiyao Wang, Hao Wang, Wen Wang, Wupeng Wang, Biao Tian, Zhentao Tan, Nan Yang, Bin Yuan, Jieping Ye, Jixing Yu, Qinglin Zhang, Kun Zou, Han Zhao, Shengkui Zhao, Jingren Zhou
- **Abstract**: In recent years, automatic speech recognition (ASR) has witnessed transformative advancements driven by three complementary paradigms: data scaling, model size scaling, and deep integration with large language models (LLMs). However, LLMs are prone to hallucination, which can significantly degrade user experience in real-world ASR applications. In this paper, we present FunAudio-ASR, a large-scale, LLM-based ASR system that synergistically combines massive data, large model capacity, LLM integration, and reinforcement learning to achieve state-of-the-art performance across diverse and complex speech recognition scenarios. Moreover, FunAudio-ASR is specifically optimized for practical deployment, with enhancements in streaming capability, noise robustness, code-switching, hotword customization, and satisfying other real-world application requirements. Experimental results show that while most LLM-based ASR systems achieve strong performance on open-source benchmarks, they often underperform on real industry evaluation sets. Thanks to production-oriented optimizations, FunAudio-ASR achieves SOTA performance on real application datasets, demonstrating its effectiveness and robustness in practical settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the FunAudio-ASR Technical Report:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FunAudio-ASR, a large-scale, LLM-based automatic speech recognition (ASR) system designed to achieve state-of-the-art performance in diverse and challenging speech recognition scenarios. It synergistically combines massive data, large model capacity, LLM integration, and reinforcement learning. The system is also optimized for practical deployment, with specific enhancements for streaming capability, noise robustness, code-switching, and hotword customization. The paper presents experimental results demonstrating SOTA performance on real application datasets, showcasing its effectiveness and robustness compared to other LLM-based ASR systems, which often underperform on real-world evaluations despite excelling on open-source benchmarks. The paper details the model architecture, training data, training paradigm (including pre-training and fine-tuning strategies), production-oriented optimizations, and evaluation results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper claims a synergistic combination of existing paradigms (data scaling, model size scaling, LLM integration, and RL). While combining these techniques is not inherently novel, the specific implementation details and the engineering efforts to optimize it for real-world deployment contribute to the paper's novelty.  The use of a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) mechanism for hotword customization is interesting. The initialization of Best-RQ with a pre-trained text LLM is also a worthwhile contribution.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems primarily from the system's practical performance and its production-oriented optimizations. Demonstrating SOTA results on real-world industrial datasets is impactful because it addresses a known gap where academic models often falter. The focus on robustness (noise, code-switching), low-latency streaming, and hotword customization increases its utility for real-world applications. This is particularly important because it tackles the hallucination problems commonly associated with LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong performance on real-world datasets, exceeding open-source models and commercial APIs.
    *   Comprehensive description of production-oriented optimizations, addressing critical challenges for real-world deployment.
    *   Detailed experimental setup and evaluation, providing strong evidence for the system's capabilities.
    *   Clear articulation of the FunRL framework for RL training, which is an important aspect to facilitate efficient reinforcement learning for LALMs.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The descriptions of individual components may lack sufficient depth for replication.
    *   The evaluation section, while thorough, could benefit from more ablation studies isolating the impact of specific optimizations.
    *   Limited language support may restrict its broader applicability. The paper should also have discussed safety aspects and privacy considerations.
*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of future ASR systems by highlighting the importance of production readiness and real-world performance, rather than solely focusing on open-source benchmarks. The RAG based hotword customization may contribute to the creation of better voice command interfaces. The insights into training with RL can contribute to reduced hallucinations.
*   **Rationale:** Although the individual components are not radically new, the combination of existing LLM techniques with production-oriented engineering does seem to have achieved excellent results compared to other open-source and commercial APIs in challenging industrial environments. It is likely that the engineering aspects represent a significant portion of the effort. Given the emphasis on practical application and robust results, the paper makes a solid contribution by focusing on the critical elements that are required to bring a large language model from research environments into the real world.

### A comparison of pipelines for the translation of a low resource language based on transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12514v1)
- **Authors**: Chiara Bonfanti, Michele Colombino, Giulia Coucourde, Faeze Memari, Stefano Pinardi, Rosa Meo
- **Abstract**: This work compares three pipelines for training transformer-based neural networks to produce machine translators for Bambara, a Mand\`e language spoken in Africa by about 14,188,850 people. The first pipeline trains a simple transformer to translate sentences from French into Bambara. The second fine-tunes LLaMA3 (3B-8B) instructor models using decoder-only architectures for French-to-Bambara translation. Models from the first two pipelines were trained with different hyperparameter combinations to improve BLEU and chrF scores, evaluated on both test sentences and official Bambara benchmarks. The third pipeline uses language distillation with a student-teacher dual neural network to integrate Bambara into a pre-trained LaBSE model, which provides language-agnostic embeddings. A BERT extension is then applied to LaBSE to generate translations. All pipelines were tested on Dokotoro (medical) and Bayelemagaba (mixed domains). Results show that the first pipeline, although simpler, achieves the best translation accuracy (10% BLEU, 21% chrF on Bayelemagaba), consistent with low-resource translation results. On the Yiri dataset, created for this work, it achieves 33.81% BLEU and 41% chrF. Instructor-based models perform better on single datasets than on aggregated collections, suggesting they capture dataset-specific patterns more effectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper compares three different pipelines for machine translation from French to Bambara, a low-resource African language. The pipelines are:
1.  A standard transformer model trained from scratch.
2.  Fine-tuning LLaMA 3 (3B-8B parameter) with instruction-based prompts.
3.  A novel pipeline called LoReB, which utilizes a combination of techniques: multilingual BERT models via knowledge distillation (using LaBSE as the teacher), LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) for parameter-efficient fine-tuning, and a lightweight BERT-based enhancement during decoding.

The pipelines were tested on several benchmarks and a new dataset (Yiri) created by the authors. The results suggest that the transformer model trained from scratch, despite its simplicity, achieves the best accuracy scores, especially on the Yiri dataset. The LLaMA 3 models show improved performance after fine-tuning but require more data. LoReB, while incorporating sophisticated techniques, performed lower than expected, indicating challenges in effectively combining these approaches for low-resource languages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The creation of the Yiri dataset is a valuable contribution to the field, providing a new resource for Bambara-French machine translation.
    *   The LoReB pipeline is novel in its specific combination of techniques (knowledge distillation, LoRA, and BERT-based decoding enhancement), though each component is individually established.
    *   The comparative study of three different machine translation approaches in the context of a very low-resource language is a worthwhile endeavor.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The study addresses a significant problem: enhancing machine translation for low-resource languages, thereby improving access to information and bridging digital divides.
    *   The Yiri dataset provides a valuable resource for future research on Bambara NLP.
    *   The paper's finding that a simple transformer model performs surprisingly well is a useful insight for researchers working with limited data. It suggests that sophisticated techniques are not always the most effective, especially when data is scarce.
    *   The analysis of the different techniques contributes to a better understanding of their effectiveness in low-resource scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-structured and clearly written.
    *   The experimental setup is well-defined.
    *   The authors provide a good overview of related work.
    *   The creation and release of the Yiri dataset is a significant contribution.
    *   The ablation experiments and analysis of the different components of LoReB is insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The results for the LoReB pipeline are somewhat disappointing, suggesting potential issues with the implementation or hyperparameter tuning of the combined approach. While the failure of LoReB is an interesting result, more thorough analysis on *why* it didn't work would be helpful. Was it the dimensionality mismatch even with the added layer? Was there a conflict between the distillation loss and the decoder's objective?
    *   While the comparison is valuable, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth error analysis to understand the types of errors made by each pipeline. For instance, a breakdown of the types of translation errors (e.g., lexical errors, syntactic errors, semantic errors) for each model would be insightful.
    *   It seems that FLORES+ consistently results in poor results. Why? The paper only mentions that similar results have been found in other papers with NMT for LRL, but offers no specific reason for *why* this is the case.

*   **Impact:**
    *   The Yiri dataset is likely to be used by other researchers working on Bambara NLP.
    *   The findings will inform future research on machine translation for low-resource languages, particularly in choosing appropriate architectures and training techniques.
    *   The LoReB pipeline provides a basis for further investigation and optimization.

*   **Rationale for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **6.5**. The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of low-resource machine translation. The creation and release of the Yiri dataset is a major positive. The comparative analysis of different approaches is useful. However, the disappointing results of the LoReB pipeline, and the lack of thorough analysis as to *why* this result occurred, detracts from the impact somewhat. The results for the FLORES+ benchmarks are consistently low, and the paper does not give a solid reason for why this is happening. A deeper error analysis would have further strengthened the paper. Given its current form, the paper serves as a valuable step, but requires further refinement before truly being a high-impact publication.

### Context-Aware Language Models for Forecasting Market Impact from Sequences of Financial News
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12519v1)
- **Authors**: Ross Koval, Nicholas Andrews, Xifeng Yan
- **Abstract**: Financial news plays a critical role in the information diffusion process in financial markets and is a known driver of stock prices. However, the information in each news article is not necessarily self-contained, often requiring a broader understanding of the historical news coverage for accurate interpretation. Further, identifying and incorporating the most relevant contextual information presents significant challenges. In this work, we explore the value of historical context in the ability of large language models to understand the market impact of financial news. We find that historical context provides a consistent and significant improvement in performance across methods and time horizons. To this end, we propose an efficient and effective contextualization method that uses a large LM to process the main article, while a small LM encodes the historical context into concise summary embeddings that are then aligned with the large model's representation space. We explore the behavior of the model through multiple qualitative and quantitative interpretability tests and reveal insights into the value of contextualization. Finally, we demonstrate that the value of historical context in model predictions has real-world applications, translating to substantial improvements in simulated investment performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how historical context improves the ability of language models to forecast the market impact of financial news. It proposes an efficient "Prefix Summary Context" (PSC) method, which uses a smaller language model to summarize historical news into concise embeddings, then aligns and prepends these embeddings to the main article input of a larger language model.  The method is evaluated on a financial news classification task, showing consistent and significant performance gains with historical context across different time horizons and compared to various baselines, including long-context methods and financial forecasting models. Furthermore, the paper includes interpretability analyses, demonstrating the model's ability to account for news staleness, and portfolio simulations, showing improved investment performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using historical context to improve financial forecasting isn't entirely new (previous work has explored using historical financial news data for predicting stock prices), but the *specific approach* using a small language model for summarizing context and aligning it with a larger LM is a solid contribution.  The architecture is novel, especially the prefix summary context and the alignment strategy. Using cross-model attention to align the context embeddings with the main article embeddings is an interesting and potentially generalizable technique. The hybrid retrieval method based on both semantic similarity and timeliness is also a valuable refinement. However, parts of the general idea are well-explored and the improvements in AUC are relatively marginal (though the authors do a good job of showing that those marginal improvements can lead to large gains in investment performance).

*   **Significance:** The paper makes several important contributions to the field. First, it empirically demonstrates the significant value of historical context in financial forecasting, a finding that could have practical implications for investment strategies. Second, it introduces a computationally efficient and effective method for incorporating context into language models, addressing the challenges of processing long sequences and potentially mitigating issues like positional bias. Finally, the interpretability analysis provides valuable insights into the model's behavior and the importance of considering factors like news staleness.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper includes a comprehensive set of baselines, including single-article models, long-context models, and financial forecasting models, providing a strong basis for comparison. The statistical significance testing and portfolio simulations further strengthen the evaluation.
    *   **Well-Designed Architecture:** The proposed PSC method is both efficient and effective, allowing for the incorporation of historical context without overwhelming the model with irrelevant information.
    *   **Interpretability Analysis:** The inclusion of interpretability analyses provides valuable insights into the model's behavior and validates the importance of historical context.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Marginal Improvements:** While statistically significant, the improvements in AUC achieved by the PSC method are relatively modest in absolute terms. However, the authors address this by showing the impact on downstream investment performance.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are limited to a specific financial news classification task and a specific set of language models (open-source LLMs under 100B parameters). While the results are promising, it's unclear how well the approach would generalize to other tasks or different language models.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research in financial forecasting and language modeling by highlighting the value of context and providing a computationally efficient method for incorporating it. The findings could also have practical implications for investment management.

*   **Caveats:** While the investment performance boost is significant, the investment simulations may not take into account other real-world considerations such as price impact or order slippage (even though the authors mention this limitation). Also, generalization of the model may not work in other environments.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7.5** out of 10. The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach for incorporating historical context into financial news forecasting. The method is computationally efficient, and the results are supported by rigorous empirical evaluation and interpretability analyses. The portfolio simulations demonstrate the practical value of the approach. While the absolute improvements in AUC are relatively modest, the authors convincingly demonstrate their significance for downstream investment performance. The limitations are acknowledged, and future research directions are identified. While the foundational idea is not entirely novel, the integration and architectural implementation are solid.

### Phi: Preference Hijacking in Multi-modal Large Language Models at Inference Time
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12521v1)
- **Authors**: Yifan Lan, Yuanpu Cao, Weitong Zhang, Lu Lin, Jinghui Chen
- **Abstract**: Recently, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have gained significant attention across various domains. However, their widespread adoption has also raised serious safety concerns. In this paper, we uncover a new safety risk of MLLMs: the output preference of MLLMs can be arbitrarily manipulated by carefully optimized images. Such attacks often generate contextually relevant yet biased responses that are neither overtly harmful nor unethical, making them difficult to detect. Specifically, we introduce a novel method, Preference Hijacking (Phi), for manipulating the MLLM response preferences using a preference hijacked image. Our method works at inference time and requires no model modifications. Additionally, we introduce a universal hijacking perturbation -- a transferable component that can be embedded into different images to hijack MLLM responses toward any attacker-specified preferences. Experimental results across various tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach. The code for Phi is accessible at https://github.com/Yifan-Lan/Phi.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel attack called "Preference Hijacking" (Phi) against Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs). Phi manipulates the output preferences of MLLMs at inference time by carefully crafting adversarial images.  These images are optimized to steer the model toward attacker-specified preferences (e.g., malicious opinions, altered personality traits) without requiring any model modifications. The paper also proposes a "universal hijacking perturbation" – a transferable component that can be embedded into different images to achieve the same effect. Experiments across various tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of Phi, raising serious safety concerns about MLLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates significant novelty in several aspects. First, the identified vulnerability of manipulating MLLM preferences through crafted images at inference time is a previously unrecognised safety risk. Second, the technique allows to manipulate MLLM's broad range of preferences (opinions, personalities) rather than simply fixed or harmful behaviors. Third, the idea of a universal hijacking perturbation is compelling, enabling efficient attacks on unseen images.

*   **Significance:** The findings of this paper are potentially significant for the field of MLLM safety and security. The attack is stealthy (the outputs remain contextually relevant and are not overtly harmful), making it hard to detect with standard methods. This represents a serious risk for real-world applications where MLLMs are deployed. The possibility of influencing users' perception of various scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation. The paper articulates the safety concerns and potential real-world implications effectively.
    *   Well-defined methodology. The Phi attack is clearly explained with an optimization objective and the steps involved.
    *   Comprehensive experiments. The study covers a diverse range of tasks (text-only and multi-modal) and critical preferences, providing substantial evidence for the effectiveness of the attack.
    *   The paper provides a useful analysis and attempts to mitigate the effects of the attacks by analyzing various defense mechanisms.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Computational cost: While universal perturbations are proposed, the optimization process is computationally expensive. The efficiency of Phi compared to training aligned models needs to be analyzed more thoroughly.
    *   Limited defense analysis. Although the paper explores defenses, the current mitigation strategies (e.g., image preprocessing) are relatively basic. More advanced adversarial training or other more robust techniques may be required for stronger protection.
    *   Generalizability limitations. The effectiveness of Phi might be influenced by specific architectures, training data, or alignment techniques used in MLLMs. Future work should investigate how well the attack transfers across different models.
    *   The study relies on GPT-40 for assessment purposes. The quality of evaluation metrics using this assessment methodology might be limited.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work could have a significant impact on MLLM research. It should encourage researchers to develop more robust defense mechanisms against adversarial attacks and carefully consider the potential for preference manipulation when designing and deploying MLLMs. The findings may also prompt a closer examination of the ethical implications of preference alignment in large language models.

**Justification of Score:**

The identified shortcomings and experimental costs are well-addressed in the paper. However, it is also essential to address how the vulnerability may be exploited in realistic scenarios. In addition, in the current setting, it is difficult to analyze the impact of the attacks on diverse groups of people.

- **Score**: 8/10

### Selective Risk Certification for LLM Outputs via Information-Lift Statistics: PAC-Bayes, Robustness, and Skeleton Design
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12527v1)
- **Authors**: Sanjeda Akter, Ibne Farabi Shihab, Anuj Sharma
- **Abstract**: Large language models often produce plausible but incorrect outputs. Existing heuristics such as HallBayes lack formal guarantees. We develop the first comprehensive theory of \emph{information-lift certificates} under selective classification. Our contributions are: (i) a PAC-Bayes \emph{sub-gamma} analysis extending beyond standard Bernstein bounds; (ii) explicit skeleton sensitivity theorems quantifying robustness to misspecification; (iii) failure-mode guarantees under assumption violations; and (iv) a principled variational method for skeleton construction. Across six datasets and multiple model families, we validate assumptions empirically, reduce abstention by 12--15\% at the same risk, and maintain runtime overhead below 20\% (further reduced via batching).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of factually incorrect outputs from Large Language Models (LLMs) by proposing a method for selective risk certification using "information-lift statistics." The core idea is to compare the probability distribution of a full LLM against a more grounded "skeleton" model to quantify uncertainty. If the full model provides significantly more information ("information lift") than the skeleton, the output is certified.  The paper's key contributions are:

1.  **PAC-Bayes Sub-Gamma Analysis:**  Develops a novel PAC-Bayes bound tailored for the sub-gamma family of distributions, making it more robust to heavy-tailed lift distributions (common in language generation) than standard Bernstein-style bounds.
2.  **Skeleton Sensitivity Theorems (n-Robustness):**  Quantifies how much the certificate quality degrades with inaccuracies in the skeleton model itself.
3.  **Variational Skeleton Design (VSD):**  Proposes a principled, automated method for constructing the skeleton model to maximize certifiability.
4.  **Empirical Validation:**  Demonstrates the effectiveness of the approach across several datasets and model families, showing reduced abstention rates while maintaining risk levels and reasonable runtime overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers significant novelty in several respects:  The sub-gamma PAC-Bayes analysis is a clear improvement over prior Bernstein-style approaches, making PAC-Bayes methods applicable to a broader class of language model outputs. The n-robustness result provides a valuable theoretical insight into the impact of skeleton model misspecification.  VSD offers a practical and principled way to design skeletons, moving beyond ad-hoc approaches. The complete framework of information-lift certification combined with the PAC-Bayes Sub-Gamma Analysis appears quite novel.

*   **Significance:**  Selective classification is a critical problem for deploying LLMs in real-world applications where factual accuracy is paramount. This paper tackles this problem by formalizing the notion of comparing an LLM to a grounded skeleton, deriving guarantees in settings that were previously inaccessible. The reduction of abstention rates (12-15% at the same risk level) with minimal runtime overhead is a tangible improvement.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Solid Theoretical Foundation:** The paper presents a comprehensive theoretical analysis, including PAC-Bayes bounds, sensitivity theorems, and failure mode analysis.
    *   **Practical Contributions:** The VSD algorithm provides a practical method for skeleton design, and the empirical results demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are thorough, covering diverse datasets and model families, and comparing against strong baselines.
    *   **Addresses a Key Limitation of Existing Methods:**  The paper explicitly addresses the limitations of Bernstein-style PAC-Bayes bounds for heavy-tailed data, a critical issue in language generation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Modest Performance Gains:** While the results are consistently positive, the absolute improvements in coverage (12-15%) are moderate. This is an increment, but not a transformative shift.
    *   **Reliance on Skeletons:** The method's performance is critically dependent on the quality of the skeleton model.  The n-robustness analysis addresses this, but a poor skeleton can still significantly limit performance.
    *   **Token-Level Access:** The approach requires access to token-level probabilities, which is not always possible with black-box APIs.
    *   **Lack of Real-World Case Studies:** While the experimental setup is comprehensive and spans across diverse question-answering datasets, having specific analysis on real-world tasks, where it would have been difficult to use another selective classification method, would have strengthened the evaluation.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper provides a strong foundation for future research on selective classification for LLMs. The sub-gamma PAC-Bayes analysis can be adapted to other applications where heavy-tailed distributions are a concern. The VSD algorithm provides a starting point for more sophisticated skeleton design techniques. It could lead to more reliable and trustworthy LLM applications in high-stakes domains.

**Overall:**

The paper provides a solid contribution to the field. It combines theoretical rigor with practical algorithmic design and empirical validation. While the improvements in coverage are modest, the formal guarantees and the principled approach to skeleton design make it a significant advance. The addressal of the limitations of Bernstein-style analysis is key. Given the importance of reliable uncertainty estimation in LLMs, this work has the potential to influence future research and development in this area.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** A score of 7.5 reflects the paper's solid theoretical contribution, novel algorithmic design, and thorough empirical validation, tempered by the modest performance gains and the method's reliance on skeleton quality. The contributions represent a valuable increment to the field, and provide a basis for further development of selective classification techniques for LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adaptive Sampling Scheduler
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12569v1)
- **Authors**: Qi Wang, Shuliang Zhu, Jinjia Zhou
- **Abstract**: Consistent distillation methods have evolved into effective techniques that significantly accelerate the sampling process of diffusion models. Although existing methods have achieved remarkable results, the selection of target timesteps during distillation mainly relies on deterministic or stochastic strategies, which often require sampling schedulers to be designed specifically for different distillation processes. Moreover, this pattern severely limits flexibility, thereby restricting the full sampling potential of diffusion models in practical applications. To overcome these limitations, this paper proposes an adaptive sampling scheduler that is applicable to various consistency distillation frameworks. The scheduler introduces three innovative strategies: (i) dynamic target timestep selection, which adapts to different consistency distillation frameworks by selecting timesteps based on their computed importance; (ii) Optimized alternating sampling along the solution trajectory by guiding forward denoising and backward noise addition based on the proposed time step importance, enabling more effective exploration of the solution space to enhance generation performance; and (iii) Utilization of smoothing clipping and color balancing techniques to achieve stable and high-quality generation results at high guidance scales, thereby expanding the applicability of consistency distillation models in complex generation scenarios. We validated the effectiveness and flexibility of the adaptive sampling scheduler across various consistency distillation methods through comprehensive experimental evaluations. Experimental results consistently demonstrated significant improvements in generative performance, highlighting the strong adaptability achieved by our method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an adaptive sampling scheduler for consistency distillation models, aiming to improve image generation speed and quality in diffusion models. The proposed scheduler dynamically selects target timesteps based on a novel "importance" metric derived from the rate of change in the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR). This approach is designed to be applicable across different consistency distillation frameworks. The scheduler also incorporates optimized alternating sampling (forward denoising and backward noise addition) and techniques to mitigate exposure issues at high guidance scales, such as smoothing clipping and color balancing.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their scheduler by integrating it with various consistency distillation methods and evaluating the results using standard image quality metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of adaptively selecting timesteps based on an "importance" metric is a valuable step. It combines elements of both deterministic and stochastic target timestep selection strategies, addressing some limitations of the existing approaches. Dynamically altering the sampling schedule based on image characteristics during generation enhances adaptability, a common weakness of existing methods. The use of the rate of SNR change as an indicator of "importance" seems intuitive and grounded in the underlying principles of diffusion models. Addressing exposure at high CFG by adaptive clipping and color balance is a needed component.

*   **Significance:** Improving sampling efficiency and image quality in diffusion models has broad practical implications. The claim that the scheduler is applicable to various consistency distillation frameworks, if thoroughly validated, would be a significant advantage. The paper shows tangible improvements in generative performance (FID, CLIP score, Inception score), reinforcing its practical relevance. The focus on high guidance scales is crucial for real-world applications where precise control over the generated content is essential.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The adaptive sampling approach is well-motivated and grounded in the theory of diffusion models.
    *   The experimental results consistently demonstrate improvements across different consistency distillation methods.
    *   The inclusion of techniques to address exposure issues is a practical and important contribution.
    *   The paper is generally well-written and provides clear explanations of the proposed methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The description of the smoothness clipping and color balancing methods in the appendix is too brief and requires greater detail.
    *   While the experiments cover multiple baselines and image resolutions, more extensive testing across a broader range of datasets and prompts would further solidify the claims.
    *   The reliance on predefined hyper-parameters for calculating importance of the timesteps could limit the approach, and warrants further justification.

*   **Impact:** The proposed adaptive sampling scheduler, with its claimed general applicability and mitigation of exposure issues, has the potential to influence research in accelerating and improving diffusion models. Wider adoption could lead to better image generation quality and speed in practical applications.

*   **Justification of score:** The paper presents a well-motivated method with convincing experimental results. It improves on existing techniques and offers a unified adaptive sampling scheme applicable to different consistency distillation methods. Despite minor issues regarding description and experimental scope, the paper makes a tangible and potentially influential contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Yet Another Watermark for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12574v1)
- **Authors**: Siyuan Bao, Ying Shi, Zhiguang Yang, Hanzhou Wu, Xinpeng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Existing watermarking methods for large language models (LLMs) mainly embed watermark by adjusting the token sampling prediction or post-processing, lacking intrinsic coupling with LLMs, which may significantly reduce the semantic quality of the generated marked texts. Traditional watermarking methods based on training or fine-tuning may be extendable to LLMs. However, most of them are limited to the white-box scenario, or very time-consuming due to the massive parameters of LLMs. In this paper, we present a new watermarking framework for LLMs, where the watermark is embedded into the LLM by manipulating the internal parameters of the LLM, and can be extracted from the generated text without accessing the LLM. Comparing with related methods, the proposed method entangles the watermark with the intrinsic parameters of the LLM, which better balances the robustness and imperceptibility of the watermark. Moreover, the proposed method enables us to extract the watermark under the black-box scenario, which is computationally efficient for use. Experimental results have also verified the feasibility, superiority and practicality. This work provides a new perspective different from mainstream works, which may shed light on future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a new watermarking framework for Large Language Models (LLMs). It embeds watermarks by manipulating the internal parameters (specifically, the weights in the output layer) of the LLM, rather than modifying the tokens directly (as in many existing methods) or requiring retraining. The proposed method subtly adjusts the weights of selected tokens to bias the generation towards those tokens. The watermark can be extracted from the generated text without accessing the LLM itself, requiring only knowledge of the token selection rule. The authors claim the method offers a good balance between robustness, imperceptibility, and computational efficiency, and they provide experimental results to support these claims.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of manipulating the internal parameters of an LLM for watermarking is a reasonable departure from existing token-level manipulation or retraining approaches. While the concept of weight modulation isn't entirely new in machine learning, its specific application and integration within the LLM text generation process, particularly targeting the output layer, represents a somewhat novel contribution. The paper also presents a structured approach to embedding watermark signals by amplifying the logits of designated tokens through small-scale manipulations of weights. However, the novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary. Similar techniques like steering vectors or manipulating attention weights have been explored in different contexts. The level of control over the watermark and the specific sparse modulation is what makes it incrementally novel.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the need to balance competing objectives in LLM watermarking: imperceptibility, robustness, and efficiency. Existing methods often compromise on at least one of these. By directly manipulating internal parameters, the proposed method aims to improve this balance. The ability to extract watermarks under a black-box scenario (without needing the LLM itself) is also a significant advantage for practical deployment. The framework's supposed generalizability to LLMs of varying sizes, without retraining, adds to its appeal.
    However, the paper's impact is tempered by the specific technical choices. Focusing solely on the output layer might limit the watermark capacity or its resilience against sophisticated attacks. Further, while the experiments explore text-level perturbations, more advanced adversarial attacks designed to remove watermarks could expose vulnerabilities.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Parameter-level embedding:** Embedding at the parameter level is potentially more stealthy than token-level manipulation.
    *   **Black-box extractability:**  The ability to extract watermarks without accessing the model enhances practicality.
    *   **Computational efficiency:** The method avoids expensive retraining.
    *   **Good balance of objectives:** Initial experiments suggest a reasonable trade-off between robustness, imperceptibility, and efficiency.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The focus on the output layer might limit the complexity of watermarks and their resilience.
    *   **Limited Adversarial Evaluation:**  The robustness evaluation focuses mainly on simple text perturbations. Advanced adversarial attacks are not considered.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:**  While the idea is good, the underlying techniques are adaptations of existing methods.
    *   **Generalizability Claims:**  The claims regarding generalizability across LLM architectures and sizes are not strongly validated experimentally.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by inspiring more investigations into parameter-level watermarking techniques for LLMs. However, its ultimate impact will depend on its resilience against sophisticated adversarial attacks and its scalability to very large models and diverse tasks. Also, this work is likely to spur a debate about responsible use and the right to modify models with watermarks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a reasonably novel and potentially significant contribution to LLM watermarking by manipulating internal parameters for a better trade-off between imperceptibility, robustness, and efficiency. However, the novelty is incremental, and the experimental evaluation is limited in scope, especially regarding adversarial attacks. The claims about generalizability could be stronger with more extensive validation. The focus on the output layer might also be a limiting factor. Thus, the benefits for robust imperceptibility could be limited and not significantly improved.
