### ECG-aBcDe: Overcoming Model Dependence, Encoding ECG into a Universal Language for Any LLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12625v1)
- **Authors**: Yong Xia, Jingxuan Li, YeTeng Sun, Jiarui Bu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) hold significant promise for electrocardiogram (ECG) analysis, yet challenges remain regarding transferability, time-scale information learning, and interpretability. Current methods suffer from model-specific ECG encoders, hindering transfer across LLMs. Furthermore, LLMs struggle to capture crucial time-scale information inherent in ECGs due to Transformer limitations. And their black-box nature limits clinical adoption. To address these limitations, we introduce ECG-aBcDe, a novel ECG encoding method that transforms ECG signals into a universal ECG language readily interpretable by any LLM. By constructing a hybrid dataset of ECG language and natural language, ECG-aBcDe enables direct fine-tuning of pre-trained LLMs without architectural modifications, achieving "construct once, use anywhere" capability. Moreover, the bidirectional convertibility between ECG and ECG language of ECG-aBcDe allows for extracting attention heatmaps from ECG signals, significantly enhancing interpretability. Finally, ECG-aBcDe explicitly represents time-scale information, mitigating Transformer limitations. This work presents a new paradigm for integrating ECG analysis with LLMs. Compared with existing methods, our method achieves competitive performance on ROUGE-L and METEOR. Notably, it delivers significant improvements in the BLEU-4, with improvements of 2.8 times and 3.9 times in in-dataset and cross-dataset evaluations, respectively, reaching scores of 42.58 and 30.76. These results provide strong evidence for the feasibility of the new paradigm.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ECG-aBcDe, a novel ECG encoding method designed to transform ECG signals into a universal "ECG language" that can be readily interpreted by any Large Language Model (LLM). This approach addresses limitations of existing methods regarding transferability, learning time-scale information, and interpretability.  ECG-aBcDe quantizes ECG signals into discrete symbolic sequences, alternating between key point values and inter-point intervals. The method is LLM-agnostic, enabling fine-tuning of pre-trained LLMs without architectural modifications. The bidirectional convertibility allows for attention heatmap extraction, enhancing interpretability.  The paper also explicitly represents time-scale information to mitigate Transformer limitations. The approach is validated on ECG-QA datasets, demonstrating competitive performance and significant improvements in BLEU-4 scores compared to baselines.  A key aspect is the use of attention visualization on reconstructed ECG signals to further improve interpretability. The paper also attempts to isolate and illustrate limitations of LLMs regarding counting and time-scale understanding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to encoding ECG signals for use with LLMs.  The core idea of creating an "ECG language" by quantizing signal features (key points and intervals) is innovative. While prior work exists on ECG encoding for LLMs, the emphasis on creating a universal, LLM-agnostic representation, combined with explicit encoding of time-scale information and emphasis on attention-based interpretability, represents a significant step forward. A unique aspect is the decomposition of the task into independent data construction and LLM fine-tuning stages, improving workflow.

*   **Significance:** The method has the potential to significantly impact the field of ECG analysis with LLMs.  By addressing the limitations of existing methods (model-specificity, poor time-scale learning, and lack of interpretability), ECG-aBcDe opens the door for wider adoption and more reliable clinical applications.  The interpretability aspect, particularly the attention visualization on reconstructed ECGs, is crucial for gaining clinician trust and understanding model behavior. It aims to tackle the black-box nature of deep learning models, which is a key barrier to adoption in healthcare.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **LLM-agnostic design:** The universal encoding enables using various LLMs with minimal adaptation.
    *   **Improved interpretability:** Attention visualization on reconstructed ECGs helps understand model reasoning.
    *   **Explicit time-scale encoding:** Addresses a key limitation of standard LLMs.
    *   **Competitive results:** Demonstrates strong performance on benchmark datasets, especially in BLEU-4.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scale of experiments:** Experiments are conducted on smaller LLMs (1B parameters). The performance on larger, more powerful LLMs isn't explored.  Scaling to more complex models might reveal different bottlenecks.
    *   **Lossy Encoding:** ECG-aBcDe is lossy, and while efforts are made to ensure morphological fidelity during reconstruction, information loss might still impact certain analyses. The effect of quantization level on performance is not thoroughly explored.
    *   **Specific scope of evaluation:** Performance is primarily evaluated using QA tasks. While useful, a more comprehensive evaluation involving diagnostic accuracy on specific cardiac conditions would strengthen the clinical relevance of the findings.
    *   **Counting limitation discussion:** While highlighting the counting limitations of LLMs is relevant, the solutions proposed are relatively basic and may not fully address the underlying issue for complex real-world ECG analysis. The improvements shown are not as impressive as the other metrics evaluated

*   **Potential Influence:**  If the method proves to be robust and scalable, it could become a standard approach for integrating ECG analysis with LLMs.  It could also inspire further research on developing universal representations for other medical time-series data. The work presents a promising path towards trustworthy and transparent AI in healthcare.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of ECG analysis with LLMs. It successfully addresses several key limitations of existing methods, particularly in terms of transferability, interpretability, and time-scale learning. The LLM-agnostic design and focus on attention-based interpretability are valuable advancements. However, the limitations regarding the scale of experiments (smaller LLMs) and scope of evaluation (primarily QA tasks) prevent it from achieving a higher score.  While the counting analysis is relevant, its impact is somewhat diminished by the limitations of the solution proposed. Overall, the paper demonstrates a promising new paradigm with significant potential for future development and impact, but it requires further validation and refinement.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Ensembling Large Language Models for Code Vulnerability Detection: An Empirical Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12629v1)
- **Authors**: Zhihong Sun, Jia Li, Yao Wan, Chuanyi Li, Hongyu Zhang, Zhi jin, Ge Li, Hong Liu, Chen Lyu, Songlin Hu
- **Abstract**: Code vulnerability detection is crucial for ensuring the security and reliability of modern software systems. Recently, Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promising capabilities in this domain. However, notable discrepancies in detection results often arise when analyzing identical code segments across different training stages of the same model or among architecturally distinct LLMs. While such inconsistencies may compromise detection stability, they also highlight a key opportunity: the latent complementarity among models can be harnessed through ensemble learning to create more robust vulnerability detection systems. In this study, we explore the potential of ensemble learning to enhance the performance of LLMs in source code vulnerability detection. We conduct comprehensive experiments involving five LLMs (i.e., DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B, CodeLlama-7B, CodeLlama-13B, CodeQwen1.5-7B, and StarCoder2-15B), using three ensemble strategies (i.e., Bagging, Boosting, and Stacking). These experiments are carried out across three widely adopted datasets (i.e., Devign, ReVeal, and BigVul). Inspired by Mixture of Experts (MoE) techniques, we further propose Dynamic Gated Stacking (DGS), a Stacking variant tailored for vulnerability detection. Our results demonstrate that ensemble approaches can significantly improve detection performance, with Boosting excelling in scenarios involving imbalanced datasets. Moreover, DGS consistently outperforms traditional Stacking, particularly in handling class imbalance and multi-class classification tasks. These findings offer valuable insights into building more reliable and effective LLM-based vulnerability detection systems through ensemble learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Ensembling Large Language Models for Code Vulnerability Detection: An Empirical Evaluation" explores the use of ensemble learning techniques to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in detecting code vulnerabilities. The authors conduct experiments using five different LLMs (DeepSeek-Coder, CodeLlama-7B/13B, CodeQwen1.5-7B, and StarCoder2-15B) and three ensemble strategies (Bagging, Boosting, and Stacking) on three vulnerability datasets (Devign, ReVeal, and BigVul). They also propose a novel Stacking variant called Dynamic Gated Stacking (DGS), inspired by Mixture of Experts (MoE), tailored for vulnerability detection. The results demonstrate that ensemble approaches, particularly Boosting and DGS, can significantly improve detection performance, especially in handling imbalanced datasets and multi-class classification tasks. The paper provides insights into how different ensemble methods affect the detection of various vulnerability types.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel application of ensemble learning techniques to the area of LLM-based vulnerability detection. While ensemble methods are well-established in machine learning, their systematic evaluation in this specific context, particularly with LLMs and the introduction of DGS, adds a new perspective.
    *   **Significance:** Code vulnerability detection is a critical area, and improving the accuracy and reliability of detection methods has significant practical implications. The paper demonstrates a tangible improvement in performance through ensemble learning, suggesting a promising direction for future research and development.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental design is comprehensive, covering multiple LLMs, ensemble strategies, datasets, and evaluation metrics. This thoroughness provides strong evidence to support the claims.
    *   **Introduction of DGS:** The proposal of Dynamic Gated Stacking (DGS) as a Stacking variant tailored for vulnerability detection is a valuable contribution. The results show that DGS consistently outperforms traditional Stacking approaches, especially for unbalanced datasets and multi-class classification tasks.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The paper offers practical guidance on selecting appropriate ensemble methods based on dataset characteristics and desired performance metrics. This is valuable for practitioners looking to apply these techniques in real-world settings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited LLMs and Datasets:** While the study uses a reasonable number of LLMs and datasets, there are many other options available, which may limit the generalizability of the findings. The rapid evolution of LLMs could also render some results obsolete quickly.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper acknowledges the computational cost of ensemble learning but doesn't delve deeply into concrete resource comparisons. A more detailed analysis of the cost-benefit trade-offs would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Novelty beyond MoE Integration:** While DGS is inspired by MoE, the integration is relatively simple. The gating mechanism could be more sophisticated to truly leverage the power of MoE.
    *   **Lack of Real-World Validation:** The experiments are conducted on benchmark datasets. Validating the effectiveness of these techniques on real-world projects or in-house codebases would further strengthen the findings.

* **Potential Influence:**
The paper has the potential to influence the field by demonstrating the advantages of ensemble learning and providing guidance on selecting appropriate methods. DGS also offers a novel framework for integrating code features into Stacking algorithms, which will inspire further research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by systematically evaluating ensemble learning in the context of LLM-based vulnerability detection and proposing the novel DGS approach. While the scope is limited and the integration of MoE principles could be more sophisticated, the comprehensive evaluation and practical insights make this a valuable addition to the field. The paper has some novelty and will definitely guide some researchers and practitioners on using ensembling techniques with LLMs.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### FinSentLLM: Multi-LLM and Structured Semantic Signals for Enhanced Financial Sentiment Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12638v1)
- **Authors**: Zijian Zhang, Rong Fu, Yangfan He, Xinze Shen, Yanlong Wang, Xiaojing Du, Haochen You, Jiazhao Shi, Simon Fong
- **Abstract**: Financial sentiment analysis (FSA) has attracted significant attention, and recent studies increasingly explore large language models (LLMs) for this field. Yet most work evaluates only classification metrics, leaving unclear whether sentiment signals align with market behavior. We propose FinSentLLM, a lightweight multi-LLM framework that integrates an expert panel of sentiment forecasting LLMs, and structured semantic financial signals via a compact meta-classifier. This design captures expert complementarity, semantic reasoning signal, and agreement/divergence patterns without costly retraining, yielding consistent 3-6% gains over strong baselines in accuracy and F1-score on the Financial PhraseBank dataset. In addition, we also provide econometric evidence that financial sentiment and stock markets exhibit statistically significant long-run comovement, applying Dynamic Conditional Correlation GARCH (DCC-GARCH) and the Johansen cointegration test to daily sentiment scores computed from the FNSPID dataset and major stock indices. Together, these results demonstrate that FinSentLLM delivers superior forecasting accuracy for financial sentiment and further establish that sentiment signals are robustly linked to long-run equity market dynamics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FINSENTLLM, a novel framework for financial sentiment analysis (FSA). This framework integrates multiple Large Language Models (LLMs) along with structured financial semantic signals through a meta-classifier. FINSENTLLM aims to improve sentiment forecasting accuracy and demonstrate a connection between sentiment signals and market behavior. The framework combines domain-specific LLMs (FinBERT) with general-purpose LLMs (RoBERTa-sentiment) and uses features derived from probability distributions, financial semantics, and multi-LLM agreement. Econometric analyses, DCC-GARCH and Johansen cointegration, are applied to show the relationship between the developed financial sentiment scores and stock market indices. Experiments on the Financial PhraseBank dataset (FPB) show improved accuracy and F1-score compared to baselines, and econometric analysis suggests a long-run comovement between sentiment and equity markets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The integration of multiple LLMs with structured financial semantics via a meta-classifier is a valuable contribution. While individual components (using FinBERT or RoBERTa for sentiment analysis, time-series analysis) are not entirely new, the *specific combination and the analysis of the interactions* between different LLMs, structured financial signals, and market behavior does add to the existing body of research. Explicit aggregation of experts' predictions to capture inter-expert complementarities is also novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper bridges the gap between purely NLP-focused evaluations of LLMs for FSA and demonstrating the actual financial relevance of the derived sentiment signals. Establishing a link between improved sentiment prediction and stock market behavior through econometric methods strengthens the practical value of the approach. The framework provides a relatively lightweight (training-free) approach to financial sentiment that is also extensible.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear methodology: The paper clearly outlines the FINSENTLLM framework and its components.
    *   Rigorous evaluation: Experiments on a benchmark dataset (FPB) with ablation studies are conducted, and econometric analyses (DCC-GARCH, cointegration tests) are employed to assess market linkages.
    *   Improved performance: FINSENTLLM consistently outperforms strong baseline models, indicating the effectiveness of the proposed integration strategy.
    *   Practical relevance: The findings establish a statistical linkage between the derived sentiment signals and equity market dynamics.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Dataset limitations: The FPB dataset, while standard, is limited in its scope (short news headlines). Using this dataset to derive long-run comovement with equity market dynamics may not adequately capture the full complexity of the relationship.
    *   Generality of LLMs: While the choice of FinBERT and RoBERTa-sentiment is justified, further exploration of other LLMs (especially those optimized for instruction following) could provide additional insight.

*   **Impact:** The framework could influence future research in FSA by emphasizing the importance of integrating diverse LLM perspectives, incorporating structured financial semantics, and validating sentiment signals with market data.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper offers a novel and well-executed approach to financial sentiment analysis by systematically integrating multiple LLMs with structured financial semantics and validating its financial relevance using econometric analyses. The integration of expert signals and high-precision financial cues is a definite strength. While the individual components may not be groundbreaking, the combination and its validation through statistical measures adds to the field. The dataset limitations, however, do restrict the wider applicability of the derived inferences. For these reasons, the score is moderate, reflecting a solid, well-executed, and important contribution, albeit one with certain limitations.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Learn to Relax with Large Language Models: Solving Nonlinear Combinatorial Optimization Problems via Bidirectional Coevolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12643v1)
- **Authors**: Beidan Liu, Zhengqiu Zhu, Chen Gao, Yong Zhao, Wei Qi, Quanjun Yin
- **Abstract**: Nonlinear Combinatorial Optimization Problems (NCOPs) present a formidable computational hurdle in practice, as their nonconvex nature gives rise to multi-modal solution spaces that defy efficient optimization. Traditional constraint relaxation approaches rely heavily on expert-driven, iterative design processes that lack systematic automation and scalable adaptability. While recent Large Language Model (LLM)-based optimization methods show promise for autonomous problem-solving, they predominantly function as passive constraint validators rather than proactive strategy architects, failing to handle the sophisticated constraint interactions inherent to NCOPs.To address these limitations, we introduce the first end-to-end \textbf{Auto}mated \textbf{C}onstraint \textbf{O}ptimization (AutoCO) method, which revolutionizes NCOPs resolution through learning to relax with LLMs.Specifically, we leverage structured LLM reasoning to generate constraint relaxation strategies, which are dynamically evolving with algorithmic principles and executable code through a unified triple-representation scheme. We further establish a novel bidirectional (global-local) coevolution mechanism that synergistically integrates Evolutionary Algorithms for intensive local refinement with Monte Carlo Tree Search for systematic global strategy space exploration, ensuring optimal balance between intensification and diversification in fragmented solution spaces. Finally, comprehensive experiments on three challenging NCOP benchmarks validate AutoCO's consistent effectiveness and superior performance over the baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AutoCO (Automated Constraint Optimization), a novel end-to-end method that utilizes Large Language Models (LLMs) to solve Nonlinear Combinatorial Optimization Problems (NCOPs).  AutoCO addresses the limitations of existing LLM-based optimization methods, which primarily act as passive constraint validators. AutoCO transforms LLMs into proactive strategy designers by learning to relax constraints.  It employs a triple-representation scheme (constraint relaxation strategies, algorithmic principles, and executable code) and a bidirectional coevolution mechanism that integrates Evolutionary Algorithms (EAs) and Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) for local refinement and global strategy exploration, respectively. Experimental results on three NCOP benchmarks demonstrate AutoCO's effectiveness and superior performance compared to existing LLM-based methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:
    * **End-to-End LLM-Driven Constraint Relaxation:**  The concept of using LLMs to autonomously design and implement constraint relaxation strategies for NCOPs is a significant step beyond existing LLM-based optimizers. This shifts the LLM's role from validator to a proactive solver.
    * **Triple-Representation Scheme:**  The unified representation of constraint relaxation strategies, algorithmic concepts, and executable code is a novel way to maintain coherence and facilitate reasoning across multiple levels of abstraction. This is crucial for NCOP's complexity.
    * **Bidirectional Coevolution:**  The integration of EA and MCTS for synergistic local refinement and global exploration is a well-established technique, but its application within an LLM-driven optimization framework is innovative.
    * **Automated Problem Understanding for Relaxation Strategy Generation:** The LLM is used for understanding and analyzing the problem structure to suggest specific relaxation strategies, instead of simply generating code.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial gap in applying LLMs to complex optimization problems. NCOPs are prevalent in real-world applications, and efficient solutions are highly valuable. By automating constraint relaxation, AutoCO can potentially reduce the reliance on expert knowledge and accelerate the development of effective optimization strategies. The experimental results support the potential of AutoCO to outperform existing methods.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines NCOPs and the challenges associated with solving them.
    * **Well-Defined Methodology:** The AutoCO framework is described in detail, with clear explanations of each component.
    * **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The experiments cover diverse NCOP benchmarks and compare AutoCO against a range of existing methods.
    * **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides insightful analysis of the results, including ablation studies and convergence dynamics. This strengthens the validity of the claims.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Cost:**  While the paper demonstrates improved optimality gaps, the "Time Efficiency Analysis" reveals longer overall runtimes compared to some baselines.  Further optimization of the AutoCO framework may be necessary.
    * **LLM Dependence:**  The performance of AutoCO is inherently dependent on the capabilities of the underlying LLM.  The use of DeepSeek-R1 raises the question of how the results would generalize to other LLMs. A broader evaluation across different LLMs would strengthen the claims of generalizability.
    * **Limited Benchmarks:**  While three benchmarks are used, expanding to more diverse and larger-scale NCOPs would provide a more robust evaluation.
    * **Scalability Concerns:** It remains an open question how AutoCO would scale to significantly larger and more complex NCOP instances.  The integration of EA and MCTS can become computationally expensive with increasing problem size.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of LLM-based optimization by demonstrating the effectiveness of LLMs for proactive strategy design.  It may inspire further research into automated constraint relaxation and the integration of LLMs with other optimization techniques.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant and novel contribution to the field by demonstrating how LLMs can be effectively used for proactive constraint relaxation in NCOPs.  The triple-representation scheme and bidirectional coevolution mechanism are innovative and address limitations of existing LLM-based approaches. The empirical results demonstrate the potential of AutoCO to outperform existing methods. However, the higher computational cost, reliance on a specific LLM, and limited number of benchmarks prevent a higher score.  Improvements in these areas would significantly enhance the paper's impact.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models Imitate Logical Reasoning, but at what Cost?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12645v1)
- **Authors**: Lachlan McGinness, Peter Baumgartner
- **Abstract**: We present a longitudinal study which evaluates the reasoning capability of frontier Large Language Models over an eighteen month period. We measured the accuracy of three leading models from December 2023, September 2024 and June 2025 on true or false questions from the PrOntoQA dataset and their faithfulness to reasoning strategies provided through in-context learning. The improvement in performance from 2023 to 2024 can be attributed to hidden Chain of Thought prompting. The introduction of thinking models allowed for significant improvement in model performance between 2024 and 2025. We then present a neuro-symbolic architecture which uses LLMs of less than 15 billion parameters to translate the problems into a standardised form. We then parse the standardised forms of the problems into a program to be solved by Z3, an SMT solver, to determine the satisfiability of the query. We report the number of prompt and completion tokens as well as the computational cost in FLOPs for open source models. The neuro-symbolic approach significantly reduces the computational cost while maintaining near perfect performance. The common approximation that the number of inference FLOPs is double the product of the active parameters and total tokens was accurate within 10\% for all experiments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a longitudinal study assessing the logical reasoning capabilities of frontier Large Language Models (LLMs) over an 18-month period (Dec 2023, Sep 2024, Jun 2025) using the PrOntoQA dataset. The study evaluates accuracy, computational cost (measured by prompt and completion tokens, and FLOPs for open-source models), and faithfulness to reasoning strategies when using in-context learning. The authors investigate the impact of various prompting techniques (Chain of Thought, Bottom-Up, Top-Down, Magic Set Transformation) on LLM performance. They also introduce a neuro-symbolic architecture that combines LLMs (to translate natural language problems into a standardized format) with an SMT solver (Z3) for deductive reasoning.  The neuro-symbolic approach significantly reduces computational costs while maintaining high accuracy. The paper also demonstrates that the improvements in LLM performance over time can be attributed to hidden Chain of Thought prompting and the introduction of "thinking models" in more recent iterations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The longitudinal aspect of this study is a clear strength. Analyzing model evolution and attributing improvements to specific techniques like hidden CoT and "thinking models" provides valuable insights. The neuro-symbolic architecture, while not entirely novel in concept (combining LLMs with symbolic reasoning), offers a practical demonstration of cost-effective logical reasoning. Testing the frontier LLMs against different reasoning strategies (Top down, bottom up, and Magic Set transformation). The use of False Ontology for hard test cases enhances testing robustness.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant for several reasons:

    *   **Evolution of LLM Reasoning:** Tracking the progress (and plateaus) in LLM reasoning abilities over time offers insights into the effectiveness of various training strategies.
    *   **Cost-Effectiveness:**  The neuro-symbolic approach highlights the potential for reducing the computational burden of LLMs by integrating them with symbolic AI systems. This is particularly important given the environmental and economic concerns associated with large models.
    *   **Faithfulness Evaluation:**  The effort to assess faithfulness to reasoning strategies, while limited by the difficulty of automated natural language parsing, points to a crucial area for further research. Showing how various ATP reasoning strategies help LLMs solve the PrOntoQA reasoning framework helps improve LLM accuracy.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on PrOntoQA:**  While PrOntoQA is a valuable benchmark, its relatively constrained domain (toy examples) might limit the generalizability of the findings. The paper notes the potential for contamination in some models.
    *   **Faithfulness Measurement:** Automating the measurement of model faithfulness to the reasoning strategy has proven to be a difficult task. Future work includes a more automated system to determine faithfullness.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The use of open source tools does offer a more precise measurement of costs compared to the use of paid or closed source systems.

*   **Overall Assessment:**

    The paper is a solid contribution. The longitudinal analysis, the demonstration of a cost-effective neuro-symbolic architecture, and the effort to evaluate faithfulness all add value to the field. The limitations related to the toy example set and the difficulty of automating reasoning correctness do restrict the significance of the paper.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper demonstrates interesting model comparisons with unique benchmarks and is technically sound. The paper's methodology is well developed, and has clear reasoning behind all test cases and prompts. The longitudinal study element is a novel piece in Large Language Model evaluation as well as comparing the performance of frontier LLMs vs. combining open source systems with SMT solvers.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Systematic Evaluation of Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning Methods for the Security of Code LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12649v1)
- **Authors**: Kiho Lee, Jungkon Kim, Doowon Kim, Hyoungshick Kim
- **Abstract**: Code-generating Large Language Models (LLMs) significantly accelerate software development. However, their frequent generation of insecure code presents serious risks. We present a comprehensive evaluation of seven parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) techniques, demonstrating substantial gains in secure code generation without compromising functionality. Our research identifies prompt-tuning as the most effective PEFT method, achieving an 80.86% Overall-Secure-Rate on CodeGen2 16B, a 13.5-point improvement over the 67.28% baseline. Optimizing decoding strategies through sampling temperature further elevated security to 87.65%. This equates to a reduction of approximately 203,700 vulnerable code snippets per million generated. Moreover, prompt and prefix tuning increase robustness against poisoning attacks in our TrojanPuzzle evaluation, with strong performance against CWE-79 and CWE-502 attack vectors. Our findings generalize across Python and Java, confirming prompt-tuning's consistent effectiveness. This study provides essential insights and practical guidance for building more resilient software systems with LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary:**

This paper presents a comprehensive evaluation of parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods for improving the security of code-generating large language models (LLMs). The authors evaluate seven PEFT techniques across eight LLMs of varying sizes and architectures, focusing on their ability to generate secure code and resist poisoning attacks. The study finds that prompt-tuning consistently outperforms other PEFT methods, particularly when combined with temperature optimization during inference. The results highlight a vulnerability complexity hierarchy, where pattern-based vulnerabilities are more easily mitigated than context-dependent ones. The paper also analyzes the impact of temperature sampling on security, revealing that higher temperatures encourage exploration of underrepresented secure coding patterns. Finally, the study demonstrates the effectiveness of PEFT methods in a cross-language setting using Java code generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes several important contributions. First, it systematically evaluates a wide range of PEFT techniques for code security, something that prior work has only touched upon in limited contexts (e.g., SVEN). Second, the analysis of the interaction between PEFT methods and model architectures provides valuable insights into why certain techniques are more effective than others. The discovery of the vulnerability complexity hierarchy is also a novel and interesting finding, offering practical guidance for future security research. The temperature-security relationship is another significant aspect, challenging common beliefs about solely relying on training data and demonstrating inference-time diversity as a factor. The evaluation of robustness against poisoning attacks and the demonstration of cross-language generalizability are also valuable contributions that advance the field.

*   **Significance:**  The paper is highly significant for several reasons. First, it addresses the critical issue of security in AI-assisted code generation, which has become increasingly important as LLMs are integrated into software development workflows. The comprehensive evaluation provides actionable guidance for practitioners looking to improve the security of their code-generating LLMs.  Second, the identification of the vulnerability complexity hierarchy helps focus future research on the most challenging security issues. Third, the findings on temperature optimization offer a simple yet powerful technique for improving code security at inference time. The study provides evidence that LLMs can be made significantly more resilient against adversarial attacks, potentially enabling safer integration into production environments.  The findings about prompt-tuning and its consistent performance highlight a direction for future research, potentially leading to even more robust code generation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive evaluation across multiple PEFT methods, LLMs, and languages.
    *   Rigorous experimental design with thorough statistical analysis.
    *   Identification of novel vulnerability complexity hierarchy.
    *   Insightful analysis of the temperature-security relationship.
    *   Demonstration of the effectiveness of PEFT methods in mitigating poisoning attacks.
    *   Cross-language validation of the findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation focuses primarily on Python and Java. While these are important languages, extending the analysis to other languages would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   While the paper addresses potential limitations regarding CWE distribution and evaluation scope, and efforts to mitigate, more attention in those areas would further reinforce the findings.
    *   Some newer models (DeepSeek, Qwen, Mistral) were excluded based on time constraints. Examining these could add depth to understanding of the models' applicability.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of AI-assisted code generation by raising awareness of security issues and providing practical solutions. The findings can inform the design of more secure LLMs and development tools. The research can also guide future work on developing more effective PEFT methods and defenses against adversarial attacks.

Score: 8

**Rationale:** The paper represents a strong and significant contribution to the field. The systematic approach, novel findings, and practical recommendations make it highly valuable for researchers and practitioners working on AI-assisted code generation. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper far outweigh its weaknesses. The impact in terms of practical security improvements and influencing future research directions is likely to be substantial.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Don't Change My View: Ideological Bias Auditing in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12652v1)
- **Authors**: Paul Kröger, Emilio Barkett
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly embedded in products used by millions, their outputs may influence individual beliefs and, cumulatively, shape public opinion. If the behavior of LLMs can be intentionally steered toward specific ideological positions, such as political or religious views, then those who control these systems could gain disproportionate influence over public discourse. Although it remains an open question whether LLMs can reliably be guided toward coherent ideological stances and whether such steering can be effectively prevented, a crucial first step is to develop methods for detecting when such steering attempts occur. In this work, we adapt a previously proposed statistical method to the new context of ideological bias auditing. Our approach carries over the model-agnostic design of the original framework, which does not require access to the internals of the language model. Instead, it identifies potential ideological steering by analyzing distributional shifts in model outputs across prompts that are thematically related to a chosen topic. This design makes the method particularly suitable for auditing proprietary black-box systems. We validate our approach through a series of experiments, demonstrating its practical applicability and its potential to support independent post hoc audits of LLM behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Don't Change My View: Ideological Bias Auditing in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the potential for ideological steering in Large Language Models (LLMs) and proposes a method for detecting such steering attempts.  Recognizing that LLMs can influence user beliefs and shape public opinion, the authors adapt a previously proposed statistical method for detecting unauthorized use of proprietary system prompts and apply it to ideological bias auditing. The approach monitors changes in model outputs across thematically related prompts over time, flagging significant distributional shifts as potential signs of ideological manipulation. The method is model-agnostic, requires no access to model internals, and is thus suitable for auditing black-box systems. The authors validate their approach through experiments using synthetic prompt pairs and a real-world Grok-4 system prompt, demonstrating its ability to detect distributional shifts resulting from religiously and politically biased system prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its *application* of an existing prompt-membership inference methodology (Levin et al., 2025) to the *new* problem of detecting ideological steering in LLMs. The underlying statistical method isn't new, but using it for ideological bias detection is. The idea of monitoring for drift isn't new in principle, but is important in this domain. The synthetic prompt experiments have very little novelty given that numerous papers already exist on bias elicitation in LLMs. However, including a "real-world" system prompt like the one from Grok adds some value.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the growing concern about the influence of LLMs on public opinion and the potential for manipulation. The paper provides a practical method for independent post hoc audits of LLM behavior, which is crucial for maintaining transparency and accountability. If these methods can be extended to the detection of more subtle changes in model outputs, it could be important for regulation and building trust. The emphasis on black-box auditing is also significant, as many commercial LLMs are proprietary.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Applicability:** The method is model-agnostic and doesn't require access to model internals, making it usable on proprietary systems.
    *   **Clear Validation:** Experiments provide evidence that the approach can detect distributional shifts due to biased system prompts.
    *   **Addresses a Real-World Problem:** The paper tackles the timely issue of potential ideological steering in LLMs.
    *   **Uses both synthetic and real world prompts** showing that the technique is applicable in more complex cases.
    *   **Addresses the realistic threat surface** of using modifications in system prompts, which is a low-cost attack vector.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Interventions:**  The paper focuses primarily on system prompt modifications. Real-world manipulation could involve fine-tuning, data poisoning, retrieval augmented generation or other more complex methods.
    *   **Sensitivity to Superficial Changes:** The detection method is sensitive to changes in output distributions, some of which are not semantically meaningful.  This means it can raise false alarms, flagging differences that don't reflect actual changes in ideological stance.
    *   **Synthetic Prompt Simplification:** The religiously biased prompt examples are too direct, and too simple, for the method to be useful. For example, the model can easily classify itself as a christian and that influences it's opinions.
    *   **Limited Coverage:** The empirical evaluation covers a small number of prompts and topics, which could hinder the real world application of the methodology. It needs to be tested at a broader topical coverage to be usable.
    *   **Lack of new Metrics** No new metrics are developed that can show a more significant and trustworthy evaluation of the methodology.

*   **Impact and Potential Influence:** The paper helps to establish a research direction for auditing the ideological stances of LLMs. It has the potential to inform the development of more robust and comprehensive auditing frameworks. However, the limited scope of interventions and potential for false alarms will need to be addressed in future work.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:** The paper makes a worthwhile, but incremental, contribution. While adapting an existing method to a relevant problem is valuable, the limited scope of interventions and sensitivity to superficial changes reduce its practical impact. The synthetic experiments have marginal novelty, and do not provide useful insight given the simplicity of the examples. While the inclusion of a "real world" example is interesting, the overall score is driven by the lack of truly novel methodology or significant insights and the heavy reliance on synthetic, unrealistic examples. Future work needs to address the limitations noted above to make a more significant contribution.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Mitigating Strategy Preference Bias in Emotional Support Conversation via Uncertainty Estimations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12661v1)
- **Authors**: Yougen Zhou, Qin Chen, Ningning Zhou, Jie Zhou, Xingjiao Wu, Liang He
- **Abstract**: Emotional support conversation (ESC) aims to alleviate distress through empathetic dialogue, yet large language models (LLMs) face persistent challenges in delivering effective ESC due to low accuracy in strategy planning. Moreover, there is a considerable preference bias towards specific strategies. Prior methods using fine-tuned strategy planners have shown potential in reducing such bias, while the underlying causes of the preference bias in LLMs have not well been studied. To address these issues, we first reveal the fundamental causes of the bias by identifying the knowledge boundaries of LLMs in strategy planning. Then, we propose an approach to mitigate the bias by reinforcement learning with a dual reward function, which optimizes strategy planning via both accuracy and entropy-based confidence for each region according to the knowledge boundaries. Experiments on the ESCov and ExTES datasets with multiple LLM backbones show that our approach outperforms the baselines, confirming the effectiveness of our approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of strategy preference bias in emotional support conversation (ESC) systems built with large language models (LLMs). The authors hypothesize that this bias stems from LLMs' internal knowledge boundaries.  They propose a reinforcement learning (RL) approach with a dual reward function to mitigate this bias.  The reward function incorporates strategy accuracy and entropy-based confidence, tailored to different knowledge regions (highly known, weakly known, unknown) within the LLM. They demonstrate improved performance on ESCov and ExTES datasets with different LLM backbones.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Identifying Knowledge Boundaries:** The paper's primary novelty lies in explicitly framing the strategy preference bias problem as a consequence of LLMs' varying confidence levels across different knowledge regions in ESC. While prior work has noted LLM limitations and bias, this paper offers a specific, well-defined characterization of the problem's root cause.
*   **Dual Reward Function:** The use of a dual reward function in RL, combining accuracy and entropy, is a reasonable approach to balance exploration and exploitation, which addresses the bias problem in ESCs. Tailoring the rewards to different knowledge regions based on uncertainty estimations is a logical extension.
*   **Incremental Contribution:**  The approach largely builds on existing techniques. The RL framework and SFT are standard, and the specific reward function formulation, while tailored, uses common components (accuracy, entropy, KL divergence). The experimental setup also largely reuses existing datasets and evaluation metrics.

**Significance:**

*   **Practical Relevance:** ESC is a growing area, and addressing bias is crucial for building reliable and helpful systems. This work directly tackles a practical problem.
*   **Improved Performance:** The experimental results indicate that the proposed approach outperforms several baselines, confirming the effectiveness of the technique. The performance gains, while noticeable, aren't particularly dramatic, which somewhat limits the practical significance.
*   **Generalizability:**  The method appears to be generalizable across different LLM backbones, which increases its practical utility. The reliance on SFT and RL, however, implies that the method requires fine-tuning and careful hyperparameter optimization for each new LLM and dataset.
*   **Clarity of Explanation:** The paper is reasonably well-written and explains the method and results in a clear and structured way. The use of figures helps visualize the process.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the paper provides a plausible explanation of the problem and solution, a stronger theoretical analysis of why the proposed approach works as intended would be beneficial. A more rigorous treatment of the connection between knowledge boundaries, uncertainty estimation, and bias reduction is warranted.
*   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While ablation studies are presented, more analysis could be performed to fully understand how each component of the reward functions contribute to the final results. This could improve the paper's insights.
*   **Incremental Improvement:** The improvements demonstrated are not substantial "game changers", which could limit the paper's immediate impact within the field.

**Influence:**

The paper provides a solid contribution to the problem of bias in ESC and its root cause within LLMs. The study could influence future research toward developing more nuanced mitigation strategies based on knowledge boundaries and uncertainty estimates. While not revolutionary, the paper offers a practical, well-motivated, and reasonably effective approach to a relevant problem.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel framing of an existing problem, strategy preference bias in emotional support conversation, and offers a well-motivated approach to address it. It achieves good performance with the experimental results confirming the effectiveness of the approach. Its contribution is incremental and requires careful implementation to see results, and the impact is ultimately limited. However, its contributions on the explicit consideration of knowledge boundaries as a means for mitigating preference bias may prove to be more significant in the long run.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Towards Inclusive Toxic Content Moderation: Addressing Vulnerabilities to Adversarial Attacks in Toxicity Classifiers Tackling LLM-generated Content
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12672v1)
- **Authors**: Shaz Furniturewala, Arkaitz Zubiaga
- **Abstract**: The volume of machine-generated content online has grown dramatically due to the widespread use of Large Language Models (LLMs), leading to new challenges for content moderation systems. Conventional content moderation classifiers, which are usually trained on text produced by humans, suffer from misclassifications due to LLM-generated text deviating from their training data and adversarial attacks that aim to avoid detection. Present-day defence tactics are reactive rather than proactive, since they rely on adversarial training or external detection models to identify attacks. In this work, we aim to identify the vulnerable components of toxicity classifiers that contribute to misclassification, proposing a novel strategy based on mechanistic interpretability techniques. Our study focuses on fine-tuned BERT and RoBERTa classifiers, testing on diverse datasets spanning a variety of minority groups. We use adversarial attacking techniques to identify vulnerable circuits. Finally, we suppress these vulnerable circuits, improving performance against adversarial attacks. We also provide demographic-level insights into these vulnerable circuits, exposing fairness and robustness gaps in model training. We find that models have distinct heads that are either crucial for performance or vulnerable to attack and suppressing the vulnerable heads improves performance on adversarial input. We also find that different heads are responsible for vulnerability across different demographic groups, which can inform more inclusive development of toxicity detection models.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards Inclusive Toxic Content Moderation: Addressing Vulnerabilities to Adversarial Attacks in Toxicity Classifiers Tackling LLM-generated Content".

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of adversarial attacks on toxicity classifiers, particularly in the context of LLM-generated content. It proposes a novel approach based on mechanistic interpretability to identify and suppress vulnerable circuits (attention heads) within fine-tuned BERT and RoBERTa models. The study analyzes different datasets (Jigsaw and ToxiGen) and uses adversarial attacks (PGD BERT-Attack) to identify vulnerable components. The authors find that crucial and vulnerable attention heads are distinct, and suppressing vulnerable heads improves performance against adversarial attacks. They also reveal fairness gaps, showing that vulnerability varies across demographic groups, suggesting structural encoding of demographic bias in the models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its application of mechanistic interpretability to the specific problem of adversarial attacks on toxicity classifiers dealing with LLM-generated content. While mechanistic interpretability techniques exist, their application to this specific problem, along with the analysis of fairness across demographic groups within this context, is a novel contribution. Previous works mainly focused on black-box defenses or reactive adversarial training. This work is proactive by aiming to identify and disable vulnerable model components.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant and timely problem. LLMs are increasingly used to generate content, including toxic content, which poses challenges for content moderation systems. Adversarial attacks can easily bypass traditional classifiers. The proposed approach provides a promising direction toward building more robust and fair toxicity classifiers. The fairness analysis, revealing demographic disparities in vulnerability, is particularly important for creating inclusive moderation systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The methodology is well-defined and utilizes established techniques (activation patching, adversarial attacks) in a new context.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive, using diverse datasets (human-written and LLM-generated).
    *   The analysis is thorough, providing insights into the specific attention heads responsible for vulnerability and their connection to demographic biases.
    *   The results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach in improving robustness against adversarial attacks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experiments are limited to English-language datasets and BERT/RoBERTa models. Generalizability to other languages, modalities, or more advanced architectures is not fully established.
    *   The computational cost of activation patching and adversarial generation may limit the scalability of the approach for very large models or real-time moderation.
    *   The work focuses mainly on attention heads. Investigating other model components, such as feedforward networks, could reveal further vulnerabilities.
    *   While the paper improves adversarial robustness, the level of improvement varies depending on the demographic group. More in-depth work may be needed to mitigate the variations to ensure fairness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    *   It encourages the use of mechanistic interpretability techniques for understanding and improving the robustness and fairness of toxicity classifiers.
    *   It highlights the importance of considering demographic disparities in adversarial vulnerability when developing content moderation systems.
    *   It provides a practical framework for identifying and suppressing vulnerable circuits in transformer-based models.
    *   It could inspire the development of new defense mechanisms based on model-internal signals.

**Overall Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of toxicity detection and content moderation. It is well-executed, presents novel findings, and offers a promising approach for building more robust and fair classifiers. The limitations are clearly acknowledged, and the potential for future research is significant. However, it's important to temper expectations on its immediate impact and applicability due to the limited scale and context of the study. While promising, the techniques might not translate directly to real-world large-scale systems without significant engineering effort.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Scalable Architecture for Efficient Multi-bit Fully Homomorphic Encryption
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12676v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaao Ma, Ceyu Xu, Lisa Wu Wills
- **Abstract**: In the era of cloud computing, privacy-preserving computation offloading is crucial for safeguarding sensitive data. Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) enables secure processing of encrypted data, but the inherent computational complexity of FHE operations introduces significant computational overhead on the server side. FHE schemes often face a tradeoff between efficiency and versatility. While the CKKS scheme is highly efficient for polynomial operations, it lacks the flexibility of the binary TFHE (Torus-FHE) scheme, which offers greater versatility but at the cost of efficiency. The recent multi-bit TFHE extension offers greater flexibility and performance by supporting native non-polynomial operations and efficient integer processing. However, current implementations of multi-bit TFHE are constrained by its narrower numeric representation, which prevents its adoption in applications requiring wider numeric representations. To address this challenge, we introduce Taurus, a hardware accelerator designed to enhance the efficiency of multi-bit TFHE computations. Taurus supports ciphertexts up to 10 bits by leveraging novel FFT units and optimizing memory bandwidth through key reuse strategies. We also propose a compiler with operation deduplication to improve memory utilization. Our experiment results demonstrate that Taurus achieves up to 2600x speedup over a CPU, 1200x speedup over a GPU, and up to 7x faster compared to the previous state-of-the-art TFHE accelerator. Moreover, Taurus is the first accelerator to demonstrate privacy-preserving inference with large language models such as GPT-2. These advancements enable more practical and scalable applications of privacy-preserving computation in cloud environments.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper based on its content.

**Summary:**

The paper presents Taurus, a hardware accelerator designed to improve the efficiency of multi-bit Fully Homomorphic Encryption (FHE) computations. It addresses the limitations of existing FHE implementations, particularly the performance bottlenecks associated with wider numeric representations in multi-bit TFHE. Taurus leverages novel FFT units, optimized memory bandwidth through key reuse strategies, and a compiler with operation deduplication to achieve significant speedups compared to CPU, GPU, and previous TFHE accelerator implementations. Notably, Taurus is the first accelerator to demonstrate privacy-preserving inference with large language models like GPT-2.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key aspects:

    *   **Addressing the wider numeric representation challenge:** The paper identifies a crucial performance bottleneck in multi-bit TFHE related to the increased size of evaluation keys and auxiliary data when using wider numeric representations. This is a significant practical problem that hinders the adoption of multi-bit TFHE in real-world applications.
    *   **Specialized Hardware Architecture:** Taurus's architecture, with its novel FFT units and key reuse strategies, is specifically designed to overcome the performance limitations associated with wider numeric representations. This represents a departure from previous designs that were less efficient at handling the larger data sizes. The heterogeneous FFT cluster design with a double-real FFT is particularly novel and effectively handles the polynomial arithmetic requirements.
    *   **Compiler Optimizations:** The key-switching (KS-dedup) and GLWE accumulator (ACC-dedup) deduplication techniques are also significant contributions. These techniques directly address the memory bandwidth bottleneck and improve overall efficiency.
    *   **End-to-End System Integration:** Taurus demonstrates an end-to-end system, capable of processing complex applications like GPT-2. This is significant as many prior accelerator designs lack such a complete, functional demonstration, relying instead on smaller benchmark evaluations.
*   **Significance:**

    *   **Practical Implications for FHE:** The work has important practical implications for FHE. By making multi-bit TFHE more efficient, Taurus enables a wider range of privacy-preserving applications, including those that require wider numeric representations for accuracy. The demonstration of large language model inference is a particularly compelling example.
    *   **Performance Gains:** The reported speedups (up to 2600x over CPU and 1200x over GPU) are substantial and highlight the potential of specialized hardware for FHE. The performance gain compared to the state-of-the-art TFHE accelerator is also notable (7x faster).
    *   **Impact on FHE Research:** Taurus provides a strong proof-of-concept for a hardware-software co-design approach to FHE acceleration. It illustrates the importance of considering the specific characteristics of FHE schemes when designing hardware architectures. The paper's detailed analysis of the architectural design space will also be valuable to other researchers in the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper presents a thorough analysis of the performance challenges and opportunities in multi-bit TFHE.
    *   **Well-Designed Architecture:** The Taurus architecture is well-designed and incorporates several innovative features that address the specific requirements of multi-bit TFHE.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper provides strong experimental results that demonstrate the effectiveness of Taurus.
    *   **End-to-End System Demonstration:** The demonstration of large language model inference is a particularly compelling example of the practical potential of Taurus.
    *   **Modular Compiler Framework:** Leveraging and extending the Concrete toolchain enables Taurus to be easily used with new programs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Comparisons to Existing Architectures:** While a comparison is made to a Morphling-style design, a more detailed comparison to a wider range of existing FHE accelerators would be beneficial.
    *   **Scalability Discussions:** While they claim to scale well, further discussion regarding the cost of scaling (area, power) and the trade-offs of different bit widths and security parameters would be helpful.
    *   **Power Consumption:** The power consumption of Taurus (167.30 W) is relatively high. Future work could focus on reducing power consumption while maintaining performance.
    *   **Lack of Security Proof:** The paper lacks a rigorous security proof of the implemented optimizations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of FHE by enabling new applications and inspiring further research on hardware acceleration techniques. The demonstrated ability to perform privacy-preserving inference with large language models is a particularly important milestone.

**Overall Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses of the paper, I assign it a score of **8.5**. This score reflects the paper's significant contributions to the field of FHE hardware acceleration, its practical implications for privacy-preserving computation, and the strong experimental results that support its claims. While some weaknesses exist, the paper's overall impact is substantial. The key differentiator is Taurus has a complete architecture that offers significant improvements that can push FHE to real-world implementations.
