### Instance-level Randomization: Toward More Stable LLM Evaluations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12678v1)
- **Authors**: Yiyang Li, Yonghuang Wu, Ying Luo, Liangtai Sun, Zishu Qin, Lin Qiu, Xuezhi Cao, Xunliang Cai
- **Abstract**: Evaluations of large language models (LLMs) suffer from instability, where small changes of random factors such as few-shot examples can lead to drastic fluctuations of scores and even model rankings. Moreover, different LLMs can have different preferences for a certain setting of random factors. As a result, using a fixed setting of random factors, which is often adopted as the paradigm of current evaluations, can lead to potential unfair comparisons between LLMs. To mitigate the volatility of evaluations, we first theoretically analyze the sources of variance induced by changes in random factors. Targeting these specific sources, we then propose the instance-level randomization (ILR) method to reduce variance and enhance fairness in model comparisons. Instead of using a fixed setting across the whole benchmark in a single experiment, we randomize all factors that affect evaluation scores for every single instance, run multiple experiments and report the averaged score. Theoretical analyses and empirical results demonstrate that ILR can reduce the variance and unfair comparisons caused by random factors, as well as achieve similar robustness level with less than half computational cost compared with previous methods.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces "Instance-Level Randomization" (ILR) to improve the stability and fairness of large language model (LLM) evaluations. It argues that current evaluation methods using fixed random factor settings (e.g., few-shot examples) can lead to unstable scores, biased comparisons, and unfair rankings between LLMs because different models have varying sensitivities to these random factors. ILR proposes randomizing these factors for *each* instance in a benchmark and averaging the results across multiple runs. The authors provide a theoretical analysis of the variance induced by random factors and demonstrate, both theoretically and empirically, that ILR reduces variance faster and mitigates unfair comparisons using fewer computational resources than simply running more trials with a fixed setup. They also introduce a new metric, Observed Reversal Probability (ORP), to quantify the stability of model rankings and show that ILR significantly reduces ORP.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a genuinely important problem in LLM evaluation: the instability and unfairness arising from seemingly arbitrary choices in evaluation settings. The theoretical analysis of the sources of variance is sound and provides a solid foundation for the ILR method. The empirical results, demonstrating reduced variance and improved ranking stability (ORP), are convincing. The comparison to simply running more trials with a fixed setting, showing improved efficiency, further strengthens the argument.

However, the "novelty" of the approach can be debated. Randomization is a well-established technique in experimental design. The key contribution is the application of this principle at the *instance level* and the clear articulation of *why* this is beneficial in the context of LLM evaluation, along with the associated theoretical and empirical justification. The ORP metric is a useful addition, providing a concrete way to quantify ranking stability. The chosen random factors are also pretty standard - prompt formatting and few-shot examples have been widely studied. It also is a computationally intensive approach - by randomizing on each instance, it needs to run a model on each data point multiple times, which could be a significant computational burden for many researchers.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a significant problem:** LLM evaluation instability.
*   **Solid theoretical foundation:** The variance analysis is well-reasoned.
*   **Empirical validation:** Experiments convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of ILR.
*   **Efficiency:** ILR achieves robustness with less computation than simply increasing the number of trials.
*   **Novel metric:** The introduction of ORP is valuable.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited novelty:** The core idea of randomization is not new. The key is the *instance level* implementation.
*   **Computational Cost:** Even with less than half the previous computational cost, it is still likely much more costly than the conventional approach of using a single fixed setting.
*   **Restricted scope of random factors:** The paper focuses primarily on prompt and few-shot variations, while other influential factors could have been studied as well. For example, the seed used for stochastic decoding algorithms.
*   **Limited investigation of extreme parameter choices**: Given it tries to address evaluation instability, an evaluation/analysis of the stability of the hyperparameters of the ILR algorithm could have given insight into its robustness.

**Significance and Potential Influence:**

The paper's significance lies in its practical contribution to improving the reliability and fairness of LLM evaluations. While the core idea isn't entirely new, its specific application to the instance level, coupled with the theoretical justification and the new ORP metric, makes it a valuable contribution. The insights into why instance-level randomization works can guide future research on evaluation methodologies. It also could be interesting to see the approach used for meta-evaluation by assessing LLM evaluation datasets by assessing if using ILR changes the model ranking significantly.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper provides a sound and well-validated solution to a clear problem in LLM evaluation. While the core principle of randomization isn't fundamentally novel, the ILR method's specific application, justification, and novel ORP metric are valuable contributions. The reduction in computational cost compared to brute-force repetition is practically relevant. While more factors should be investigated, and a more detailed investigation into the cost of ILR may be necessary, the work offers a significant and measurable improvement to evaluation methodologies.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Harnessing the Power of AI in Qualitative Research: Role Assignment, Engagement, and User Perceptions of AI-Generated Follow-Up Questions in Semi-Structured Interviews
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12709v1)
- **Authors**: He Zhang, Yueyan Liu, Xin Guan, Jie Cai, John M. Carroll
- **Abstract**: Semi-structured interviews highly rely on the quality of follow-up questions, yet interviewers' knowledge and skills may limit their depth and potentially affect outcomes. While many studies have shown the usefulness of large language models (LLMs) for qualitative analysis, their possibility in the data collection process remains underexplored. We adopt an AI-driven "Wizard-of-Oz" setup to investigate how real-time LLM support in generating follow-up questions shapes semi-structured interviews. Through a study with 17 participants, we examine the value of LLM-generated follow-up questions, the evolving division of roles, relationships, collaborative behaviors, and responsibilities between interviewers and AI. Our findings (1) provide empirical evidence of the strengths and limitations of AI-generated follow-up questions (AGQs); (2) introduce a Human-AI collaboration framework in this interview context; and (3) propose human-centered design guidelines for AI-assisted interviewing. We position LLMs as complements, not replacements, to human judgment, and highlight pathways for integrating AI into qualitative data collection.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of AI, specifically Large Language Models (LLMs), to generate follow-up questions in semi-structured interviews. It uses an AI-driven "Wizard-of-Oz" methodology where a researcher acts as a co-interviewer, voicing AI-generated questions to a participant. The study examines the perceived usefulness of these AI-generated follow-up questions (AGQs), focusing on the impact on interview depth, role assignments between human interviewers and AI, and user perceptions. The findings reveal that AGQs can be helpful in deepening exploration and supplementing human interviewers, but their usefulness depends on factors like contextual relevance, timing, ethical considerations, and the researcher's comfort level in relinquishing some control. The paper proposes a human-AI collaboration framework for interviews, discussing different interaction modes and offering design guidelines for AI-assisted interviewing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on integrating LLMs *directly* into the data collection phase of qualitative research, specifically within the *real-time* dynamics of semi-structured interviews. While previous work has explored LLMs for qualitative data analysis and automated interview systems, the investigation of *real-time AGQ support* and its impact on interview flow, interviewer authority, and participant trust represents a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in the literature. Despite the widespread use of semi-structured interviews and the increasing capabilities of LLMs, little research has explored the potential of AI to aid interviewers during the interview itself. The findings have several implications:
    *   *Practical Design Implications:*  The paper provides design guidelines for integrating AI into qualitative interview tools, considering ethical implications, privacy, and the need for maintaining human judgment.  The analysis of different human-AI interaction modes (AI as a backstage assistant, AI as a direct co-interviewer) is valuable for developers designing AI-supported interview systems.
    *   *Theoretical Implications:* The findings contribute to the understanding of human-AI collaboration in qualitative research.  The framework for role allocation and the identification of factors influencing interviewer trust and control are valuable for the broader field of HCI.
    *   *Methodological Implications:* The paper showcases a robust AI-driven Wizard-of-Oz methodology for evaluating AI interventions in qualitative research.  The inductive thematic analysis and detailed qualitative findings provide a solid foundation for future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   *Rigorous Methodology:*  The AI-driven Wizard-of-Oz methodology, the systematic variation of AI intervention modes, and the detailed qualitative analysis strengthen the paper's findings.
    *   *Rich Empirical Data:*  The study's recruitment process ensures the participants are well-versed in qualitative research.  The participant voices, before and after the disclosure, offer nuanced insights into the value and limitations of AGQs.
    *   *Balanced Perspective:*  The paper acknowledges both the potential benefits and the limitations/risks of AI-generated follow-up questions, avoiding an overly optimistic view. It addresses potential concerns about ethical and pragmatic issues.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   *Generalizability:* While the sample size is reasonable for a qualitative study (n=17), the generalizability of the findings may be limited due to the specific population recruited (experience with qualitative research).  Future research should consider diverse populations and settings.
    *   *Limited focus on technical Capabilities:* The paper doesn't delve deeply into the specific LLM (GPT-4o) capabilities or how different LLM architectures might influence the outcomes.  While it highlights the *potential* of AGQs, a more detailed technical analysis would be beneficial.
    *   *Role of "Oz":* The study's design involves a "Wizard" and an "Oz" (simulated interviewee). This setup could introduce biases, as the researcher's portrayal as interviewee may impact the real participant's role. A full discussion of any measures taken to counter this effect would be welcome.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper presents a significant and novel exploration of the role of LLMs in qualitative interview data collection. It addresses a gap in the literature and offers valuable design implications and theoretical insights. While it has a few limitations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 8/10

### Joint AoI and Handover Optimization in Space-Air-Ground Integrated Network
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12716v1)
- **Authors**: Zifan Lang, Guixia Liu, Geng Sun, Jiahui Li, Jiacheng Wang, Weijie Yuan, Dusit Niyato, Dong In Kim
- **Abstract**: Despite the widespread deployment of terrestrial networks, providing reliable communication services to remote areas and maintaining connectivity during emergencies remains challenging. Low Earth orbit (LEO) satellite constellations offer promising solutions with their global coverage capabilities and reduced latency, yet struggle with intermittent coverage and limited communication windows due to orbital dynamics. This paper introduces an age of information (AoI)-aware space-air-ground integrated network (SAGIN) architecture that leverages a high-altitude platform (HAP) as intelligent relay between the LEO satellites and ground terminals. Our three-layer design employs hybrid free-space optical (FSO) links for high-capacity satellite-to-HAP communication and reliable radio frequency (RF) links for HAP-to-ground transmission, and thus addressing the temporal discontinuity in LEO satellite coverage while serving diverse user priorities. Specifically, we formulate a joint optimization problem to simultaneously minimize the AoI and satellite handover frequency through optimal transmit power distribution and satellite selection decisions. This highly dynamic, non-convex problem with time-coupled constraints presents significant computational challenges for traditional approaches. To address these difficulties, we propose a novel diffusion model (DM)-enhanced dueling double deep Q-network with action decomposition and state transformer encoder (DD3QN-AS) algorithm that incorporates transformer-based temporal feature extraction and employs a DM-based latent prompt generative module to refine state-action representations through conditional denoising. Simulation results highlight the superior performance of the proposed approach compared with policy-based methods and some other deep reinforcement learning (DRL) benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of providing reliable communication services in remote areas and during emergencies using a space-air-ground integrated network (SAGIN). It proposes a three-layer architecture where a high-altitude platform (HAP) acts as a relay between low Earth orbit (LEO) satellites and ground terminals, utilizing hybrid free-space optical (FSO) and radio frequency (RF) links. The core contribution is a joint optimization problem to minimize the age of information (AoI) and satellite handover frequency by strategically managing transmit power distribution and satellite selection.  To tackle the complexity of this problem, the authors introduce a novel diffusion model (DM)-enhanced dueling double deep Q-network with action decomposition and state transformer encoder (DD3QN-AS) algorithm. Simulation results demonstrate the superiority of the proposed algorithm compared to other methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Architecture:** While the concept of SAGINs with HAPs and hybrid FSO/RF links isn't entirely new, the paper's specific three-layer architecture with the focus on AoI and intelligent relaying is a reasonable contribution.
*   **Problem Formulation:** The joint optimization of AoI and handover frequency is a practical and valuable approach in managing the dynamic and constrained resources of LEO satellite networks. However, the specific formulation could be considered incremental improvements to existing network optimization problems.
*   **Algorithm (DD3QN-AS):** This is where the paper's primary novelty lies.  The fusion of a state transformer encoder (STE) and a diffusion model (DM) based prompt generator within a DRL framework (D3QN) is a non-trivial extension and shows an attempt to address the specific challenges posed by the SAGIN environment. The action decomposition is a standard technique but provides a structured approach to power allocation decisions.

**Significance:**

*   **Practical Relevance:**  The paper directly addresses the practical challenges faced in LEO satellite communications, particularly the intermittent coverage, handover issues, and the need for timely information delivery.
*   **Performance Improvement:** The simulation results show that the DD3QN-AS algorithm outperforms baselines in terms of AoI and handover frequency. This is a significant aspect of the study demonstrating concrete improvements and the potential benefits of the proposed method.
*   **Theoretical Contribution:**  The paper contributes to the development of DRL algorithms tailored for highly dynamic and stochastic networking environments. The STE and DM components offer improvements on standard D3QN.

**Strengths:**

*   Well-defined problem statement and clear motivation.
*   The proposed DD3QN-AS algorithm appears to be a carefully engineered solution tailored to the specific challenges of the SAGIN environment.
*   Comprehensive simulation results and performance comparisons demonstrate the advantages of the proposed approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the algorithm is novel in its integration of components, the core ideas of STE and DMs are adapted from other domains. The contribution is more on how they are used and integrated. The use of convex optimization for power allocation is a standard procedure.
*   **Complexity:** The DD3QN-AS algorithm is complex, which may hinder its practical deployment in resource-constrained environments. A detailed analysis of the computational cost and memory requirements is needed.
*   **Generalizability:** The simulations are performed under specific system settings. The generalizability of the results to other SAGIN architectures and scenarios should be investigated.
*   **Baseline Selection:**  The baseline selection could be improved. Comparing with more state-of-the-art DRL algorithms would enhance the impact of the paper. Policy-based algorithms like PPO are not the strongest comparisons for D3QN variants.
*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While there are claims of stability, more detailed theoretical results would solidify the contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of SAGIN optimization by addressing a relevant problem with a novel DRL-based algorithm. The integration of STE and diffusion models into a D3QN framework to tackle the challenges of non-stationary environments provides a worthwhile advance. The empirical results demonstrate the algorithm's effectiveness.  However, the novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking, as the individual components of the algorithm are adaptations of existing techniques. The complexity of the algorithm and the limited generalizability of the results are also drawbacks.

### HistoryBankQA: Multilingual Temporal Question Answering on Historical Events
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12720v1)
- **Authors**: Biswadip Mandal, Anant Khandelwal, Manish Gupta
- **Abstract**: Temporal reasoning about historical events is a critical skill for NLP tasks like event extraction, historical entity linking, temporal question answering, timeline summarization, temporal event clustering and temporal natural language inference. Yet efforts on benchmarking temporal reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) are rather limited. Existing temporal reasoning datasets are limited in scale, lack multilingual coverage and focus more on contemporary events. To address these limitations, we present HistoryBank, a multilingual database of 10M+ historical events extracted from Wikipedia timeline pages and article infoboxes. Our database provides unprecedented coverage in both historical depth and linguistic breadth with 10 languages. Additionally, we construct a comprehensive question answering benchmark for temporal reasoning across all languages. This benchmark covers a diverse set of 6 temporal QA reasoning tasks, and we evaluate a suite of popular language models (LLaMA-3-8B, Mistral-7B, Gemma-2-9b, Qwen3-8B, GPT4o) to assess their performance on these tasks. As expected GPT4o performs best across all answer types and languages; Gemma-2 outperforms the other small language models. Our work aims to provide a comprehensive resource for advancing multilingual and temporally-aware natural language understanding of historical events. To facilitate further research, we will make our code and datasets publicly available upon acceptance of this paper.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces HistoryBank, a large-scale multilingual database of over 10 million historical events extracted from Wikipedia's "On This Day" pages and article infoboxes, spanning ten languages. To assess the temporal reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) on this data, the authors also present HistoryBankQA, a comprehensive question-answering benchmark comprising six diverse temporal QA tasks: FactQA, SequenceQA, DurationQA, RelationQA, CountQA, and RecurrenceQA. The benchmark is designed to evaluate different dimensions of temporal reasoning across multiple languages and historical events. The authors evaluate a suite of popular language models (LLaMA-3-8B, Mistral-7B, Gemma-2-9b, Qwen3-8B, GPT4o) on the benchmark in a zero-shot setting, providing initial baseline results and highlighting the strengths and limitations of current models in handling temporally grounded factual reasoning. The authors promise to release the code, dataset, and QA benchmarks publicly.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a significant gap in current NLP research: the limited availability of large-scale, multilingual datasets for benchmarking temporal reasoning capabilities over historical events. Existing datasets tend to be smaller, lack multilingual coverage, or focus primarily on contemporary events. HistoryBank addresses these limitations by providing a substantially larger and more diverse resource.

The creation of HistoryBankQA is also a notable contribution. The benchmark is well-designed, covering a range of temporal reasoning tasks and providing a framework for evaluating models on both factual recall and temporal inference. The evaluation of several popular LLMs offers valuable insights into their current capabilities and limitations in this domain.

**Novelty:** The novelty lies in the scale and multilingual nature of the event database combined with the comprehensive QA benchmark designed specifically for historical events. While there exist other temporal reasoning benchmarks, HistoryBank stands out for its focus on grounded historical knowledge and its cross-lingual scope. The data extraction method, while relying on LLMs, effectively transforms semi-structured Wikipedia infoboxes into structured event data on a large scale.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its potential to advance research in several areas:

*   **Temporal Reasoning:** The benchmark can drive the development of more sophisticated temporal reasoning models.
*   **Multilingual NLP:** The multilingual nature of the dataset encourages the creation of models that can reason about time across different languages and cultures.
*   **Historical NLP:** The resource opens up opportunities for research in areas such as historical entity linking, timeline summarization, and narrative understanding.
*   **LLM Evaluation:** Provides a more challenging real-world benchmark for evaluating LLMs' capabilities.

**Strengths:**

*   **Large-scale and Multilingual Dataset:** HistoryBank is a valuable resource for the NLP community.
*   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** HistoryBankQA covers diverse temporal reasoning tasks.
*   **Clear Evaluation and Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed evaluation of several LLMs and identifies their strengths and weaknesses.
*   **Public Release:** Making the code, data, and benchmarks publicly available will facilitate further research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Bias in Wikipedia Data:** The dataset inherits biases present in Wikipedia, potentially underrepresenting certain cultures and regions.
*   **Synthetic Question Generation:** The use of automatically generated questions may introduce artifacts and limit the diversity of the benchmark.
*   **Limited Evaluation Scope:** The zero-shot evaluation may not fully capture the potential of the models, and further exploration with fine-tuning, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), or other prompt strategies would be valuable.
*   **Lack of Human Evaluation:** Relying on automatic metrics omits insights human evaluation would provide.
*   **Overly Generic Event Descriptions:** Some of the extracted descriptions lack sufficient context.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant contribution by providing a unique, large-scale, and multilingual historical event dataset along with a comprehensive QA benchmark for temporal reasoning. The initial evaluation of LLMs provides valuable insights. While there are limitations related to bias and synthetic question generation, the paper's strengths outweigh these weaknesses. It will likely serve as a valuable resource for advancing research in temporal reasoning, multilingual NLP, and historical NLP. The work provides a useful data collection approach using semi-structured sources and a pragmatic approach to using LLMs for data transformation and annotation at scale.

- **Score**: 8/10

### Generalizable Holographic Reconstruction via Amplitude-Only Diffusion Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12728v1)
- **Authors**: Jeongsol Kim, Chanseok Lee, Jong Chul Ye, Mooseok Jang
- **Abstract**: Phase retrieval in inline holography is a fundamental yet ill-posed inverse problem due to the nonlinear coupling between amplitude and phase in coherent imaging. We present a novel off-the-shelf solution that leverages a diffusion model trained solely on object amplitude to recover both amplitude and phase from diffraction intensities. Using a predictor-corrector sampling framework with separate likelihood gradients for amplitude and phase, our method enables complex field reconstruction without requiring ground-truth phase data for training. We validate the proposed approach through extensive simulations and experiments, demonstrating robust generalization across diverse object shapes, imaging system configurations, and modalities, including lensless setups. Notably, a diffusion prior trained on simple amplitude data (e.g., polystyrene beads) successfully reconstructs complex biological tissue structures, highlighting the method's adaptability. This framework provides a cost-effective, generalizable solution for nonlinear inverse problems in computational imaging, and establishes a foundation for broader coherent imaging applications beyond holography.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel approach for holographic reconstruction, particularly focusing on the challenging problem of phase retrieval in inline holography. The method leverages a diffusion model trained *solely* on amplitude data to reconstruct both the amplitude and phase components of an object's complex field. This is achieved through a predictor-corrector sampling framework with separate likelihood gradients for amplitude and phase, effectively bypassing the need for ground-truth phase data during training. The authors demonstrate the method's generalization capabilities across various object shapes, imaging system configurations (including lensless setups), and even different modalities (training on simple beads and reconstructing complex tissue structures). The paper claims this approach offers a cost-effective and generalizable solution for nonlinear inverse problems in computational imaging.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a promising approach for phase retrieval, addressing a core challenge in holography. The use of a diffusion model trained only on amplitude data is the central innovation, as it sidesteps the practical difficulties of acquiring paired amplitude and phase datasets. The purported generalization capabilities are significant and, if fully validated, could make this a valuable tool for diverse imaging applications.

*Strengths:*

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using amplitude-only trained diffusion models for *joint* amplitude and phase reconstruction is innovative. While diffusion models have been applied to imaging problems, this particular application to holographic phase retrieval, and the specific predictor-corrector framework, appears novel.
*   **Practical Significance:** Eliminating the need for ground-truth phase data during training is a major practical advantage. This reduces the experimental complexity and cost associated with traditional holographic setups. The demonstrated generalization across different object types and modalities suggests broader applicability than many existing techniques.
*   **Technical Soundness:** The paper outlines the method clearly, including the predictor-corrector framework and the use of distinct likelihood gradients. The incorporation of Tweedie's formula for physical constraint enforcement adds to the technical rigor.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The results presented, including simulations and experimental validations with both polystyrene beads and tissue sections, are generally compelling. The comparison with existing methods (multi-height, Deep Decoder, Deep Image Prior) supports the claim of superior reconstruction quality.

*Weaknesses and Concerns:*

*   **Clarity on Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion on the sensitivity of the method to hyperparameters (e.g., step size in the predictor-corrector, learning rate of the diffusion model, etc.). It is important to analyze the robustness of these parameters for generalization in different environments.
*   **Computational Cost:** The paper does not address the computational cost of the diffusion-based reconstruction. Diffusion models can be computationally intensive, and the inference time might be a limiting factor for certain applications, especially real-time or high-throughput imaging.
*   **Error analysis and quantification:** The paper discusses the accuracy of the reconstructed complex fields, but it would be beneficial to include a thorough and detailed error analysis. The experimental data provided is limited, and it lacks a thorough analysis of error propagation from the measurement stage to the result of complex amplitude reconstruction.
*   **Limitations of the Generalization:** The "extreme case" demonstration of the ability of the proposed method is still limited in many aspects. More detail should be given on the differences between the benchtop and lensless setup. Furthermore, the paper should analyze the generalization capabilities for the cases when there are multiple objects in the view.
*   **Lack of details on the noise characteristics** The model should be tested under different conditions of measurement, and it should be tested with different levels of noise to ensure generalizability in the variety of practical cases.
*   **Comparison with current methods:** Comparisons with recent advanced methods should be discussed to demonstrate the superiority of the proposed method. Also, more details should be added to the results section to ensure its clear superiority over the other available methods.

*Significance and Potential Impact:*

The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of holographic imaging by offering a more accessible and versatile phase retrieval solution. The amplitude-only training paradigm could open up new avenues for applying holography in resource-constrained settings or in scenarios where acquiring phase data is impractical. The claimed generalization capabilities could also accelerate the adoption of holography in diverse applications, such as biomedical imaging, material science, and industrial inspection.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially significant contribution to the field of holographic imaging. The amplitude-only training approach, coupled with the predictor-corrector framework, offers a promising solution to the phase retrieval problem. The experimental results demonstrate the method's feasibility and potential for generalization. However, the paper has some notable limitations, including the sensitivity of the method to hyperparameters, the lack of evaluation of the computational cost of the method, and the need for a more rigorous error analysis and quantification. The score is therefore high, reflecting the novelty and potential impact, but tempered by the need for further research and validation to address the identified weaknesses.

- **Classification**: physics.optics
- **Score**: 7/10

### Zero-shot Graph Reasoning via Retrieval Augmented Framework with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12743v1)
- **Authors**: Hanqing Li, Kiran Sheena Jyothi, Henry Liang, Sharika Mahadevan, Diego Klabjan
- **Abstract**: We propose a new, training-free method, Graph Reasoning via Retrieval Augmented Framework (GRRAF), that harnesses retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) alongside the code-generation capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to address a wide range of graph reasoning tasks. In GRRAF, the target graph is stored in a graph database, and the LLM is prompted to generate executable code queries that retrieve the necessary information. This approach circumvents the limitations of existing methods that require extensive finetuning or depend on predefined algorithms, and it incorporates an error feedback loop with a time-out mechanism to ensure both correctness and efficiency. Experimental evaluations on the GraphInstruct dataset reveal that GRRAF achieves 100% accuracy on most graph reasoning tasks, including cycle detection, bipartite graph checks, shortest path computation, and maximum flow, while maintaining consistent token costs regardless of graph sizes. Imperfect but still very high performance is observed on subgraph matching. Notably, GRRAF scales effectively to large graphs with up to 10,000 nodes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GRRAF, a novel, training-free, zero-shot method for graph reasoning tasks. GRRAF leverages Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) along with the code-generation capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The target graph is stored in a graph database (Neo4j or NetworkX), and the LLM is prompted to generate executable code queries to retrieve relevant information. The approach incorporates an error feedback loop with a time-out mechanism to ensure correctness and efficiency. Experiments on the GraphInstruct dataset demonstrate that GRRAF achieves high accuracy on various graph reasoning tasks, scales well to large graphs, and outperforms state-of-the-art benchmarks like GraphWiz and GAR.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel approach by applying RAG to the domain of graph reasoning. This is significant because previous methods relied on extensive finetuning or predefined algorithms, limiting their flexibility. GRRAF's approach of generating executable code queries to interact with a graph database is innovative. However, the underlying components (RAG, code generation by LLMs) are not entirely new in themselves, but their combination and application to graph reasoning is what sets it apart. The incorporation of an error feedback loop and timeout mechanism further enhance the method's practicality and efficiency.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from several factors:

    *   **Accuracy and Scalability:** The paper demonstrates GRRAF's ability to achieve high accuracy on a range of graph reasoning tasks, including tasks where prior methods performed poorly. The ability to scale effectively to large graphs (up to 10,000 nodes) without performance degradation is a major advantage.
    *   **Training-Free Approach:** The method being training-free is crucial.  Finotuning LLMs is resource-intensive and can lead to overfitting or poor generalization to new graph structures. GRRAF offers a more adaptable and readily deployable solution.
    *   **Zero-Shot Capability:** It allows to solve previously unseen tasks.
    *   **Practical Applications:** Graph reasoning is a core task in numerous domains (network analysis, social networks, etc.). An accurate, scalable, and training-free method like GRRAF can have a significant impact.
    *   **Limitations Acknowledged:** The authors clearly identify limitations, particularly in solving NP-complete problems like subgraph matching, where the generated code can have exponential time complexity. They also note the potential for generating lower-quality Cypher queries compared to Python code. This transparency strengthens the paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **NP-complete Tasks Handling:** The workaround for NP-complete problems (falling back to directly prompting the LLM) is a concession and means the method isn't a complete solution for *all* graph reasoning tasks. The lack of a robust solution here is a clear limitation.
    *   **Dependencies on LLM Code Generation:** The method is inherently dependent on the quality of code generated by the LLM. While GPT-40 performs well, there's a reliance on the LLM's continued improvement in this area.
    *   **Overhead of Code Generation:** While the paper claims token efficiency related to graph size, there's still the overhead of code generation and execution. A direct reasoning method, if it could achieve similar accuracy, might be faster.
    *   **Potential for prompt engineering:** The performance could be a little bit sensitive with the prompts.
    *   **Cypher Query Quality:** The poorer quality of Cypher queries generated is a negative.
    *   **Complexity and Error Feedback:** The error feedback loop, while beneficial, adds complexity to the system. The performance of the error loop depends on the LLM's ability to identify and fix errors in the generated code, which is not always perfect.

*   **Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of graph reasoning research. It demonstrates the viability of RAG-based approaches and highlights the importance of code generation capabilities in LLMs. It will likely stimulate further work on improving code generation for graph queries and addressing the limitations in handling NP-complete problems.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel and significant contribution to the field of graph reasoning. The innovative application of RAG and code generation techniques offers a promising solution that addresses key limitations of existing methods. The scalability, accuracy, and training-free nature of GRRAF make it a valuable tool with potential for practical applications.

While the paper has some limitations, particularly with handling NP-complete problems and the dependency on LLM code generation quality, these do not overshadow the overall impact and novelty. The weaknesses are acknowledged by the authors, demonstrating a good understanding of the method's scope. The paper's potential influence on the field, stimulating further research and development in RAG-based graph reasoning and code generation, justifies a high score. It's not a perfect solution (hence not a 9 or 10), but it's a substantial step forward. The score is based on the overall balance of novelty, significance, and limitations, with a particular emphasis on the method's potential to drive future research in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Toward Ownership Understanding of Objects: Active Question Generation with Large Language Model and Probabilistic Generative Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12754v1)
- **Authors**: Saki Hashimoto, Shoichi Hasegawa, Tomochika Ishikawa, Akira Taniguchi, Yoshinobu Hagiwara, Lotfi El Hafi, Tadahiro Taniguchi
- **Abstract**: Robots operating in domestic and office environments must understand object ownership to correctly execute instructions such as ``Bring me my cup.'' However, ownership cannot be reliably inferred from visual features alone. To address this gap, we propose Active Ownership Learning (ActOwL), a framework that enables robots to actively generate and ask ownership-related questions to users. ActOwL employs a probabilistic generative model to select questions that maximize information gain, thereby acquiring ownership knowledge efficiently to improve learning efficiency. Additionally, by leveraging commonsense knowledge from Large Language Models (LLM), objects are pre-classified as either shared or owned, and only owned objects are targeted for questioning. Through experiments in a simulated home environment and a real-world laboratory setting, ActOwL achieved significantly higher ownership clustering accuracy with fewer questions than baseline methods. These findings demonstrate the effectiveness of combining active inference with LLM-guided commonsense reasoning, advancing the capability of robots to acquire ownership knowledge for practical and socially appropriate task execution.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Active Ownership Learning (ActOwL), a framework that allows robots to actively learn object ownership through question generation. ActOwL combines a probabilistic generative model with commonsense reasoning from Large Language Models (LLMs). First, the LLM classifies objects as shared or owned. The robot then generates ownership-related questions for objects classified as owned, selecting questions to maximize information gain (IG) and improve learning efficiency. Experiments in simulated and real-world environments demonstrate that ActOwL achieves higher ownership clustering accuracy with fewer questions than baseline methods. The paper demonstrates that combining active inference with LLM-guided commonsense reasoning improves the ability of robots to acquire ownership knowledge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of human-robot interaction by addressing the challenge of object ownership understanding. The approach of using an LLM for initial classification and then using active question generation is logical and practical.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Integration:** The integration of an LLM for initial object classification with active inference via IG is a novel approach that leverages the strengths of both methods.  This helps to focus the questioning and reduce the burden on the user.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The paper provides thorough experimental validation in both simulated and real-world settings.  The inclusion of ablation studies further strengthens the analysis and provides insights into the contribution of individual components.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of object ownership in human-robot interaction and provides a practical solution.
*   **Strong Baselines:** Using several baselines enables the reader to understand the effectiveness of ActOwL.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLM Accuracy:** The framework is dependent on the accuracy of the LLM for the initial classification of objects as shared or owned. The LLM output may vary, introducing a risk of misclassification and impacting performance, as highlighted in experiment 2.
*   **Limited Representation of Ownership:** The paper simplifies ownership as a binary relation (owned or shared).  This is a limitation, as real-world ownership can be more complex, including group ownership or partial ownership.
*   **Lack of Autonomy:** The robot relies on manually set parameters, such as multimodal feature weights. It also requires the ground-truth labels to be assigned to it, removing some autonomy.
*   **Context-Specific Results:** The effectiveness of the approach may depend on the specific environment and objects being considered.  The color information was useful in one experiment, but it isn't expected to be universally effective.
*   **Spatial Relationship Accuracy:** The LLM-generated questions can sometimes miss accurate spatial relationships. The authors highlight this limitation when they point out that LLM cannot fully capture spatial relationships.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work lies in its potential to enable robots to interact more effectively with humans in shared environments. By learning object ownership, robots can perform tasks more accurately and appropriately. The active question generation approach reduces the burden on users and improves the efficiency of learning. While the paper has limitations, it is a significant step towards more socially intelligent robots.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the novelty of integrating LLMs with active question generation for object ownership, the thorough experimental validation, and clear problem definition, a score of 7 is appropriate. The limitations regarding the reliance on LLM accuracy, simplified ownership representation, and lack of autonomy prevent a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### InfoGain-RAG: Boosting Retrieval-Augmented Generation via Document Information Gain-based Reranking and Filtering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12765v1)
- **Authors**: Zihan Wang, Zihan Liang, Zhou Shao, Yufei Ma, Huangyu Dai, Ben Chen, Lingtao Mao, Chenyi Lei, Yuqing Ding, Han Li
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has emerged as a promising approach to address key limitations of Large Language Models (LLMs), such as hallucination, outdated knowledge, and lacking reference. However, current RAG frameworks often struggle with identifying whether retrieved documents meaningfully contribute to answer generation. This shortcoming makes it difficult to filter out irrelevant or even misleading content, which notably impacts the final performance. In this paper, we propose Document Information Gain (DIG), a novel metric designed to quantify the contribution of retrieved documents to correct answer generation. DIG measures a document's value by computing the difference of LLM's generation confidence with and without the document augmented. Further, we introduce InfoGain-RAG, a framework that leverages DIG scores to train a specialized reranker, which prioritizes each retrieved document from exact distinguishing and accurate sorting perspectives. This approach can effectively filter out irrelevant documents and select the most valuable ones for better answer generation. Extensive experiments across various models and benchmarks demonstrate that InfoGain-RAG can significantly outperform existing approaches, on both single and multiple retrievers paradigm. Specifically on NaturalQA, it achieves the improvements of 17.9%, 4.5%, 12.5% in exact match accuracy against naive RAG, self-reflective RAG and modern ranking-based RAG respectively, and even an average of 15.3% increment on advanced proprietary model GPT-4o across all datasets. These results demonstrate the feasibility of InfoGain-RAG as it can offer a reliable solution for RAG in multiple applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces InfoGain-RAG, a novel framework that enhances Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) by incorporating a Document Information Gain (DIG) metric.  DIG quantifies the contribution of retrieved documents to the LLM's confidence in generating correct answers. InfoGain-RAG uses DIG scores to train a reranker, prioritizing documents that significantly improve answer generation confidence and filtering out irrelevant or misleading ones. The authors demonstrate InfoGain-RAG's effectiveness across multiple models and benchmarks, showing improvements over existing RAG approaches in both single and multi-retriever settings. Key benefits include improved accuracy, efficient document selection (by avoiding multiple LLM calls), and a plug-and-play reranking module.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central novelty lies in the DIG metric and its use in training a reranker. While reranking in RAG is not new, the focus on *information gain* (measured by the change in LLM confidence) rather than semantic similarity is a valuable contribution. This helps in selecting documents that are truly *helpful* for answering the question, not just semantically related. The multi-task training approach for the reranker, combining cross-entropy and margin loss, also contributes to the paper's novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its ability to address a key challenge in RAG: the selection of relevant and useful documents. By explicitly quantifying the information gain of documents, the framework facilitates the filtering of noise and the prioritization of valuable context. The empirical results demonstrate considerable performance gains across various benchmarks and models, indicating practical utility.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Principled Approach:** The DIG metric offers a more direct and interpretable way of evaluating document relevance compared to relying solely on semantic similarity or self-reflection mechanisms.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper includes extensive experiments across different models (both open-source and proprietary), datasets, and RAG settings (single and multi-retriever). This strengthens the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Efficiency:** InfoGain-RAG only requires a single LLM call during inference, making it computationally efficient compared to approaches involving multiple LLM calls for self-reflection or retrieval.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Solution:** The paper clearly articulates the problem, proposes a well-defined solution, and provides a thorough analysis of its effectiveness.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **DIG Calculation Complexity:** While presented as efficient overall, the method still relies on calculating DIG scores during the data collection/training phase. The complexity of this calculation relative to standard semantic similarity metrics is not thoroughly discussed.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The method introduces several hyperparameters (W, α, b1, b2, β, γ) that influence performance. The selection and tuning of these hyperparameters could significantly impact results and might require careful optimization for different tasks and models. While some details are provided, a more detailed sensitivity analysis would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Scope of Modalities:** The current evaluation is limited to textual data. The paper does not explore the applicability of InfoGain-RAG to other modalities (e.g., images, code).
    *   **Focus on factual accuracy:** The DIG metric focuses on the LLM's confidence in generating *correct* answers. The method does not directly address broader issues like bias, safety, or the overall quality/coherence of the generated text beyond accuracy.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the RAG research community by shifting the focus towards information gain and more direct metrics of document utility. The proposed framework can be adopted and extended by other researchers to develop more effective RAG systems.

Overall, the paper presents a novel and promising approach to address a key challenge in RAG. While some limitations exist, the strengths of the paper, particularly its principled approach and empirical results, outweigh the weaknesses. Therefore a high score is assigned with emphasis on the importance of focusing on information gain of documents rather than only semantic similarities.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 8/10

### BATR-FST: Bi-Level Adaptive Token Refinement for Few-Shot Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12768v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammed Al-Habib, Zuping Zhang, Abdulrahman Noman
- **Abstract**: Vision Transformers (ViTs) have shown significant promise in computer vision applications. However, their performance in few-shot learning is limited by challenges in refining token-level interactions, struggling with limited training data, and developing a strong inductive bias. Existing methods often depend on inflexible token matching or basic similarity measures, which limit the effective incorporation of global context and localized feature refinement. To address these challenges, we propose Bi-Level Adaptive Token Refinement for Few-Shot Transformers (BATR-FST), a two-stage approach that progressively improves token representations and maintains a robust inductive bias for few-shot classification. During the pre-training phase, Masked Image Modeling (MIM) provides Vision Transformers (ViTs) with transferable patch-level representations by recreating masked image regions, providing a robust basis for subsequent adaptation. In the meta-fine-tuning phase, BATR-FST incorporates a Bi-Level Adaptive Token Refinement module that utilizes Token Clustering to capture localized interactions, Uncertainty-Aware Token Weighting to prioritize dependable features, and a Bi-Level Attention mechanism to balance intra-cluster and inter-cluster relationships, thereby facilitating thorough token refinement. Furthermore, Graph Token Propagation ensures semantic consistency between support and query instances, while a Class Separation Penalty preserves different class borders, enhancing discriminative capability. Extensive experiments on three benchmark few-shot datasets demonstrate that BATR-FST achieves superior results in both 1-shot and 5-shot scenarios and improves the few-shot classification via transformers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BATR-FST (Bi-Level Adaptive Token Refinement for Few-Shot Transformers), a two-stage framework designed to improve the performance of Vision Transformers (ViTs) in few-shot learning scenarios.  The first stage involves pre-training the ViT using Masked Image Modeling (MIM) to learn robust patch-level features.  The second stage, meta-finetuning, employs a Bi-Level Adaptive Token Refinement module (BATR) to refine token representations. This module includes token clustering for local interactions, uncertainty-aware token weighting to prioritize reliable features, and a bi-level attention mechanism to balance intra- and inter-cluster relationships.  Graph Token Propagation ensures semantic consistency between support and query instances, while a class separation penalty encourages discriminative feature learning.  Experiments on mini-ImageNet, tiered-ImageNet, and CIFAR-FS demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Relevance:** Addressing the limitations of ViTs in few-shot learning is a significant and timely problem. ViTs, while powerful, often struggle in low-data regimes without proper inductive biases.
*   **Comprehensive Approach:** The BATR-FST framework integrates several components (MIM pre-training, token clustering, uncertainty weighting, bi-level attention, graph propagation, and class separation) in a well-defined manner. This multi-faceted approach has the potential to effectively address the challenges of few-shot learning with transformers.
*   **Uncertainty Aware Weighting:** The use of monte carlo dropout variance to give weights to tokens is a good idea. It allows the network to ignore noisy or untrustworthy tokens.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents solid experimental results on standard benchmarks, demonstrating improved performance compared to existing methods, including other transformer-based approaches and methods using ResNet backbones. The Ablation study gives great information to allow the reader to understand how important each part of the network is.
*   **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is generally well-structured and clearly explains the proposed method and experimental setup.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the integration of several components is a strength, the individual components themselves (e.g., token clustering, self-attention, graph propagation) are not entirely novel. The novelty lies primarily in their combination and adaptation for few-shot learning with ViTs. The reliance on established techniques could be seen as a lack of significant innovation at the individual component level.
*   **Complexity:** The framework is relatively complex, involving several modules and hyperparameters.  This complexity may make it difficult to implement and tune in practice. The paper's reliance on several components makes the network harder to understand.
*   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** While the paper presents overall performance improvements, it lacks a detailed analysis of specific scenarios where the method might fail or underperform.  Understanding these limitations is crucial for practical applications. It would have been better to see in what situations the network falls short.

*   **Reproducibility**: Even with all the information, the experiments can be very hard to reproduce due to many hyperparameters.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by demonstrating a way to effectively adapt Vision Transformers for few-shot learning. The proposed BATR-FST framework shows promising results and provides a strong baseline for future research in this area. The emphasis on token refinement and the integration of uncertainty-aware weighting are particularly noteworthy contributions. However, the incremental nature of the novelty and the complexity of the framework somewhat limit the paper's overall significance.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid engineering effort that effectively combines existing techniques to achieve improved performance in a relevant problem domain. While the individual components lack significant novelty, the well-integrated framework and strong experimental results warrant a positive assessment. However, the relative complexity of the approach and the absence of detailed failure case analysis prevent it from achieving a higher score. Therefore, it is more of an engineering accomplishment rather than a groundbreaking scientific discovery.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Double Helix Diffusion for Cross-Domain Anomaly Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12787v1)
- **Authors**: Linchun Wu, Qin Zou, Xianbiao Qi, Bo Du, Zhongyuan Wang, Qingquan Li
- **Abstract**: Visual anomaly inspection is critical in manufacturing, yet hampered by the scarcity of real anomaly samples for training robust detectors. Synthetic data generation presents a viable strategy for data augmentation; however, current methods remain constrained by two principal limitations: 1) the generation of anomalies that are structurally inconsistent with the normal background, and 2) the presence of undesirable feature entanglement between synthesized images and their corresponding annotation masks, which undermines the perceptual realism of the output. This paper introduces Double Helix Diffusion (DH-Diff), a novel cross-domain generative framework designed to simultaneously synthesize high-fidelity anomaly images and their pixel-level annotation masks, explicitly addressing these challenges. DH-Diff employs a unique architecture inspired by a double helix, cycling through distinct modules for feature separation, connection, and merging. Specifically, a domain-decoupled attention mechanism mitigates feature entanglement by enhancing image and annotation features independently, and meanwhile a semantic score map alignment module ensures structural authenticity by coherently integrating anomaly foregrounds. DH-Diff offers flexible control via text prompts and optional graphical guidance. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DH-Diff significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in diversity and authenticity, leading to significant improvements in downstream anomaly detection performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Double Helix Diffusion (DH-Diff)," a novel framework for generating realistic anomaly images and their corresponding segmentation masks. The method addresses two key limitations of existing anomaly generation techniques: structural inconsistencies between anomalies and the background, and feature entanglement between the generated image and mask. DH-Diff employs a diffusion model with a unique architecture inspired by a double helix, cycling through modules for feature separation, connection, and merging. Key components include a domain-decoupled attention mechanism (DDA) to mitigate feature entanglement and a semantic score map modification module (SSM) to ensure structural authenticity by aligning anomaly foregrounds. The framework is controllable through text prompts and optional graphical guidance. The authors demonstrate through experiments that DH-Diff outperforms state-of-the-art methods in terms of diversity and authenticity, leading to improvements in downstream anomaly detection performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty in several aspects. The double-helix architecture is a conceptually interesting way to structure the diffusion process for anomaly generation. The DDA and SSM modules specifically target the critical challenges of feature entanglement and structural consistency, which are not adequately addressed by prior work. The cyclical approach offers a way to combine the benefit of one-stage and two-stage methods. The simultaneous generation of high-fidelity anomaly images and masks with explicit control is also a contribution.
*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to improve anomaly detection systems by providing more realistic and diverse training data. The lack of real anomaly data is a long-standing problem, and better synthetic data generation techniques directly address this issue. The improvements in downstream anomaly detection tasks demonstrated by the authors support this claim.  The method may be especially useful in scenarios where anomalies are subtle or complex, as the structural consistency enforced by SSM is critical.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear explanation of the limitations of existing methods.
    *   The proposed architecture is innovative and addresses the identified limitations in a thoughtful manner.
    *   The individual components (DDA, SSM) are justified, and their contributions are demonstrated through ablation studies.
    *   Extensive experiments compare the method against a wide range of state-of-the-art techniques on multiple datasets.
    *   Qualitative results visually support the claims of improved realism and diversity.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the visual results look impressive, it is difficult to judge subtle differences in structural consistency without more detailed analysis of the generated data and potentially human evaluations.
    *   The performance gains are not always dramatic.  While generally outperforming other methods, the difference is often incremental, suggesting that while the improvements are consistent, they are not revolutionary.
    *   The "double helix" analogy, while visually appealing, is somewhat superficial, since it mainly describes the cyclic iteration of the components, but not a deeper integration as the DNA strands. The link between a double helix and anomaly detection is not entirely obvious, weakening the conceptual framing.
    *   The description of the SSM module, especially the fusion of the score maps, could be clearer. The rationale for the convolutional layer and mean function requires further explanation.
    *   The computational cost of the method relative to simpler approaches is not explicitly discussed.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in anomaly generation. The focus on structural consistency and feature disentanglement is likely to be a valuable direction. The proposed architecture and modules can serve as building blocks for more advanced anomaly generation systems. Also, it will likely be adapted to new generative models to produce higher-quality anomaly examples for training models.

**Overall Assessment:**

DH-Diff provides a solid contribution to anomaly generation by addressing crucial challenges in structural consistency and feature entanglement. The framework introduces a novel architecture and specialized modules that show promising results across multiple datasets. While the performance gains may not be transformative, the consistent improvements, well-designed components, and extensive evaluation make this a valuable and influential paper.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### When Large Language Models Meet UAVs: How Far Are We?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12795v1)
- **Authors**: Yihua Chen, Xingle Que, Jiashuo Zhang, Ting Chen, Guangshun Li, Jiachi Chen
- **Abstract**: The integration of unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) and large language models (LLMs) has emerged as a research direction of growing interest, with the potential to address challenges in autonomous decision-making, human-UAV interaction, and real-time adaptability. However, existing studies have remained largely in preliminary exploration with a limited understanding of real-world practice, risking a misalignment between academic research and practical needs and hindering the translation of results. To examine and address these potential challenges, we conducted an empirical study of 74 selected papers and 56 public GitHub projects, identified nine task types for LLMs in UAV systems, and quantified their distribution. Our findings show that academic research emphasizes theoretical modeling and task optimization with dispersed attention across tasks. In contrast, industrial projects focus on flight control, task planning, and human-machine interaction, prioritizing operability and efficiency. To further capture industry perspectives, we distributed an online questionnaire. We obtained 52 valid responses: 40.4% of practitioners have attempted to apply LLMs to UAV tasks. We further identify factors that impede real-world integration, including technological maturity, performance, safety, cost, and other considerations. Finally, we highlight challenges for future development and provide recommendations.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, with a focus on its novelty and significance:

**Summary:**

The paper "When Large Language Models Meet UAVs: How Far Are We?" presents an empirical study investigating the integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) with Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs). The authors conduct a systematic literature review, analyze open-source GitHub projects, and perform a practitioner survey to understand the current research status, differences between academic and industrial approaches, and challenges in real-world deployments of UAV-LLM systems. They identify nine task types for LLMs in UAVs, quantify their distribution, and highlight gaps between academic research and industrial practices. The study reveals that academic research emphasizes theoretical modeling and task optimization, while industrial projects prioritize flight control, task planning, and human-machine interaction, focusing on operability and efficiency.  Key findings include that industry is more focused on stable onboard solutions and that practitioners are concerned about LLM performance, safety, cost, and lack of training data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive and Multi-faceted Approach:** The paper's strength lies in its comprehensive approach. The combination of a systematic literature review, GitHub project analysis, and a practitioner survey provides a well-rounded understanding of the UAV-LLM landscape. This is far more comprehensive than most papers in this emerging area.
*   **Identification of Key Tasks and Gaps:** Identifying and categorizing the nine tasks that LLMs can perform in UAV systems is a valuable contribution. Highlighting the discrepancies between academia and industry is also significant, providing a roadmap for future research. The findings from the practitioner survey provide compelling real-world perspectives that are often missing in purely theoretical studies.
*   **Timeliness and Relevance:** This is a very timely topic given the rapid advancement of both LLMs and UAV technologies. The paper addresses a real need to understand how these two technologies can be effectively integrated, and the discussion of challenges and recommendations is highly relevant.
*   **Data Availability:** Making the data and results publicly available increases the reproducibility of the work and provides a resource for other researchers.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Industrial Project Data:** While analyzing GitHub projects provides some insight into industry practices, GitHub is often not representative of all industry work, particularly given proprietary concerns. Many deployed industrial systems will not have publicly available codebases. This limits the generalizability of the analysis of the industrial side.
*   **Survey Limitations:** The survey response rate (6.85%) is relatively low, raising concerns about potential response bias. Although the survey provides useful insights, it would be stronger with a larger and more representative sample. A more detailed discussion of non-response bias would be beneficial.
*   **Depth of Analysis:** While the paper is broad in scope, some areas could benefit from a deeper dive. For example, a more detailed analysis of specific safety concerns or a more in-depth comparison of specific algorithms used in academia vs. industry would strengthen the paper.
*   **Lack of Predictive Power/Future Directions:** While the challenges for future work are highlighted, a more concrete articulation of predictive directions for future research and specific areas for innovation is lacking. The paper does a solid job assessing the current state but doesn't boldly project the *next* big thing beyond addressing the existing limitations.
* **Novelty and Significance** While timely and of interest to the research community, the insights provided by the analysis seem rather obvious. Industrial projects need to work and be cost-effective, whereas academic projects can be more exploratory.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper offers a notable contribution by systematically dissecting the UAV-LLM integration landscape. The approach is methodologically sound, and the findings have clear practical implications. That said, much of the findings in industrial practices are "obvious" (of course, industrial practices must focus on cost-effective and safe systems), which reduces the value of the paper. It's more of an extensive assessment, rather than a groundbreaking new insight.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **6**.

*   It's above average because it presents a well-structured, empirical analysis of a timely and relevant topic. The methodology is sound, and the combination of literature review, project analysis, and practitioner survey is a strong point.
*   It is brought down by the limitations discussed above, particularly the data limitations of capturing industrial projects (not just Github repositories), and the (somewhat) limited number of survey responses. While the work has practical value, it doesn't represent a significant paradigm shift or major breakthrough. It's more of a solid, incremental contribution that thoroughly assesses a landscape. Further the "obviousness" of some of the results, reduces the impact of the work.
