### LLM-Based Approach for Enhancing Maintainability of Automotive Architectures
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12798v1)
- **Authors**: Nenad Petrovic, Lukasz Mazur, Alois Knoll
- **Abstract**: There are many bottlenecks that decrease the flexibility of automotive systems, making their long-term maintenance, as well as updates and extensions in later lifecycle phases increasingly difficult, mainly due to long re-engineering, standardization, and compliance procedures, as well as heterogeneity and numerosity of devices and underlying software components involved. In this paper, we explore the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) when it comes to the automation of tasks and processes that aim to increase the flexibility of automotive systems. Three case studies towards achieving this goal are considered as outcomes of early-stage research: 1) updates, hardware abstraction, and compliance, 2) interface compatibility checking, and 3) architecture modification suggestions. For proof-of-concept implementation, we rely on OpenAI's GPT-4o model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) to enhance the maintainability and flexibility of automotive architectures.  It focuses on automating tasks related to system evolution, addressing challenges like long re-engineering cycles, standardization, and heterogeneity in automotive systems. The authors present three case studies using OpenAI's GPT-4o: (1) system updates, hardware abstraction, and compliance, (2) interface compatibility checking, and (3) architecture modification suggestions.  They leverage LLMs in conjunction with model-driven engineering (MDE) to create and manipulate system models, analyze interface compatibility, and suggest architectural modifications while considering standardization constraints.  The goal is to reduce the cognitive load and effort involved in integrating new software components and adapting systems to new requirements.  Experiments are conducted on scenarios inspired by autonomous driving reference architectures and Autoware HAL to evaluate the performance of the LLM-driven approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Relevant Problem:**  The paper addresses a critical and timely issue in the automotive industry: the need for more flexible and maintainable software architectures to cope with rapid technological advancements and evolving standards.
    * **Novel Application of LLMs:**  While LLMs are increasingly used in various domains, their application to automating specific tasks within automotive architecture evolution, particularly in conjunction with MDE, is a relatively novel approach. The use of LLMs to generate OCL rules for compliance checking is also interesting.
    * **Clear Case Studies:** The paper presents three distinct case studies that provide concrete examples of how LLMs can be applied to different aspects of automotive system evolution.  These case studies illustrate the potential benefits and challenges of the proposed approach.
    * **Integration with MDE:** The synergy of LLMs and MDE is a strong point. By leveraging formally grounded methods with model-based representation, the authors are able to improve the verifiability of tasks. The MDE approach also improves LLM promptability, and reduces token consumption.
    * **Experimentation:**  The paper includes experimental results, providing quantitative data on the performance (execution time and correctness) of the LLM-driven approaches. This grounding in experimental results improves the paper's credibility.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Depth of Implementation:** The paper focuses primarily on the conceptual design and proof-of-concept implementation of the proposed approaches. It lacks a detailed discussion of the practical challenges and limitations of deploying these LLM-driven solutions in real-world automotive development environments.
    * **Evaluation Metrics:** While execution time and correctness are reported, a deeper analysis of the quality of the LLM-generated outputs would be beneficial. Metrics such as the relevance, completeness, and feasibility of the architecture modification suggestions could be included.
    * **Generalizability:** The case studies are based on specific scenarios inspired by autonomous driving. The extent to which these approaches can be generalized to other automotive domains or different types of software architectures is unclear.
    * **Lack of Comparison with Existing Approaches:** The paper doesn't provide a thorough comparison of the LLM-driven approaches with traditional methods for automotive system evolution. A discussion of the advantages and disadvantages of using LLMs compared to existing techniques would strengthen the paper.
    * **LLM Reliance:** The paper heavily relies on a single LLM (GPT-4o). There's no discussion regarding comparison with other LLMs with differing characteristics or potential optimization through fine tuning. It is unknown whether other LLMs can produce similar or better results.
    * **Impact of Prompt Engineering:** The paper acknowledges that prompt engineering is key. Providing more details on the prompt engineering process and the prompts used would increase the reproducibility and value of the study.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper makes a novel contribution by exploring the potential of LLMs for automating and improving various tasks related to the evolution of automotive architectures. The combination of LLMs with MDE shows promise for enhancing the flexibility, maintainability, and adaptability of automotive systems. The case studies provide concrete examples of how this approach can be applied, and the experimental results offer preliminary evidence of its effectiveness. However, the paper's limited depth of implementation, evaluation, and comparison with existing approaches limits its immediate practical impact.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a promising and novel application of LLMs to a relevant problem in the automotive industry. It presents a clear and well-structured approach, supported by concrete case studies and experimental results. While the paper has some limitations in terms of depth of implementation, evaluation, and comparison with existing approaches, its potential for improving automotive system evolution justifies a score of **7**. The combination of LLMs and MDE is promising, and the early results suggest that LLMs can play a significant role in automating and improving architecture modification and interface compatibility tasks in the future. The paper provides a good starting point for further research in this area.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### ConvergeWriter: Data-Driven Bottom-Up Article Construction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12811v1)
- **Authors**: Binquan Ji, Jiaqi Wang, Ruiting Li, Xingchen Han, Yiyang Qi, Shichao Wang, Yifei Lu, Yuantao Han, Feiliang Ren
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable prowess in text generation, yet producing long-form, factual documents grounded in extensive external knowledge bases remains a significant challenge. Existing "top-down" methods, which first generate a hypothesis or outline and then retrieve evidence, often suffer from a disconnect between the model's plan and the available knowledge, leading to content fragmentation and factual inaccuracies. To address these limitations, we propose a novel "bottom-up," data-driven framework that inverts the conventional generation pipeline. Our approach is predicated on a "Retrieval-First for Knowledge, Clustering for Structure" strategy, which first establishes the "knowledge boundaries" of the source corpus before any generative planning occurs. Specifically, we perform exhaustive iterative retrieval from the knowledge base and then employ an unsupervised clustering algorithm to organize the retrieved documents into distinct "knowledge clusters." These clusters form an objective, data-driven foundation that directly guides the subsequent generation of a hierarchical outline and the final document content. This bottom-up process ensures that the generated text is strictly constrained by and fully traceable to the source material, proactively adapting to the finite scope of the knowledge base and fundamentally mitigating the risk of hallucination. Experimental results on both 14B and 32B parameter models demonstrate that our method achieves performance comparable to or exceeding state-of-the-art baselines, and is expected to demonstrate unique advantages in knowledge-constrained scenarios that demand high fidelity and structural coherence. Our work presents an effective paradigm for generating reliable, structured, long-form documents, paving the way for more robust LLM applications in high-stakes, knowledge-intensive domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ConvergeWriter: Data-Driven Bottom-Up Article Construction" introduces a novel "bottom-up" approach to long-form text generation, especially for factual documents relying on external knowledge bases. Unlike traditional "top-down" methods that first create an outline and then retrieve information, ConvergeWriter prioritizes knowledge retrieval, clustering, and then outline generation.  The method involves iteratively retrieving relevant documents, using unsupervised clustering to organize them into knowledge clusters, and then generating a hierarchical outline and content based on these clusters. This approach aims to improve factual accuracy, reduce hallucinations, and ensure structural coherence by strictly grounding the generated text in the available source material. Experiments using Wikipedia as a knowledge source and 14B and 32B parameter models show performance comparable to or exceeding state-of-the-art baselines, particularly in knowledge-constrained scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the "bottom-up" approach to long-form text generation. While retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is well-established, inverting the process by first delineating knowledge boundaries through retrieval and clustering *before* generating an outline is a significant departure from the norm. The "Retrieval-First for Knowledge, Clustering for Structure" strategy is a clear and well-defined contribution.  The hierarchical summarization and knowledge cluster organization also add a layer of technical innovation. The idea of using the retrieved and clustered data as the *sole* driver for outline creation directly addresses the problem of outline hallucination, making it more effective in closed-domain scenarios.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a critical issue in LLM-based text generation: factual accuracy and the avoidance of hallucinations.  By strictly grounding the generation in retrieved knowledge, the method tackles this head-on.  The focus on knowledge-intensive domains (e.g., finance, science) further enhances the significance, as these areas demand high levels of reliability. The ability to adapt to the finite scope of the knowledge base is a key advantage for real-world applications where curated knowledge is used, not just the open internet. The detailed evaluation metrics, including the coverage metric which directly tackles the hallucination problem, provide compelling evidence.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of using LLMs for generating factual long-form text with external knowledge.
    *   **Well-Defined Method:** ConvergeWriter's steps are clearly described and easy to understand.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation includes a strong set of baselines (including state-of-the-art approaches like STORM and OmniThink), relevant metrics, and ablation studies. The use of Qwen3 models as both generation and evaluation models is also a sensible and relevant choice.
    *   **Solid Results:** The results consistently demonstrate the effectiveness of ConvergeWriter, especially in terms of document coverage and achieving high average scores in Rubric grading. The clustering ablation study effectively highlights the importance of structure.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Knowledge Source:** While using Wikipedia as the knowledge source provides a controlled environment, it might not fully represent the complexities of real-world, more specialized knowledge bases.  The claim of generalizability to knowledge-intensive domains could be strengthened by experimenting with at least one different domain.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While not explicitly discussed, the iterative retrieval and clustering process may incur higher computational costs compared to simpler RAG methods.  A discussion of the computational trade-offs would be beneficial.
    *   **Ranking Model Dependency:** The method depends on the performance of the ranking model used to re-rank documents. The paper mentions the specific ranking model used but doesn't provide sensitivity analysis of the overall system to different ranking models. This could be a potential limitation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The "bottom-up" approach has the potential to significantly influence the design of future long-form text generation systems, particularly those focused on factual accuracy and knowledge grounding. It could also inspire further research into novel knowledge organization and structuring techniques. The increased traceability of content facilitated by the approach could become a valuable feature in applications where transparency and provenance are critical.

**Rationale for Score:**

ConvergeWriter offers a novel and significant contribution to the field of long-form text generation. The "bottom-up" approach, combined with the emphasis on factual accuracy and the comprehensive evaluation, makes this paper a valuable contribution. The weaknesses, while present, are not severe enough to significantly detract from the overall impact. The potential for the method to influence future research and development in knowledge-grounded text generation is high. However, additional experimentation with different knowledge sources and a more in-depth analysis of computational complexity and sensitivity analysis could strengthen the paper.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### A Pressure-Based Diffusion Model for Influence Maximization on Social Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12822v1)
- **Authors**: Curt Stutsman, Eliot W. Robson, Abhishek K. Umrawal
- **Abstract**: In many real-world scenarios, an individual's local social network carries significant influence over the opinions they form and subsequently propagate to others. In this paper, we propose a novel diffusion model -- the Pressure Threshold model (PT) -- for dynamically simulating the spread of influence through a social network. This new model extends the popular Linear Threshold Model (LT) by adjusting a node's outgoing influence proportional to the influence it receives from its activated neighbors. We address the Influence Maximization (IM) problem, which involves selecting the most effective seed nodes to achieve maximal graph coverage after a diffusion process, and how the problem manifests with the PT Model. Experiments conducted on real-world networks, facilitated by enhancements to the open-source network-diffusion Python library, CyNetDiff, demonstrate unique seed node selection for the PT Model when compared to the LT Model. Moreover, analyses demonstrate that densely connected networks amplify pressure effects more significantly than sparse networks.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided research paper titled "A Pressure-Based Diffusion Model for Influence Maximization on Social Networks." Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel diffusion model called the Pressure Threshold (PT) model, designed as an extension of the widely used Linear Threshold (LT) model. The key innovation of the PT model lies in its dynamic feedback mechanism, where a node's outgoing influence is adjusted proportionally to the influence it receives from its activated neighbors. This aims to better capture reinforcement effects observed in real-world social networks, where repeated exposure to an idea strengthens existing beliefs. The authors address the Influence Maximization (IM) problem in the context of this new model, demonstrating its NP-hardness and exploring its properties. They conducted experiments on real-world networks, showcasing that the PT model can generate distinct seed sets for IM compared to the LT model and can achieve a wider spread of influence. The paper also explores conditions under which the PT model is particularly effective and discusses the limitations related to submodularity when the amplification parameter exceeds zero.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The PT model presents a reasonably novel approach to diffusion modeling by incorporating the concept of influence pressure/amplification based on incoming influence. This is a valuable addition, addressing some limitations of static models like LT in capturing reinforcement effects. This adaptive mechanism offers a more nuanced representation of social influence dynamics. However, dynamic diffusion models are not new, and the literature review, while sufficient, should contextualize the PT model amongst other such models.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in providing a more realistic representation of influence propagation within social networks, particularly those characterized by strong echo chambers or reinforcement mechanisms. The identification of conditions where the PT model performs notably better than the LT model is valuable. The study on the impact of varying `alpha` (the amplification factor) on influence spread offers a quantitative understanding of the model's behavior. Further testing this model on data outside of simulation can provide valuable insights to its performance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear presentation of the PT model with a well-defined mathematical formulation.
    *   Demonstration of NP-hardness, monotonicity, and exploration of submodularity properties, enhancing the theoretical understanding of the model.
    *   Empirical validation on real-world networks, providing practical insights into its behavior and comparing it with the LT model.
    *   A discussion of the limitations (non-submodularity in general cases) gives the reader a good overview of possible problems with the model.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper does not offer a thorough empirical comparison against other existing dynamic diffusion models or similar approaches to influence amplification. This omission weakens the argument for the PT model as a uniquely valuable contribution.
    *   The paper doesn't delve deeply into how one *chooses* the alpha parameter. While experimenting with it is valuable, a guide to setting it could be valuable for users.
    *   The analysis of when the PT model retains submodularity in practice could be stronger. A more rigorous mathematical analysis or additional experimental investigation could solidify that observation.

*   **Impact on the Field:** The PT model offers a valuable framework for researchers interested in capturing reinforcement effects in social networks. It extends the existing toolkit of diffusion models and can potentially lead to better predictions of influence spread in specific contexts.
    *   The discussion on limitations gives an added practical component for researchers who seek to use this model in their own work.

* **Reasoning for Score:**

The paper's contribution, while novel and of reasonable practical utility, lacks a degree of originality that would place it at the top of the scale. While the PT model offers a useful extension to the LT model, the lack of a deep dive into existing dynamic diffusion models weakens the argument for its uniqueness. Also, this work can benefit from showing strong results on a variety of real-world data outside of a simulation environment. Thus, a more moderate score is most suitable.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.SI
- **Score**: 6/10

### DiffHash: Text-Guided Targeted Attack via Diffusion Models against Deep Hashing Image Retrieval
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12824v1)
- **Authors**: Zechao Liu, Zheng Zhou, Xiangkun Chen, Tao Liang, Dapeng Lang
- **Abstract**: Deep hashing models have been widely adopted to tackle the challenges of large-scale image retrieval. However, these approaches face serious security risks due to their vulnerability to adversarial examples. Despite the increasing exploration of targeted attacks on deep hashing models, existing approaches still suffer from a lack of multimodal guidance, reliance on labeling information and dependence on pixel-level operations for attacks. To address these limitations, we proposed DiffHash, a novel diffusion-based targeted attack for deep hashing. Unlike traditional pixel-based attacks that directly modify specific pixels and lack multimodal guidance, our approach focuses on optimizing the latent representations of images, guided by text information generated by a Large Language Model (LLM) for the target image. Furthermore, we designed a multi-space hash alignment network to align the high-dimension image space and text space to the low-dimension binary hash space. During reconstruction, we also incorporated text-guided attention mechanisms to refine adversarial examples, ensuring them aligned with the target semantics while maintaining visual plausibility. Extensive experiments have demonstrated that our method outperforms state-of-the-art (SOTA) targeted attack methods, achieving better black-box transferability and offering more excellent stability across datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DiffHash: Text-Guided Targeted Attack via Diffusion Models against Deep Hashing Image Retrieval" introduces a novel adversarial attack method specifically designed to target deep hashing models used for image retrieval.  The approach, named DiffHash, leverages a diffusion model to optimize adversarial examples in the latent space, guided by text information generated by a Large Language Model (LLM).  A key component is a multi-space hash alignment network (HAN) that aligns image and text representations in a shared hash space.  The method aims to overcome limitations of previous attacks, such as a lack of multimodal guidance, reliance on labeling information, and dependence on pixel-level operations, by focusing on latent representations and using text for semantic guidance.  The authors demonstrate through experiments that DiffHash outperforms state-of-the-art targeted attack methods, achieving better black-box transferability and stability across datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a few novel aspects:
    *   **Latent Space Manipulation:**  Moving the attack to the latent space of a diffusion model is a significant improvement, circumventing the gradient blockage problem caused by the non-differentiable sign function inherent in hashing and quantization errors. While diffusion model-based attacks are becoming more common in other areas, it's a relatively new direction for deep hashing.
    *   **Text Guidance:**  Using LLM-generated text to guide the attack is a useful approach, particularly in scenarios where labels are scarce or unreliable. This provides a richer semantic grounding than simple label-based attacks.
    *   **Multi-Space Alignment:** The introduction of HAN to align text and image features is a clever design choice that promotes semantic consistency and helps the attack target the desired hash codes effectively.
    *   **Overall Integration:** While individual components (diffusion models, text embeddings, adversarial attacks) are not entirely novel on their own, the way the authors combine these techniques *specifically for targeting deep hashing models* demonstrates ingenuity.

* **Significance:**
    *   **Practical Relevance:**  Adversarial attacks on deep hashing models pose a real threat, especially in applications like e-commerce and content recommendation. Demonstrating a more effective attack highlights the vulnerability of these systems and motivates the need for robust defenses.
    *   **Performance Gains:** The experimental results convincingly show that DiffHash outperforms existing targeted attack methods across multiple datasets and hashing architectures.  The improvements in black-box transferability are particularly valuable, indicating that the attack can generalize to unseen hashing models.
    *   **Insightful Design:**  The ablation study provides important insights into the contribution of each component of the DiffHash framework (the text-guided optimization, the latent space manipulation, and the HAN alignment). This helps to understand the effectiveness of each design decision.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on LLMs:** The attack's reliance on LLMs introduces a new set of potential vulnerabilities and biases. The quality of the text description directly influences the attack's success. This is a potential weakness to be aware of.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are computationally intensive, making DiffHash more resource-intensive than some simpler attacks. The paper mentions the specific GPU used, but it would benefit from more extensive discussion on the computational trade-offs. This might limit its practicality.
    *   **Limited Defense Discussion:** While the paper focuses on the attack, a brief discussion of potential defenses against DiffHash would strengthen the work.
    *   **Incremental nature:** The components are mostly drawn from existing research. The major novel aspect is the application to Deep Hashing.

* **Impact:** The paper's potential influence comes from demonstrating a strong and transferable attack on deep hashing models. This will likely spur further research into both more robust defenses and more sophisticated attack methods. The LLM text-guided approach could be adopted in other adversarial attack scenarios.

* **Justification for Score:** I assign a score of 7. The paper is a solid contribution to the field of adversarial attacks against deep hashing. The integration of diffusion models and LLM text guidance is innovative and leads to significant performance improvements. However, the reliance on computationally expensive diffusion models and the LLM, along with the relatively incremental innovation, limits its overall impact. While the results are compelling, the individual components aren't truly groundbreaking, and the overall impact could be somewhat limited by the practicality of running such large models.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Lightweight Pipeline for Noisy Speech Voice Cloning and Accurate Lip Sync Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12831v1)
- **Authors**: Javeria Amir, Farwa Attaria, Mah Jabeen, Umara Noor, Zahid Rashid
- **Abstract**: Recent developments in voice cloning and talking head generation demonstrate impressive capabilities in synthesizing natural speech and realistic lip synchronization. Current methods typically require and are trained on large scale datasets and computationally intensive processes using clean studio recorded inputs that is infeasible in noisy or low resource environments. In this paper, we introduce a new modular pipeline comprising Tortoise text to speech. It is a transformer based latent diffusion model that can perform high fidelity zero shot voice cloning given only a few training samples. We use a lightweight generative adversarial network architecture for robust real time lip synchronization. The solution will contribute to many essential tasks concerning less reliance on massive pre training generation of emotionally expressive speech and lip synchronization in noisy and unconstrained scenarios. The modular structure of the pipeline allows an easy extension for future multi modal and text guided voice modulation and it could be used in real world systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a lightweight pipeline for generating lip-synced talking-head videos from noisy speech and limited data. It leverages two pre-trained models: Tortoise TTS for zero-shot voice cloning and Wav2Lip for lip synchronization. The pipeline is designed to be modular, efficient, and robust, addressing the limitations of existing methods that often require large datasets, clean audio, and significant computational resources. The authors demonstrate the feasibility of their approach using publicly available data of Angelina Jolie, showing that it can produce reasonably personalized speech and realistic lip movements without extensive training or fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practicality and Accessibility:** The paper addresses a real-world problem by focusing on low-resource settings and noisy inputs. This makes the technology more accessible and applicable to various scenarios where clean data and high computational power are not readily available.
    *   **Modularity:** The modular design of the pipeline allows for flexibility and potential extensions, such as adding emotion control or language switching.
    *   **Use of Pre-trained Models:** The paper effectively utilizes pre-trained models, showcasing their capabilities for zero-shot transfer and generalization, which reduces the need for extensive training.
    *   **Comprehensive System-Level Implementation:** The authors address the task of generating talking-head videos as a whole, connecting voice cloning and lip synchronization instead of tackling these two parts separately.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The evaluation is primarily qualitative and based on a single speaker (Angelina Jolie), which limits the generalizability of the findings. Quantitative metrics, such as lip-sync error rates or MOS scores with multiple participants, would have strengthened the evaluation.
    *   **Lack of Novelty in Model Architecture:** The paper primarily combines existing models (Tortoise TTS and Wav2Lip) without proposing novel architectural components or training methods. The novelty lies mainly in the pipeline integration and its focus on low-resource scenarios.
    *   **Computational Requirements:** The authors acknowledge that audio synthesis with Tortoise TTS can be computationally intensive, requiring a GPU and taking several minutes per sentence. This limits the real-time applicability of the system.
    *   **Ethical Considerations:** While the authors discuss ethical concerns related to identity synthesis and misuse, they do not provide concrete solutions for mitigating these risks.
    *   **Missing Comparisons with Baselines:** The lack of comparisons against existing methods or baseline models makes it difficult to assess the relative performance of the proposed pipeline.

*   **Significance:**
    The paper contributes to the field by demonstrating a practical and accessible pipeline for generating lip-synced talking-head videos in challenging conditions. Its focus on low-resource scenarios and noisy inputs makes it relevant to real-world applications where clean data and high computational power are limited.

    However, the paper's lack of novel model architecture and limited evaluation constrain its overall significance. The contribution is more of a practical integration and demonstration of existing technologies rather than a groundbreaking advancement in the underlying algorithms.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6**. Here's why:

*   **6/10**: The paper does present a solution, but one composed of two pre-existing components. It demonstrates a working, practically relevant system, particularly within low-resource constraints, but its novelty is incremental. The lack of quantitative evaluations and comparisons limits our understanding of its performance against alternative approaches. The paper presents a combination of tools, but it does so within a rather niche application. It doesn't present a breakthrough in voice cloning or lip syncing itself. It is a meaningful step in a particular direction (practical implementation).

Score: 6

- **Score**: 6/10

### Multi-Robot Task Planning for Multi-Object Retrieval Tasks with Distributed On-Site Knowledge via Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12838v1)
- **Authors**: Kento Murata, Shoichi Hasegawa, Tomochika Ishikawa, Yoshinobu Hagiwara, Akira Taniguchi, Lotfi El Hafi, Tadahiro Taniguchi
- **Abstract**: It is crucial to efficiently execute instructions such as "Find an apple and a banana" or "Get ready for a field trip," which require searching for multiple objects or understanding context-dependent commands. This study addresses the challenging problem of determining which robot should be assigned to which part of a task when each robot possesses different situational on-site knowledge-specifically, spatial concepts learned from the area designated to it by the user. We propose a task planning framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) and spatial concepts to decompose natural language instructions into subtasks and allocate them to multiple robots. We designed a novel few-shot prompting strategy that enables LLMs to infer required objects from ambiguous commands and decompose them into appropriate subtasks. In our experiments, the proposed method achieved 47/50 successful assignments, outperforming random (28/50) and commonsense-based assignment (26/50). Furthermore, we conducted qualitative evaluations using two actual mobile manipulators. The results demonstrated that our framework could handle instructions, including those involving ad hoc categories such as "Get ready for a field trip," by successfully performing task decomposition, assignment, sequential planning, and execution.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper. Here's a summary and a critical evaluation.

**Summary:**

The paper presents a multi-robot task planning framework that combines Large Language Models (LLMs) with spatial concept models to enable cooperative object retrieval in household environments. The key idea is to decompose natural language instructions into subtasks and assign them to robots based on their individual, localized knowledge of the environment (room names and object presence probabilities). A novel few-shot prompting strategy is used to help the LLM infer objects from ambiguous commands and allocate subtasks. The framework is evaluated through simulations and real-world experiments using two mobile manipulators in a RoboCup environment. The results show that the proposed method outperforms baseline approaches in terms of successful task assignment, and the real-world experiments demonstrate the framework's feasibility in handling complex instructions and environmental conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*
The paper's novelty lies in its explicit integration of spatial concept models with LLM-based task decomposition and allocation for multi-robot coordination. While previous works have explored LLMs for robot planning and multi-robot coordination, they often lack explicit consideration of distributed, on-site knowledge. The authors' approach of using spatial concept models to encode room names and object presence probabilities and feeding this information into the LLM prompts is a valuable contribution. The few-shot prompting strategy for handling ambiguous instructions is also a worthwhile addition. However, the individual components (LLMs for planning, spatial concept models) are not entirely novel on their own. The *integration* is the key contribution.

*Significance:*

The paper addresses a significant problem in robotics: enabling effective multi-robot collaboration in realistic environments with distributed knowledge. The proposed framework has the potential to improve the efficiency and robustness of robot teams in various applications, such as household assistance, search and rescue, and logistics. The real-world experiments in a RoboCup environment provide strong evidence of the framework's practical viability. However, the limitations of the current implementation (synchronous tasks, no dynamic reallocation, homogenous team) somewhat limit its immediate impact. The real-world demonstrations, while helpful for feasibility, are qualitative in nature.

*Strengths:*

*   Clear problem definition and motivation
*   Well-defined framework with a logical pipeline
*   Novel integration of spatial concept models and LLMs
*   Comprehensive experiments in simulation and a realistic environment
*   Detailed analysis of results and failure cases
*   Well structured and well written

*Weaknesses:*

*   Limited handling of tasks requiring synchronous cooperation
*   Lack of robustness to execution-time failures and dynamic reallocation
*   Homogeneous robot team assumption
*   Qualitative real-world results only
*   Limited evaluation regarding long-distance navigation, task duration, energy consumption

*Potential Influence:*

The paper is likely to influence future research on multi-robot coordination, LLM-based robotics, and spatial reasoning. It provides a solid foundation for developing more sophisticated and robust multi-robot systems that can effectively collaborate in complex environments. The framework's modular design allows for easy extension and adaptation to different robot platforms and task domains. The insights gained from the simulation and real-world experiments can inform the design of future experiments and evaluation metrics.

*Justification for Score:*

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by effectively integrating spatial concept models with LLMs for multi-robot task planning. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the specific combination and the detailed evaluation demonstrate the potential of this approach for enabling more effective multi-robot collaboration. The paper is well-written, well-structured, and provides a comprehensive analysis of the results and limitations.

However, the limitations of the current implementation, particularly the lack of dynamic reallocation and the homogenous robot team assumption, somewhat limit its immediate impact. The qualitative real-world results also detract from the score. These limitations represent important directions for future research, but they also indicate that the framework is not yet fully mature. It also has to be kept in mind that this isn't a brand new field, and the contributions can be seen as an incremental improvement. Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7 is appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leveraging Large Language Models to Effectively Generate Visual Data for Canine Musculoskeletal Diagnoses
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12866v1)
- **Authors**: Martin Thißen, Thi Ngoc Diep Tran, Barbara Esteve Ratsch, Ben Joel Schönbein, Ute Trapp, Beate Egner, Romana Piat, Elke Hergenröther
- **Abstract**: It is well-established that more data generally improves AI model performance. However, data collection can be challenging for certain tasks due to the rarity of occurrences or high costs. These challenges are evident in our use case, where we apply AI models to a novel approach for visually documenting the musculoskeletal condition of dogs. Here, abnormalities are marked as colored strokes on a body map of a dog. Since these strokes correspond to distinct muscles or joints, they can be mapped to the textual domain in which large language models (LLMs) operate. LLMs have demonstrated impressive capabilities across a wide range of tasks, including medical applications, offering promising potential for generating synthetic training data. In this work, we investigate whether LLMs can effectively generate synthetic visual training data for canine musculoskeletal diagnoses. For this, we developed a mapping that segments visual documentations into over 200 labeled regions representing muscles or joints. Using techniques like guided decoding, chain-of-thought reasoning, and few-shot prompting, we generated 1,000 synthetic visual documentations for patellar luxation (kneecap dislocation) diagnosis, the diagnosis for which we have the most real-world data. Our analysis shows that the generated documentations are sensitive to location and severity of the diagnosis while remaining independent of the dog's sex. We further generated 1,000 visual documentations for various other diagnoses to create a binary classification dataset. A model trained solely on this synthetic data achieved an F1 score of 88% on 70 real-world documentations. These results demonstrate the potential of LLM-generated synthetic data, which is particularly valuable for addressing data scarcity in rare diseases. While our methodology is tailored to the medical domain, the insights and techniques can be adapted to other fields.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate synthetic visual training data for canine musculoskeletal diagnoses.  The authors focus on a novel method for visually documenting a dog's condition, where abnormalities are marked as colored strokes on a body map.  They developed a mapping to segment these visual documentations into labeled anatomical regions and used techniques like guided decoding, chain-of-thought reasoning, and few-shot prompting to generate synthetic data for patellar luxation diagnosis.  They demonstrate that an AI model trained solely on this synthetic data achieves a good F1 score (88%) on real-world data, suggesting the potential of LLM-generated synthetic data to address data scarcity. They also conduct an ablation study to demonstrate the benefit of including Region Information and few-shot examples in the input prompt.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of LLMs to generate *visual* training data for musculoskeletal diagnosis, *specifically using this colored stroke documentation method*, is moderately novel.  While LLMs have been used for synthetic data generation in medical contexts, the specific visual documentation and the detailed anatomical mapping contribute to its uniqueness. The work cleverly leverages the LLM's textual reasoning abilities to create visual representations.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a significant challenge – data scarcity in specialized medical domains. The demonstration that synthetic data can train a model that performs reasonably well on real-world data has potential implications for improving diagnostic tools, especially for rare conditions. The idea of distilling LLM knowledge into smaller, locally deployable models is also a valuable contribution considering real-world constraints in veterinary practices.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear explanation of the methodology, including the mapping, prompt engineering, and rendering process.
    *   Detailed analysis of the generated data, evaluating faithfulness, controllability, and diversity.
    *   Ablation study demonstrating the importance of key components in the data generation pipeline (few-shot examples and Region information).
    *   Real-world validation using a classification task and comparison to a rule-based baseline. The performance of the classification model based on the LLM generated documentation (88% F1) clearly outperforms using a rule-based system (60% F1).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited real-world validation data (only 70 samples). The results would be more convincing with a larger, more diverse real-world dataset.
    *   The lack of significant influence from the metadata attributes (age, sex, weight) raises concerns about the controllability of the data generation process. The paper acknowledges this but doesn't fully resolve it. The assumption that data from Wikipedia accurately portrays what is a normal weight may not hold true.
    *   While the authors perform some degree of analysis (average number of strokes/abnormalities, regional shifts), a more detailed qualitative assessment of the types of errors/hallucinations the LLM produces when generating documentation would be valuable.
    *   The duplication rate identified may be an underestimation. Some documents can be visually similar but not exact duplicates.

*   **Impact and Influence:** The paper provides a proof-of-concept that can inspire further research in using LLMs for synthetic data generation in visual medical domains. It establishes a useful methodology and highlights important considerations for faithfulness, controllability, and diversity.  The results could motivate the development of more sophisticated synthetic data generation techniques.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid, well-executed study with promising results. The methodology is clear, the analysis is thorough, and the results are encouraging. However, the limitations regarding the validation data and the controllability of metadata attributes prevent it from achieving a higher score. The reliance on Wikipedia data for determining normal ranges of parameters (i.e. weight) is flawed. The relatively small size of the real-world dataset, limits the generalizability of the findings. The study's novelty is moderate, and its significance, while promising, needs further validation with larger and more diverse real-world datasets.

### Tool-R1: Sample-Efficient Reinforcement Learning for Agentic Tool Use
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12867v1)
- **Authors**: Yabo Zhang, Yihan Zeng, Qingyun Li, Zhen Hu, Kavin Han, Wangmeng Zuo
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in language understanding and reasoning, yet they remain limited when tackling real-world tasks that require up-to-date knowledge, precise operations, or specialized tool use. To address this, we propose Tool-R1, a reinforcement learning framework that enables LLMs to perform general, compositional, and multi-step tool use by generating executable Python code. Tool-R1 supports integration of user-defined tools and standard libraries, with variable sharing across steps to construct coherent workflows. An outcome-based reward function, combining LLM-based answer judgment and code execution success, guides policy optimization. To improve training efficiency, we maintain a dynamic sample queue to cache and reuse high-quality trajectories, reducing the overhead of costly online sampling. Experiments on the GAIA benchmark show that Tool-R1 substantially improves both accuracy and robustness, achieving about 10\% gain over strong baselines, with larger improvements on complex multi-step tasks. These results highlight the potential of Tool-R1 for enabling reliable and efficient tool-augmented reasoning in real-world applications. Our code will be available at https://github.com/YBYBZhang/Tool-R1.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Tool-R1: Sample-Efficient Reinforcement Learning for Agentic Tool Use":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Tool-R1, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to improve the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to effectively utilize external tools in complex, real-world tasks. Tool-R1 focuses on generating executable Python code for tool interaction, which allows for greater flexibility compared to JSON-based tool calling methods.  The framework incorporates a dynamic sample queue to cache high-quality trajectories and an outcome-based reward function that combines LLM-based answer judgment with code execution success.  The authors demonstrate that Tool-R1 achieves significant performance gains on the GAIA benchmark, particularly on complex multi-step tasks, while also improving robustness and reflection capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of techniques, some of which have been explored in isolation before, but their integration within the Tool-R1 framework shows clear engineering effort:
    *   The use of executable Python code for tool use isn't entirely novel, as mentioned in the related work (ViperGPT, Creator). However, Tool-R1 demonstrates the benefits of this approach in a reinforcement learning setting.
    *   The dynamic sample queue is a common RL technique for improving sample efficiency, but the paper's specific implementation with difficulty-aware sampling adds a practical element tailored to the challenges of tool use.
    *   The outcome-based reward function, combining LLM-based judgment and code execution success, is a reasonable and practical way to guide policy optimization for tool use.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key limitation of LLMs: their inability to effectively interact with the real world and leverage external information and tools. By improving tool use capabilities, Tool-R1 has the potential to make LLMs more useful in a wider range of applications.
    *   The empirical results on the GAIA benchmark demonstrate substantial improvements over existing open-source baselines, which supports the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   The ablation study provides insights into the contribution of each component of Tool-R1, which is valuable for future research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical focus:** Tool-R1 addresses a real-world challenge and provides a practical solution.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The paper includes thorough experiments on a challenging benchmark.
    *   **Ablation study:** The study isolates the impact of different components.
    *   **Clear writing and presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scope of tools:** While the use of Python code is flexible, the actual tools available in the experiments are relatively limited (web search, multimedia parsing, image visualization). The framework's scalability to a much larger and more diverse set of tools could be a concern.
    *   **Reliance on LLM-based judgment:** Using an LLM for answer evaluation introduces a potential bias and variability. The paper acknowledges this but doesn't fully address how to mitigate these issues. Also, relying on LLM-as-judge makes the performance dependent on the capabilities and biases of the selected LLM.
    *   **Lack of comparison to state-of-the-art proprietary models:**  The comparison is limited to open-source baselines. Comparing against stronger, proprietary LLMs would provide a better understanding of Tool-R1's relative performance.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of agentic LLMs. The integration of Python-based tool use, a dynamic sample queue, and a well-designed reward function is a practical and effective approach. The empirical results are compelling, and the ablation study provides valuable insights. However, the limitations regarding the tool scope and reliance on LLM-based evaluation prevent a higher score. While Python-based tool use has been explored before, Tool-R1's implementation within an RL-based framework geared towards multi-step reasoning is a valuable addition.

### LTA-thinker: Latent Thought-Augmented Training Framework for Large Language Models on Complex Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12875v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaqi Wang, Binquan Ji, Haibo Luo, Yiyang Qi, Ruiting Li, Huiyan Wang, Yuantao Han, Cangyi Yang, jiaxu Zhang, Feiliang Ren
- **Abstract**: Complex Reasoning in Large Language Models can be dynamically optimized using Test-Time Scaling (TTS) to mitigate Overthinking. Methods such as Coconut, SoftCoT and its variant are effective in continuous latent space inference, the core bottleneck still lies in the efficient generation and utilization of high-quality Latent Thought. Drawing from the theory of SoftCoT++ that a larger variance in the generated Latent Thought distribution more closely approximates the golden truth distribution, we propose a Latent Thought-Augmented Training Framework--LTA-Thinker, which improves distributional variance and enhances reasoning performance from two perspectives. First, LTA-Thinker constructs a Latent Thought generation architecture based on a learnable prior. This architecture aims to increase the variance distribution of generated Latent Thought Vectors in order to simplify the overall structure and raise the performance ceiling. Second, LTA-Thinker introduces a distribution-based directional optimization paradigm that jointly constrains both distribution locality and distribution scale. This mechanism improves information efficiency and computational cost through a multi-objective co-training strategy, which combines standard Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) loss with two novel losses: Semantic Alignment Loss, which utilizes KL divergence to ensure that the Latent Thought is highly relevant to the semantics of the question; Reasoning Focus Loss, which utilizes a contrastive learning mechanism to guide the model to focus on the most critical reasoning steps. Experiments show that LTA-thinker achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance among various baselines and demonstrates a higher performance ceiling and better scaling effects.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LTA-Thinker, a Latent Thought-Augmented Training Framework designed to improve complex reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). Addressing the "overthinking" problem, LTA-Thinker leverages Test-Time Scaling (TTS) techniques. The core idea is to enhance the generation and utilization of high-quality Latent Thoughts within LLMs.  The framework consists of two main components: (1) a learnable prior-based Latent Thought generation architecture that aims to increase the variance of generated Latent Thought Vectors, and (2) a distribution-based directional optimization paradigm.  This second component introduces a multi-objective co-training strategy that combines standard Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) loss with two novel losses: Semantic Alignment Loss (ensuring relevance to the question) and Reasoning Focus Loss (guiding the model to focus on critical reasoning steps).  Experiments demonstrate that LTA-Thinker achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance compared to various baselines and exhibits a higher performance ceiling and better scaling effects.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a moderately novel approach.  The idea of using latent thoughts to guide reasoning is not entirely new (Coconut, SoftCoT, etc., have explored this), but the specific architecture and training paradigm introduced in LTA-Thinker have merit. The learnable prior-based latent thought generation architecture is a definite improvement over simply using a pre-trained smaller LLM, offering greater flexibility. The distribution-based directional optimization paradigm is also innovative, specifically the combined use of Semantic Alignment Loss and Reasoning Focus Loss to guide the distribution of Latent Thoughts.

*   **Significance:**  The paper has the potential to be significant within the field of LLM reasoning. By addressing the "overthinking" problem and improving the efficiency and quality of latent thought generation, LTA-Thinker can contribute to more accurate and efficient LLMs. The experimental results, showcasing SOTA performance on several datasets, provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed framework.  The insights gained into the importance of variance in latent thought distributions, and how to effectively control and utilize it, are valuable. The better scaling effects are also a key advantage, implying the framework could remain effective as model sizes increase.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of "overthinking" in LLMs and the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   **Well-Defined Architecture and Training Paradigm:** The proposed LTA-Thinker framework is well-structured and its components are clearly explained. The two main components are well-thought-out and logically connected.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides theoretical justification for its approach, building upon existing work such as SoftCoT++.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experimental evaluation is thorough, with comparisons to strong baselines on multiple datasets covering different reasoning categories (mathematical, commonsense, symbolic). The ablation studies offer valuable insights into the contributions of individual components.
    *   **Strong Results:** The results showing SOTA performance provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:**  While the results are promising, the evaluation might benefit from testing on an even broader range of datasets and LLM architectures. Also it would be important to confirm that the observed benefits can transfer to LLMs significantly larger that the Qwen series, e.g. those that are publicly accessible.
    *   **Implementation Details:** While the architecture is described, some implementation details (e.g. the specific hyperparameters for the losses) could be further elaborated. Reproducibility relies on such specifics.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper claims improved information efficiency and computational cost, but a direct comparison of training and inference time/cost with other methods (e.g., SoftCoT++) would strengthen this claim. While the "scaling effects" are a positive signal, a more detailed cost analysis is necessary.
    *   **Theoretical Depth:** While building on previous theoretical results, the paper lacks strong formal theoretical justifications, such as precise convergence rates and sample complexity.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    Given the above analysis, I assign a score of **7**. The paper presents a novel and promising framework for improving complex reasoning in LLMs. It builds upon existing work but introduces significant improvements in architecture and training paradigm. The experimental results are strong, but the paper could benefit from a more detailed cost analysis and further theoretical justification. The scope remains limited as the experiments have not yet been extended to state-of-the-art LLMs.

### Few to Big: Prototype Expansion Network via Diffusion Learner for Point Cloud Few-shot Semantic Segmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12878v1)
- **Authors**: Qianguang Zhao, Dongli Wang, Yan Zhou, Jianxun Li, Richard Irampa
- **Abstract**: Few-shot 3D point cloud semantic segmentation aims to segment novel categories using a minimal number of annotated support samples. While existing prototype-based methods have shown promise, they are constrained by two critical challenges: (1) Intra-class Diversity, where a prototype's limited representational capacity fails to cover a class's full variations, and (2) Inter-set Inconsistency, where prototypes derived from the support set are misaligned with the query feature space. Motivated by the powerful generative capability of diffusion model, we re-purpose its pre-trained conditional encoder to provide a novel source of generalizable features for expanding the prototype's representational range. Under this setup, we introduce the Prototype Expansion Network (PENet), a framework that constructs big-capacity prototypes from two complementary feature sources. PENet employs a dual-stream learner architecture: it retains a conventional fully supervised Intrinsic Learner (IL) to distill representative features, while introducing a novel Diffusion Learner (DL) to provide rich generalizable features. The resulting dual prototypes are then processed by a Prototype Assimilation Module (PAM), which adopts a novel push-pull cross-guidance attention block to iteratively align the prototypes with the query space. Furthermore, a Prototype Calibration Mechanism (PCM) regularizes the final big capacity prototype to prevent semantic drift. Extensive experiments on the S3DIS and ScanNet datasets demonstrate that PENet significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods across various few-shot settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Prototype Expansion Network (PENet), a novel framework for few-shot 3D point cloud semantic segmentation (FS-PCS). PENet addresses the limitations of existing prototype-based methods by constructing high-capacity prototypes that can better represent class diversity and are well-aligned with the query feature space. The framework utilizes a dual-stream learner architecture, with an Intrinsic Learner (IL) extracting representative features and a Diffusion Learner (DL) providing rich generalizable features. A Prototype Assimilation Module (PAM) with a push-pull attention mechanism iteratively aligns the prototypes with the query space, while a Prototype Calibration Mechanism (PCM) prevents semantic drift.  Extensive experiments on S3DIS and ScanNet datasets demonstrate that PENet significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in various few-shot settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in repurposing a diffusion model's conditional encoder as a feature learner for prototype expansion in the context of few-shot 3D point cloud segmentation. The dual-stream architecture, PAM with push-pull attention, and PCM, while borrowing concepts from existing works, are intelligently combined and adapted to this specific problem. The idea of explicitly addressing intra-class diversity *and* inter-set inconsistency with dedicated modules is a clear strength. Integrating a diffusion model pre-trained component is a novel contribution, and it provides a new direction for research for FS-PCS. The overall design seems reasonable with two source prototypes.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates significant performance gains over existing methods on standard benchmark datasets.  The consistent improvements across different few-shot settings and the ability to handle more challenging N-way segmentation tasks highlight the robustness and generalizability of the proposed approach. Ablation studies clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of each key module. The method addresses a core limitation of current few-shot learning approaches. However, the reliance on pre-trained diffusion models might limit wider adoption due to computational resource requirements and the need for retraining when applied to new datasets. The improvement is meaningful considering how many methods are trying to improve the problem.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the challenges of intra-class diversity and inter-set inconsistency in FS-PCS.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The integration of a diffusion model's conditional encoder for prototype expansion is a novel idea.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents convincing experimental results that demonstrate the superiority of PENet over existing methods.
    *   **Thorough Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the effectiveness of each key module.
    *   **Well-written and easy to follow:**

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Pre-trained Models:** The reliance on pre-trained diffusion models may limit its applicability and raise concerns about domain transfer.
    *   **Computational Complexity:** The use of diffusion models and Transformer-based encoders might introduce significant computational overhead. A thorough complexity analysis is missing in the paper.
    *   **Limited Explanation:** While the paper presents convincing results, a more in-depth analysis of the underlying mechanisms that contribute to the observed performance gains would be beneficial. How does the DL learn features for expansion, and what are the differences to IL?
    *  **Incremental Contributions:** The proposed framework combines several existing techniques, and the novelty primarily stems from the integration rather than radical new components.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in FS-PCS by demonstrating the effectiveness of leveraging pre-trained generative models for feature learning. It also highlights the importance of explicitly addressing intra-class diversity and inter-set inconsistency. However, the dependency on diffusion models may limit its immediate adoption.

*   **Missing:**

    *   Complexity Analysis
    *   Analysis on DL and IL features.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper introduces a novel and effective approach to FS-PCS by repurposing a pre-trained diffusion model and combining it with a traditional learner, resulting in substantial performance improvements. While the approach depends on existing techniques and introduces a computational burden, it tackles a well-defined problem and demonstrates significant improvements over state-of-the-art methods. Considering the importance of the problem and the empirical results in improving FS-PCS and incorporating pre-trained diffusion model, a fair score for this submission is 7.

### The LLM Already Knows: Estimating LLM-Perceived Question Difficulty via Hidden Representations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12886v1)
- **Authors**: Yubo Zhu, Dongrui Liu, Zecheng Lin, Wei Tong, Sheng Zhong, Jing Shao
- **Abstract**: Estimating the difficulty of input questions as perceived by large language models (LLMs) is essential for accurate performance evaluation and adaptive inference. Existing methods typically rely on repeated response sampling, auxiliary models, or fine-tuning the target model itself, which may incur substantial computational costs or compromise generality. In this paper, we propose a novel approach for difficulty estimation that leverages only the hidden representations produced by the target LLM. We model the token-level generation process as a Markov chain and define a value function to estimate the expected output quality given any hidden state. This allows for efficient and accurate difficulty estimation based solely on the initial hidden state, without generating any output tokens. Extensive experiments across both textual and multimodal tasks demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms existing baselines in difficulty estimation. Moreover, we apply our difficulty estimates to guide adaptive reasoning strategies, including Self-Consistency, Best-of-N, and Self-Refine, achieving higher inference efficiency with fewer generated tokens.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "The LLM Already Knows: Estimating LLM-Perceived Question Difficulty via Hidden Representations":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel method for estimating the difficulty of questions as perceived by Large Language Models (LLMs) by directly analyzing their hidden representations, rather than relying on output sampling or auxiliary models. The core idea involves modeling the token generation process as a Markov chain and defining a value function that estimates the expected output quality based on the hidden state at each step.  The difficulty of a question is then estimated by evaluating the value function at the initial hidden state, derived directly from the input question. The authors demonstrate that this approach consistently outperforms existing difficulty estimation methods across various textual and multimodal tasks. They also show that these difficulty estimates can be used to guide adaptive reasoning strategies, such as Self-Consistency, Best-of-N, and Self-Refine, leading to improved inference efficiency with fewer generated tokens.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its innovative approach.  Shifting the focus from generated outputs to the internal hidden states of LLMs for difficulty estimation is a significant departure from existing techniques. Modeling the generation process as a Markov chain to quantify the link between input and expected output quality is clever. While there's increasing interest in probing hidden representations, this application to difficulty estimation is novel.

*   **Significance:** Accurate difficulty estimation is becoming increasingly important for a number of reasons:
    *   **Better evaluation:** Allows researchers to assess models more precisely, understanding performance across different difficulty levels.
    *   **Adaptive training:** Enhances robustness and performance on challenging examples.
    *   **Efficient Inference:** Enables dynamic adjustment of inference strategies, saving computational resources.
    Therefore, the significance is clearly present. By providing a more efficient and less disruptive method (no need for fine-tuning or auxiliary models) to achieve this, it has the potential to become widely adopted. The improvement in adaptive reasoning strategies confirms this.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Efficiency:** The method is computationally efficient because it doesn't require generating multiple outputs or using auxiliary models.
    *   **Generality:** The approach works across various tasks (textual and multimodal) and different LLMs.
    *   **Preservation of LLM Capabilities:**  The method avoids fine-tuning or modifying the target LLM, preserving its general capabilities and safety.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** Solid experimental validation on diverse datasets and with multiple LLMs.
    *   **Adaptive Reasoning:** The successful integration with adaptive reasoning strategies is a practical demonstration of the method's utility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Access to Hidden Representations:** A limitation is the reliance on access to the LLM's hidden representations, which may not be available for closed-source models or in all API configurations. This reduces the applicability of the method in real-world scenarios where some models are only available as black boxes.
    *   **Complexity:** While efficient, the Markov Chain modeling and the value function concept is not straightforward. There is a degree of mathematical complexity involved that might hinder quick adoption by practitioners.
    *   **Single-turn Limitation:** As the authors acknowledge, the current implementation focuses on single-turn inputs, restricting the use in more complex dialogue or multi-turn interactive settings.

*   **Impact:**  If widely adopted, this method could significantly impact the field by providing a more accessible and efficient means of difficulty estimation. It could foster research in adaptive reasoning and LLM evaluation. The integration with existing techniques like Self-Consistency further increases its potential impact.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite its limitations regarding access to hidden representations, the paper presents a solid and innovative solution to an important problem. The method is well-motivated, experimentally validated, and presents a clear advance over existing approaches. The core strength is a smart integration of internal characteristics. The experiments, particularly on different datasets, adaptive reasoning, and model scaling, are convincing. This innovation in estimating difficulty makes adaptive strategies more readily applicable. A lot of potential impact is present. The downsides are accessibility in the real-world because of API restrictions and the single-turn limitation.

### Runge-Kutta Approximation and Decoupled Attention for Rectified Flow Inversion and Semantic Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12888v1)
- **Authors**: Weiming Chen, Zhihan Zhu, Yijia Wang, Zhihai He
- **Abstract**: Rectified flow (RF) models have recently demonstrated superior generative performance compared to DDIM-based diffusion models. However, in real-world applications, they suffer from two major challenges: (1) low inversion accuracy that hinders the consistency with the source image, and (2) entangled multimodal attention in diffusion transformers, which hinders precise attention control. To address the first challenge, we propose an efficient high-order inversion method for rectified flow models based on the Runge-Kutta solver of differential equations. To tackle the second challenge, we introduce Decoupled Diffusion Transformer Attention (DDTA), a novel mechanism that disentangles text and image attention inside the multimodal diffusion transformers, enabling more precise semantic control. Extensive experiments on image reconstruction and text-guided editing tasks demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance in terms of fidelity and editability. Code is available at https://github.com/wmchen/RKSovler_DDTA.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses two key challenges in using Rectified Flow (RF) models for image editing: (1) low inversion accuracy that limits consistency with the source image, and (2) entangled text and image attention in diffusion transformers, hindering precise semantic control. To tackle these, the authors propose two main contributions. First, they introduce a high-order inversion method for RF models based on the Runge-Kutta (RK) solver for differential equations. This aims to improve inversion fidelity. Second, they present Decoupled Diffusion Transformer Attention (DDTA), a mechanism to disentangle text and image attention within multimodal diffusion transformers (MM-DiTs). This is intended to enable more precise semantic control during editing. Experimental results on image reconstruction and text-guided editing demonstrate improved performance in fidelity and editability compared to existing RF-based methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of the RK solver for RF inversion and the DDTA mechanism for disentangling attention in MM-DiTs. While the use of Runge-Kutta methods in numerical ODE solving is well-established, its application to RF inversion, especially coupled with the DDTA, appears novel. DDTA's approach to decoupling text and image attention within the specific context of MM-DiT architectures is also a significant contribution. It specifically addresses the challenges posed by the unified transformer framework used in RF models, offering a way to regain fine-grained control over semantic editing that is not easily transferable from UNet-based diffusion models.

*   **Significance:** Improving inversion accuracy and semantic control in RF models is significant because it expands the applicability of these models in real-world image manipulation tasks. The demonstrated improvements in fidelity and editability contribute to generating more realistic and controllable edited images. The approach is training-free, adding to its potential for adoption.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly identified problems and proposed targeted solutions.
    *   Well-motivated and technically sound approach. The RK solver is a natural fit for the smooth ODE trajectory of RF models.
    *   The DDTA mechanism directly addresses the attention entanglement issue in MM-DiTs.
    *   Comprehensive experiments and evaluations on image reconstruction and text-guided editing.
    *   The qualitative results showcase the practical benefits of the proposed approach. The ablation studies thoroughly investigate the impact of the various components.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper acknowledges the increased computational overhead. Although the paper presents the runtime and GPU memory usage, there is no discussion on optimizations that can be made to improve the efficiency of the proposed solver. More effort should be made to show the efficiency gains of the new method.
    *   The results should be further analyzed as it is not evident how much better the proposed methods are than the baselines.
    *   In some experiments, it can be seen that the results of the ablation studies are not consistent.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in RF-based image editing. The RK-solver significantly increases inversion fidelity compared to existing methods. DDTA provides a more flexible way to control editing in MM-DiT architectures. If the computational overhead can be mitigated, the proposed method could become a standard approach in the field.

*   **Score Justification:**
    Given the novelty in combining RK solvers with RF inversion and the DDTA mechanism for MM-DiTs, along with the demonstrable improvements in fidelity and editability, the paper represents a solid contribution. However, the added computation overhead and less than dramatic (although consistent) performance gains over baselines reduces the impact. The paper presents a strong engineering solution to a defined problem, well-executed.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Conan-Embedding-v2: Training an LLM from Scratch for Text Embeddings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12892v1)
- **Authors**: Shiyu Li, Yang Tang, Ruijie Liu, Shi-Zhe Chen, Xi Chen
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have recently demonstrated excellent performance in text embedding tasks. Previous work usually use LoRA to fine-tune existing LLMs, which are limited by the data and training gap between LLMs and embedding models. In this work, we introduce Conan-embedding-v2, a new 1.4B-parameter LLM trained from scratch and fine-tuned as a text embedder. First, we add news data and multilingual pairs for LLM pretraining to bridge the data gap. Based on this, we propose a cross-lingual retrieval dataset that enables the LLM to better integrate embeddings across different languages. Second, whereas LLMs use a causal mask with token-level loss, embedding models use a bidirectional mask with sentence-level loss. This training gap makes full fine-tuning less effective than LoRA. We introduce a soft-masking mechanism to gradually transition between these two types of masks, enabling the model to learn more comprehensive representations. Based on this, we propose a dynamic hard negative mining method that exposes the model to more difficult negative examples throughout the training process. Being intuitive and effective, with only approximately 1.4B parameters, Conan-embedding-v2 achieves SOTA performance on both the Massive Text Embedding Benchmark (MTEB) and Chinese MTEB (May 19, 2025).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Conan-Embedding-v2," a 1.4B parameter Large Language Model (LLM) trained from scratch and fine-tuned for text embeddings.
