Addressing limitations of prior work that fine-tune existing LLMs, the authors propose several key innovations: (1) pre-training on a combination of news data and multilingual pairs to bridge the data gap between LLMs and embedding data; (2) a soft-masking mechanism to gradually transition from causal masking (used in LLMs) to bidirectional masking (used in embedding models); and (3) a dynamic hard negative mining method to improve the quality of negative samples during training. The results on MTEB and Chinese MTEB benchmarks show state-of-the-art performance. They also introduce a novel cross-lingual retrieval dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of techniques that, while individually not entirely novel, are combined in a novel and effective way.
    *   *Data Preprocessing:* Combining diverse news data and multilingual pairs for pre-training is a good engineering practice, but not a groundbreaking concept on its own.
    *   *Soft Masking:* The idea of gradually transitioning between causal and bidirectional masking is more innovative. It addresses a critical training discrepancy and seems empirically effective.
    *   *Dynamic Hard Negative Mining:* Dynamic HNM has been explored before, but the proposed implementation and integration with the soft-masking strategy seem refined and beneficial.
    *   *Cross-Lingual Retrieval Dataset:* The introduction of the cross-lingual retrieval dataset is a valuable contribution, addressing a relevant limitation in current models and datasets.

*   **Significance:** The results clearly demonstrate a significant improvement over existing methods. The performance on MTEB benchmarks is state-of-the-art. The focus on a relatively small model size (1.4B parameters) is significant. It enables practical applications that larger models would struggle with. The introduction of the novel cross-lingual retrieval dataset helps in the research direction.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the motivations and proposed methods.
    *   The empirical results are strong, with comprehensive evaluations across multiple benchmarks.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of individual components.
    *   The analysis of the data distribution and potential biases is commendable.
    *   The smaller model size and reported inference time are practically valuable.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the proposed methods are effective, they may not be entirely conceptually revolutionary. They are more akin to clever engineering improvements.
    *   The cross-lingual retrieval evaluation is limited to the MKQA dataset. Additional experiments on other cross-lingual benchmarks would strengthen the results.
    *   More detailed discussion on the computational costs of different components would be beneficial for practitioners.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the direction of text embedding research. The emphasis on data-driven LLM from scratch along with the soft-masking technique could lead to the development of more efficient and effective models.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The paper doesn't present a paradigm shift but instead delivers a set of well-engineered improvements that yield significant performance gains. The soft-masking technique and the cross-lingual retrieval dataset are notable contributions. The strong empirical results and the focus on a smaller model size are practically significant. Taking all of this into account, this is a good contribution but may not have a substantial theoretical breakthrough.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### All Roads Lead to Rome: Graph-Based Confidence Estimation for Large Language Model Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12908v1)
- **Authors**: Caiqi Zhang, Chang Shu, Ehsan Shareghi, Nigel Collier
- **Abstract**: Confidence estimation is essential for the reliable deployment of large language models (LLMs). Existing methods are primarily designed for factual QA tasks and often fail to generalize to reasoning tasks. To address this gap, we propose a set of training-free, graph-based confidence estimation methods tailored to reasoning tasks. Our approach models reasoning paths as directed graphs and estimates confidence by exploiting graph properties such as centrality, path convergence, and path weighting. Experiments with two LLMs on three reasoning datasets demonstrate improved confidence estimation and enhanced performance on two downstream tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel, training-free, graph-based approach to confidence estimation for large language models (LLMs) when applied to reasoning tasks.  It addresses the limitations of existing confidence estimation methods that are primarily designed for factual question answering (QA) and don't generalize well to reasoning, where the model outputs involve intermediate steps. The proposed method models the reasoning process as a directed graph, where nodes represent reasoning steps or answers, and edges represent logical connections.  Confidence is then estimated based on graph properties like centrality, path convergence, and path weighting.  The approach is model-agnostic and is evaluated on Llama3.1-8B and Gemma2-9B using reasoning-intensive benchmarks like MATH500, MMLU-Pro, and FOLIO. The paper demonstrates improved confidence estimation compared to baselines and explores downstream applications, such as selective self-reflection and LLM cascading, to improve performance and efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The use of graph-based modeling for confidence estimation in *reasoning* tasks is the key novelty.  While graph-based approaches exist for QA, applying graph theory to represent the logical structure of reasoning paths, especially the concepts of path convergence and node centrality within that graph, is a fresh perspective.  The proposed techniques are also training-free, making them readily applicable without the need for task-specific training data.  However, the core building blocks (graph centrality, path counting) are well-established, so the novelty resides primarily in their *application* to this specific problem.

* **Significance:** Accurate confidence estimation is crucial for reliable deployment of LLMs, especially in high-stakes domains.  Reasoning is a critical aspect of LLM capabilities, and the paper tackles the challenge of uncertainty in this area. The proposed method improves confidence scores, allowing for more informed decisions on when to trigger alternative strategies such as self-reflection or escalating the task to a larger model. The downstream applications demonstrate the practical value of better confidence estimation. The performance improvements in the downstream task application, selective self-reflection and LLM cascading, validate the significance of this work.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing confidence estimation methods for reasoning tasks.
    * **Novel Approach:** The graph-based modeling approach is a significant and potentially fruitful direction.
    * **Training-Free:** The training-free nature is a major advantage.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The results consistently show improvements over baselines on multiple datasets and models. The improvements over a strong self-consistency baseline are particularly compelling.
    * **Downstream Task Validation:** Demonstrating the utility of the confidence scores in downstream applications strengthens the paper's significance.
    * **Model-Agnostic:** The method can be applied to different LLMs.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Overhead:** Graph construction and computation can be expensive, as acknowledged in the paper. This may limit its applicability in real-time, resource-constrained environments. The discussion of strategies to mitigate this is somewhat brief.
    * **Sensitivity to Graph Construction:** The performance relies heavily on accurate step decomposition and equivalence detection. Errors in these processes can negatively impact the topology of the graph and the resulting confidence scores. The paper briefly mentions strategies to improve robustness but could explore this in greater detail. This could be quantified in future work.
    * **Limited Ablation:**  The ablation study, although present, is somewhat limited. More detailed analysis of the individual components' contributions (centrality, path convergence, and weighting) would be beneficial. For example, a study demonstrating that the different components contribute meaningfully to the overall system would increase the strength of the contribution.
    * **Equivalence Checking Reliance:** The method relies on an auxiliary model for equivalence checking, meaning it is not *completely* training-free from a system perspective.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in confidence estimation for reasoning in LLMs.  It opens up avenues for exploring graph-based techniques and for developing more robust and efficient confidence estimation methods. The work is also likely to spur research into better methods for step decomposition and equivalence detection.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and promising approach to a significant problem. The empirical results are compelling, and the downstream task validation demonstrates practical value.  However, the computational overhead and sensitivity to graph construction, along with a reliance on an auxiliary model, are limitations. The limitations of the graph methods due to step segmentation and cross-path alignment (requiring yet another LLM) need to be acknowledged.  The relative lack of ablation, in terms of looking at individual components, also detracts from the score. The novelty is also primarily in the application of existing graph theory concepts to the confidence estimation problem, which does not elevate it to the highest level. Overall, the paper makes a solid contribution.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Stochastic Streets: A Walk Through Random LLM Address Generation in four European Cities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12914v1)
- **Authors**: Tairan Fu, David Campo-Nazareno, Javier Coronado-Blázquez, Javier Conde, Pedro Reviriego, Fabrizio Lombardi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are capable of solving complex math problems or answer difficult questions on almost any topic, but can they generate random street addresses for European cities?
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate random street addresses in four major European cities (Amsterdam, Madrid, Paris, and Rome).  It finds that, despite being instructed to generate addresses randomly, LLMs exhibit strong biases. They repeatedly select a limited set of streets, and in some cases, specific street numbers.  The selected streets don't always correspond to the most prominent or iconic streets in the cities.  The paper attributes this to LLMs' tendency to favor specific tokens (like "Calle" or "Rue") and potentially biases present in their training data.  Furthermore, some models generate invalid addresses.  The paper concludes that LLMs struggle with this seemingly simple task, revealing limitations in their representation and retrieval of geographic or structured information and hinting toward underlying biases during the address generation process. The authors highlight the importance of understanding these biases as reliance on LLMs for data generation and task completion increases.

**Novelty and Significance Evaluation:**

The paper presents a valuable exploration of LLM capabilities in a specific, yet practically relevant, task: random address generation. While LLMs have been extensively studied for their abilities in tasks such as text generation, question answering, and even code creation, their proficiency in seemingly simple tasks like address generation hasn't been as comprehensively investigated. This exploration highlights the non-trivial challenges that LLMs face when dealing with structured information and its ties to the physical world.

Here’s a breakdown of the novelty and significance:

*   **Novelty:**  The specific application – random address generation in major European cities – is relatively novel. While previous work has examined LLM randomness in number generation, this paper adds the additional layer of spatial information and address structure. Applying this framework to evaluate performance in this way is unique to the study. The exploration of bias toward specific street designators (e.g., "Calle") is another interesting contribution.
*   **Significance:**  The paper's findings have practical implications. Consider applications where LLMs are used to generate synthetic data for urban planning, logistics simulations, or emergency response scenarios. If the addresses generated are not truly random and are biased towards specific areas, it could lead to skewed or inaccurate simulations. It also brings to light potentially harmful biases that could be inherent to many modern LLM models. The paper's investigation into address structure constraints (specific languages favoring particular address components occurring first in the sequence) is also a valuable observation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The research question is well-defined and the motivation is clear.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough empirical evaluation across multiple LLMs and cities.
*   **Reproducibility:** The authors make their data and code publicly available, enhancing the reproducibility and transparency of their work.
*   **Analysis:** The paper provides solid analysis of the results, attempting to explain the observed biases (token bias, training data bias).
*   **Practical Implications:**  The discussion of potential implications for various applications (simulations, tourism) is well-articulated.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** While the focus on four European cities allows for detailed analysis, generalizing these results to other geographic regions or languages may be limited.
*   **Prompt Dependence:** The paper acknowledges the influence of the prompt. However, a more extensive exploration of prompt engineering, with different phrasing styles, might have provided further insights into LLM behavior.
*   **Limited Countermeasures:** While the paper identifies biases, it does not propose or evaluate any specific techniques to mitigate them or fine-tune address generation parameters. It only mentions finetuning in passing.

**Score Justification:**

The paper makes a worthwhile contribution by exposing the limitations of LLMs in a specific real-world task that, on the surface, seems trivial. By uncovering these biases, the authors provide valuable information to developers and users who rely on LLMs for data generation and other applications.  The detailed analysis and exploration of potential causes make the paper more than just a descriptive study; the potential practical implications are evident.
Considering the existing body of work on LLMs, this is certainly not a foundational discovery but rather a specific and practically relevant addition.

For the above reasons, I assign a score of:

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### The Anatomy of Alignment: Decomposing Preference Optimization by Steering Sparse Features
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12934v1)
- **Authors**: Jeremias Ferrao, Matthijs van der Lende, Ilija Lichkovski, Clement Neo
- **Abstract**: Aligning large language models is critical for their usability and safety. However, the prevailing approach of Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) induces diffuse, opaque parameter changes, making it difficult to discern what the model has internalized. Hence, we introduce Feature Steering with Reinforcement Learning (FSRL), a transparent alignment framework that trains a lightweight adapter to steer behavior by modulating interpretable features from a Sparse Autoencoder (SAE). First, we demonstrate that FSRL is an effective method for preference optimization and is comparable with current RLHF methods. We then perform mechanistic analysis on the trained adapter, and find that its policy systematically promotes style features over explicit alignment concepts, suggesting that the preference optimization process rewards stylistic presentation as a proxy for quality. Ultimately, we hope that FSRL provides a tool for both interpretable model control and diagnosing the internal mechanisms of alignment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Feature Steering with Reinforcement Learning (FSRL), a novel framework for aligning large language models (LLMs) by steering interpretable features from a Sparse Autoencoder (SAE).  Unlike traditional Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) that induces opaque parameter changes, FSRL trains a lightweight adapter to modulate SAE features, providing transparency and enabling mechanistic analysis. The authors demonstrate that FSRL effectively optimizes a preference objective, achieving performance comparable to standard RLHF methods. Through mechanistic analysis, they find the trained adapter prioritizes style and formatting features over explicit alignment concepts, suggesting that preference optimization rewards stylistic presentation as a proxy for quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in combining feature steering and reinforcement learning for LLM alignment.  Using an SAE to create an interpretable feature space upon which to learn a steering policy is a significant contribution. It moves beyond simply observing model behavior to actively controlling it through understandable components. The paper builds on existing work in feature steering but applies it to alignment in a unique and transparent manner.
*   **Significance:** FSRL addresses a crucial problem in LLM alignment: opacity.  By enabling mechanistic analysis, it allows researchers to understand *how* alignment is achieved, rather than just *whether* it is achieved. The finding that style is prioritized over substance in the preference optimization process offers valuable insights into the limitations of current alignment techniques and the potential pitfalls of using stylistic proxies for quality. This has significant implications for the design of future alignment strategies. The FSRL framework provides a tool to evaluate the effects of alignment at the feature-level that could potentially catch emergent failure modes early on.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Transparency:** FSRL provides a framework for transparent LLM alignment and control.
    *   **Mechanistic Insight:** The paper offers valuable insights into the biases learned during preference optimization.
    *   **Effective Alignment:** The method achieves comparable performance to RLHF, demonstrating its practical utility.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly details the system architecture, training procedure, and experimental setup.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on SAE Quality:** The framework's effectiveness relies heavily on the quality and interpretability of the SAE. The authors acknowledge that generating high-quality SAEs and reliable feature explanations is computationally expensive. The paper also acknowledges that there are potential limitations in relying on automated feature explanations.
    *   **Single-Layer Intervention:** The current implementation only intervenes at a single layer, which may limit its ability to fully capture the complexities of alignment.
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The evaluation is primarily focused on a single dataset (UltraFeedback) and a specific SimPO algorithm. The transferability of the findings to other datasets, models, and alignment techniques needs further investigation.
    *   **Lack of Causality:** While the paper identifies correlations between steering vector and alignment-related concepts, it does not demonstrate causality. It remains unclear whether manipulating these features directly leads to improved alignment or if they are merely correlated with other underlying factors.
    *   **Sparsity:** The paper notes the importance of inducing sparsity in the steering vector, but the performance gains with sparsity have not been explored fully.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by promoting more transparent and interpretable alignment methods. The FSRL framework could serve as a valuable tool for diagnosing biases and vulnerabilities in aligned LLMs. The findings about the prioritization of style also raise important questions about the design of preference datasets and the potential for reward hacking.

*   **Overall:** The paper offers a promising approach to LLM alignment that prioritizes transparency and interpretability. The mechanistic insights are valuable, and the framework has the potential to advance our understanding of how alignment is achieved. However, it is important to recognize the limitations of the method and the need for further research to address the dependencies on SAE quality, evaluation against multiple data sets, and demonstrating causality.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Rethinking the Evaluation of Alignment Methods: Insights into Diversity, Generalisation, and Safety
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12936v1)
- **Authors**: Denis Janiak, Julia Moska, Dawid Motyka, Karolina Seweryn, Paweł Walkowiak, Bartosz Żuk, Arkadiusz Janz
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) require careful alignment to balance competing objectives - factuality, safety, conciseness, proactivity, and diversity. Existing studies focus on individual techniques or specific dimensions, lacking a holistic assessment of the inherent trade-offs. We propose a unified evaluation framework that compares LLM alignment methods (PPO, DPO, ORPO, KTO) across these five axes, using both in-distribution and out-of-distribution datasets. Leveraging a specialized LLM-as-Judge prompt, validated through human studies, we reveal that DPO and KTO excel in factual accuracy, PPO and DPO lead in safety, and PPO best balances conciseness with proactivity. Our findings provide insights into trade-offs of common alignment methods, guiding the development of more balanced and reliable LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of evaluating alignment methods for large language models (LLMs). It proposes a unified evaluation framework to compare alignment techniques (PPO, DPO, ORPO, and KTO) across five key dimensions: factuality, safety, conciseness, proactivity, and diversity. The evaluation uses both in-distribution (ID) and out-of-distribution (OOD) datasets. A central aspect of the work is the use of a specialized LLM-as-a-Judge prompt, validated via human studies, to automate the multi-dimensional assessment. The study reveals trade-offs between alignment methods, highlighting that DPO and KTO excel in factuality, PPO and DPO are superior in safety, and PPO strikes a better balance between conciseness and proactivity.  The authors analyze how safety-focused alignment affects other model capabilities and the relationship between safety optimization, generalisation, and response diversity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the comprehensive evaluation framework that integrates multiple dimensions of LLM alignment. While previous work has examined individual alignment methods or specific dimensions, this study attempts a more holistic and systematic comparison, especially by incorporating OOD datasets and analyzing generalization ability. The LLM-as-a-Judge approach, though not entirely novel in itself, is implemented with a specific prompt design and validated through human evaluation, adding credibility. A further point of novelty lies in explicitly quantifying the trade-offs between the various alignment objectives (e.g., safety vs. diversity).
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its potential to inform the development and selection of alignment methods for LLMs.  By providing a structured evaluation framework and revealing the trade-offs inherent in different techniques, the study can guide practitioners towards choices that best suit their specific application needs. The results demonstrate the strengths and weaknesses of common alignment methods, offering valuable insights for researchers aiming to create more balanced and reliable LLMs.

    *   **Strengths:**
        *   Comprehensive evaluation framework covering multiple alignment objectives and methods.
        *   Use of both ID and OOD datasets to assess generalization.
        *   LLM-as-a-Judge approach with human validation, enhancing reliability and enabling more granular analysis.
        *   Explicit quantification of trade-offs between alignment objectives.
    *   **Weaknesses:**
        *   While the LLM-as-a-Judge approach is validated, it's not inherently immune to biases or errors. Further investigation of potential biases in this evaluation method would strengthen the findings.
        *   The selection of specific alignment methods and datasets may limit the generalizability of the conclusions. Including other techniques and datasets could offer a more comprehensive picture.
        *   The reliance on the performance of the base SFT model (Dubois et al., 2023) could influence the results and comparability.

*   **Impact:** The paper's impact depends on its ability to influence future research and development in LLM alignment. The evaluation framework could be adopted by other researchers as a standard benchmark. The identified trade-offs could spur new research directions aimed at developing alignment techniques that better balance competing objectives. Given the growing importance of safe and reliable LLMs, the insights from this paper have the potential to make a tangible contribution to the field.
*   **Clarity and Reproducibility**: The method is explained in sufficient detail, and hyperparameter settings are documented. The inclusion of source code would be beneficial for replicability.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM alignment by providing a comprehensive evaluation framework and quantifying trade-offs between alignment objectives. The LLM-as-a-Judge approach is well-validated and allows for a granular analysis.  However, the novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary. While the multi-dimensional evaluation is a step forward, the individual components (LLM judging, specific alignment methods) are not entirely new. The reliance on specific datasets and the potential for biases in the LLM-as-a-Judge protocol also limit the impact and generalizability to some degree. Given the comprehensiveness and practical insights, but acknowledging the limitations in novelty and generalizability, a score of 7 is appropriate.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Jailbreaking Large Language Models Through Content Concretization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12937v1)
- **Authors**: Johan Wahréus, Ahmed Hussain, Panos Papadimitratos
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed for task automation and content generation, yet their safety mechanisms remain vulnerable to circumvention through different jailbreaking techniques. In this paper, we introduce \textit{Content Concretization} (CC), a novel jailbreaking technique that iteratively transforms abstract malicious requests into concrete, executable implementations. CC is a two-stage process: first, generating initial LLM responses using lower-tier, less constrained safety filters models, then refining them through higher-tier models that process both the preliminary output and original prompt. We evaluate our technique using 350 cybersecurity-specific prompts, demonstrating substantial improvements in jailbreak Success Rates (SRs), increasing from 7\% (no refinements) to 62\% after three refinement iterations, while maintaining a cost of 7.5\textcent~per prompt. Comparative A/B testing across nine different LLM evaluators confirms that outputs from additional refinement steps are consistently rated as more malicious and technically superior. Moreover, manual code analysis reveals that generated outputs execute with minimal modification, although optimal deployment typically requires target-specific fine-tuning. With eventual improved harmful code generation, these results highlight critical vulnerabilities in current LLM safety frameworks.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Jailbreaking Large Language Models Through Content Concretization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Content Concretization" (CC), a novel jailbreaking technique for Large Language Models (LLMs). CC iteratively refines abstract malicious requests into concrete, executable implementations using a two-stage process. First, a lower-tier LLM generates a preliminary solution draft, bypassing stricter safety filters. Second, a higher-tier LLM refines this draft, producing production-quality code by utilizing both the draft and original prompt. Experiments using cybersecurity-specific prompts demonstrate that CC significantly improves jailbreak success rates compared to directly prompting high-tier LLMs, while maintaining cost efficiency. The authors evaluate their technique using a multi-model evaluation framework, highlighting vulnerabilities in current LLM safety measures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM security, specifically in jailbreaking research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of "Content Concretization" is a genuinely new approach. It moves beyond simple prompt engineering and obfuscation by iteratively building upon LLM-generated content, which offers a different perspective for attack vectors. It is an incremental step towards a more rigorous adversarial approach.

*   **Methodology:** The two-stage architecture (lower-tier draft + higher-tier refinement) leverages the strengths of different LLMs effectively. The controlled experiments and thorough evaluations, incorporating both automated and manual assessments, are a significant strength. The use of multiple LLM evaluators further strengthens the validity of the results.

*   **Empirical Results:** The results are compelling. The significant increase in jailbreak success rates (from 7% to 62% after three refinement steps) clearly demonstrates the effectiveness of CC.  The A/B testing showing preference for outputs from higher refinement iterations provides solid evidence for improved code quality. The inclusion of cost analysis makes the method practically relevant.

*   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written, clearly explaining the concepts and experimental setup. The figures and tables are informative.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Domain Specificity:** The study focuses almost exclusively on cybersecurity-related code generation. While this provides a strong evaluation in a high-stakes domain, it limits the generalizability of the findings to other areas where LLM safety is crucial (e.g., generating harmful text, misinformation). The authors acknowledge this limitation.
*   **Model Diversity:** The paper relies on a specific LLM pairing (GPT-40-mini and Claude 3.7 Sonnet). While the choice is justified, testing with other LLM combinations would strengthen the conclusions. The authors also state that they would have liked to use other more complex combinations of models.
*   **Scalability of Manual Evaluation:** Although hybrid assessment is adopted, reliance on scalable automated solutions can introduce bias.

*   **Incremental Improvements in Safety:** The improvements in safety standards introduced due to this paper could likely be rapidly surpassed with newer, more complex LLMs. Future jailbreaking methods will need to address even more complex methods of detection.
*  **Limited Real-World Evaluation:** While functional evaluations were conducted, a more robust assessment in various real-world, hardened systems is needed.

**Significance:**

The paper is significant because it:

*   Identifies a new attack vector against LLM safety mechanisms.
*   Highlights the vulnerability of current safety filters to iterative content transformation.
*   Provides practical insights into how adversaries can exploit LLMs for malicious code generation.
*   Offers potential avenues for developing more robust countermeasures.

The paper's findings have implications for LLM developers, security researchers, and organizations that rely on LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite its limitations, the paper demonstrates significant novelty in its approach and presents compelling empirical evidence for its effectiveness.  The identified vulnerability is practically relevant, and the proposed technique is cost-effective. However, the limited domain specificity and model diversity prevent a higher score.  While the paper opens up exciting new directions, some further validation steps would solidify the impact of this work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Black-box Model Merging for Language-Model-as-a-Service with Massive Model Repositories
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12951v1)
- **Authors**: Shilian Chen, Jie Zhou, Tianyu Huai, Yujiang Lu, Junsong Li, Bihao Zhan, Qianjun Pan, Yutao Yang, Xin Li, Qin Chen, Hang Yan, Liang He
- **Abstract**: Model merging refers to the process of integrating multiple distinct models into a unified model that preserves and combines the strengths and capabilities of the individual models. Most existing approaches rely on task vectors to combine models, typically under the assumption that model parameters are accessible. However, for extremely large language models (LLMs) such as GPT-4, which are often provided solely as black-box services through API interfaces (Language-Model-as-a-Service), model weights are not available to end users. This presents a significant challenge, which we refer to as black-box model merging (BMM) with massive LLMs. To address this challenge, we propose a derivative-free optimization framework based on the evolutionary algorithm (Evo-Merging) that enables effective model merging using only inference-time API queries. Our method consists of two key components: (1) sparsity-based denoising, designed to identify and filter out irrelevant or redundant information across models, and (2) sign-aware scaling, which dynamically computes optimal combination weights for the relevant models based on their performance. We also provide a formal justification, along with a theoretical analysis, for our asymmetric sparsification. Extensive experimental evaluations demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art results on a range of tasks, significantly outperforming existing strong baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of model merging (integrating multiple models into one) when dealing with large language models (LLMs) offered as black-box services (Language-Model-as-a-Service), where model weights are inaccessible. This is referred to as black-box model merging (BMM).  The authors propose a derivative-free optimization framework called Evo-Merging, based on evolutionary algorithms, that enables model merging using only inference-time API queries. Evo-Merging includes two key components: 1) sparsity-based denoising to filter irrelevant information across models, and 2) sign-aware scaling to dynamically compute combination weights for relevant models based on their performance.  The paper provides theoretical justification and experimental results showing state-of-the-art performance on various tasks, outperforming existing baselines. The framework demonstrates the ability to effectively merge over 100 models while avoiding performance degradation, indicating generalization and knowledge reuse.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in tackling the practical problem of model merging for black-box LLMs accessible only via APIs. Most existing merging techniques assume access to model parameters. The approach is derivative-free, which is important for the black-box setting. The sparsity-based denoising and sign-aware scaling are potentially novel components tailored to this specific problem and the large-scale aspect of merging multiple models.
* **Significance:** The work is significant because it makes model merging more accessible and applicable to real-world scenarios where access to underlying model parameters is restricted.  The ability to merge multiple LLMs without direct access has substantial implications for creating powerful, task-adapted models using readily available services. The experiments demonstrate strong empirical performance, suggesting that the proposed approach is effective. The theoretical justification provides a solid foundation. The ability to fuse >100 models effectively distinguishes this work from prior methods.
* **Strengths:**
    * Addresses a practical and important problem (black-box model merging).
    * Proposes a novel derivative-free evolutionary framework.
    * Includes sparsity-based denoising and sign-aware scaling modules to handle noisy data in massive model repositories.
    * Provides theoretical justification and analysis.
    * Achieves state-of-the-art results with extensive experimental evaluation.
    * Demonstrates scalability by merging over 100 models.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The core of Evo-Merging relies on an evolutionary algorithm, which might be computationally expensive. The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the computational complexity and efficiency, particularly for extremely large models and datasets.
    * Although the paper provides theoretical justification, a more rigorous mathematical analysis of the convergence properties of the evolutionary algorithm and the effectiveness of the denoising and scaling techniques could be beneficial.
    * The paper could discuss potential failure cases or limitations more explicitly.  For instance, are there types of tasks or models for which Evo-Merging might not be effective?

* **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and scalable solution for black-box model merging. It can encourage more research on derivative-free optimization methods for LLMs and facilitate the creation of customized models by combining existing API-based services.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**  The paper presents a novel and significant solution to a practical problem in the LLM space.  The novelty of addressing the black-box setting and the scalability demonstrated by the experiments are compelling. While the reliance on evolutionary algorithms might have computational limitations, the substantial performance gains and accessibility improvements make it a valuable contribution. Further analysis of computational complexity and potential failure cases would strengthen the work, but the current contribution is substantial.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Do LLMs Understand Wine Descriptors Across Cultures? A Benchmark for Cultural Adaptations of Wine Reviews
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12961v1)
- **Authors**: Chenye Zou, Xingyue Wen, Tianyi Hu, Qian Janice Wang, Daniel Hershcovich
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have opened the door to culture-aware language tasks. We introduce the novel problem of adapting wine reviews across Chinese and English, which goes beyond literal translation by incorporating regional taste preferences and culture-specific flavor descriptors. In a case study on cross-cultural wine review adaptation, we compile the first parallel corpus of professional reviews, containing 8k Chinese and 16k Anglophone reviews. We benchmark both neural-machine-translation baselines and state-of-the-art LLMs with automatic metrics and human evaluation. For the latter, we propose three culture-oriented criteria -- Cultural Proximity, Cultural Neutrality, and Cultural Genuineness -- to assess how naturally a translated review resonates with target-culture readers. Our analysis shows that current models struggle to capture cultural nuances, especially in translating wine descriptions across different cultures. This highlights the challenges and limitations of translation models in handling cultural content.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the novel problem of cross-cultural adaptation of wine reviews, focusing on Chinese and English. The authors create CulturalWR, a bidirectional Chinese-English wine review dataset, benchmarking machine translation models and LLMs.  They propose three culture-oriented evaluation criteria: Cultural Proximity, Cultural Neutrality, and Cultural Genuineness. The paper investigates lexical and semantic differences in wine reviews between cultures, evaluating LLMs on adapting content and style. The findings reveal that current models struggle to capture cultural nuances in wine descriptions, especially translating flavors, highlighting limitations in handling cultural content. The paper explores various prompting strategies to improve LLMs' performance in cross-cultural adaptation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core strength lies in its introduction of a previously unaddressed problem: cross-cultural *adaptation* of wine reviews, not just simple translation. This is particularly relevant in our increasingly globalized world where cultural nuances often get lost in direct translations. The creation of the CulturalWR dataset is a valuable contribution, providing a resource for future research in this area.  The introduction of the evaluation criteria (Cultural Proximity, Neutrality, and Genuineness) provides a structured way to assess cultural appropriateness beyond simple accuracy.

*   **Significance:** The paper has significance both within NLP and potentially beyond. Within NLP, it opens up a new area of focus on cultural adaptation, providing a case study on a domain with rich subjective language. Beyond NLP, the work highlights the importance of considering cultural context when providing information to international consumers, which has relevance for marketing, tourism, and other fields. However, the specialization on wine reviews could be seen as a limiting factor in its wider appeal within NLP.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly defines a novel and relevant problem.
    *   Creates and makes available a new multilingual dataset.
    *   Introduces culture-oriented evaluation metrics.
    *   Conducts comprehensive experiments with both MT models and LLMs.
    *   Provides a detailed analysis of the challenges and limitations of current models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dataset size, while a contribution, is relatively small compared to datasets used in mainstream NLP tasks, particularly considering the domain complexity. The limitation to Chinese and English is also a constraint.
    *   The evaluation focuses mainly on English to Chinese and vice-versa, but doesn't thoroughly explore the individual language's nuanced characteristics as much as it focuses on the transfer.
    *   Generalizability could be a concern. While the principles of cultural adaptation are applicable to other domains, the specific flavor descriptors and cultural references related to wine are niche, and it may require significant effort to translate these findings to significantly different areas.
    *   The use of certain automatic evaluation metrics may be misleading due to the subjectivity of the task. Direct comparison of scores across models may not be the best method of assessment.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to spur further research in the area of cultural adaptation in NLP. The dataset and evaluation metrics can be used by other researchers working on cross-cultural NLP tasks. The findings can also inform the development of more culturally sensitive machine translation models. However, its relative niche focus may limit its appeal to those not already involved in cross-cultural or adaptation-focused NLP.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7**.

*   The paper makes a clear and distinct contribution by tackling the task of cross-cultural adaptation in wine reviews.
*   The dataset and proposed evaluation metrics provide a solid foundation for further research.
*   The analysis is comprehensive and well-reasoned, highlighting the challenges of capturing cultural nuances.

However, a higher score is not warranted due to the following:

*   The relatively small size of the dataset and limited language scope.
*   Potential limitations in generalizability.
*   Reliance on auto-evaluation methods.

The paper takes an important step toward building culturally sensitive NLP systems, even within a niche domain, making it a valuable and relevant contribution.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluating Large Language Models for Code Translation: Effects of Prompt Language and Prompt Design
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12973v1)
- **Authors**: Aamer Aljagthami, Mohammed Banabila, Musab Alshehri, Mohammed Kabini, Mohammad D. Alahmadi
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise for automated source-code translation, a capability critical to software migration, maintenance, and interoperability. Yet comparative evidence on how model choice, prompt design, and prompt language shape translation quality across multiple programming languages remains limited. This study conducts a systematic empirical assessment of state-of-the-art LLMs for code translation among C++, Java, Python, and C#, alongside a traditional baseline (TransCoder). Using BLEU and CodeBLEU, we quantify syntactic fidelity and structural correctness under two prompt styles (concise instruction and detailed specification) and two prompt languages (English and Arabic), with direction-aware evaluation across language pairs. Experiments show that detailed prompts deliver consistent gains across models and translation directions, and English prompts outperform Arabic by 13-15%. The top-performing model attains the highest CodeBLEU on challenging pairs such as Java to C# and Python to C++. Our evaluation shows that each LLM outperforms TransCoder across the benchmark. These results demonstrate the value of careful prompt engineering and prompt language choice, and provide practical guidance for software modernization and cross-language interoperability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Evaluating Large Language Models for Code Translation: Effects of Prompt Language and Prompt Design" systematically assesses the performance of several large language models (LLMs) for code translation between C++, Java, Python, and C#. The study investigates the impact of prompt design (concise vs. detailed) and prompt language (English vs. Arabic) on translation quality. Performance is measured using BLEU and CodeBLEU metrics.  The key findings are that detailed prompts improve translation accuracy, English prompts outperform Arabic prompts, and that contemporary LLMs generally outperform the traditional TransCoder baseline.  The paper also highlights direction-dependent performance variations between language pairs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic, controlled evaluation of LLMs for code translation across multiple programming languages *and* its focused analysis of prompt engineering factors (style and language).  While LLMs have been used for code translation before, the rigorous comparative aspect, especially the direction-aware analysis and prompt variation experiments, adds incremental value. The baseline comparison is useful.

*   **Significance:** The work has significance for several reasons:

    *   **Practical Guidance:** The identified best practices for prompt design (detailed prompts) and language (English) offer actionable insights for practitioners seeking to leverage LLMs for code modernization and interoperability.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:** The head-to-head comparison of different LLMs provides valuable data points for model selection based on specific translation needs.
    *   **Identified Asymmetries:**  The observation of direction-dependent performance highlights the nuances of code translation and suggests areas where future LLM development could focus.
    *   **Unified Evaluation:** Contributing a rigorous evaluation under a unified setup using BLEU and CodeBLEU increases comparability.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic and Controlled Experiments:** The study is well-designed with clear controls for prompt style, language, and translation direction.
    *   **Relevant Metrics:** The use of BLEU and CodeBLEU is appropriate for evaluating both surface-level and structural code similarity.
    *   **Baseline Comparison:** Including TransCoder as a baseline provides a valuable point of reference for assessing the progress made by LLMs.
    *   **Direction-Aware Analysis:** The examination of translation performance across language pairs reveals important asymmetries.
    *   **Replication Package:** Providing a replication package shows that the study values reproducibility and promotes further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Size:** The dataset size of 24 functions is relatively small. While the functions were selected to represent different languages, the limited scale restricts the generalizability of the results.
    *   **Limited Prompt Styles:** The study only compared two prompt styles (concise and detailed). Future research could explore other prompt engineering techniques, such as few-shot learning or chain-of-thought prompting.
    *   **Zero-Shot Setting:** While zero-shot performance is interesting, the study did not investigate the potential benefits of fine-tuning LLMs on code translation tasks, which could improve accuracy further.
    *   **Language Variety:** Only English and Arabic were evaluated. There may be other languages that yield better or worse results.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development and application of LLMs for code translation. By highlighting the importance of prompt engineering and identifying performance variations across language pairs, the study can guide future research and inform best practices for practitioners. It encourages researchers to investigate more complex prompt strategies and to develop LLMs that are more sensitive to the nuances of code semantics.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a well-executed and systematic evaluation of LLMs for code translation, with a focus on prompt engineering. While the limited dataset size is a constraint, the study provides valuable insights into the factors that influence translation quality and offers actionable guidance for practitioners. The work contributes incrementally to the field, building upon existing research and offering new perspectives on the challenges and opportunities of LLM-based code translation.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The score reflects the paper's incremental novelty and moderate significance. While the study is well-designed and provides valuable insights, the relatively small dataset size and limited exploration of prompt styles and languages limit its impact on the field. The paper offers a rigorous framework for evaluating LLMs for code translation and provides actionable guidance for practitioners, but it lacks the transformative potential of a truly groundbreaking contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Toward PDDL Planning Copilot
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12987v1)
- **Authors**: Yarin Benyamin, Argaman Mordoch, Shahaf S. Shperberg, Roni Stern
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly being used as autonomous agents capable of performing complicated tasks. However, they lack the ability to perform reliable long-horizon planning on their own. This paper bridges this gap by introducing the Planning Copilot, a chatbot that integrates multiple planning tools and allows users to invoke them through instructions in natural language. The Planning Copilot leverages the Model Context Protocol (MCP), a recently developed standard for connecting LLMs with external tools and systems. This approach allows using any LLM that supports MCP without domain-specific fine-tuning. Our Planning Copilot supports common planning tasks such as checking the syntax of planning problems, selecting an appropriate planner, calling it, validating the plan it generates, and simulating their execution. We empirically evaluate the ability of our Planning Copilot to perform these tasks using three open-source LLMs. The results show that the Planning Copilot highly outperforms using the same LLMs without the planning tools. We also conducted a limited qualitative comparison of our tool against Chat GPT-5, a very recent commercial LLM. Our results shows that our Planning Copilot significantly outperforms GPT-5 despite relying on a much smaller LLM. This suggests dedicated planning tools may be an effective way to enable LLMs to perform planning tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the "Planning Copilot," a chatbot that integrates multiple planning tools with a Large Language Model (LLM) via the Model Context Protocol (MCP). This copilot assists users in performing complex planning tasks by enabling the LLM to invoke tools for syntax checking, planner selection, plan validation, and simulation. The authors evaluate the Planning Copilot's ability to perform these tasks using open-source LLMs, demonstrating that integrating planning tools significantly improves performance compared to using the LLMs alone. They also conduct a qualitative comparison against a recent, powerful commercial LLM (Chat GPT-5), showing that the Planning Copilot, even with a smaller underlying LLM, outperforms it. This suggests that dedicated planning tools are a valuable way to improve LLM-based planning capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Integration:** The paper presents a novel and practical approach by integrating established AI planning tools with LLMs. This addresses a known weakness of LLMs (long-horizon planning) by leveraging the strengths of symbolic AI.
*   **MCP utilization:** Effectively uses the Model Context Protocol (MCP) to facilitate the modular integration of planning tools, which avoids the need for domain-specific fine-tuning. This significantly improves the accessibility and adaptability of the copilot.
*   **Empirical Validation:** Provides a thorough empirical evaluation using open-source LLMs, with and without planning tools, showcasing a clear performance improvement. The comparison to the recent GPT-5 is a valuable demonstration of the approach's effectiveness.
*   **Addresses a key challenge:** Tackles the challenging problem of reliably generating and validating plans with LLMs and provides a concrete solution with associated tools.
*   **Clear Architecture:** The described architecture using LangGraph presents a practical and repeatable approach to integrating LLMs and external tools.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Task Types:** The evaluation focuses primarily on core tasks such as checking syntax, plan validation, and simulation. It would be beneficial to explore how the Planning Copilot facilitates more complex planning scenarios and problem-solving.
*   **Qualitative GPT-5 comparison:** While the comparison to GPT-5 is interesting, it's qualitative and limited by access to the chat version only. A more controlled quantitative comparison using the GPT-5 API (if accessible) would strengthen the results. The reliance on GPT-5's advertised status as 'state-of-the-art' in 2025 also relies on a potentially speculative claim of continued LLM development that may not occur in that time frame.
*   **Dependency on Planning Tools:** The system’s performance is inherently limited by the capabilities of the underlying planning tools. While the paper acknowledges this, it would be beneficial to discuss potential approaches for extending the Planning Copilot with new tools or improving existing ones.
*   **Lack of ablations:** No ablations are performed to explore the relative contribution of each planning tool in the overall system performance. This makes it harder to establish whether each component is truly important.
*   **Reproducibility Concerns:** While the authors provide a GitHub repository, fully replicating the experiments may be challenging due to dependencies on specific LLM versions and the availability of GPT-5 (or its future equivalent).

**Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty lies in the practical integration of a diverse set of planning tools into an LLM-based agent to overcome the shortcomings of LLMs in long-horizon planning. While prior work has explored using LLMs for planning, this paper's systematic approach to tool integration, the use of MCP, and the strong empirical results highlight the value of this approach.

The significance stems from addressing a critical limitation of LLMs and providing a viable path towards more reliable and effective AI planning systems. The Planning Copilot has the potential to enable non-experts to leverage the power of AI planning tools. Furthermore, the system could act as a robust alternative to direct planning by relying on robust planning tools, providing a more predictable interface for a task. The use of an LLM-based copilot could make classical planning more accessible and usable.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7** out of 10. While the paper presents a valuable and practical contribution, some of the limitations and the reliance on potentially speculative claims about future LLM development prevent it from achieving a higher score. The integration of planning tools with LLMs is a novel and impactful idea and the empirical results are compelling. The weaknesses in the quantitative comparison and scope of the evaluation, along with the lack of ablation studies, hold it back.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### HPIM: Heterogeneous Processing-In-Memory-based Accelerator for Large Language Models Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12993v1)
- **Authors**: Cenlin Duan, Jianlei Yang, Rubing Yang, Yikun Wang, Yiou Wang, Lingkun Long, Yingjie Qi, Xiaolin He, Ao Zhou, Xueyan Wang, Weisheng Zhao
- **Abstract**: The deployment of large language models (LLMs) presents significant challenges due to their enormous memory footprints, low arithmetic intensity, and stringent latency requirements, particularly during the autoregressive decoding stage. Traditional compute-centric accelerators, such as GPUs, suffer from severe resource underutilization and memory bandwidth bottlenecks in these memory-bound workloads. To overcome these fundamental limitations, we propose HPIM, the first memory-centric heterogeneous Processing-In-Memory (PIM) accelerator that integrates SRAM-PIM and HBM-PIM subsystems designed specifically for LLM inference. HPIM employs a software-hardware co-design approach that combines a specialized compiler framework with a heterogeneous hardware architecture. It intelligently partitions workloads based on their characteristics: latency-critical attention operations are mapped to the SRAM-PIM subsystem to exploit its ultra-low latency and high computational flexibility, while weight-intensive GEMV computations are assigned to the HBM-PIM subsystem to leverage its high internal bandwidth and large storage capacity. Furthermore, HPIM introduces a tightly coupled pipeline strategy across SRAM-PIM and HBM-PIM subsystems to maximize intra-token parallelism, thereby significantly mitigating serial dependency of the autoregressive decoding stage. Comprehensive evaluations using a cycle-accurate simulator demonstrate that HPIM significantly outperforms state-of-the-art accelerators, achieving a peak speedup of up to 22.8x compared to the NVIDIA A100 GPU. Moreover, HPIM exhibits superior performance over contemporary PIM-based accelerators, highlighting its potential as a highly practical and scalable solution for accelerating large-scale LLM inference.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper "HPIM: Heterogeneous Processing-In-Memory-based Accelerator for Large Language Models Inference" presents a novel PIM-based accelerator architecture designed to address the memory bandwidth bottleneck and latency challenges associated with LLM inference, particularly during the autoregressive decoding phase. HPIM integrates both SRAM-PIM (for low latency and computational flexibility) and HBM-PIM (for high bandwidth and large storage capacity) subsystems. A software-hardware co-design approach is employed, with a specialized compiler framework partitioning workloads based on their characteristics. Latency-critical attention operations are mapped to SRAM-PIM, while weight-intensive GEMV computations are assigned to HBM-PIM. The architecture features a tightly-coupled pipeline strategy across subsystems to maximize intra-token parallelism. The authors evaluate HPIM using a cycle-accurate simulator, demonstrating significant performance improvements over state-of-the-art accelerators like NVIDIA A100 GPUs and other PIM-based approaches.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in the *integration* of heterogeneous PIM subsystems (SRAM-PIM and HBM-PIM) specifically tailored for LLM inference. While individual PIM components have been explored, the *synergistic combination* and *hardware-aware workload partitioning* appear to be a significant advancement. The authors clearly identify the limitations of homogeneous PIM solutions and provide a well-reasoned justification for their heterogeneous approach. The tightly-coupled pipeline strategy to exploit intra-token parallelism is another notable contribution, directly addressing the serial dependency issue in autoregressive decoding. The compiler-level co-design is also a good touch.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to overcome fundamental bottlenecks in LLM deployment.  The memory bandwidth limitations and latency constraints of LLM inference are well-recognized challenges. The authors directly address these issues with a carefully designed architecture that optimizes resource utilization and reduces data movement. The experimental results showing substantial speedups compared to a A100 and other PIM approaches are compelling and suggest that HPIM could enable more efficient and scalable LLM inference. The claim that HPIM's throughput exceeds that of another promising recent architecture (CXL-PNM) by nearly sixfold is particularly noteworthy, although the detailed comparison with IANUS shows a slightly more nuanced picture.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined problem statement and clear motivation for the proposed architecture.
    *   Thoughtful design that addresses the diverse computational and memory requirements of LLM inference.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation using a cycle-accurate simulator.
    *   Significant performance improvements over state-of-the-art accelerators.
    *   Detailed microarchitecture design information.
    *   Comprehensive workload analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The work relies heavily on simulation. While the simulator appears to be well-calibrated, a physical prototype would provide stronger evidence of HPIM's practical viability.
    *   There is not an area comparison. The authors should have included an area comparison of the different PIMs.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of the limitations of the proposed architecture. For instance, how does HPIM scale to even larger LLMs, and what are the energy efficiency implications?
    *   A sensitivity analysis of the workload mapping strategies would add value. How sensitive is the performance to variations in model size, sequence length, and hardware parameters?

*   **Potential Influence:**  HPIM has the potential to influence the design of future accelerators for LLM inference. The concept of integrating heterogeneous PIM subsystems tailored to specific workload characteristics could be widely adopted. The work also highlights the importance of hardware-aware workload partitioning and tight integration between memory and compute.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a well-designed and thoroughly evaluated PIM-based accelerator for LLM inference. The heterogeneous architecture and intra-token parallelism strategy are particularly promising. While a physical prototype would strengthen the claims, the simulation results are compelling and suggest that HPIM could significantly improve the efficiency and scalability of LLM deployment. Overall, the paper makes a substantial contribution to the field and has the potential to influence future research and development in LLM acceleration.

Score: 8.5

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 8/10

### SitLLM: Large Language Models for Sitting Posture Health Understanding via Pressure Sensor Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12994v1)
- **Authors**: Jian Gao, Fufangchen Zhao, Yiyang Zhang, Danfeng Yan
- **Abstract**: Poor sitting posture is a critical yet often overlooked factor contributing to long-term musculoskeletal disorders and physiological dysfunctions. Existing sitting posture monitoring systems, although leveraging visual, IMU, or pressure-based modalities, often suffer from coarse-grained recognition and lack the semantic expressiveness necessary for personalized feedback. In this paper, we propose \textbf{SitLLM}, a lightweight multimodal framework that integrates flexible pressure sensing with large language models (LLMs) to enable fine-grained posture understanding and personalized health-oriented response generation. SitLLM comprises three key components: (1) a \textit{Gaussian-Robust Sensor Embedding Module} that partitions pressure maps into spatial patches and injects local noise perturbations for robust feature extraction; (2) a \textit{Prompt-Driven Cross-Modal Alignment Module} that reprograms sensor embeddings into the LLM's semantic space via multi-head cross-attention using the pre-trained vocabulary embeddings; and (3) a \textit{Multi-Context Prompt Module} that fuses feature-level, structure-level, statistical-level, and semantic-level contextual information to guide instruction comprehension.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SitLLM, a novel multimodal framework that combines flexible pressure sensors with large language models (LLMs) to provide fine-grained sitting posture understanding and personalized health-oriented feedback.  SitLLM tackles the limitations of existing posture monitoring systems, which often offer coarse-grained recognition and lack semantic expressiveness. The framework consists of three main components: a Gaussian-Robust Sensor Embedding Module for robust feature extraction from pressure maps, a Prompt-Driven Cross-Modal Alignment Module to align sensor data with the LLM's semantic space, and a Multi-Context Prompt Module that integrates various contextual cues to guide instruction comprehension.  The authors also created a new pressure-text alignment dataset to facilitate high-quality cross-modal alignment.  Experimental results demonstrate that SitLLM outperforms existing baselines on tasks like sitting posture analysis, health-aware question answering, and generating posture correction suggestions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of integrating low-dimensional pressure sensor data with the semantic richness of LLMs for personalized posture health monitoring is a valuable contribution.  The architecture is well-designed, with each module (sensor embedding, alignment, prompting) addressing specific challenges in bridging the modality gap. The creation of a custom dataset is also a significant strength, as it's a key enabler for training such a system.
*   **Significance:** Poor posture is a widespread health issue. A system that can provide personalized feedback and understanding based on subtle pressure variations has the potential to significantly improve preventative healthcare and well-being. The focus on efficient edge deployment further enhances the practical applicability of the work.
*   **Technical Soundness:**  The paper provides a clear description of the SitLLM architecture and the data creation process. The experimental evaluation appears comprehensive, with comparisons to relevant baselines and a detailed analysis of performance metrics.
*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and organized, making it easy to understand the problem, proposed solution, and experimental results. The use of diagrams and examples further enhances clarity.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalization:** While the authors demonstrate the effectiveness of SitLLM, the experiments are limited to a specific dataset they created. Further evaluation on more diverse datasets and real-world scenarios is needed to assess its generalization capability. A discussion of potential biases introduced in their data creation process would also improve the analysis.
*   **LLM Dependency:** SitLLM relies on the capabilities of the underlying LLM. The choice of Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct is reasonable, but the framework's performance is inherently tied to the LLM's ability to understand and generate contextually relevant responses. Further experimentation on different LLMs (especially on medical foundation models) would strengthen the paper.
*   **Dataset Quality:** The data construction process relies heavily on GPT-4o for generation and scoring. The potential biases and limitations of GPT-4o itself are not fully addressed. Even with expert annotations, there's a risk of inheriting biases or generating responses that are "medically correct" but not practically useful or personalized. The relatively small dataset size (9,255 samples) raises concerns about overfitting.
*   **Edge Deployment Details:** While the paper mentions efficiency suitable for edge deployment, it lacks concrete details about the actual resource requirements (memory, computational power). Quantitative analysis of inference time and energy consumption would make the claim more convincing.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially significant approach to posture monitoring by integrating pressure sensors with LLMs. The architecture is well-designed, and the experimental results demonstrate promising performance on various tasks. However, the limitations related to generalization, LLM dependency, dataset quality, and edge deployment details need to be addressed in future research. This reduces the overall score of the paper, given the strong reliance on a potentially biased or overfitting dataset.

**Score: 7**

The paper is a strong contribution, well above average, showcasing a solid foundation for future work. However, the limitations must be acknowledged and tackled in future research, to fully realize the approach's potential.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ReTrack: Data Unlearning in Diffusion Models through Redirecting the Denoising Trajectory
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13007v1)
- **Authors**: Qitan Shi, Cheng Jin, Jiawei Zhang, Yuantao Gu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models excel at generating high-quality, diverse images but suffer from training data memorization, raising critical privacy and safety concerns. Data unlearning has emerged to mitigate this issue by removing the influence of specific data without retraining from scratch. We propose ReTrack, a fast and effective data unlearning method for diffusion models. ReTrack employs importance sampling to construct a more efficient fine-tuning loss, which we approximate by retaining only dominant terms. This yields an interpretable objective that redirects denoising trajectories toward the $k$-nearest neighbors, enabling efficient unlearning while preserving generative quality. Experiments on MNIST T-Shirt, CelebA-HQ, CIFAR-10, and Stable Diffusion show that ReTrack achieves state-of-the-art performance, striking the best trade-off between unlearning strength and generation quality preservation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ReTrack: Data Unlearning in Diffusion Models through Redirecting the Denoising Trajectory":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes "ReTrack," a new data unlearning method for diffusion models.  The core idea is to "redirect" the denoising trajectory away from the specific data to be unlearned and toward the k-nearest neighbors in the remaining (non-unlearned) dataset. This is achieved by:

1.  Constructing a loss function based on importance sampling that focuses on samples from the unlearning set, improving efficiency.
2.  Approximating the loss function by considering only the k-nearest neighbors of the data to be unlearned, reducing computational cost.

The authors argue that redirecting to real data points (neighbors) helps preserve generation quality. Experiments on MNIST T-Shirt, CelebA-HQ, CIFAR-10, and Stable Diffusion demonstrate the effectiveness of ReTrack in achieving a good balance between unlearning strength and generation quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The approach of *redirecting* the denoising trajectory is a relatively novel perspective in the context of data unlearning for diffusion models. Unlike methods that simply erase information or add noise, ReTrack actively guides the denoising process toward plausible, existing data points.
*   **Efficiency:** By using importance sampling and k-nearest neighbor approximation, ReTrack aims to reduce the computational cost associated with data unlearning, which is a major practical concern.
*   **Preservation of Generation Quality:** The emphasis on redirecting towards real neighbors is a key strength. This approach aims to mitigate the quality degradation often associated with existing unlearning methods, making it practically relevant.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents experiments across a diverse range of datasets, including a relatively complex Stable Diffusion scenario, providing substantial empirical evidence. Quantitative metrics are also used appropriately.
*   **Clear Intuition and Explanation:** The paper presents a clear, step-by-step walkthrough and an intuitive explanation of the method with a diagram.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **k-NN Dependency:** The method relies on the k-NN search, which can be computationally expensive, especially for high-dimensional data. The paper attempts to address this through approximations, but the scalability for very large datasets remains a potential concern. The paper could provide a more detailed analysis of the cost for finding the k-nearest neighbors.
*   **Sensitivity to k:** While an ablation study on k is provided, a more thorough analysis would be helpful, especially regarding the trade-off between unlearning strength and generation quality as k varies.  The optimal value of k might be dataset-dependent, and practical guidelines are needed.
*   **Dataset limitations:** Datasets are too simple and too small to evaluate in practice on real-world scenarios, especially for the Stable Diffusion experiments, since only 48 text-image pairs are used for evaluation.
*   **Limited comparison:**  The method only compares against a few existing methods such as NegGrad and SISS. A more thorough comparison with a wider range of recent unlearning methods for diffusion models would strengthen the paper.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the growing field of data unlearning for diffusion models. The idea of redirecting the denoising trajectory is innovative and has the potential to be extended and refined in future research. If the computational challenges of k-NN search can be effectively addressed, ReTrack could become a practical and widely adopted unlearning technique.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and promising approach, it also exhibits some limitations, as described above. The k-NN dependency and the limited evaluation on real-world data warrant some reservation. Additionally, the comparison is not exhaustive, and more insights are needed to fully understand its strengths and weaknesses. Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7 is appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Visualized Framework for Event Cooperation with Generative Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13011v1)
- **Authors**: Yuyang Tian, Shunqiang Mao, Wenchang Gao, Lanlan Qiu, Tianxing He
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have revolutionized the simulation of agent societies, enabling autonomous planning, memory formation, and social interactions. However, existing frameworks often overlook systematic evaluations for event organization and lack visualized integration with physically grounded environments, limiting agents' ability to navigate spaces and interact with items realistically. We develop MiniAgentPro, a visualization platform featuring an intuitive map editor for customizing environments and a simulation player with smooth animations. Based on this tool, we introduce a comprehensive test set comprising eight diverse event scenarios with basic and hard variants to assess agents' ability. Evaluations using GPT-4o demonstrate strong performance in basic settings but highlight coordination challenges in hard variants.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "MiniAgentPro," a visualized framework built on Unity for simulating events involving generative agents. It aims to improve existing simulation frameworks by adding a map editor for environment customization, a simulation player for observation, and enhancements for on-the-fly planning, basic physics, and item interactions. The authors also present a test set of eight diverse event scenarios with basic and hard variants to evaluate agents' event coordination abilities.
