The evaluation using GPT-4 shows promising results in basic settings but highlights challenges in coordination for more complex hard settings that requires invitation based communication and planning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Visualized Framework:** The visualization aspect (map editor and simulation player) addresses a significant gap in the existing literature, where such tools are often lacking or less user-friendly. This makes simulation analysis and customization significantly easier for researchers.
    * **Comprehensive Test Set:** The introduction of a test set specifically designed to evaluate event organization capabilities is a valuable contribution.  The division into basic and hard variants adds granularity to the evaluation, allowing for the identification of specific challenges.
    * **Incorporation of Physical Grounding:** Adding basic physics and item interactions enhances the realism of the simulations, which is often missing in solely text-based agent simulations.
    * **Practical Implementation:** The open-source nature of the tool promotes accessibility and reproducibility within the research community.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Novelty in Core Agent Framework:** The agent framework itself largely builds upon existing work (GA by Park et al., dialogue system by Wang, Chiu, and Chiu). The major contribution lies in the visualization and integration of these elements with enhanced planning.
    * **Reliance on GPT-4 for Evaluation:** While using GPT-4 for evaluation provides a degree of objectivity, the subjectivity inherent in LLM-based evaluations remains a concern. The reliance on LLM to score another LLM introduces its own set of potential biases.
    * **Scope of Scenarios:** While the test set encompasses a variety of event types, further expanding the complexity and nuance of these scenarios (e.g., adding more agents, longer time horizons, overlapping events) would strengthen the evaluation.
    * **Limited Depth of Physics Simulation:**  The "basic physics" is not deeply explored. A more sophisticated physics engine that incorporates collisions, realistic object manipulation, and agent navigation would significantly increase the framework's robustness.
    * **Missing Comparative Evaluation:**  The paper doesn't explicitly compare MiniAgentPro against existing agent simulation platforms in terms of usability, features, or performance. Such a comparison would better highlight the tool's advantages.

* **Novelty:**  The combination of a visual environment, an event-focused test set, and integration of existing agent frameworks constitutes a moderate degree of novelty. The core agent algorithms are based on the previous work, but the overall packaged system and evaluation framework are original.
* **Significance:** The tool could significantly aid researchers in exploring generative agents, evaluating their abilities in event coordination, and creating more realistic and interactive simulations. The test set provides a benchmark for evaluating agent capabilities in organizational tasks, a relatively underexplored area.  However, the dependency on an external evaluation agent and limited physics simulation limits its overall utility.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a practical and valuable contribution to the field of generative agent research by providing a visualized environment and a test set for event organization. The combination of existing agent architectures with physics and item interactions, however basic they may be, adds to the usability and realism. While the underlying agent framework is not entirely novel, the integration of these elements and the focus on event coordination represent a worthwhile advancement. The limited scope and need for explicit testing further limit the overall performance for more robust tasks. Therefore, a score of 6 reflects this assessment, considering the tool's potential impact in streamlining agent research but also acknowledging the limitations in original contributions and depth of the work.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### xOffense: An AI-driven autonomous penetration testing framework with offensive knowledge-enhanced LLMs and multi agent systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13021v1)
- **Authors**: Phung Duc Luong, Le Tran Gia Bao, Nguyen Vu Khai Tam, Dong Huu Nguyen Khoa, Nguyen Huu Quyen, Van-Hau Pham, Phan The Duy
- **Abstract**: This work introduces xOffense, an AI-driven, multi-agent penetration testing framework that shifts the process from labor-intensive, expert-driven manual efforts to fully automated, machine-executable workflows capable of scaling seamlessly with computational infrastructure. At its core, xOffense leverages a fine-tuned, mid-scale open-source LLM (Qwen3-32B) to drive reasoning and decision-making in penetration testing. The framework assigns specialized agents to reconnaissance, vulnerability scanning, and exploitation, with an orchestration layer ensuring seamless coordination across phases. Fine-tuning on Chain-of-Thought penetration testing data further enables the model to generate precise tool commands and perform consistent multi-step reasoning. We evaluate xOffense on two rigorous benchmarks: AutoPenBench and AI-Pentest-Benchmark. The results demonstrate that xOffense consistently outperforms contemporary methods, achieving a sub-task completion rate of 79.17%, decisively surpassing leading systems such as VulnBot and PentestGPT. These findings highlight the potential of domain-adapted mid-scale LLMs, when embedded within structured multi-agent orchestration, to deliver superior, cost-efficient, and reproducible solutions for autonomous penetration testing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the xOffense paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces xOffense, an AI-driven, multi-agent penetration testing framework designed to automate the penetration testing process more effectively and efficiently.  xOffense uses a fine-tuned, mid-scale open-source LLM (Qwen3-32B) to drive decision-making across three specialized agents: Reconnaissance, Vulnerability Analysis, and Exploitation. It incorporates a "grey-box prompting" scheme for context-aware decision-making. The framework is evaluated on AutoPenBench and AI-Pentest-Benchmark and shows superior performance compared to existing methods like VulnBot and PentestGPT. The key claims are that a domain-adapted, mid-scale LLM within a structured multi-agent system can provide a superior and cost-effective solution for autonomous penetration testing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in the combination of several elements:  (1) the focus on a *mid-sized, open-source LLM* for penetration testing, directly addressing the cost and access barriers associated with larger, proprietary models. (2) The integration of a *grey-box prompting* mechanism.  (3) A comprehensive *multi-agent system* based on a Penetration Task Graph (PTG) that seems well-thought-out. While multi-agent systems in penetration testing aren't entirely new (VulnBot also uses this), the xOffense implementation and the explicit emphasis on cost-effectiveness are distinct. The grey-box prompting, too, is a useful refinement, providing more contextual awareness to the agents without completely compromising the black-box testing scenario.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem: the widening gap between the number of vulnerabilities and the capacity to address them with manual penetration testing.  By showing that a more affordable and accessible approach can outperform systems relying on large, expensive LLMs, xOffense has the potential to democratize automated penetration testing. The results, if validated further, suggest a shift towards more efficient and specialized models rather than solely relying on model scale. This could have a significant impact on how security teams approach automated testing. The results suggesting that a medium sized LLM can even match or exceed the results from manually assisted frameworks also deserves attention.

* **Strengths:**
    * The experimental design is relatively thorough, using two well-established benchmark datasets (AutoPenBench and AI-Pentest-Benchmark).
    * The comparison against a range of existing systems, including both open-source and proprietary models, is valuable.
    * The detailed description of the system architecture, task coordination, and fine-tuning methodology adds credibility.
    * The identified threats to validity demonstrate a good awareness of the limitations of the study. The framework, even though automated, is easily interpretable for Security teams.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The reliance on virtualized environments, while standard, may not fully capture the complexities and nuances of real-world network environments.
    * While the AutoPenBench benchmark includes some real-world CVEs, a more extensive evaluation on a larger and more diverse set of real-world systems would further strengthen the findings.
    * The discussion of stealth, time-to-compromise, and resource utilization is limited. These are crucial aspects of real-world penetration testing and deserve more attention.
    * Even after using LoRA, Zeus and FlashAttention, the experiments still require a A100, so its "cost-effectiveness" may be relevant for an organization that already has a server with high-end GPU. The system's actual effectiveness in lower-resource environments remains unclear.

* **Potential Influence:**  xOffense has the potential to influence the design of future automated penetration testing systems.  The focus on cost-effectiveness and the use of domain-adapted, mid-scale LLMs could lead to more practical and accessible solutions for a wider range of organizations. The grey-box prompting approach could also be adopted in other AI-driven security tools. The work also underscores the importance of well-structured multi-agent architectures for complex security tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of automated penetration testing. While the concept of using multi-agent systems and LLMs in security is not entirely new, xOffense distinguishes itself through its focus on a mid-sized open-source LLM, grey-box prompting, cost-effectiveness, and relatively thorough evaluation. The reported results are promising and suggest a viable alternative to systems relying on large proprietary models. Given the current emphasis on large language models, demonstrating that a smaller, focused model can achieve competitive results is important and justifies a higher score. However, the limitations regarding real-world environments, limited attention to stealth and time-to-compromise, and the still high hardware requirements prevent it from achieving a higher score.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Validating Solidity Code Defects using Symbolic and Concrete Execution powered by Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13023v1)
- **Authors**: Ştefan-Claudiu Susan, Andrei Arusoaie, Dorel Lucanu
- **Abstract**: The high rate of false alarms from static analysis tools and Large Language Models (LLMs) complicates vulnerability detection in Solidity Smart Contracts, demanding methods that can formally or empirically prove the presence of defects. This paper introduces a novel detection pipeline that integrates custom Slither-based detectors, LLMs, Kontrol, and Forge. Our approach is designed to reliably detect defects and generate proofs. We currently perform experiments with promising results for seven types of critical defects. We demonstrate the pipeline's efficacy by presenting our findings for three vulnerabilities -- Reentrancy, Complex Fallback, and Faulty Access Control Policies -- that are challenging for current verification solutions, which often generate false alarms or fail to detect them entirely. We highlight the potential of either symbolic or concrete execution in correctly classifying such code faults. By chaining these instruments, our method effectively validates true positives, significantly reducing the manual verification burden. Although we identify potential limitations, such as the inconsistency and the cost of LLMs, our findings establish a robust framework for combining heuristic analysis with formal verification to achieve more reliable and automated smart contract auditing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of false positives and missed detections in smart contract vulnerability detection using static analysis tools and Large Language Models (LLMs).  It proposes a novel three-stage pipeline that combines custom Slither-based detectors, LLMs, and either Kontrol (for formal verification) or Forge (for concrete execution). The pipeline first uses Slither to identify potential vulnerabilities, then employs an LLM to generate a concrete proof-of-exploit, and finally validates this exploit using Kontrol or Forge. The authors present promising experimental results for seven defect types, focusing on Reentrancy, Complex Fallback, and Faulty Access Control Policies. They argue that this combined approach leverages the strengths of each component to achieve more reliable and automated smart contract auditing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining static analysis, LLM-based test generation, and formal/concrete execution for smart contract vulnerability validation is moderately novel.  Existing approaches often focus on one or two of these elements. While there have been individual studies looking at LLMs, static analysis, or formal verification, the integrated pipeline presented here represents a sensible and practical approach. The novelty resides in the orchestrated workflow and the claim that it significantly reduces the burden of manual verification. The paper also contributes by systematically demonstrating the limitations of current tools and LLMs on specific vulnerability types, thereby motivating the need for their combined approach. However, the components themselves (Slither, LLMs, Forge, Kontrol) are well-established.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the practical need for more reliable smart contract auditing. Given the high stakes associated with smart contract security, any method that reduces false positives and increases the confidence in vulnerability detection is valuable. The focus on challenging vulnerabilities (Reentrancy, Fallback, Access Control) that often elude existing tools enhances its relevance. Successfully automating test case generation is an impactful contribution, but the paper does not thoroughly address the costs of using LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problems of false positives and missed detections in existing tools.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** The three-stage pipeline is well-defined and easy to understand.
    *   **Motivating Examples:** The case studies provide compelling evidence for the limitations of existing tools and motivate the need for the proposed approach.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The emphasis on practical applicability and automation makes the research relevant to industry.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation of 7 types of defects:** provides a wide range of use cases for the applicability of the method

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM Dependency:** The reliance on LLMs introduces potential inconsistencies and cost concerns. The paper acknowledges this but doesn't fully explore the implications. What if the LLM refuses to generate a test case due to security concerns, or generates incorrect or hallucinated tests?
    *   **Scalability:** The paper does not thoroughly address the scalability of the approach to larger, more complex smart contracts. The manual interventions required might become a bottleneck.
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** While promising, the evaluation is still preliminary. More comprehensive benchmarks on standard smart contract datasets are needed to fully assess the effectiveness of the pipeline.
    *   **Lack of Comparison:** The paper does not directly compare the performance of its approach against other state-of-the-art combined approaches. A comparative analysis would strengthen the claims of improved accuracy and reduced manual effort.
    *   **Implementation Details:** The paper provides limited details on the custom Slither-based detectors and the specific prompting strategies used with the LLMs. Greater transparency in these aspects would enhance reproducibility.
    *   **No empirical cost analysis of using LLMs:** The paper talks about LLM costs but does not provide a thorough analysis.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and automated smart contract auditing tools. The combined approach offers a promising direction for addressing the limitations of existing methods. If proven effective at scale, it could significantly improve the security and reliability of decentralized applications.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6**. While the paper presents a promising and moderately novel approach to smart contract vulnerability detection, it has limitations in its evaluation, scalability, and implementation details. The dependency on LLMs, the lack of comprehensive comparisons, and the need for more extensive benchmarking hold back the score. The paper clearly identifies weaknesses in existing tools and proposes a potential solution, and the case studies offer compelling motivation. The potential for impact is there, but the evaluation and further development are needed to fully realize it.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### GView: A Survey of Binary Forensics via Visual, Semantic, and AI-Enhanced Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13025v1)
- **Authors**: Raul Zaharia, Dragoş Gavriluţ, Gheorghiţă Mutu
- **Abstract**: Cybersecurity threats continue to become more sophisticated and diverse in their artifacts, boosting both their volume and complexity. To overcome those challenges, we present GView, an open-source forensic analysis framework with visual and AI-enhanced reasoning. It started with focus on the practical cybersecurity industry. It has evolved significantly, incorporating large language models (LLMs) to dynamically enhance reasoning and ease the forensic workflows. This paper surveys both the current state of GView with its published papers alongside those that are in the publishing process. It also includes its innovative use of logical inference through predicates and inference rules for both the analyzed documents and the user's actions for better suggestions. We highlight the extensible architecture, showcasing its potential as a bridge between the practical forensics worlds with the academic research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the GView paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents GView, an open-source forensic analysis framework designed to streamline binary analysis through visual, semantic, and AI-enhanced techniques. GView aims to address the increasing complexity and volume of cybersecurity threats by providing an integrated environment that automates content extraction, facilitates correlation and inference, offers multi-granularity visualization, and provides expert-system guidance. The framework uses a plug-and-play architecture with data identifiers and smart viewers, and incorporates large language models (LLMs) to enhance reasoning and decision support.  The paper highlights GView's architecture, key functionalities, and presents two use cases (network-based infection analysis and suspicious email dissection) to demonstrate its capabilities. Future work focuses on integration with fine-tuned LLMs and the development of an advanced inference engine based on logical programming.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** GView's novelty lies in its attempt to unify various forensic analysis techniques (visual, semantic, and AI-driven) within a single, extensible framework.  The integration of LLMs for interactive chat and automated analytical support is a notable effort. However, the paper doesn't delve deeply into the specific algorithms or methods used for LLM integration, which limits the perceived novelty. The core architectural concept of plug-and-play systems in forensic analysis is not entirely new. Existing tools often have plugin-based capabilities.

**Significance:** The potential significance of GView stems from its goal of simplifying and accelerating the forensic analysis process.  By automating extraction, correlation, and inference, GView could potentially reduce analyst workload and improve accuracy. The use cases illustrate how GView can be applied to real-world scenarios, but the paper lacks empirical evidence or benchmarks to quantitatively demonstrate its performance advantages over existing tools. This absence of quantifiable results makes it harder to gauge its true impact. The extensible architecture is a positive, as it allows for the incorporation of new analysis techniques and threat intelligence.

**Strengths:**

*   **Integrated approach:** Combines multiple analysis techniques in a single framework.
*   **Extensibility:** Modular architecture enables easy addition of new functionalities.
*   **Practical focus:** Designed with the needs of cybersecurity industry in mind.
*   **Use cases:** Demonstrates application of GView in realistic scenarios.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of quantitative evaluation:** No benchmarks or performance comparisons with existing tools.
*   **Limited detail on LLM implementation:**  The specific methods for LLM integration are not described in detail.
*   **Overstated novelty:** The core architectural concepts are not entirely new within the field.
*   **Limited depth in some technical areas:**  For example, the description of the logic engine and its integration with GView could be more detailed. The description of prompt engineering lacks technical detail.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a reasonable contribution to the field of forensic analysis by combining existing techniques within a new framework and incorporating LLMs. However, the limited quantitative evaluation and lack of significant technical depth in certain areas temper its overall impact. While the concept is promising, the lack of detail on the novel aspects and missing empirical validation hinder a higher score.
The lack of technical details is noticeable especially when discussing LLMs since the models may behave differently if the prompt is designed slightly differently or based on the context provided, or even with different models (especially when closed-source, the results may vary a lot from the provided descriptions).

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Perception Before Reasoning: Two-Stage Reinforcement Learning for Visual Reasoning in Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13031v1)
- **Authors**: Yan Chen, Long Li, Teng Xi, Long Zeng, Jingdong Wang
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has proven highly effective in eliciting the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Inspired by this success, recent studies have explored applying similar techniques to vision-language models (VLMs), aiming to enhance their reasoning performance. However, directly transplanting RL methods from LLMs to VLMs is suboptimal, as the tasks faced by VLMs are inherently more complex. Specifically, VLMs must first accurately perceive and understand visual inputs before reasoning can be effectively performed. To address this challenge, we propose a two-stage reinforcement learning framework designed to jointly enhance both the perceptual and reasoning capabilities of VLMs. To mitigate the vanishing advantage issue commonly observed in RL training, we first perform dataset-level sampling to selectively strengthen specific capabilities using distinct data sources. During training, the first stage focuses on improving the model's visual perception through coarse- and fine-grained visual understanding, while the second stage targets the enhancement of reasoning abilities. After the proposed two-stage reinforcement learning process, we obtain PeBR-R1, a vision-language model with significantly enhanced perceptual and reasoning capabilities. Experimental results on seven benchmark datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach and validate the superior performance of PeBR-R1 across diverse visual reasoning tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Perception Before Reasoning: Two-Stage Reinforcement Learning for Visual Reasoning in Vision-Language Models" addresses the challenge of directly applying Reinforcement Learning (RL) techniques developed for Large Language Models (LLMs) to Vision-Language Models (VLMs).  The authors argue that VLMs require a stronger emphasis on visual perception *before* reasoning can be effectively improved via RL.  They propose a two-stage RL framework.  The first stage focuses on improving visual perception through coarse and fine-grained visual understanding, guided by carefully designed reward signals (CLIP score, keyword matching).  The second stage enhances reasoning abilities.  To mitigate vanishing gradients, they use a sample filtering mechanism (Easy/Medium/Hard cases) to focus RL training on examples most appropriate for each stage. Their model, PeBR-R1, achieves strong results on various visual reasoning benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its explicit focus on visual perception as a necessary precursor to effective RL-based reasoning enhancement in VLMs. While RL has been applied to VLMs before, this two-stage approach, prioritizing perception and using a sample filtering strategy to address vanishing advantages, represents a distinct contribution.  The use of FG-CLIP and keyword matching for fine-grained perception rewards is also a valuable component.

**Significance:** The work is significant because it acknowledges and addresses a crucial limitation of simply transferring LLM-centric RL techniques to the multimodal VLM domain. By emphasizing visual grounding and perception, the paper highlights the importance of multimodal understanding as a whole. The strong empirical results on a diverse set of benchmarks further solidify the significance of the approach.  The performance gains compared to existing open-source VLMs are notable, and the method's effectiveness in improving specific perceptual capabilities like object recognition and spatial reasoning adds practical value. The ablation studies provide a solid basis for understanding the contribution of the separate components of their proposed framework.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clearly defined problem:** The paper identifies a real issue in applying LLM RL techniques directly to VLMs.
*   **Well-motivated approach:** The two-stage design logically addresses the need for strong perceptual capabilities before reasoning can be effectively enhanced.
*   **Carefully designed reward signals:** The CLIP score and keyword matching provide a good balance between coarse and fine-grained visual understanding.
*   **Effective sample filtering:** The Easy/Medium/Hard case filtering is a smart way to address the vanishing gradient problem and ensure that RL focuses on the most informative examples for each stage.
*   **Strong empirical results:** The improvements across multiple benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework.
*   **Comprehensive ablations:** The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of the two-stage approach and the length penalty used during visual learning.
*   **Illustrative examples:**  The visualization with sample images and qualitative examples clarifies the approach and highlights its impact.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Teacher model:** While the use of Seed1.5-VL for teacher guided learning helps to identify critical areas, it can be a point of contention since the performance may be limited by capabilities of this model.
*   **Limited novelty in RL framework:** The core RL framework (GRPO) is adopted from prior work. The main contribution is in its application and adaptation for VLMs, specifically focusing on the visual perception aspect.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on multimodal RL, encouraging a more holistic approach that considers the specific challenges and requirements of VLMs.  It could also spur the development of new reward signals and training strategies specifically tailored for improving visual perception in multimodal models.
The performance improvement of the model is demonstrated across a diverse number of benchmarks.
**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8** for this paper.

*   The problem is well-defined and addresses a critical gap in the application of RL to VLMs.
*   The proposed two-stage framework is well-motivated, clearly explained, and supported by strong empirical results.
*   The novelty is good, particularly in the specific adaptation of RL for VLM perception and the sample filtering strategy.
*   The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

The main limitations are a lack of strong originality in the underlying RL framework, which diminishes the impact factor slightly.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### MIA-EPT: Membership Inference Attack via Error Prediction for Tabular Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13046v1)
- **Authors**: Eyal German, Daniel Samira, Yuval Elovici, Asaf Shabtai
- **Abstract**: Synthetic data generation plays an important role in enabling data sharing, particularly in sensitive domains like healthcare and finance. Recent advances in diffusion models have made it possible to generate realistic, high-quality tabular data, but they may also memorize training records and leak sensitive information. Membership inference attacks (MIAs) exploit this vulnerability by determining whether a record was used in training. While MIAs have been studied in images and text, their use against tabular diffusion models remains underexplored despite the unique risks of structured attributes and limited record diversity. In this paper, we introduce MIAEPT, Membership Inference Attack via Error Prediction for Tabular Data, a novel black-box attack specifically designed to target tabular diffusion models. MIA-EPT constructs errorbased feature vectors by masking and reconstructing attributes of target records, disclosing membership signals based on how well these attributes are predicted. MIA-EPT operates without access to the internal components of the generative model, relying only on its synthetic data output, and was shown to generalize across multiple state-of-the-art diffusion models. We validate MIA-EPT on three diffusion-based synthesizers, achieving AUC-ROC scores of up to 0.599 and TPR@10% FPR values of 22.0% in our internal tests. Under the MIDST 2025 competition conditions, MIA-EPT achieved second place in the Black-box Multi-Table track (TPR@10% FPR = 20.0%). These results demonstrate that our method can uncover substantial membership leakage in synthetic tabular data, challenging the assumption that synthetic data is inherently privacy-preserving. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/eyalgerman/MIA-EPT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "MIA-EPT: Membership Inference Attack via Error Prediction for Tabular Data" introduces a novel black-box membership inference attack (MIA) specifically designed for tabular data generated by diffusion models.  MIA-EPT operates by training attribute prediction models on synthetic data and using the prediction errors (error profiles) to distinguish between members and non-members of the diffusion model's training set. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of MIA-EPT on three state-of-the-art tabular diffusion models, achieving strong performance on both internal evaluations and the MIDST 2025 competition. The key finding is that even under strict black-box settings, tabular diffusion models are vulnerable to membership leakage, challenging the assumption that synthetic data is inherently privacy-preserving.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the adaptation of error-based membership inference techniques specifically for tabular data generated by diffusion models. While MIAs are not new, their application to tabular diffusion models and the exploitation of structural relationships within the data, particularly mixed-type attributes, is a valuable contribution. The idea of using attribute prediction models to create error profiles as a signal for membership is insightful. The black-box nature of the attack, requiring only access to the synthetic data, is also a practical and important feature.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its demonstration that tabular diffusion models, which are increasingly used for generating privacy-preserving synthetic data, are susceptible to membership leakage. This has important implications for organizations using these models, as it raises concerns about the privacy guarantees of synthetic tabular data. The paper's empirical results, especially the strong performance in the MIDST 2025 challenge, provide concrete evidence of this vulnerability. The detailed ablation study sheds light on which features are most effective in detecting membership, informing future defenses.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The attack is practical, requiring only black-box access.
    *   The methodology is clearly explained and well-motivated.
    *   The experiments are thorough and include comparisons to state-of-the-art baselines (where available).
    *   The authors participate in a public competition to benchmark their attack
    *   The ablation study provides valuable insights into the features that contribute to the attack's effectiveness.
    *   The method appears generalizable across various diffusion models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The comparison to other black-box MIAs targeting tabular models (especially those not specifically designed for diffusion models) is somewhat limited. The authors primarily compare against Wu et al. [35], but a broader comparison of general black-box MIAs in the tabular domain might be useful to better understand the paper's added value compared to generic tabular MIA.
    *   The paper does not provide specific methods of defending against MIA-EPT and doesn't evaluate any defenses, only hinting at the potential application of regularization, differential privacy etc.
    *   While the black box aspect is a strength, exploring how the attack could be enhanced with some knowledge or access to the model (e.g. architecture) would provide additional insight.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence the research community by raising awareness of the privacy risks associated with tabular diffusion models. The proposed attack and the insights gained from the experiments can serve as a benchmark for evaluating future privacy-enhancing techniques for synthetic tabular data generation. The paper could also prompt the development of new defense mechanisms to mitigate membership leakage in tabular diffusion models.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**  The paper offers a novel and significant contribution by demonstrating the vulnerability of tabular diffusion models to membership inference attacks under realistic black-box conditions. The practical nature of the attack, the strong experimental results (including the competition success), and the detailed analysis make it a valuable addition to the field. While it could benefit from a broader comparison to general tabular black-box MIAs and evaluation of some potential defense strategies, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, warranting a high score.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multi-Model Synthetic Training for Mission-Critical Small Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13047v1)
- **Authors**: Nolan Platt, Pragyansmita Nayak
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across many domains, yet their application to specialized fields remains constrained by the scarcity and complexity of domain-specific training data. We present a novel approach that achieves a 261x cost reduction for maritime intelligence by using LLMs as one-time teachers rather than using them directly for inference. Our method transforms 3.2 billion Automatic Identification System (AIS) vessel tracking records into 21,543 synthetic question and answer pairs through multi-model generation (GPT-4o and o3-mini), preventing overfitting and ensuring accurate reasoning. The resulting fine-tuned Qwen2.5-7B model achieves 75% accuracy on maritime tasks, while being substantially cheaper than using a larger model for inference. We show that smaller, cheaper models -- when fine tuned properly -- can provide similar accuracy compared to larger models that are prohibitively expensive. Our work contributes to the growing field of synthetic dataset generation for specialized AI applications and presents a highly reproducible framework for domains where manual annotation is infeasible. Beyond expanding research in the growing field of specialized small language models, our approach has immediate applications in maritime safety, security operations, and vessel traffic management systems in various industries.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a novel approach to training small language models (SLMs) for mission-critical applications, specifically maritime intelligence.  Instead of relying on expensive, continuous inference from large language models (LLMs) or costly manual annotation, the authors propose using LLMs as "one-time teachers" to generate synthetic training data from raw Automatic Identification System (AIS) vessel tracking data. They use a multi-model generation strategy (GPT-4o and o3-mini) to create a synthetic dataset, which is then used to fine-tune a smaller, cheaper Qwen2.5-7B model.  The fine-tuned SLM achieves comparable accuracy to LLMs on maritime tasks while significantly reducing inference costs. The authors emphasize the reproducibility of their framework and its potential for deploying cost-effective maritime intelligence systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core idea of using LLMs as one-time teachers for synthetic data generation to train SLMs is not entirely novel in isolation. However, the specific application to the maritime domain, the comprehensive approach to data sampling and synthetic data generation, the careful attention to preventing overfitting through a multi-model approach, and the focus on creating a *reproducible* framework *in a real-world mission-critical domain* represent a significant contribution. The demonstration of a *substantial* cost reduction (261x) while *maintaining acceptable accuracy* adds considerable practical value.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in the deployment of AI for specialized domains: the cost of inference from large models and the difficulty of creating high-quality training data. By demonstrating that SLMs can achieve comparable performance at a fraction of the cost, the authors open up new possibilities for deploying AI in resource-constrained environments. The maritime intelligence application is particularly relevant, as it has implications for safety, security, and efficiency. The provision of a public dataset and the demonstrated reproducibility further enhance the paper's impact and potential for driving future research. The analysis of why common NLP metrics failed is also a valuable contribution, highlighting the need for application-specific evaluation methodologies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical focus:** The paper addresses a concrete, real-world problem with a practical, deployable solution.
    *   **Reproducibility:**  The emphasis on reproducibility and the public availability of the dataset and code are strong points.
    *   **Comprehensive methodology:**  The paper provides a detailed description of the data sampling, synthetic data generation, fine-tuning, and evaluation processes.
    *   **Cost-effectiveness:**  The demonstrated cost reduction is a compelling argument for the proposed approach.
    *   **Addressing Overfitting:** The explicit discussion and mitigation strategies to prevent overfitting are commendable.
    *   **Clear evaluation:** The paper presents a clear and thorough evaluation of the model's performance, including both automated and manual assessments.  The confidence intervals are also important.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Generalizability:** While the method is successful for maritime intelligence, the extent to which it can be generalized to other specialized domains without significant modifications is not fully explored. There's mention of the method benefitting domains where data is abundant and expertise/compute is limited, which is helpful, but the boundaries of where this is most effective could be more precise.
    *   **Temporal Degradation and Robustness:** The limitations section discusses temporal degradation and the potential for AIS manipulation to circumvent the model. Future work should more thoroughly investigate the trade-off between complexity and robustness to malicious attacks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a useful and timely solution for specialized AI applications. While the foundational idea of synthetic data generation and SLM fine-tuning is not entirely new, the *specific application*, the *thorough methodology*, the *emphasis on reproducibility*, the *explicit treatment of preventing overfitting,* the *detailed real-world impact*, and the *demonstrated cost-effectiveness* warrant a high score.  The paper provides a significant step forward in making AI accessible to organizations with limited resources.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Automating Code Generation for Semiconductor Equipment Control from Developer Utterances with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13055v1)
- **Authors**: Youngkyoung Kim, Sanghyeok Park, Misoo Kim, Gangho Yoon, Eunseok Lee, Simon S. Woo
- **Abstract**: Semiconductors form the backbone of modern electronics, with their manufacturing and testing relying on highly specialized equipment and domain-specific programming languages. Equipment languages such as the Algorithmic Pattern Generator (ALPG) are critical for precise hardware control but are challenging to program due to their low-level syntax and steep learning curve. While large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in generating high-level code from natural language, their effectiveness on low-level equipment languages remains limited. To address this, we propose Progressive Knowledge Enhancement (PKE), a novel multi-stage prompting framework that progressively extracts and activates the latent knowledge within LLMs, guiding them from simple to complex examples without extensive fine-tuning. Empirical evaluation on an industrial ALPG dataset shows that PKE significantly outperforms standard prompting and surpasses state-of-the-art methods in generating correct ALPG code, achieving 11.1\% and 15.2\% higher exact match scores compared to the second-best technique. Further analysis of individual components confirms that progressive knowledge extraction based on difficulty enhances accuracy. Our study offer a practical approach to boosting LLM capabilities for specialized low-level programming, supporting greater productivity in semiconductor software development.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of automating code generation for semiconductor equipment control using low-level, domain-specific languages like ALPG (Algorithmic Pattern Generator). It notes that while Large Language Models (LLMs) are effective at generating high-level code, their performance suffers when applied to these specialized languages due to limited training data and the complexity of the languages. The authors propose a novel prompting framework called Progressive Knowledge Enhancement (PKE). PKE involves three key stages: (1) LLM-based difficulty annotation of training examples, (2) progressive background knowledge construction from easy to hard examples, and (3) guided code generation using the enhanced knowledge.  Experiments on an industrial ALPG dataset demonstrate that PKE significantly outperforms standard prompting and even adapted state-of-the-art methods, achieving higher exact match scores in code generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel prompting framework (PKE) tailored for low-resource, domain-specific languages, which is a valuable contribution. The idea of progressively building knowledge in LLMs by guiding them from simple to complex examples is inspired by human learning principles and curriculum learning, adding a layer of theoretical grounding. While the concept of curriculum learning isn't brand new, its specific application within prompting techniques for low-level code generation is novel.  The combination of difficulty annotation with similarity-based retrieval is also a nuanced approach.
* **Significance:**  The problem addressed is practically significant. Semiconductor equipment control is a critical and complex area. Automating code generation in this domain can lead to significant productivity gains and reduce the learning curve for new developers. The experimental results, showing substantial improvements over baselines and adapted state-of-the-art methods, convincingly demonstrate the potential impact of PKE. The use of an industrial dataset adds to the real-world applicability of the research. However, the in-house nature of LLM and the non-public access to the dataset limit the replicability and generalizability to other settings.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of applying LLMs to low-level, domain-specific code generation.
    * **Well-Designed Framework:** PKE is a well-structured and intuitive prompting approach.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of PKE. The ablation studies and analysis of individual components provide insights into the framework's performance.
    * **Practical Relevance:** The work has direct applications in the semiconductor industry and other domains with specialized programming languages.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Generalizability:**  The use of in-house LLMs and a non-public dataset raises concerns about the generalizability of the findings to other LLMs and domains.  It would be stronger if the authors could demonstrate the effectiveness of PKE using publicly available LLMs (e.g., open-source models) on a similar task.
    * **Dataset Details:** Providing more detailed characteristics about the ALPG dataset would enhance the reproducibility of the results. What specific types of instructions do the NL-ALPG pairs cover? How large is the dataset of NL-ALPG pairs used for few-shot learning and knowledge enhancement?
    * **SoTA Baselines Adaptation:** Though SoTA baselines were adapted, more details on the adaptation for ALPG code generation are necessary.
    * **Lack of a detailed cost-benefit analysis:** While the paper demonstrates performance improvements, it lacks a detailed cost-benefit analysis, especially regarding the effort required for LLM-driven difficulty annotation.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in several areas:
    * **Prompt Engineering:** It provides a valuable case study on designing effective prompts for specialized domains.
    * **Low-Resource Code Generation:** It offers a practical approach for improving LLM performance in low-resource settings.
    * **Curriculum Learning for LLMs:** It demonstrates the benefits of applying curriculum learning principles to LLM prompting.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the paper's novelty in applying progressive knowledge enhancement through prompting for low-level code generation, strong empirical results, and potential practical impact, but taking into account limitations of generalizability due to using a in-house LLMs and dataset and missing detailed cost-benefit analysis, a score of 7 is justified. The work is a significant advancement in automating code generation for specialized domains but needs further validation with publicly available LLMs and detailed cost-benefit analysis to demonstrate broader applicability.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Shaping Explanations: Semantic Reward Modeling with Encoder-Only Transformers for GRPO
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13081v1)
- **Authors**: Francesco Pappone, Ruggero Marino Lazzaroni, Federico Califano, Niccolò Gentile, Roberto Marras
- **Abstract**: While Large Language Models (LLMs) excel at generating human-like text, aligning their outputs with complex, qualitative goals like pedagogical soundness remains a significant challenge. Standard reinforcement learning techniques often rely on slow and expensive LLM-as-a-judge evaluations or on brittle, keyword-based metrics like ROUGE, which fail to capture the semantic essence of a high-quality explanation. In this work, we introduce a novel approach to reward shaping within the Group Relative Policy Optimisation (GRPO) framework. Our central contribution is the use of a small, efficient encoder-only transformer as a semantic reward model. This model provides a dense, semantically rich reward signal based on the cosine similarity between a generated explanation and a ground-truth reference, guiding the policy towards explanations that are not just factually correct but also structurally and conceptually aligned with expert reasoning. We apply this method to the task of training a model for the Italian medical-school entrance examinations, following standard domain-adaptive continued pre-training (CPT) and supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Our results demonstrate that GRPO with our proposed semantic reward significantly improves explanation faithfulness and clarity over a strong SFT baseline, showcasing the power of using lightweight encoder models for nuanced reward shaping in complex generation tasks
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel reward shaping methodology within the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) framework to improve the quality of explanations generated by Large Language Models (LLMs).  The core idea is to use a small, efficient, encoder-only transformer model as a semantic reward model. This model computes a dense, semantically-grounded reward signal based on the cosine similarity between the embeddings of generated explanations and ground-truth references. This approach aims to guide the policy towards explanations that are not just factually correct, but also structurally and conceptually aligned with expert reasoning. The method is applied to training a model for the Italian medical-school entrance examinations. The results show significant improvements in explanation faithfulness and clarity compared to a strong supervised fine-tuning (SFT) baseline. The paper also includes ablation studies on the use of the Muon optimizer and a cross-benchmark comparison to assess out-of-domain reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques: using a lightweight, encoder-only transformer for *semantic reward modeling within the GRPO framework* to improve the quality of *pedagogical explanations*. While encoder-only models have been used as reward models before (e.g., with BERTScore in summarization), the application to high-stakes explanation generation and the specific focus on semantic similarity (rather than simple keyword overlap) for pedagogical quality is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a significant challenge in LLM alignment: ensuring that explanations are not only plausible but also factually correct and conceptually sound. The proposed method provides a practical and efficient alternative to expensive LLM-as-a-judge evaluations or brittle keyword-based metrics. Improving explanation quality in high-stakes domains like medical education is directly relevant. The result contributes to the line of works investigating alternatives to LLM-as-a-judge and explores ways to reduce the computational overhead of RLHF.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem and the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   The proposed method is well-motivated and technically sound.
    *   The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of the semantic reward shaping approach.
    *   Ablation studies provide useful insights into the contribution of different components.
    *   Cross-benchmark comparison shows the generalizability of the approach.
    *   The code and data are publicly available, which promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The encoder model architecture is not fully defined (what's the exact encoder? The paper indicates using qwen3-0.6B [31], but a more specific definition would be beneficial). This is mitigated by referencing to a specific implementation that is readily available.
    *   The improvement, while significant, is still bounded by the quality of the training data, particularly the ground-truth explanations. Further work could explore ways to generate or refine reference explanations.
    *   The study is limited to one specific domain (Italian medical-school entrance exams). While this allows for a focused evaluation, it would be beneficial to explore the generalizability of the approach to other domains and languages. The paper already shows its capability to solve out-of-domain tasks, yet the reward model is tailored to a specific task. A more general reward model would improve generalisability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in LLM alignment, reward modeling, and explanation generation. The proposed method is relatively simple to implement and can be readily applied to other domains. The use of lightweight encoder models for reward shaping could be a promising direction for future research.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and effective method for improving LLM explanation quality. The approach is well-motivated, technically sound, and empirically validated. The results demonstrate significant improvements over existing approaches. While the study is limited to a specific domain and there is room for improvement in terms of the reward architecture, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM alignment and has the potential to influence future research.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Empowering LLMs with Parameterized Skills for Adversarial Long-Horizon Planning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13127v1)
- **Authors**: Sijia Cui, Shuai Xu, Aiyao He, Yanna Wang, Bo Xu
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models(LLMs) have led to the development of LLM-based AI agents. A key challenge is the creation of agents that can effectively ground themselves in complex, adversarial long-horizon environments. Existing methods mainly focus on (1) using LLMs as policies to interact with the environment through generating low-level feasible actions, and (2) utilizing LLMs to generate high-level tasks or language guides to stimulate action generation. However, the former struggles to generate reliable actions, while the latter relies heavily on expert experience to translate high-level tasks into specific action sequences. To address these challenges, we introduce the Plan with Language, Act with Parameter (PLAP) planning framework that facilitates the grounding of LLM-based agents in long-horizon environments. The PLAP method comprises three key components: (1) a skill library containing environment-specific parameterized skills, (2) a skill planner powered by LLMs, and (3) a skill executor converting the parameterized skills into executable action sequences. We implement PLAP in MicroRTS, a long-horizon real-time strategy game that provides an unfamiliar and challenging environment for LLMs. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of PLAP. In particular, GPT-4o-driven PLAP in a zero-shot setting outperforms 80% of baseline agents, and Qwen2-72B-driven PLAP, with carefully crafted few-shot examples, surpasses the top-tier scripted agent, CoacAI. Additionally, we design comprehensive evaluation metrics and test 6 closed-source and 2 open-source LLMs within the PLAP framework, ultimately releasing an LLM leaderboard ranking long-horizon skill planning ability. Our code is available at https://github.com/AI-Research-TeamX/PLAP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Empowering LLMs with Parameterized Skills for Adversarial Long-Horizon Planning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PLAP (Plan with Language, Act with Parameter), a novel framework for grounding Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents in complex, adversarial long-horizon environments. PLAP combines a skill library of environment-specific parameterized skills, an LLM-powered skill planner, and a skill executor that translates parameterized skills into executable actions. The framework is evaluated in MicroRTS, a challenging real-time strategy game. The results demonstrate that PLAP improves the performance of LLM agents, even in zero-shot settings, and surpasses top-tier scripted agents with carefully crafted few-shot examples.  The authors also introduce Skill-RTS, a benchmark designed to evaluate the skill planning abilities of LLMs in a long-horizon setting and release an LLM leaderboard ranking long-horizon skill planning capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific combination of parameterized skills with LLMs for long-horizon planning, particularly in adversarial environments. Existing methods often rely on either directly generating low-level actions (which can be unreliable) or translating high-level tasks (which often requires expert knowledge). PLAP attempts to bridge this gap by using parameterized skills as an intermediate representation.  The introduction of Skill-RTS as a dedicated benchmark for LLM skill planning is also a valuable contribution, although RTS games have been used for LLM evaluation before, a dedicated benchmark focused on skills is new. The idea of using parameterized skills isn't completely new, existing in robotics, but the integration with language models and the application to an adversarial RTS setting is a novel aspect.

**Significance:** The work has potential significance for several reasons:

*   **Addressing a Key Challenge:** Grounding LLMs in complex environments is a major hurdle in developing practical AI agents. PLAP offers a promising approach to tackle this.
*   **Improved Performance:** The experimental results show tangible improvements in performance compared to baselines, indicating that PLAP is a viable strategy. The benchmark helps in understanding which LLMs are suited for skill-based planning.
*   **Skill-RTS Benchmark:** The released Skill-RTS benchmark could facilitate future research by providing a standardized evaluation platform for LLMs in long-horizon skill planning.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of long-horizon planning with LLMs and identifies the limitations of existing approaches.
*   **Well-Defined Framework:** PLAP is a well-structured framework with clear components and a logical workflow.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments in MicroRTS provide evidence for the effectiveness of PLAP.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation Metrics:** The authors define comprehensive evaluation metrics to assess the LLMs' skill planning capabilities.
*   **Reproducibility:** The code release encourages reproducibility and further research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is primarily confined to MicroRTS, which may not fully represent the complexities of real-world scenarios. Further validation is needed in more diverse and realistic environments. The design of Parameterized Skills is also manually designed which can limit generalization.
*   **Manual Skill Design:** The reliance on manually designed skill functions limits the scalability and adaptability of PLAP. Automatically generating or learning these skills would be a significant improvement.
*   **Lack of Opponent Modeling:** The current version of PLAP doesn't explicitly model the opponent's strategy, which could limit its performance in highly competitive settings. Although opponent behaviour is sensed through its actions.
*   **Limited Novelty in Core Ideas:** While the integration is novel, the underlying concepts of parameterized skills and LLM-based planning have been explored previously.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7.  While the paper does not present completely revolutionary ideas, it makes a significant contribution by effectively integrating existing concepts (parameterized skills and LLMs) into a novel framework designed for a challenging problem.  The PLAP framework demonstrates tangible performance improvements and offers a practical approach to grounding LLMs in adversarial long-horizon environments. The Skill-RTS benchmark is a valuable resource for the research community. However, the manual skill design and limited scope of the evaluation are weaknesses that need to be addressed in future work. A higher score would be warranted if the framework could automatically generate skills or if a more realistic and broad evaluation had been performed.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reasoning with Preference Constraints: A Benchmark for Language Models in Many-to-One Matching Markets
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13131v1)
- **Authors**: Marylou Fauchard, Florian Carichon, Margarida Carvalho, Golnoosh Farnadi
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in reasoning with large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong performance on complex mathematical tasks, including combinatorial optimization. Techniques such as Chain-of-Thought and In-Context Learning have further enhanced this capability, making LLMs both powerful and accessible tools for a wide range of users, including non-experts. However, applying LLMs to matching problems, which require reasoning under preferential and structural constraints, remains underexplored. To address this gap, we introduce a novel benchmark of 369 instances of the College Admission Problem, a canonical example of a matching problem with preferences, to evaluate LLMs across key dimensions: feasibility, stability, and optimality. We employ this benchmark to assess the performance of several open-weight LLMs. Our results first reveal that while LLMs can satisfy certain constraints, they struggle to meet all evaluation criteria consistently. They also show that reasoning LLMs, like QwQ and GPT-oss, significantly outperform traditional models such as Llama, Qwen or Mistral, defined here as models used without any dedicated reasoning mechanisms. Moreover, we observed that LLMs reacted differently to the various prompting strategies tested, which include Chain-of-Thought, In-Context Learning and role-based prompting, with no prompt consistently offering the best performance. Finally, we report the performances from iterative prompting with auto-generated feedback and show that they are not monotonic; they can peak early and then significantly decline in later attempts. Overall, this work offers a new perspective on model reasoning performance and the effectiveness of prompting strategies in combinatorial optimization problems with preferential constraints.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel benchmark for evaluating the reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in the context of many-to-one matching markets, specifically the College Admission Problem. The authors create a synthetic dataset of 369 instances with varying numbers of students, college capacities, and student preferences. They then evaluate the performance of several open-weight LLMs (Llama, Qwen, Mistral, and GPT-oss) under different prompting strategies, including Chain-of-Thought (CoT), In-Context Learning (ICL), and iterative prompting. The LLMs are assessed on their ability to generate solutions that are feasible, stable, and student-optimal. The results highlight that while LLMs can satisfy some constraints, they struggle to consistently meet all evaluation criteria. The study also shows that reasoning-focused LLMs generally outperform traditional models, and that the effectiveness of different prompting strategies varies across models. Finally, the authors report on the non-monotonic behavior of iterative prompting, where performance can decline after initial improvements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty of this paper lies in the creation of a tailored benchmark dataset specifically designed to test LLMs' reasoning capabilities in the context of many-to-one matching problems, which is previously underexplored for LLM evaluation. While the College Admission Problem itself is well-established, its use as a controlled, synthetic benchmark for LLM reasoning is a valuable contribution. The paper explicitly fills a gap in existing benchmarks that typically focus on cost minimization, and provides a valuable dataset that allow precise evaluation under several important constraints.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its investigation of LLMs' ability to reason under preferential and structural constraints in combinatorial optimization problems. This area is relevant to real-world applications like resource allocation, scheduling, and personalized recommendations, highlighting the importance of LLMs that can do well. The results also contribute to a better understanding of the strengths and limitations of different prompting strategies in complex reasoning tasks. The negative results for iterative prompting are significant and question the effectiveness of the self-correction process with current models. The insights into the interplay between model architecture, training paradigm, and prompting effectiveness are also valuable for the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The College Admission Problem provides a clear and well-understood framework for evaluating LLM reasoning.
    *   **Controlled benchmark:** The synthetic dataset allows for systematic experimentation with different problem parameters.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper assesses multiple LLMs and prompting strategies across key dimensions (feasibility, stability, optimality).
    *   **Interesting Findings:** The finding about the limitations of iterative prompting and the lack of a universally optimal prompting strategy are thought-provoking.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic data:** The use of synthetic data, while providing control, limits the generalizability of the results to real-world scenarios where data may be more noisy and complex.
    *   **Limited model scope:** The paper focuses primarily on open-weight LLMs. Including larger, proprietary models (if resources allowed) could have provided a broader perspective.
    *   **Limited iterative prompting:** The paper uses external feedback which is reliable. However, AI-generated feedback has also been explored and it would have been interesting to analyse and compare the two approches.

*   **Potential Influence:** The benchmark dataset and the insights gained from this study have the potential to influence future research in several ways:

    *   The benchmark dataset can be used to evaluate new LLMs and prompting strategies.
    *   The results can inform the development of more effective prompting techniques for complex reasoning tasks.
    *   The study can inspire further research on the application of LLMs to real-world optimization problems with preferential constraints.

Overall, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by introducing a novel benchmark dataset and providing insights into the reasoning abilities of LLMs in the context of many-to-one matching markets. While it has some limitations, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, and its findings have the potential to influence future research in this area.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper is a strong contribution, clearly addressing an important but under-explored problem: evaluating LLM reasoning under complex structural and preferential constraints. The creation of the benchmark is significant. It would be even more compelling with real-world data and a broader scope. The current benchmark can enable future research, leading to a greater impact.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### An Uncertainty-Weighted Decision Transformer for Navigation in Dense, Complex Driving Scenarios
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13132v1)
- **Authors**: Zhihao Zhang, Chengyang Peng, Minghao Zhu, Ekim Yurtsever, Keith A. Redmill
- **Abstract**: Autonomous driving in dense, dynamic environments requires decision-making systems that can exploit both spatial structure and long-horizon temporal dependencies while remaining robust to uncertainty. This work presents a novel framework that integrates multi-channel bird's-eye-view occupancy grids with transformer-based sequence modeling for tactical driving in complex roundabout scenarios. To address the imbalance between frequent low-risk states and rare safety-critical decisions, we propose the Uncertainty-Weighted Decision Transformer (UWDT). UWDT employs a frozen teacher transformer to estimate per-token predictive entropy, which is then used as a weight in the student model's loss function. This mechanism amplifies learning from uncertain, high-impact states while maintaining stability across common low-risk transitions. Experiments in a roundabout simulator, across varying traffic densities, show that UWDT consistently outperforms other baselines in terms of reward, collision rate, and behavioral stability. The results demonstrate that uncertainty-aware, spatial-temporal transformers can deliver safer and more efficient decision-making for autonomous driving in complex traffic environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Uncertainty-Weighted Decision Transformer (UWDT), a novel offline reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to improve autonomous driving in complex, dense environments like roundabouts. UWDT integrates multi-channel bird's-eye-view occupancy grids for spatial representation with a transformer-based sequence model to capture long-horizon temporal dependencies.  The key innovation lies in using a frozen "teacher" Decision Transformer (DT) to estimate per-token predictive entropy, which is then used as a weight in the "student" DT's loss function. This re-weighting mechanism amplifies learning from uncertain, high-impact states (e.g., near-collision scenarios) while maintaining stability during common, low-risk transitions. The authors demonstrate, through simulations, that UWDT outperforms baseline methods in terms of reward, collision rate, and behavioral stability in complex roundabout scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addressing a critical challenge:** The paper directly tackles the problem of robust decision-making in autonomous driving under uncertainty, particularly the challenge of imbalanced datasets where rare, critical events are under-represented.
    * **Novelty in approach:** The integration of uncertainty weighting into a Decision Transformer framework is a significant contribution.  While uncertainty estimation and re-weighting are not entirely new concepts, their specific application within the DT architecture for tactical driving appears novel. The "teacher-student" approach for uncertainty estimation is also well-motivated and practical.
    * **Comprehensive evaluation:** The authors present a thorough experimental evaluation using a realistic roundabout simulation. The comparison against multiple baselines (CQL, SAC, BC Transformer, DT) provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of UWDT.  The analysis of velocity and reward profiles under varying traffic densities offers valuable insights into the agents' behaviors.
    * **Practical implications:** The framework is designed for offline RL, which avoids the safety concerns associated with online exploration. The results suggest that UWDT could lead to safer and more efficient autonomous driving systems in complex urban environments.
    * **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and experimental setup.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Scenario specificity:** While the roundabout scenario is complex, the results' generalizability to other driving scenarios is not fully established.  The gains might be specific to the particular dynamics and constraints of roundabouts.
    * **Teacher DT dependence:** The performance of UWDT relies heavily on the quality of the frozen teacher DT. The paper does not fully explore the sensitivity of UWDT to different teacher DT architectures or training datasets. It's not clearly stated how the quality of the teacher model affects the student model.
    * **Computational cost:** Although the paper mentions benefits in terms of complexity as number of vehicles increases, it does not mention that training with this approach does require training two models (teacher and student). Also, how does this model perform in a real world environment? Would the performance be the same with the added variables?
    * **Limited discussion of limitations:** The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the limitations of UWDT and potential areas for future work.  For example, how does UWDT handle adversarial attacks or sensor failures?

* **Novelty and Significance:**
    * The combination of uncertainty-aware training with Decision Transformers for autonomous driving represents a non-trivial advancement in the field.  It effectively addresses the imbalance problem in offline RL and improves the robustness of driving policies. The potential influence on the development of safer and more efficient autonomous driving systems is significant.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7** to this paper. The core idea of integrating uncertainty weighting into the Decision Transformer framework for autonomous driving shows genuine novelty, and the detailed experimental evaluation bolsters the claim that this improves driving performance and the system's tolerance for the uncertainty.

However, the paper could be improved by further exploring the generalizability of the approach to diverse driving scenarios, and more carefully analyzing the sensitivity of the UWDT to teacher DT performance. Also, further analyzing the algorithm's limits and more clearly defining the areas for future study, such as computational expenses, adversarial attacks, or sensor defects, would be beneficial. Further exploring these issues would make the model more complete and usable.
