### Towards the Next Generation of Software: Insights from Grey Literature on AI-Native Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13144v1)
- **Authors**: Lingli Cao, Shanshan Li, Ying Fan, Danyang Li, Chenxing Zhong
- **Abstract**: Background: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) has given rise to AI-native applications, a new paradigm in software engineering that fundamentally redefines how software is designed, developed, and evolved. Despite their growing prominence, AI-native applications still lack a unified engineering definition and architectural blueprint, leaving practitioners without systematic guidance for system design, quality assurance, and technology selection. Objective: This study seeks to establish a comprehensive understanding of AI-native applications by identifying their defining characteristics, key quality attributes, and typical technology stacks, as well as by clarifying the opportunities and challenges they present. Method: We conducted a grey literature review, integrating conceptual perspectives retrieved from targeted Google and Bing searches with practical insights derived from leading open-source projects on GitHub. A structured protocol encompassing source selection, quality assessment, and thematic analysis was applied to synthesize findings across heterogeneous sources. Results: We finally identified 106 studies based on the selection criteria. The analysis reveals that AI-native applications are distinguished by two core pillars: the central role of AI as the system's intelligence paradigm and their inherently probabilistic, non-deterministic nature. Critical quality attributes include reliability, usability, performance efficiency, and AI-specific observability. In addition, a typical technology stack has begun to emerge, comprising LLM orchestration frameworks, vector databases, and AI-native observability platforms. These systems emphasize response quality, cost-effectiveness, and outcome predictability, setting them apart from conventional software systems. Conclusion: This study is the first to propose a dual-layered engineering blueprint...
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a grey literature review of AI-native applications. The authors aim to establish a comprehensive understanding of this emerging software engineering paradigm by identifying key characteristics, quality attributes, typical technology stacks, opportunities, and challenges. They analyze 106 sources from industry reports, technical blogs, and open-source projects.  The authors propose a dual-layered engineering blueprint for AI-native applications that offers design guidelines and technical recommendations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   **Strengths:** The paper addresses a timely and important gap in the software engineering literature. AI-native applications represent a significant shift in how software is designed, developed, and evolved, but a clear engineering definition and architectural blueprint are lacking. The grey literature review approach is valuable as it captures industry trends and practical experiences not yet formalized in academic publications.  The dual-layered blueprint, if well-received and adopted, could provide much-needed guidance to practitioners. The emphasis on areas like observability and economic efficiency is also novel. The systematic methodology for GLR in this context also adds some value.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The reliance on grey literature is both a strength and a weakness. While it captures current trends, it also introduces potential biases (e.g., overemphasis on positive aspects, lack of peer review). The study's conclusions are limited by the quality and representativeness of the selected grey literature.  The proposed blueprint, while potentially useful, needs further validation through case studies and empirical evaluations to demonstrate its effectiveness in real-world projects.  The distinction between "AI-assisted" and "AI-native" could be further refined and sharpened with more rigorous criteria. Also, some of the characteristics outlined, such as Scalability and Security, are very general and could be applied to any software paradigm; more focus on attributes *unique* to AI-native apps would be beneficial. Furthermore, the paper doesn't offer any *new* solutions or technologies; it mainly synthesizes and categorizes existing information. The contributions, although beneficial, are more descriptive than prescriptive.
    *   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research and practice in AI-native software engineering. By providing a framework for understanding and designing these applications, it could foster more systematic development efforts and promote the reuse of best practices. The identification of key challenges can guide future research towards developing solutions for issues like observability and economic efficiency.

*   **Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** for this paper.

*   The paper has adequate novelty because, although the core technologies are well-known, the paper consolidates information on the *applications* of those technologies in a novel way. The rigorous approach to GLR and well-defined characteristics, QAs, and tech stacks increase the value.
*   This paper addresses a vital area: the need for engineering standards and guides around new software architecture and technologies. The identification of potential risks and their consequences creates greater awareness and provides avenues to overcome issues.
*   The use of grey literature creates inherent uncertainty about the overall truthfulness of the claims since they aren't typically peer-reviewed. Also, the lack of evaluation of their proposed architectural blueprint is an issue; its ultimate utility has yet to be confirmed. Thus, the value is limited.
*   Thus, the paper has sufficient value to guide future development and research but is lacking the validation needed to bring it to a higher level.

### UTI-LLM: A Personalized Articulatory-Speech Therapy Assistance System Based on Multimodal Large Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13145v1)
- **Authors**: Yudong Yang, Xiaokang Liu, Shaofeng zhao, Rongfeng Su, Nan Yan, Lan Wang
- **Abstract**: Speech therapy plays a critical role in training speech disorders caused by neurological impairments such as stroke. However, traditional manual and computer-assisted systems are limited in real-time accessibility and articulatory motion feedback, constraining their practical utility. Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated significant potential in healthcare, particularly through their ability to integrate multimodal data for adaptive assessment and therapeutic feedback. Nevertheless, challenges including insufficient acquisition and fusion of articulatory information, inadequate parsing of articulatory organ motion trajectories, and the scarcity of high-quality domain-specific datasets hinder the application of MLLMs in speech therapy. To address these limitations, we propose an MLLM-based speech rehabilitation assistance system that synergistically leverages ultrasound tongue imaging and speech signals to deliver precise, interactive articulatory feedback. We construct a high-quality domain-specific dataset comprising UTI-speech dialogue pairs. This dataset facilitates fine-tuning to enhance the model's clinical adaptability. Building on this dataset, our methods achieves spatiotemporal fusion training strategy of ultrasound videos and speech signals, enabling fine-grained articulatory impairment analysis and ultimately generating actionable feedback.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UTI-LLM, a novel system designed to assist in personalized articulatory-speech therapy. It leverages a multimodal large language model (MLLM) that integrates ultrasound tongue imaging (UTI) and speech signals to provide precise, interactive feedback on articulatory movements. The core contributions include:

1.  **A high-quality UTI-speech dialogue dataset:** This dataset comprises UTI and speech data paired with relevant dialogue for speech rehabilitation, enabling fine-tuning of the MLLM.
2.  **An UTI-LLM architecture:** A specific MLLM architecture is proposed that effectively processes both UTI and speech signals to offer personalized therapeutic recommendations.
3.  **A spatiotemporal fusion training strategy:** This approach enables fine-grained analysis of articulatory impairments by fusing ultrasound videos and speech signals, leading to actionable feedback.

The system aims to address limitations in existing speech therapy approaches by providing real-time, accessible feedback on articulatory motion, thereby enhancing the utility of computer-assisted rehabilitation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel approach by combining ultrasound tongue imaging with multimodal large language models for speech therapy. While MLLMs have been used in healthcare, the specific application to speech rehabilitation, particularly with UTI data, appears to be a unique contribution. The creation of a specialized dataset for this purpose is also a significant step.
*   **Significance:** Speech disorders are a significant healthcare challenge, and the limitations of current therapy options highlight the need for innovative solutions. If successful, this approach could potentially improve patient outcomes by offering accessible, personalized, and real-time feedback during therapy. The integration of multimodal data for a more comprehensive assessment of speech impairments is also a valuable direction.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a Real-World Problem:** The paper directly tackles the shortage of speech therapists and the need for objective, personalized rehabilitation tools.
    *   **Multimodal Integration:**  The use of both UTI and speech signals allows for a more holistic understanding of articulatory patterns.
    *   **Dataset Creation:** The construction of a specific dataset is a crucial step toward bridging the gap in available training data for MLLMs in speech therapy.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation includes multiple aspects like accuracy, expert evaluation and module ablation studies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited details on the prompt engineering** More details would be helpful regarding how the prompt was designed to guide the models to provide useful information.
    *   **Generalizability to other languages/accents:** The research focuses on the use of mandarin speech data. It would be useful to see if these techniques can be adapted to improve rehabilitation for other dialects and languages as well.
    *   **Translation to real application:** The research does not detail how it would be applied in real life. Although it is still on an early stage, it will be helpful to provide more insights on this aspect.
    *   **Comparison with other methods:** The experiments compare with other multimodal large language models. It will be helpful to compare against more traditional speech recognition techniques.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by demonstrating the application of MLLMs to speech rehabilitation, encouraging further research in this direction. The created dataset could also serve as a valuable resource for other researchers in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates promising results and offers a novel approach to a significant healthcare challenge. The specific integration of ultrasound imaging with an MLLM, and the creation of a specialized dataset, contribute meaningfully to the field. However, some important experimental validations and details need to be provided. The limited experiment and analysis sections hold back the overall score.

### Can Large Audio Language Models Understand Audio Well? Speech, Scene and Events Understanding Benchmark for LALMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13148v1)
- **Authors**: Han Yin, Jung-Woo Choi
- **Abstract**: Recently, Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) have progressed rapidly, demonstrating their strong efficacy in universal audio understanding through cross-modal integration. To evaluate the LALM's audio understanding performance, researchers have proposed different benchmarks. However, key aspects for real-world interactions are underexplored in existing benchmarks, i.e., audio signals typically contain both speech and non-speech components, and energy levels of these components can vary significantly across different scenarios. Moreover, most benchmarks do not consider the joint understanding of speech, scene, and events within the same audio clip. In this work, we introduce SSEU-Bench, the first versatile audio understanding benchmark that explicitly accounts for energy differences between speech and non-speech audio, with both independent and joint understanding settings for speech, scene, and events. Furthermore, we demonstrate that some LALMs tend to underperform on certain tasks in a joint understanding setting. To address this issue, we introduce Chain-of-Thought, which effectively improves the LALM's joint audio understanding performance by decomposing complex tasks into simpler reasoning steps
- **Summary**: Okay, I have analyzed the provided document. Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SSEU-Bench, a novel benchmark for evaluating Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) in realistic audio understanding scenarios.  SSEU-Bench focuses on the joint understanding of speech, scene, and events within audio clips, explicitly accounting for energy differences between speech and non-speech audio components via Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) control. The benchmark includes three tasks: Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR), Acoustic Scene Classification (ASC), and Audio Tagging (AT). The paper also proposes a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) guided inference method to improve LALMs' joint understanding capabilities by breaking down complex tasks into simpler reasoning steps.  The paper presents experimental results using several LALMs and CLAP-based models, demonstrating the challenges and potential improvements in joint audio understanding. The authors make their data and code publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of SSEU-Bench, which is the *first* benchmark to explicitly address the joint understanding of speech, scene, and events in audio while controlling for SNR variations. This focus on realistic acoustic environments is a clear improvement over existing benchmarks that often treat these aspects in isolation. The integration of a CoT approach to improve joint understanding is a helpful practical technique, although the CoT idea itself is not entirely new and has been applied in other contexts.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a critical gap in evaluating LALMs for real-world applications. Current benchmarks often fail to capture the complexities of mixed audio environments with varying SNR levels. By providing a more realistic and comprehensive evaluation framework, SSEU-Bench can drive research toward LALMs that are more robust and capable of handling the challenges of human-machine interaction in diverse acoustic settings. The benchmark's focus on joint understanding forces models to consider the interdependencies between speech, scene, and events, which is vital for truly understanding the audio environment. The release of the dataset is of substantial value to the research community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Realistic Scenario Design:** The creation of audio mixtures with controlled SNR levels is a major strength, bringing the evaluation closer to real-world conditions.
    *   **Joint Understanding Focus:** The emphasis on the combined understanding of speech, scene, and events is a significant and relevant advancement.
    *   **Clearly Defined Tasks and Metrics:** The paper clearly defines the tasks (ASR, ASC, AT) and uses standard evaluation metrics, making it easy to compare different models.
    *   **CoT Application:** While not entirely novel in itself, the application of CoT to this specific problem domain is well-motivated and shows promising results.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper evaluates a range of LALMs and CLAP models, providing a good starting point for future research.
    *   **Data and Code Availability:** This increases reproducibility and promotes further research in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scale:** While valuable, the benchmark is of a reasonably limited scale (21.72 hours). Expanding the dataset with more diverse scenes, events, and speakers could further improve its representativeness.
    *   **SNR Assumption:** The uniform distribution of SNRs between -10dB and 10dB is a somewhat simplified assumption. Collecting or simulating more realistic SNR distributions for different environments could be a future improvement. The authors acknowledge this limitation.
    *   **CLAP Baseline Improvement:** While the paper acknowledges the strong performance of CLAP-based models, it could be made stronger by discussing the models in detail to see why they perform as well as the LALMs. This will help contextualize the significance of the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** SSEU-Bench has the potential to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating LALMs. It can influence the development of more robust and context-aware audio understanding models. The CoT-guided inference approach can serve as a starting point for further research on improving joint understanding in LALMs.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper presents a significant contribution to the field by addressing a gap in existing LALM evaluation methods. The novelty of SSEU-Bench lies in its explicit consideration of SNR and joint understanding of audio, scenes, and events, thus increasing the practicality of the benchmark for real-world scenarios. Although there are some minor limitations regarding dataset size, the creation and publication of the dataset are strengths of the paper. This benchmark has the potential to become a standard evaluation resource. Taking these factors into consideration, the paper is assigned a score of **7**. The CoT is not groundbreaking but the SSEU-Bench provides a platform to develop LALMs in a more realistic environment.

### LLM Hallucination Detection: A Fast Fourier Transform Method Based on Hidden Layer Temporal Signals
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13154v1)
- **Authors**: Jinxin Li, Gang Tu, ShengYu Cheng, Junjie Hu, Jinting Wang, Rui Chen, Zhilong Zhou, Dongbo Shan
- **Abstract**: Hallucination remains a critical barrier for deploying large language models (LLMs) in reliability-sensitive applications. Existing detection methods largely fall into two categories: factuality checking, which is fundamentally constrained by external knowledge coverage, and static hidden-state analysis, that fails to capture deviations in reasoning dynamics. As a result, their effectiveness and robustness remain limited. We propose HSAD (Hidden Signal Analysis-based Detection), a novel hallucination detection framework that models the temporal dynamics of hidden representations during autoregressive generation. HSAD constructs hidden-layer signals by sampling activations across layers, applies Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) to obtain frequency-domain representations, and extracts the strongest non-DC frequency component as spectral features. Furthermore, by leveraging the autoregressive nature of LLMs, HSAD identifies optimal observation points for effective and reliable detection. Across multiple benchmarks, including TruthfulQA, HSAD achieves over 10 percentage points improvement compared to prior state-of-the-art methods. By integrating reasoning-process modeling with frequency-domain analysis, HSAD establishes a new paradigm for robust hallucination detection in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HSAD (Hidden Signal Analysis-based Detection), a novel framework for detecting hallucinations in large language models (LLMs).  HSAD models the temporal dynamics of hidden representations during autoregressive generation. It samples activations across layers to construct hidden-layer signals, applies Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) to derive frequency-domain representations, and extracts the dominant non-DC frequency component as spectral features.  HSAD also leverages the autoregressive nature of LLMs to determine optimal observation points for detection. The authors claim HSAD achieves significant performance gains (over 10 percentage points improvement) compared to state-of-the-art methods across various benchmarks like TruthfulQA.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The core idea of using FFT to analyze the temporal dynamics of hidden states is innovative.  It moves beyond static analysis of hidden states and factuality checking. The inspiration from cognitive neuroscience principles of deception detection adds conceptual appeal.
*   **Performance Improvement:**  The reported improvement of over 10% compared to existing methods is substantial and demonstrates the potential effectiveness of the approach.  The paper mentions achieving SOTA performance, a notable achievement.
*   **Robustness to External Knowledge:** HSAD's reliance on internal signals rather than external knowledge bases addresses a key limitation of many hallucination detection methods. This makes it more practical in scenarios where comprehensive external knowledge is unavailable or computationally expensive to access.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The detailed ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of various components of the framework, such as frequency-domain modeling, cross-layer structure, and key-node fusion.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:**  The paper uses multiple datasets and compares HSAD against a comprehensive set of baseline approaches, making the evaluation fairly rigorous.
*   **Potential Theoretical Contribution:** By modeling the reasoning process and looking at frequency domain changes, the approach suggests a method for potentially explaining LLM reasoning and how it deviates during hallucinations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Signal Processing:** While applying FFT to hidden states is a novel context, the FFT itself is a well-established signal processing technique.  The novelty largely lies in *how* FFT is applied and interpreted within the LLM context.
*   **Implementation Details:** The paper lacks detailed implementation specifics for the spectral feature extraction and hallucination detector. Replicability is difficult without these. Hyperparameter choices (e.g., for the L1 regularization and the similarity threshold) could significantly impact performance, but these aspects are not discussed sufficiently.
*   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:**  While performance metrics are reported, the paper does not delve deeply into the types of hallucinations HSAD effectively detects versus those it misses.  Qualitative error analysis would strengthen the analysis.
*   **Scope of Benchmarks:** Although the benchmarks used are standard QA datasets, they may not fully represent the diverse range of LLM applications and potential hallucination scenarios (e.g., creative writing, code generation).
*   **Claims of Mimicking Human Deception Detection:** The analogy to human lie detection, while conceptually interesting, is somewhat tenuous.  There is limited direct evidence presented to support the claim that the identified spectral features *directly* correspond to cognitive processes similar to deception in humans.

**Significance:**

The paper offers a promising new direction for hallucination detection in LLMs.  The approach of analyzing temporal dynamics of hidden states is compelling and appears to offer advantages over existing methods. It has the potential to improve the reliability of LLMs in various applications, which is a significant problem. The paper introduces a new paradigm for thinking about this problem.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite its strengths, the paper isn't groundbreaking.  The innovation lies primarily in applying FFT to hidden states within LLMs, rather than in developing fundamentally new signal processing techniques or LLM architectures. The lack of detailed implementation information, deep error analysis, and a broad evaluation of the models and datasets also reduces its value. However, the reported performance improvements and innovative approach warrant a relatively high score, given the crucial importance of mitigating hallucinations in LLMs.

### Enhancing Video Large Language Models with Structured Multi-Video Collaborative Reasoning (early version)
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13161v1)
- **Authors**: Zhihao He, Tianyao He, Tieyuan Chen, Yun Xu, Huabin Liu, Chaofan Gan, Gui Zou, Weiyao Lin
- **Abstract**: Despite the prosperity of the video language model, the current pursuit of comprehensive video reasoning is thwarted by the inherent spatio-temporal incompleteness within individual videos, resulting in hallucinations and inaccuracies. A promising solution is to augment the reasoning performance with multiple related videos. However, video tokens are numerous and contain redundant information, so directly feeding the relevant video data into a large language model to enhance responses could be counterproductive. To address this challenge, we propose a multi-video collaborative framework for video language models. For efficient and flexible video representation, we establish a Video Structuring Module to represent the video's knowledge as a spatio-temporal graph. Based on the structured video representation, we design the Graph Fusion Module to fuse the structured knowledge and valuable information from related videos into the augmented graph node tokens. Finally, we construct an elaborate multi-video structured prompt to integrate the graph, visual, and textual tokens as the input to the large language model. Extensive experiments substantiate the effectiveness of our framework, showcasing its potential as a promising avenue for advancing video language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Enhancing Video Large Language Models with Structured Multi-Video Collaborative Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of current video language models (VLMs) in comprehensive video reasoning, which stems from spatio-temporal incompleteness within individual videos. To mitigate this, the authors propose a multi-video collaborative reasoning framework. This framework features:

1.  **Video Structuring Module (VSM):** This module generates a spatio-temporal graph representation of each video, capturing key targets and their relationships, effectively summarizing the video's content in a data-efficient manner.
2.  **Graph Fusion Module (GFM):** This module fuses structured knowledge and relevant information from multiple related videos by integrating structure information via Graph Attention Network and fuses information using a cross-graph attention mechanism. This module create graph tokens which are friendly to VLMs.
3.  **Structured Multi-Video Prompt:** This elaborately designed prompt integrates the graph, visual, and textual tokens as input to the large language model.

The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their framework through extensive experiments, showing that it can improve the reliability and accuracy of VLMs by leveraging multi-video information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its structured approach to multi-video collaboration.  While multi-modal integration and graph-based representations are individually established concepts, the specific combination for video reasoning and the design of the VSM and GFM contribute meaningfully. The idea of constructing a spatio-temporal graph to represent videos, and then using this graph to fuse information across multiple related videos is, to the best of my knowledge, relatively novel. Most prior work has focused on single-video reasoning or naive concatenation of multiple videos, which this work directly addresses as being problematic.
*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it directly tackles the challenge of information incompleteness in video reasoning, a major obstacle for current VLMs.  The paper proposes a promising solution for improving VLM accuracy and reliability by incorporating relevant information from multiple sources. This is especially important for real-world scenarios where comprehensive understanding relies on integrating knowledge from multiple perspectives.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of single-video VLMs and motivates the need for multi-video collaboration.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The proposed framework is logically structured and well-explained, with clear descriptions of each module.
    *   **Data-Efficient Representation:**  The use of a spatio-temporal graph allows for efficient representation of video content, preventing token overload.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the framework and its individual components. The creation of a new domain-specific dataset (InternVid-QA) to test the framework is a significant contribution that is especially suited for evaluating multi-video reasoning capabilities, as most existing VQA datasets do not support such capabilities.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on External Components:** The framework relies on external modules like GroundingDINO and DEVA for object detection and tracking. The performance of these components directly impacts the accuracy of the graph representation. Furthermore, the retrieval step for related videos could introduce noise if not handled effectively. The paper mentions different video vectorization methods in the supplementary materials, but does not discuss the specific method used, or the impact of the video retrieval step on the overall performance.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Graph Structure:** While the paper leverages graph attention networks, it could explore more sophisticated graph-based reasoning techniques to better utilize the relational information captured in the spatio-temporal graphs. The current implementation of HF-GAT and cross-graph attention may not be fully exploiting the potential of the graph structure for reasoning.

* **Overall Assessment:**
The paper presents a compelling and well-executed approach to enhancing video language models through structured multi-video collaborative reasoning. The proposed framework addresses a significant limitation of existing VLMs, and the experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness. The paper is well-written and the approach seems novel. Although it builds upon existing concepts from the literature, the innovative integration of these concepts and the design of the individual modules are novel. Potential for future work lies in exploring more advanced graph reasoning techniques and improving the robustness of the object detection and tracking components.

### More performant and scalable: Rethinking contrastive vision-language pre-training of radiology in the LLM era
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13175v1)
- **Authors**: Yingtai Li, Haoran Lai, Xiaoqian Zhou, Shuai Ming, Wenxin Ma, Wei Wei, Shaohua Kevin Zhou
- **Abstract**: The emergence of Large Language Models (LLMs) presents unprecedented opportunities to revolutionize medical contrastive vision-language pre-training. In this paper, we show how LLMs can facilitate large-scale supervised pre-training, thereby advancing vision-language alignment. We begin by demonstrate that modern LLMs can automatically extract diagnostic labels from radiology reports with remarkable precision (>96\% AUC in our experiments) without complex prompt engineering, enabling the creation of large-scale "silver-standard" datasets at a minimal cost (~\$3 for 50k CT image-report pairs). Further, we find that vision encoder trained on this "silver-standard" dataset achieves performance comparable to those trained on labels extracted by specialized BERT-based models, thereby democratizing the access to large-scale supervised pre-training. Building on this foundation, we proceed to reveal that supervised pre-training fundamentally improves contrastive vision-language alignment. Our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance using only a 3D ResNet-18 with vanilla CLIP training, including 83.8\% AUC for zero-shot diagnosis on CT-RATE, 77.3\% AUC on RAD-ChestCT, and substantial improvements in cross-modal retrieval (MAP@50=53.7\% for image-image, Recall@100=52.2\% for report-image). These results demonstrate the potential of utilizing LLMs to facilitate {\bf more performant and scalable} medical AI systems. Our code is avaiable at https://github.com/SadVoxel/More-performant-and-scalable.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper and can provide a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the performance, scalability, and development cost challenges of using vision-language models (VLMs) in radiology. It proposes a framework where large language models (LLMs) are used to automatically extract diagnostic labels from radiology reports, creating a large-scale "silver-standard" dataset for supervised pre-training of vision encoders. The paper demonstrates that vision encoders trained on these LLM-extracted labels achieve performance comparable to those trained on labels extracted by specialized BERT-based models, significantly reducing annotation costs. Furthermore, it shows that supervised pre-training improves contrastive vision-language alignment, leading to state-of-the-art results in zero-shot diagnosis, image-to-image retrieval, and image-to-report cross-modal retrieval, even with a relatively simple 3D ResNet-18 architecture. The paper analyzes the data scaling laws of their approach and demonstrates its data efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:**  The paper provides a novel and compelling approach to tackling the cost and scalability issues in medical image analysis by leveraging LLMs for automated label extraction. Using LLMs for this purpose and showing it's competitive with specialized BERT-based approaches is a significant contribution.  Prior works have explored using LLMs in medical imaging, but this paper presents a particularly efficient and scalable way to perform supervised pre-training.
    *   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates substantial performance improvements in several key radiology tasks (zero-shot diagnosis, cross-modal retrieval) using a relatively simple model.  The increased data efficiency (achieving SOTA with only 10% of the data compared to previous works) is highly significant, making the approach practical and accessible. The potential to democratize access to large-scale supervised pre-training through the automation enabled by LLMs is a very valuable aspect.
    *   **Completeness:** The paper thoroughly evaluates the LLM label extraction quality, performs ablation studies to justify design choices, and evaluates the performance on both internal and external datasets.  The analysis of data scaling laws adds further value.
    *   **Clarity:**  The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed framework, experimental setup, and results.  The figures are helpful and the contributions are well-defined.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM limitations:** Although the paper shows impressive performance of LLMs for label extraction, the process is not flawless. LLMs can hallucinate and make errors.  The variability observed in label extraction for ambiguous classes ("Medical material", "Atelectasis", etc.) underscores the need for careful validation and potential refinement of the LLM-based labeling process. Also, the use of comma-separated binary values in the output format could be limiting and prone to errors.
    *   **Architectural choices:** While the use of a 3D ResNet-18 is a strength in terms of simplicity and efficiency, it raises the question of whether even better performance could be achieved with a larger or more sophisticated architecture (e.g., a Vision Transformer). However, exploring this would likely shift the focus away from the central contribution, which is the LLM-based label extraction.
    *   **Generality:** The experiments are specific to chest CT scans and a limited set of abnormalities. While the proposed approach is likely generalizable to other imaging modalities and diseases, this is not explicitly demonstrated in the paper.  Further studies in different medical imaging domains would strengthen the claims.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a significant advancement in the field by effectively combining LLMs and supervised pre-training to address critical limitations in medical image analysis. The novelty of the approach, combined with its practical benefits (reduced annotation costs, improved data efficiency), warrants a high score. The thorough evaluation and clear presentation further strengthen the contribution. However, there are some limitations related to the imperfect nature of LLMs and the scope of the experiments. I think that a score of 8 is appropriate.

### The Few-shot Dilemma: Over-prompting Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13196v1)
- **Authors**: Yongjian Tang, Doruk Tuncel, Christian Koerner, Thomas Runkler
- **Abstract**: Over-prompting, a phenomenon where excessive examples in prompts lead to diminished performance in Large Language Models (LLMs), challenges the conventional wisdom about in-context few-shot learning. To investigate this few-shot dilemma, we outline a prompting framework that leverages three standard few-shot selection methods - random sampling, semantic embedding, and TF-IDF vectors - and evaluate these methods across multiple LLMs, including GPT-4o, GPT-3.5-turbo, DeepSeek-V3, Gemma-3, LLaMA-3.1, LLaMA-3.2, and Mistral. Our experimental results reveal that incorporating excessive domain-specific examples into prompts can paradoxically degrade performance in certain LLMs, which contradicts the prior empirical conclusion that more relevant few-shot examples universally benefit LLMs. Given the trend of LLM-assisted software engineering and requirement analysis, we experiment with two real-world software requirement classification datasets. By gradually increasing the number of TF-IDF-selected and stratified few-shot examples, we identify their optimal quantity for each LLM. This combined approach achieves superior performance with fewer examples, avoiding the over-prompting problem, thus surpassing the state-of-the-art by 1% in classifying functional and non-functional requirements.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "The Few-shot Dilemma: Over-prompting Large Language Models" investigates the phenomenon of "over-prompting," where providing too many in-context examples in prompts for Large Language Models (LLMs) can degrade performance.  The authors outline a prompting framework using random sampling, semantic embedding, and TF-IDF vectors to select few-shot examples and evaluate its effectiveness across several LLMs. Experiments on software requirement classification datasets reveal that performance peaks with an optimal number of examples and then declines, contradicting the assumption that more relevant examples always benefit LLMs. By finding the optimal number of examples using TF-IDF selection, they achieve state-of-the-art performance on a software requirement classification task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in identifying and empirically demonstrating the "over-prompting" dilemma, especially in the context of domain-specific tasks and contemporary LLMs. While prior work has touched upon the importance of example quality and relevance, the paper provides compelling evidence of a performance decrease with an *increase* in domain-specific examples, which is a valuable contribution. However, the few-shot selection methods (random, embedding, TF-IDF) are not themselves novel.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant for practical applications of LLMs, particularly in domains where collecting labeled data is costly or time-consuming.  The demonstration that less can be more, especially with intelligent example selection, has immediate implications for prompt engineering strategies.  The application to software requirement classification enhances its relevance to a specific area of software engineering. It is important to acknowledge existing efforts on data selection or example selection. The paper's contribution builds upon this body of work, presenting a concrete example of the over-prompting dilemma and presenting TF-IDF as a suitable alternative. It highlights the intricate dynamics between prompt design, model capabilities, and task-specific knowledge.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents a robust set of experiments across multiple LLMs and datasets, providing solid empirical evidence for the over-prompting dilemma.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The prompting framework and the comparison of different few-shot selection methods are well-structured and allow for a systematic analysis of the problem.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The focus on software requirement classification and the use of real-world datasets increase the practical relevance of the findings.
    *   **Model-Specific Analysis:**  The paper analyzes the behavior of various LLMs, revealing model-specific optimal settings. This granular analysis helps shed light on the interplay between model architecture and prompting strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the paper demonstrates over-prompting, the mechanisms behind this phenomenon are not fully explored. The paper lacks deep insights into *why* certain models are more susceptible to over-prompting.
    *   **TF-IDF as SOTA:** While the TF-IDF approach yields improvements in some contexts, there is an opportunity to contextualize this finding with contemporary data selection algorithms. It is imperative to contextualize this approach against modern state-of-the-art example selection methods.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Explanation:** The paper is primarily empirical. A theoretical framework explaining the underlying reasons for over-prompting would significantly enhance its impact.
    *   **Dataset Specificity:** It could be argued that the results might be specific to the software requirement classification domain. However, the observation that "more examples is not necessarily better" is generalizable and likely to hold true for other tasks.
    *   **Clarity:** Better visualizations of the embedding and TF-IDF spaces could aid in understanding the selected examples.
    *   The comparison to previous SOTA could have been further elaborated on by explaining the nuances of the prior art.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on prompt engineering and few-shot learning, encouraging a more nuanced approach to example selection. It will also likely lead to further investigation of the internal mechanisms that cause LLMs to degrade under certain prompting conditions.

*   **Justification:** Despite some limitations, the paper makes a valuable contribution by highlighting the over-prompting dilemma and its impact on LLM performance in a domain-specific task. The empirical validation and systematic approach strengthen the findings. The discovery that more relevant few-shot examples do not always translate to better performance challenges the conventional wisdom and has immediate practical implications. However, the lack of deeper theoretical insights and comparisons with the cutting edge algorithms slightly reduce its overall score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### End4: End-to-end Denoising Diffusion for Diffusion-Based Inpainting Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13214v1)
- **Authors**: Fei Wang, Xuecheng Wu, Zheng Zhang, Danlei Huang, Yuheng Huang, BoWang
- **Abstract**: The powerful generative capabilities of diffusion models have significantly advanced the field of image synthesis, enhancing both full image generation and inpainting-based image editing. Despite their remarkable advancements, diffusion models also raise concerns about potential misuse for malicious purposes. However, existing approaches struggle to identify images generated by diffusion-based inpainting models, even when similar inpainted images are included in their training data. To address this challenge, we propose a novel detection method based on End-to-end denoising diffusion (End4). Specifically, End4 designs a denoising reconstruction model to improve the alignment degree between the latent spaces of the reconstruction and detection processes, thus reconstructing features that are more conducive to detection. Meanwhile, it leverages a Scale-aware Pyramid-like Fusion Module (SPFM) that refines local image features under the guidance of attention pyramid layers at different scales, enhancing feature discriminability. Additionally, to evaluate detection performance on inpainted images, we establish a comprehensive benchmark comprising images generated from five distinct masked regions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our End4 effectively generalizes to unseen masking patterns and remains robust under various perturbations. Our code and dataset will be released soon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces End4, an end-to-end denoising diffusion framework designed for detecting diffusion-based inpainting forgeries.  End4 improves upon existing reconstruction-based detection methods by 1) updating the diffusion reconstruction model itself through a noise-prediction objective, better aligning the latent spaces of reconstruction and detection, 2) incorporating a Scale-aware Pyramid-like Fusion Module (SPFM) to extract fine-grained, region-aware features from both original and reconstructed images, and 3) evaluating the model on a new, comprehensive benchmark, InpaintingForensics, designed to assess performance on images with various inpainting masks. The results demonstrate that End4 significantly outperforms existing methods in detecting inpainting forgeries and is robust to various perturbations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addressing a Specific Limitation:** The paper directly tackles a crucial limitation of existing diffusion forgery detection methods: their inability to effectively detect localized inpainting manipulations. This is a timely and relevant problem given the increasing prevalence of inpainting-based image editing.
    * **End-to-End Training:**  The key novelty lies in the end-to-end training approach of the diffusion reconstruction model.  This is a significant improvement because it allows the reconstruction process to be optimized specifically for the detection task. This is a better solution than using pre-trained diffusion models which were not designed for this task.
    * **Scale-Aware Pyramid-like Fusion Module (SPFM):**  The SPFM module offers a plausible mechanism for capturing the subtle, region-specific artifacts often missed by global feature-based detectors. The architecture is sensible and combines attention and convolution in a reasonable manner.
    * **Comprehensive Benchmark:** The InpaintingForensics dataset is a valuable contribution. The five mask types provide a more thorough evaluation than existing datasets, allowing for a better understanding of detector robustness.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:**  The results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of End4 over existing methods, particularly in terms of accuracy and robustness. The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of the SPFM and the chosen loss function.
    * **Code and Dataset Availability:**  The authors promise to release the code and dataset, which will significantly benefit the research community and promote further progress in this area.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Novelty in Components:** While the *integration* of components is novel, the individual components themselves (U-Net-based diffusion model, attention mechanisms, pyramid-like structures) are not fundamentally new. This reduces the novelty score.
    * **Incremental Improvement:** The paper provides a solid engineering effort that addresses a real problem. However, it can be argued that the proposed improvements, while significant, are more incremental than revolutionary. The problem of aligning latent spaces is known in other domains of generative models and a natural next step in diffusion models as well.
    * **Dependency on SD 1.5:** All experiments are conducted with SD v1.5 for inpainting. Newer, more sophisticated models are already prevalent. While testing SD 1.5 helps to isolate the core contributions, it does raise concerns about the generalizability to future inpainting models (although the robustness experiments provide some evidence in this area).
    * **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** While the paper presents quantitative results, a more detailed qualitative analysis of the failure cases would provide further insights into the limitations of End4 and potential avenues for future research.

* **Significance:**
    * **Addresses a Growing Threat:** The paper directly addresses the critical need for robust forgery detection in the face of increasingly powerful generative models, which pose a threat to public trust and information integrity.
    * **Sets a New Benchmark:** The InpaintingForensics dataset is valuable in providing more standardized evaluation metrics for comparing various defense strategies.
    * **Potential for Real-World Impact:** The proposed method has the potential to be deployed in real-world applications, such as social media platforms, to detect and flag potentially manipulated images.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution by improving the state-of-the-art in diffusion-based inpainting forgery detection. While the individual components of the architecture are not entirely novel, their integration within an end-to-end framework, coupled with the creation of a new benchmark dataset and strong experimental results, justifies a high score.  However, the reliance on SD 1.5, the limited novelty of the individual components, and the relatively incremental nature of the improvements prevent it from achieving a score closer to 10. The paper is well-written and tackles a highly relevant problem; the release of the code and data will make it easier for other researchers to build off of this research.

### Single-stream Policy Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13232v1)
- **Authors**: Zhongwen Xu, Zihan Ding
- **Abstract**: We revisit policy-gradient optimization for Large Language Models (LLMs) from a single-stream perspective. Prevailing group-based methods like GRPO reduce variance with on-the-fly baselines but suffer from critical flaws: frequent degenerate groups erase learning signals, and synchronization barriers hinder scalability. We introduce Single-stream Policy Optimization (SPO), which eliminates these issues by design. SPO replaces per-group baselines with a persistent, KL-adaptive value tracker and normalizes advantages globally across the batch, providing a stable, low-variance learning signal for every sample. Being group-free, SPO enables higher throughput and scales effectively in long-horizon or tool-integrated settings where generation times vary. Furthermore, the persistent value tracker naturally enables an adaptive curriculum via prioritized sampling. Experiments using Qwen3-8B show that SPO converges more smoothly and attains higher accuracy than GRPO, while eliminating computation wasted on degenerate groups. Ablation studies confirm that SPO's gains stem from its principled approach to baseline estimation and advantage normalization, offering a more robust and efficient path for LLM reasoning. Across five hard math benchmarks with Qwen3 8B, SPO improves the average maj@32 by +3.4 percentage points (pp) over GRPO, driven by substantial absolute point gains on challenging datasets, including +7.3 pp on BRUMO 25, +4.4 pp on AIME 25, +3.3 pp on HMMT 25, and achieves consistent relative gain in pass@$k$ across the evaluated $k$ values. SPO's success challenges the prevailing trend of adding incidental complexity to RL algorithms, highlighting a path where fundamental principles, not architectural workarounds, drive the next wave of progress in LLM reasoning.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Single-stream Policy Optimization".

**Summary:**

The paper addresses limitations in existing policy gradient optimization methods for Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically those based on group-based approaches like GRPO.  GRPO, while reducing variance, suffers from degenerate groups (where all responses have the same outcome, yielding no signal) and synchronization bottlenecks that hinder scalability, especially in tasks with variable interaction times (e.g., tool use). The authors propose Single-stream Policy Optimization (SPO), which returns to a single-stream paradigm (one prompt-response pair per sample) but tackles the resulting high variance challenge with three key components: a Bayesian value tracker for a stable baseline, global advantage normalization, and prioritized sampling for efficient learning.  Experiments with Qwen3-8B demonstrate that SPO outperforms GRPO on math reasoning benchmarks, improves training stability, eliminates wasted computation on degenerate groups, and scales better in simulated agentic settings. The paper argues that SPO's success highlights the potential of fundamental RL principles over complex architectural workarounds in LLM reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the *integration* of existing concepts into a specific framework tailored for LLM reasoning. The single-stream approach itself isn't new in RL, but its adaptation to the LLM context, combined with the Bayesian value tracker, global normalization, and prioritized sampling, represents a valuable contribution. The paper *revisits* the single stream approach with significant modification.
    *   *Strength:*  The combination of components addresses the specific drawbacks of group-based methods within the LLM domain. The Bayesian value tracker is a clever way to maintain a stable baseline without the instability and resource requirements of a separate critic network.
    *   *Weakness:* While the *combination* is novel, the individual components are established RL techniques. The degree of innovation in these techniques is limited. Additionally, the application of Bayesian methods to a tabular tracker is relatively standard practice.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates a tangible improvement over a widely used method (GRPO) on a challenging task (math reasoning). The findings are significant because they:

    *   *Highlight Efficiency:*  The SPO approach is demonstrably more computationally efficient, avoiding wasted resources and improving scalability, features which are critical for LLM training. The elimination of synchronization barriers is a crucial advantage for agentic settings.
    *   *Promote Simplicity:* The paper argues for a return to simpler, principled approaches over increasingly complex architectures. This is a valuable message for the field. It encourages researchers to prioritize understanding fundamental RL principles, rather than solely focusing on ad-hoc solutions.
    *   *Provide Empirical Evidence:* The comprehensive experimental results (using Qwen3-8B and math benchmarks) provide strong evidence supporting the claims. The ablation studies further validate the contributions of each component.

*   **Limitations:**

    *   *Domain-Specific Evaluation:* The evaluation is primarily focused on math reasoning tasks.  It would be beneficial to see how SPO performs on a wider range of LLM tasks, including language generation and instruction following, to further demonstrate its general applicability. The experiments are performed with Qwen3-8B, a moderately sized LLM. The value should be explored with state-of-the-art or frontier-sized LLMs.
    *   *Simulated Agentic Settings:*  The agentic training results are based on simulations. Real-world agentic experiments, while more challenging to conduct, would strengthen the claim about improved scalability in these settings.
    *   *The "No Baseline" baseline is surprisingly effective:* The authors state "if we use “no baseline" (i.e., υ = 0), it is an extremely simple and valid algorithm but may suffer from high policy gradient variance." This implies that using 'no baseline' results in a viable algorithm, and therefore the importance of this technique might be diminished.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research by:

    *   *Encouraging Single-Stream Approaches:* It could shift the focus away from group-based methods and inspire further exploration of single-stream approaches with enhanced variance reduction techniques.
    *   *Promoting Fundamental RL Principles:*  It reinforces the importance of understanding and applying core RL concepts in the context of LLMs.
    *   *Providing a Strong Baseline:*  SPO could serve as a strong baseline for future research in LLM policy optimization.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper presents a valuable contribution, it relies on the smart integration and adaptation of existing techniques, rather than introducing fundamentally new RL algorithms. The empirical results are strong, but the evaluation is somewhat limited in scope. It will require more evidence on larger models and broader tasks to validate its long-term impact. Nonetheless, the practical benefits (efficiency, scalability) and the emphasis on simplicity make it a worthwhile advancement.
Considering these factors, a score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contribution and potential influence, while acknowledging its limitations and the incremental nature of its novelty.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Simulating Clinical AI Assistance using Multimodal LLMs: A Case Study in Diabetic Retinopathy
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13234v1)
- **Authors**: Nadim Barakat, William Lotter
- **Abstract**: Diabetic retinopathy (DR) is a leading cause of blindness worldwide, and AI systems can expand access to fundus photography screening. Current FDA-cleared systems primarily provide binary referral outputs, where this minimal output may limit clinical trust and utility. Yet, determining the most effective output format to enhance clinician-AI performance is an empirical challenge that is difficult to assess at scale. We evaluated multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for DR detection and their ability to simulate clinical AI assistance across different output types. Two models were tested on IDRiD and Messidor-2: GPT-4o, a general-purpose MLLM, and MedGemma, an open-source medical model. Experiments included: (1) baseline evaluation, (2) simulated AI assistance with synthetic predictions, and (3) actual AI-to-AI collaboration where GPT-4o incorporated MedGemma outputs. MedGemma outperformed GPT-4o at baseline, achieving higher sensitivity and AUROC, while GPT-4o showed near-perfect specificity but low sensitivity. Both models adjusted predictions based on simulated AI inputs, but GPT-4o's performance collapsed with incorrect ones, whereas MedGemma remained more stable. In actual collaboration, GPT-4o achieved strong results when guided by MedGemma's descriptive outputs, even without direct image access (AUROC up to 0.96). These findings suggest MLLMs may improve DR screening pipelines and serve as scalable simulators for studying clinical AI assistance across varying output configurations. Open, lightweight models such as MedGemma may be especially valuable in low-resource settings, while descriptive outputs could enhance explainability and clinician trust in clinical workflows.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for diabetic retinopathy (DR) detection and their potential to simulate clinical AI assistance.  It compares GPT-4o and MedGemma on DR detection tasks using the IDRID and Messidor-2 datasets.  The study explores three experimental conditions: baseline performance, simulated AI assistance with varying accuracies of external predictions, and actual AI-to-AI collaboration where GPT-4o uses MedGemma's outputs.  The results demonstrate that both models can adjust their predictions based on external AI inputs. MedGemma shows robustness to incorrect inputs, while GPT-4o's performance benefits significantly from descriptive outputs from MedGemma, even without direct image access. The authors conclude that MLLMs can serve as scalable simulators for studying clinical AI assistance in DR screening and potentially improve pipelines by integrating descriptive outputs for enhanced explainability and clinician trust.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic exploration of MLLMs as simulators for clinical AI assistance, specifically in the context of DR screening.  While MLLMs have been applied to medical imaging before, this paper demonstrates a framework for evaluating different output configurations and collaboration strategies in a scalable manner.  The focus on simulating the interaction *between* AI systems, rather than just evaluating individual AI models, is a valuable contribution. It moves beyond just DR detection to consider the workflow and clinical application.
*   **Significance:** The findings suggest that MLLMs can be valuable tools for optimizing clinical AI workflows.  The demonstration that descriptive outputs (generated by a smaller, open-source model like MedGemma) can improve the performance of a larger model (GPT-4o), even without image access, is particularly significant. This has implications for explainability and clinician trust, addressing a crucial challenge in the deployment of AI in healthcare. The potential for scaling up the MLLMs is a benefit as well.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined experimental design with clear comparisons across different conditions.
    *   Use of two distinct models (a general-purpose and a medical-specific MLLM) provides a valuable comparative analysis.
    *   The investigation of different output types (binary predictions and descriptive text) is practically relevant for clinical implementation.
    *   Clear articulation of the implications for explainability, clinician trust, and workflow optimization.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The datasets used are relatively small. This limits the generalizability of the findings. A higher number of images would provide more confidence to the results.
    *   The simulation of AI assistance is inherently limited. Actual clinician-AI interaction may involve more nuanced factors than simply feeding AI predictions as prompts. However, a more complex prompt could be more beneficial.
    *   The focus is primarily on *simulating* the collaboration. While valuable for initial exploration, the ultimate test is real-world evaluation with clinicians.
    *   The paper does not deeply analyze the types of errors made by the models or the reasons why MedGemma's descriptions are effective. This would strengthen the analysis.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of future clinical AI systems for DR screening and other medical imaging applications. The emphasis on explainability, the framework for simulating collaboration, and the exploration of different output configurations can guide the development of more user-friendly and clinically useful AI tools. This also motivates the application of these simulations in other medical scenarios.
*   **Overall:** The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating a novel application of MLLMs for simulating clinical AI assistance. While there are limitations in the scope and methodology, the findings have practical implications for the design and deployment of AI in healthcare.

### Metacognitive Reuse: Turning Recurring LLM Reasoning Into Concise Behaviors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13237v1)
- **Authors**: Aniket Didolkar, Nicolas Ballas, Sanjeev Arora, Anirudh Goyal
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) now solve multi-step problems by emitting extended chains of thought. During the process, they often re-derive the same intermediate steps across problems, inflating token usage and latency. This saturation of the context window leaves less capacity for exploration. We study a simple mechanism that converts recurring reasoning fragments into concise, reusable "behaviors" (name + instruction) via the model's own metacognitive analysis of prior traces. These behaviors are stored in a "behavior handbook" which supplies them to the model in-context at inference or distills them into parameters via supervised fine-tuning. This approach achieves improved test-time reasoning across three different settings - 1) Behavior-conditioned inference: Providing the LLM relevant behaviors in-context during reasoning reduces number of reasoning tokens by up to 46% while matching or improving baseline accuracy; 2) Behavior-guided self-improvement: Without any parameter updates, the model improves its own future reasoning by leveraging behaviors from its own past problem solving attempts. This yields up to 10% higher accuracy than a naive critique-and-revise baseline; and 3) Behavior-conditioned SFT: SFT on behavior-conditioned reasoning traces is more effective at converting non-reasoning models into reasoning models as compared to vanilla SFT. Together, these results indicate that turning slow derivations into fast procedural hints enables LLMs to remember how to reason, not just what to conclude.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces "metacognitive reuse," a novel approach to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of large language model (LLM) reasoning. The core idea is to extract recurring reasoning fragments from LLM's own past solution traces, convert them into concise, reusable "behaviors," and store them in a "behavior handbook." This handbook then serves as a procedural memory, providing in-context guidance during inference or enabling distillation via supervised fine-tuning (SFT). The paper presents three key settings: behavior-conditioned inference (BCI), behavior-guided self-improvement, and behavior-conditioned SFT, demonstrating improvements in token efficiency and reasoning accuracy across challenging mathematical benchmarks like MATH and AIME. The work highlights the potential of turning slow derivations into fast procedural hints, enabling LLMs to "remember how to reason" rather than just what to conclude.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The central idea of metacognitive reuse and the behavior handbook is novel. While retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a well-established technique, this paper distinguishes itself by focusing on procedural knowledge (how to think) rather than declarative knowledge (factual information).  The idea of having an LLM introspect and distill its own reasoning patterns into reusable behaviors is also innovative, moving beyond simply retrieving pre-existing knowledge. The three settings (BCI, self-improvement, BC-SFT) are a logical progression, exploring various ways to utilize the learned behaviors. However, the novelty is somewhat tempered by the existing literature on skill extraction from LLMs, and structured reasoning methods.

**Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to address a crucial bottleneck in LLM reasoning: inefficiency.  By reducing token usage and latency, the approach can make complex problem-solving more practical and scalable.  The improvement in accuracy in certain settings, particularly BC-SFT, suggests that distilled procedural knowledge can enhance the reasoning capabilities of smaller models. The experimental results on MATH and AIME provide compelling evidence of the benefits of the proposed framework. However, the approach is heavily focused on mathematical reasoning, and it's not immediately clear how well it will generalize to other domains.  The discussion of limitations, such as the lack of dynamic behavior retrieval during reasoning, is important for future research.  The efficiency gains from pre-computing and lower pricing for input tokens are also a significant contribution.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the inefficiency of LLM reasoning due to redundant derivations.
*   **Novel Approach:** Metacognitive reuse and the behavior handbook offer a creative solution to the problem.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The three experimental settings provide a thorough evaluation of the framework's capabilities.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents compelling evidence of improved token efficiency and reasoning accuracy.
*   **Well-Discussed Limitations:** The authors acknowledge and discuss the limitations of their approach, providing directions for future research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Domain Specificity:** The experiments are primarily focused on mathematical reasoning, limiting the generalizability of the results. The retrieval mechanisms are also adapted to the dataset.
*   **Limited Generalization Evaluation**: While the general concepts seem applicable across domains, there's a lack of explicit evaluation across multiple datasets with diverse characteristics.
*   **Implementation Complexity:** Implementing the full pipeline, including behavior extraction and handbook management, may be non-trivial.
*   **Behavior Retrieval Limitations**: The current retrieval methods are static. A more dynamic and iterative retrieval approach could be more robust in capturing behaviors needed during reasoning.
*   **Clarity around behaviour composition:** The paper doesn't discuss how the behaviours can compose or interact, which impacts how valuable they can be within a larger setting.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM reasoning by addressing the problem of inefficiency and exploring a metacognitive approach to knowledge reuse. The empirical results are compelling, and the limitations are clearly discussed. However, the domain specificity of the experiments, the implementation complexity, and the static retrieval methods somewhat limit the immediate impact of the work. Further, the reliance on existing evaluation datasets limits the potential for discovering general application. Although there is plenty of room for improvements, the gains from pre-computing and lower pricing are a solid contribution. Thus a score of '7' is justified, reflecting its strong potential and demonstrated benefits while acknowledging its limitations and areas for future research.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Don't Forget the Nonlinearity: Unlocking Activation Functions in Efficient Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13240v1)
- **Authors**: Bo Yin, Xingyi Yang, Xinchao Wang
- **Abstract**: Existing parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods primarily adapt weight matrices while keeping activation functions fixed. We introduce \textbf{NoRA}, the first PEFT framework that directly adapts nonlinear activation functions in pretrained transformer-based models. NoRA replaces fixed activations with learnable rational functions and applies structured low-rank updates to numerator and denominator coefficients, with a group-wise design that localizes adaptation and improves stability at minimal cost. On vision transformers trained on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100, NoRA matches or exceeds full fine-tuning while updating only 0.4\% of parameters (0.02M), achieving accuracy gains of +0.17\% and +0.27\%. When combined with LoRA (\textbf{NoRA++}), it outperforms LoRA and DoRA under matched training budgets by adding fewer trainable parameters. On LLaMA3-8B instruction tuning, NoRA++ consistently improves generation quality, yielding average MMLU gains of +0.3\%--0.8\%, including +1.6\% on STEM (Alpaca) and +1.3\% on OpenOrca. We further show that NoRA constrains adaptation to a low-dimensional functional subspace, implicitly regularizing update magnitude and direction. These results establish activation-space tuning as a complementary and highly parameter-efficient alternative to weight-based PEFT, positioning activation functions as first-class objects for model adaptation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NoRA (Nonlinear Rational Adapter), a novel parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) method that adapts nonlinear activation functions instead of weight matrices, the typical focus of existing PEFT techniques. NoRA replaces fixed activations with learnable rational functions and uses structured low-rank updates on the numerator and denominator coefficients of these functions. This allows for task-specific adaptation of activation functions while maintaining stability and minimizing the number of trainable parameters. Experiments on vision transformers and large language models demonstrate that NoRA matches or exceeds the performance of full fine-tuning and other PEFT methods while using a significantly smaller number of trainable parameters. The paper also explores the scalability of NoRA through group expansion and demonstrates its compatibility with weight-based PEFT methods like LoRA. The results suggest that adapting activation functions offers a complementary and highly efficient alternative to weight-based fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of adapting activation functions instead of weights in PEFT is the paper's primary novelty. While learnable activation functions have been explored before, their application within a PEFT framework, using low-rank adaptation and a group-wise design for rational functions, is a significant contribution. This shifts the focus of PEFT from weight matrices to the nonlinear components of neural networks. The theoretical justification for why tuning activations matters is also helpful and reinforces the core concepts.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in demonstrating that activation functions can be adapted efficiently to achieve performance comparable to or even better than existing PEFT methods, with significantly fewer trainable parameters. This opens up new avenues for research in PEFT and highlights the potential of adapting non-linear components, which have been largely overlooked. The findings have practical implications for deploying and customizing large models on resource-constrained devices. Demonstrating consistent improvements on both vision and language tasks bolsters the significance and generalizability of the method.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical results, showing improvements over baselines and even full fine-tuning in some cases.
    *   The group-wise low-rank adaptation strategy is well-designed to balance flexibility and stability.
    *   The paper offers a comprehensive analysis, including ablation studies and visualizations, to understand the effects of NoRA.
    *   Compatibility with existing rational function approximation techniques, as well as PEFT strategies like LoRA, makes NoRA highly practical.
    * The introduction of a hybrid NoRA++ is great for showing the applicability in various architectures.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The implementation relies on rational functions, which can be more computationally expensive than simple activation functions like ReLU during inference. While the paper shows that the added cost isn't significant, a thorough exploration of the runtime impact of using rational functions in very large models is required.
    *   Although comprehensive, additional ablations exploring the architecture and group sizes could reinforce the findings.
    * The paper should discuss limitations of the approach, such as the need for rational approximations of activations to exist.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of future PEFT methods. By demonstrating the importance of activation functions, it encourages researchers to explore other non-linear components of neural networks for adaptation. It also offers a practical and efficient technique that can be integrated with existing PEFT methods to further improve performance.

*   **Justification:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of parameter-efficient fine-tuning. It introduces a new approach that leverages a different aspect of the model than traditional methods, and shows strong empirical results. The combination of theoretical insights, practical techniques, and experimental validation makes the paper a solid addition to the existing literature.
