- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Evaluating LLM Alignment on Personality Inference from Real-World Interview Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13244v1)
- **Authors**: Jianfeng Zhu, Julina Maharjan, Xinyu Li, Karin G. Coifman, Ruoming Jin
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in roles requiring nuanced psychological understanding, such as emotional support agents, counselors, and decision-making assistants. However, their ability to interpret human personality traits, a critical aspect of such applications, remains unexplored, particularly in ecologically valid conversational settings. While prior work has simulated LLM "personas" using discrete Big Five labels on social media data, the alignment of LLMs with continuous, ground-truth personality assessments derived from natural interactions is largely unexamined. To address this gap, we introduce a novel benchmark comprising semi-structured interview transcripts paired with validated continuous Big Five trait scores. Using this dataset, we systematically evaluate LLM performance across three paradigms: (1) zero-shot and chain-of-thought prompting with GPT-4.1 Mini, (2) LoRA-based fine-tuning applied to both RoBERTa and Meta-LLaMA architectures, and (3) regression using static embeddings from pretrained BERT and OpenAI's text-embedding-3-small. Our results reveal that all Pearson correlations between model predictions and ground-truth personality traits remain below 0.26, highlighting the limited alignment of current LLMs with validated psychological constructs. Chain-of-thought prompting offers minimal gains over zero-shot, suggesting that personality inference relies more on latent semantic representation than explicit reasoning. These findings underscore the challenges of aligning LLMs with complex human attributes and motivate future work on trait-specific prompting, context-aware modeling, and alignment-oriented fine-tuning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the gap in evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs) for personality inference using real-world interview data and validated personality scores.  It introduces a novel benchmark dataset comprising semi-structured interview transcripts paired with continuous Big Five trait scores. The study systematically evaluates LLMs under three paradigms: zero-shot/chain-of-thought prompting (GPT-4.1 Mini), LoRA-based fine-tuning (RoBERTa, Meta-LLaMA), and regression using static embeddings (BERT, OpenAI text-embedding-3-small). The results indicate a limited alignment of current LLMs with validated psychological constructs, with low correlations between model predictions and ground truth. Chain-of-thought prompting offered minimal gains, suggesting the importance of latent semantic representation over explicit reasoning in personality inference. The paper concludes by highlighting the challenges of aligning LLMs with complex human attributes and calls for future research focusing on trait-specific prompting, context-aware modeling, and alignment-oriented fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's main strength lies in its novelty in using real-world interview data for evaluating LLMs' personality inference capabilities. Prior works often relied on social media data or synthetic datasets, which often lack ecological validity and represent a simplified view of personality. Introducing a benchmark with continuous Big Five scores derived from validated psychometric instruments represents a significant step forward. The systematic comparison of different LLM paradigms (zero-shot, fine-tuning, and embedding regression) is also valuable.

* **Significance:** The paper's findings have important implications for the use of LLMs in applications requiring nuanced psychological understanding, such as mental health support or personalized human-computer interaction. The observation that current LLMs struggle to accurately infer personality traits from natural language interactions is a crucial cautionary tale.  The study highlights that existing methods oversimplify the complex nuances of human personality. It also provides valuable insights into the limitations of chain-of-thought prompting and suggests alternative approaches, like focusing on latent semantic representations or trait-specific alignment.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Ecological Validity:** Using real-world interview data is a major strength.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper systematically compares several LLM architectures and training paradigms.
    * **Validated Labels:** Employing validated Big Five personality scores as ground truth is essential for robust evaluation.
    * **Clear Research Question:** The paper directly addresses the question of how well LLMs align with actual human personality traits in realistic settings.
    * **Reproducibility:** The provision of code improves the reproducibility of the results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Model Variety:**  While the study investigates several models, it could benefit from including more recent and powerful LLMs, like the latest generations of GPT, Mistral, or other open-source alternatives.  The use of GPT-4.1 Mini instead of the full GPT-4 is also a notable limitation as the mini version is a less capable model.
    * **Interview Protocol Details:** More detailed information about the interview protocol and potential interviewer effects would strengthen the study. How interviewers were trained and how their biases might have influenced responses could be addressed.
    * **Dataset Size:** Although the dataset is a contribution, its size (518 participants) could be considered relatively modest for fine-tuning large LLMs, potentially limiting the performance gains from LoRA adaptation.
   *  **Lack of in-depth error analysis**: The paper reports the overall performance metrics but doesn't delve deeply into the types of errors the models are making (e.g., are certain types of statements misclassified more often? Are specific traits confused with each other?).

* **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence future research on LLM-based personality inference by emphasizing the need for more ecologically valid data, rigorous evaluation metrics, and more sophisticated alignment strategies. The benchmark dataset created will likely be a valuable resource for the community. It serves as a call to action for the field to move beyond simplified tasks and consider the complexities of human personality when developing AI systems.

* **Significance of Results:** While the negative results are important (demonstrating current LLMs' limitations), they are not as impactful as a positive result that shows significant alignment.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper's strengths lie in its novelty in data and systematicity in the evaluation. The focus on real-world data is a significant leap forward.  The systematic investigation of different approaches is thorough. However, the relatively limited range of models (especially the use of the "mini" version of GPT), potential limitations of the dataset, and negative results (demonstrating limitations rather than breakthroughs) hold it back from a higher score. The lack of in-depth error analysis also weakens the analysis.  Nevertheless, the paper addresses an important problem, makes a significant contribution to the evaluation methodology, and will likely shape future research directions.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Model-assisted Meta-optimizer for Automated Design of Constrained Evolutionary Algorithm
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13251v1)
- **Authors**: Xu Yang, Rui Wang, Kaiwen Li, Wenhua Li, Weixiong Huang
- **Abstract**: Meta-black-box optimization has been significantly advanced through the use of large language models (LLMs), yet in fancy on constrained evolutionary optimization. In this work, AwesomeDE is proposed that leverages LLMs as the strategy of meta-optimizer to generate update rules for constrained evolutionary algorithm without human intervention. On the meanwhile, $RTO^2H$ framework is introduced for standardize prompt design of LLMs. The meta-optimizer is trained on a diverse set of constrained optimization problems. Key components, including prompt design and iterative refinement, are systematically analyzed to determine their impact on design quality. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed approach outperforms existing methods in terms of computational efficiency and solution accuracy. Furthermore, AwesomeDE is shown to generalize well across distinct problem domains, suggesting its potential for broad applicability. This research contributes to the field by providing a scalable and data-driven methodology for automated constrained algorithm design, while also highlighting limitations and directions for future work.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AwesomeDE, a novel framework for automated design of constrained evolutionary algorithms (CEAs). It leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) as a meta-optimizer within a Meta-Black-Box Optimization (MetaBBO) framework. The LLM generates update rules for CEAs, eliminating human intervention in the design process. The authors introduce the RTO2H framework for standardizing prompt design for LLMs. The meta-optimizer is trained on a diverse set of constrained optimization problems (COPs). The performance of AwesomeDE is evaluated on the CEC2010 benchmark suite, demonstrating its superiority over existing methods in terms of computational efficiency and solution accuracy. The paper claims AwesomeDE generalizes well across distinct problem domains. The authors highlight limitations and future directions for research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs as a meta-optimizer for designing CEAs, particularly with constraint handling, is promising but *not entirely novel*. Previous works have explored LLMs for evolutionary optimization in unconstrained settings. The addition of constraint handling and automated update rule generation is an incremental improvement. The RTO2H framework is a useful contribution towards standardized prompt design, but it doesn't represent a *radical breakthrough*. However, the elimination of human intervention is interesting.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work hinges on the empirical results. If AwesomeDE demonstrably outperforms existing constrained optimization algorithms across a wide range of problems, it could be a valuable contribution. The claims of good generalization capabilities also enhance its significance. However, the results in Table 1 only show superior performance in six benchmark functions out of 12, and this is not super compelling. Further more, AwesomeDE completes execution in 1.12-1.45 normalized time units compared to 1.60-1.80 units for MadDE and LSHADE, which means that it is not faster, so it does not achieve better computational efficiency. In fact, according to Figure 3, AwesomeDE is slower than LSHADE in most of the problems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Automated algorithm design is a desirable goal.
    *   Addresses the relatively underexplored area of using LLMs for constrained evolutionary optimization.
    *   Systematic evaluation on a standard benchmark (CEC2010).
    *   The RTO2H framework provides a structured approach to prompt engineering.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The "novelty" claim is a bit overstated. It's an extension of existing LLM-based optimization techniques to a specific problem domain.
    *   The empirical results are not overwhelmingly convincing, superior performance in just half the benchmark functions is not very impressive.
    *   The generalization claim needs stronger support with more diverse and real-world problems.
    *   The computational efficiency is not impressive either.

*   **Justification for Score:**
The paper presents a potentially valuable contribution to the field of constrained evolutionary optimization by leveraging LLMs for automated algorithm design. However, the actual novelty and significance are somewhat limited by the incremental nature of the innovation and the mixed empirical results.

Score: 6/10

- **Classification**: cs.NE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Beyond Private or Public: Large Language Models as Quasi-Public Goods in the AI Economy
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13265v1)
- **Authors**: Yukun Zhang, TianYang Zhang
- **Abstract**: This paper conceptualizes Large Language Models (LLMs) as a form of mixed public goods within digital infrastructure, analyzing their economic properties through a comprehensive theoretical framework. We develop mathematical models to quantify the non-rivalry characteristics, partial excludability, and positive externalities of LLMs. Through comparative analysis of open-source and closed-source development paths, we identify systematic differences in resource allocation efficiency, innovation trajectories, and access equity. Our empirical research evaluates the spillover effects and network externalities of LLMs across different domains, including knowledge diffusion, innovation acceleration, and industry transformation. Based on these findings, we propose policy recommendations for balancing innovation incentives with equitable access, including public-private partnership mechanisms, computational resource democratization, and governance structures that optimize social welfare. This interdisciplinary approach contributes to understanding the economic nature of foundation AI models and provides policy guidance for their development as critical digital infrastructure
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided research paper. Here's a summary and a critical evaluation with a novelty/significance score:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the question of how to conceptualize and govern Large Language Models (LLMs) within the AI economy, arguing they don't neatly fit into traditional public or private goods categories but are better understood as *quasi-public goods*. It develops a theoretical framework to analyze the economic properties of LLMs (non-rivalry, excludability, externalities), creates mathematical models to quantify these properties, and empirically introduces a "Public Goods Index" (PGI) to measure the "publicness" of different LLMs (ChatGPT, Claude, Llama, Qwen, Gemini). Through a longitudinal case study of OpenAI's GPT models, the paper identifies a "publicness decay" phenomenon and proposes policy recommendations for balancing innovation incentives with equitable access and social welfare, including tiered governance systems and computational resource democratization. The paper employs agent-based modeling (ABM) to simulate the impact of policy interventions and validate theoretical predictions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel Conceptualization:** The paper offers a significant conceptual advance by framing LLMs as quasi-public goods. This is a more nuanced and accurate representation than treating them as purely public or private, which has important implications for governance and policy.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The theoretical framework is well-developed, integrating insights from public goods theory, platform economics, and innovation policy.
    *   **Empirical Contribution:** The Public Goods Index (PGI) is an innovative attempt to quantify the publicness of LLMs. While the measurement is complex and relies on proxies, it provides a useful starting point for comparative analysis.
    *   **Longitudinal Study & ABM validation:** The OpenAI case study and the policy simulations provide valuable empirical evidence to support the theoretical framework and policy recommendations.
    *   **Policy Relevance:** The paper directly addresses policy concerns and offers actionable recommendations for balancing innovation and social welfare.
    *   **Interdisciplinary Approach:** The paper bridges economics, computer science, and policy analysis, creating a holistic perspective.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **PGI Proxy Limitations:** The PGI relies on potentially imperfect proxies for measuring key attributes (non-rivalry, excludability, and externalities). The assigned equal weights may also be debatable and can be changed easily. The paper acknowledges these limitations but could benefit from a more thorough discussion of their potential impact on the results.
    *   **Model Simplifications:** As with any economic model, the framework makes simplifying assumptions. The impact of these simplifications on the generalizability of the findings should be discussed in more detail.
    *   **Data Availability & Transparency:** The availability and transparency of the data used to calculate the PGI should be improved.
    *   **Generalization to other AI models:** The models may not generalize easily to other types of foundation AI models such as image generation models.
    *   **Limited scope:** Some important aspects of the LLM ecosystem were not covered, such as the environmental impacts.

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The conceptualization of LLMs as quasi-public goods and the development of the PGI are both novel contributions. This departs from existing literature.
    *   The longitudinal study is unique.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper's findings have significant implications for policy debates regarding the governance and regulation of LLMs. It provides a framework for assessing the social value of different LLM development models and for designing targeted policy interventions.
    *   It contributes to the ongoing discussion about the appropriate balance between innovation incentives and equitable access in the AI economy.
    *   The PGI, despite its limitations, could become a useful tool for policymakers and researchers.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, the paper offers a strong contribution to the field. The conceptual framework and the empirical analysis are well-executed and provide valuable insights into the economic nature of LLMs and their policy implications. While the PGI has limitations, it's a useful attempt to quantify the publicness of these complex systems and offers a tangible basis for comparing different development models. The longitudinal case study, along with the agent-based modeling validations, further strengthens the paper. The weaknesses are acknowledged and do not negate the core findings.

Therefore, I'm assigning a score of **7.5**. It is a significant contribution with a potential for medium-high impact on future policy development in the LLM field.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLMs for energy and macronutrients estimation using only text data from 24-hour dietary recalls: a parameter-efficient fine-tuning experiment using a 10-shot prompt
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13268v1)
- **Authors**: Rodrigo M Carrillo-Larco
- **Abstract**: BACKGROUND: Most artificial intelligence tools used to estimate nutritional content rely on image input. However, whether large language models (LLMs) can accurately predict nutritional values based solely on text descriptions of foods consumed remains unknown. If effective, this approach could enable simpler dietary monitoring without the need for photographs. METHODS: We used 24-hour dietary recalls from adolescents aged 12-19 years in the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey (NHANES). An open-source quantized LLM was prompted using a 10-shot, chain-of-thought approach to estimate energy and five macronutrients based solely on text strings listing foods and their quantities. We then applied parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) to evaluate whether predictive accuracy improved. NHANES-calculated values served as the ground truth for energy, proteins, carbohydrates, total sugar, dietary fiber and total fat. RESULTS: In a pooled dataset of 11,281 adolescents (49.9% male, mean age 15.4 years), the vanilla LLM yielded poor predictions. The mean absolute error (MAE) was 652.08 for energy and the Lin's CCC <0.46 across endpoints. In contrast, the fine-tuned model performed substantially better, with energy MAEs ranging from 171.34 to 190.90 across subsets, and Lin's CCC exceeding 0.89 for all outcomes. CONCLUSIONS: When prompted using a chain-of-thought approach and fine-tuned with PEFT, open-source LLMs exposed solely to text input can accurately predict energy and macronutrient values from 24-hour dietary recalls. This approach holds promise for low-burden, text-based dietary monitoring tools.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to estimate energy and macronutrient content from 24-hour dietary recall data.  The authors used an open-source LLM prompted with a 10-shot, chain-of-thought approach on text-based food data from NHANES. They then applied parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) and found that the fine-tuned model substantially improved the accuracy of energy and macronutrient estimation compared to the baseline LLM.  The study suggests that LLMs, when appropriately trained, can accurately predict nutritional values from text alone, potentially simplifying dietary monitoring and reducing the need for image-based assessments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a Relevant Problem:** Dietary assessment is a crucial but often burdensome task. The paper investigates a potentially low-burden alternative using widely available text data.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:**  The use of a large, nationally representative dataset (NHANES) provides a strong foundation. The comparison between the baseline LLM and the fine-tuned model offers clear evidence of the benefits of fine-tuning.  The use of well-established evaluation metrics (MAE, RMSE, R2, Lin's CCC, Bland-Altman plots) ensures a robust assessment of performance.
    *   **Use of Open-Source Resources:**  The selection of an open-source LLM and the application of PEFT via Unsloth contribute to the accessibility and reproducibility of the research, enhancing its potential for wider adoption.
    *   **Chain-of-Thought Prompting:** Using Chain-of-Thought prompting methods also improves performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Nutrients:** The study focuses solely on energy and macronutrients. Expanding to micronutrients would enhance its practical utility.
    *   **Preprocessed Data:** The use of preprocessed NHANES data, as opposed to free-text user input, limits the assessment of real-world applicability.  The paper acknowledges that free-text input could introduce complexities in food descriptions and portions.
    *   **English-Language Bias:**  The analysis is limited to English. The generalizability to other languages requires further investigation.
    *   **Generalizability of Fine-Tuned Model:** The study only fine-tuned a single model. Other dietary tools might pose different challenges.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The study's novelty lies in its exploration of LLMs for nutrient estimation using *only* text input, differentiating it from much of the existing literature focused on image-based analysis. The chain-of-thought approach is significant.
    *   The findings suggest potential benefits for simplifying dietary data collection, improving the efficiency of real-time monitoring, and enabling personalized nutritional feedback.
    *   While other studies have used AI in food analysis, the focus on purely text-based data and parameter-efficient fine-tuning makes this work a valuable contribution.
    *   Acknowledging the role of the limitations and considering more complex data will be essential in future work.

**Justification for Score:**

This paper provides a solid contribution by demonstrating the feasibility of text-based nutrient estimation with LLMs. The rigorous methodology, inclusion of comparison and performance results, and open-source approach are definite strengths. However, the limitations related to data preprocessing, nutrient scope, language bias, and the novelty and potential for widespread impact is reduced.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### RepIt: Representing Isolated Targets to Steer Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13281v1)
- **Authors**: Vincent Siu, Nathan W. Henry, Nicholas Crispino, Yang Liu, Dawn Song, Chenguang Wang
- **Abstract**: While activation steering in large language models (LLMs) is a growing area of research, methods can often incur broader effects than desired. This motivates isolation of purer concept vectors to enable targeted interventions and understand LLM behavior at a more granular level. We present RepIt, a simple and data-efficient framework for isolating concept-specific representations. Across five frontier LLMs, RepIt enables precise interventions: it selectively suppresses refusal on targeted concepts while preserving refusal elsewhere, producing models that answer WMD-related questions while still scoring as safe on standard benchmarks. We further show that the corrective signal localizes to just 100-200 neurons and that robust target representations can be extracted from as few as a dozen examples on a single A6000. This efficiency raises a dual concern: manipulations can be performed with modest compute and data to extend to underrepresented data-scarce topics while evading existing benchmarks. By disentangling refusal vectors with RepIt, this work demonstrates that targeted interventions can counteract overgeneralization, laying the foundation for more granular control of model behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces REPIT (Representing Isolated Targets), a data-efficient framework for isolating concept-specific representations in large language models (LLMs).  REPIT aims to address the problem of overly broad steering effects when intervening in LLMs, where adjusting one behavior unintentionally shifts others. The core idea is to isolate "purer" concept vectors to enable more targeted interventions. The method involves computing difference-in-means (DIM) vectors, then applying reweighting, whitening, and orthogonalization to extract cleaner concept-specific vectors.  The authors demonstrate that REPIT enables selective suppression of refusal on targeted concepts (e.g., Weapons of Mass Destruction-related questions) while preserving refusal elsewhere, and importantly, that this can be achieved with relatively little data and computation. The authors investigate the inner workings of the method, showing where in the network these manipulations happen and how they relate to other steering/refusal techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The novelty lies in the simplicity and data efficiency of the REPIT framework for isolating concept-specific representations.  While prior work has explored activation steering and concept removal, REPIT's combination of techniques (reweighting, whitening, orthogonalization, and control through parameter `rho`) appears to offer a compelling trade-off between effectiveness and efficiency, with the capacity to work robustly with as few as a dozen samples.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is multifaceted:
    *   It provides a useful methodological tool for researchers interested in targeted interventions and understanding LLM behavior at a more granular level.
    *   It highlights a potential vulnerability in current LLM safety evaluations. The paper convincingly demonstrates that models can appear safe on standard benchmarks while still harboring exploitable behaviors related to specific concepts.  This finding has implications for how we audit and govern LLMs. The observation that even concept-matched benchmarks may underestimate harmful capabilities is quite concerning.
    *   The paper's analysis of the internal workings of REPIT is valuable for the mechanistic interpretability community. It sheds light on how refusal behavior is encoded and manipulated within LLMs, supporting the idea that concepts are represented in complex ways beyond simple linear directions.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the REPIT framework and its motivation.
    *   The experiments are thorough and cover a range of LLMs, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of REPIT.
    *   The data efficiency of REPIT is a major strength, making it practical for exploring a wide range of concepts.
    *   The ablation studies and analyses of the internal workings of REPIT add depth to the paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper discusses ethical implications, more could be said about responsible disclosure and defense against the vulnerabilities exposed by REPIT. The "here's how to bypass" aspect of such research requires careful handling.
    *   The choice of `rho` is presented as a grid search. More investigation into learning this parameter, or tying it to some kind of measurement, might have been valuable.
    *   The paper acknowledges that their definition of harmful and "WMD-related" are still human-determined. A stronger consideration and incorporation of human diversity (cultural, political) in defining harm could strengthen future iterations of this work.

*   **Impact:**
    *   The paper's findings are likely to influence research in LLM safety, alignment, and interpretability. The REPIT framework could be used to develop more targeted interventions, improve safety evaluations, and gain a better understanding of how LLMs represent concepts.  The paper's emphasis on data efficiency could also make this area of research more accessible.
    *   The warning about potential misuse is important and may prompt discussion within the AI safety community.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 8. While not groundbreaking, the paper presents a novel and useful tool with significant practical implications for the field of LLM safety and interpretability. The REPIT framework is clearly defined, well-evaluated, and addresses an important problem (overly broad steering effects). The demonstration of data efficiency and the identification of potential vulnerabilities in current safety evaluations are significant contributions. The paper provides mechanistic insights that align with current perspectives in the field. The weaknesses are relatively minor.  The potential for dual use necessitates some conservatism in assigning a higher score, but the paper's warning and proposed framework are steps in the right direction.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Scaling Agents via Continual Pre-training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13310v1)
- **Authors**: Liangcai Su, Zhen Zhang, Guangyu Li, Zhuo Chen, Chenxi Wang, Maojia Song, Xinyu Wang, Kuan Li, Jialong Wu, Xuanzhong Chen, Zile Qiao, Zhongwang Zhang, Huifeng Yin, Shihao Cai, Runnan Fang, Zhengwei Tao, Wenbiao Yin, Chenxiong Qian, Yong Jiang, Pengjun Xie, Fei Huang, Jingren Zhou
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have evolved into agentic systems capable of autonomous tool use and multi-step reasoning for complex problem-solving. However, post-training approaches building upon general-purpose foundation models consistently underperform in agentic tasks, particularly in open-source implementations. We identify the root cause: the absence of robust agentic foundation models forces models during post-training to simultaneously learn diverse agentic behaviors while aligning them to expert demonstrations, thereby creating fundamental optimization tensions. To this end, we are the first to propose incorporating Agentic Continual Pre-training (Agentic CPT) into the deep research agents training pipeline to build powerful agentic foundational models. Based on this approach, we develop a deep research agent model named AgentFounder. We evaluate our AgentFounder-30B on 10 benchmarks and achieve state-of-the-art performance while retains strong tool-use ability, notably 39.9% on BrowseComp-en, 43.3% on BrowseComp-zh, and 31.5% Pass@1 on HLE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Agentic Continual Pre-training (Agentic CPT) as a method to build robust agentic foundation models. Recognizing that standard post-training methods often struggle in agentic tasks due to the lack of pre-existing agentic capabilities in general-purpose foundation models, the authors propose incorporating Agentic CPT.  This involves pre-training on diverse agentic data using First-order Action Synthesis (FAS) and Higher-order Action Synthesis (HAS), and a two-stage training strategy. The paper presents AgentFounder, a model trained using Agentic CPT, and demonstrates its state-of-the-art performance on various deep research agent benchmarks, retaining strong tool-use capabilities. The authors conduct ablation studies, explore scaling laws, and analyze tool call patterns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of incorporating Agentic CPT to create agentic *foundation* models is novel. Previous work focused primarily on post-training general-purpose models for agentic tasks. The authors are the first to directly address the problem of weak agentic inductive biases in initial foundation models by focusing on continual pre-training with agentic data. The FAS and HAS data generation methods contribute to the novelty, especially the techniques used to generate agentic data in offline environments without API costs. The two-stage training strategy, while intuitively reasonable, is well-motivated.
*   **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing the bottleneck faced by open-source implementations when building agentic systems on top of general-purpose language models. The performance gains of AgentFounder over existing open-source and even commercial models, particularly in BrowseComp-en, BrowseComp-zh, HLE, and Xbench-DeepSearch, highlights the potential impact of Agentic CPT. The comprehensive experiments and analyses provide valuable insights into scaling agentic capabilities.  The detailed ablation studies offer a deeper understanding of the contributions of different components of the proposed framework.  The work will likely influence future research directions in the field of building more capable and effective deep research agents.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly identifies a critical problem in current agentic systems – the lack of agentic inductive biases in foundation models.
    *   The proposed Agentic CPT method offers a novel solution to address this problem.
    *   The FAS and HAS data generation techniques are well-motivated and contribute to the practical applicability of the method.
    *   Comprehensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of AgentFounder compared to strong baselines.
    *   Detailed ablation studies provide a good understanding of the contribution of different components.
    *   The paper is well-written and organized.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper emphasizes the offline nature of the data generation, further discussion on the representativeness and potential biases introduced during the data synthesis process would be valuable. Specifically, the synthesis methods may inadvertently restrict the scope of agentic behaviors or introduce biases reflecting the LLM's understanding of 'good' agentic behavior.
    *   While strong, the experiments primarily focus on benchmarks. A qualitative analysis and case studies showcasing the AgentFounder's strengths and weaknesses in real-world scenarios, along with associated failure cases, could strengthen the paper.
    *   The dependence on Qwen3 series models could be seen as a limiting factor, although the authors argue the method should be generally applicable.
    *   Although the authors mention potential for general-purpose agents, there is a limited analysis showcasing this capability specifically outside the domain of complex research tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a significant contribution to the field of agentic systems by presenting a novel pre-training approach and demonstrating its effectiveness on diverse benchmarks. The method addresses a critical bottleneck faced by current open-source implementations. While some limitations and potential avenues for further research exist, the strengths of the paper significantly outweigh the weaknesses. The impact of the work on the field of agentic systems is anticipated to be substantial, especially in the development of open-source agents.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Towards General Agentic Intelligence via Environment Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.13311v1)
- **Authors**: Runnan Fang, Shihao Cai, Baixuan Li, Jialong Wu, Guangyu Li, Wenbiao Yin, Xinyu Wang, Xiaobin Wang, Liangcai Su, Zhen Zhang, Shibin Wu, Zhengwei Tao, Yong Jiang, Pengjun Xie, Fei Huang, Jingren Zhou
- **Abstract**: Advanced agentic intelligence is a prerequisite for deploying Large Language Models in practical, real-world applications. Diverse real-world APIs demand precise, robust function-calling intelligence, which needs agents to develop these capabilities through interaction in varied environments. The breadth of function-calling competence is closely tied to the diversity of environments in which agents are trained. In this work, we scale up environments as a step towards advancing general agentic intelligence. This gives rise to two central challenges: (i) how to scale environments in a principled manner, and (ii) how to effectively train agentic capabilities from experiences derived through interactions with these environments. To address these, we design a scalable framework that automatically constructs heterogeneous environments that are fully simulated, systematically broadening the space of function-calling scenarios. We further adapt a two-phase agent fine-tuning strategy: first endowing agents with fundamental agentic capabilities, then specializing them for domain-specific contexts. Extensive experiments on agentic benchmarks, tau-bench, tau2-Bench, and ACEBench, demonstrate that our trained model, AgentScaler, significantly enhances the function-calling capability of models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards General Agentic Intelligence via Environment Scaling":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of developing general agentic intelligence, particularly focusing on the function-calling capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  It argues that the diversity of environments in which agents are trained significantly impacts their function-calling competence. To address this, the paper introduces a framework for scaling up environments by automatically constructing heterogeneous and fully simulated environments. This framework consists of two key parts: 1) principled environment construction and scaling based on a read-write database abstraction of APIs, and 2) a two-phase agent experience learning strategy, where agents first acquire fundamental agentic skills and then specialize in domain-specific contexts. The authors evaluate their approach, called AgentScaler, on several agentic benchmarks and demonstrate improved function-calling capability compared to baseline models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its systematic approach to environment scaling for agentic training.  While the idea of simulated environments isn't entirely new (as the paper acknowledges), the automatic construction and scaling of *heterogeneous* environments based on an API-centric view *and* a rigorous two-stage training process sets it apart. The read-write database abstraction for representing environments offers a principled way to formalize and manipulate the environment state. A clear strength is the effort to construct complex scenarios, which goes beyond simply using static datasets. Furthermore, the explicit attention to *verifiability* during task construction is a valuable contribution, making the training and evaluation process more reliable.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from its potential to address a key bottleneck in agentic AI: the scarcity and limited diversity of agentic data. By scaling environments, the authors enable the creation of more diverse training data, potentially leading to more robust and generalizable agents. The experiments, showing state-of-the-art performance among open-source models on established benchmarks, provide empirical evidence supporting the effectiveness of the approach.  The analysis of long-horizon tool-calling is also insightful, highlighting a current limitation in the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Systematic and principled approach to environment scaling.
    *   Emphasis on verifiability in task construction.
    *   Demonstrated performance improvements on multiple benchmarks.
    *   Good analysis of model limitations (long-horizon tool calling).
    *   Focus on improving the function-calling ability of open source LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The "simulation" of users and environments, while automated, might still oversimplify real-world complexities. A careful analysis of potential biases introduced through the automated environment generation pipeline is also missing.
    *   While achieving SOTA among *open-source* models is notable, the performance still lags behind closed-source LLMs. The paper doesn't deeply analyze *why* this gap persists, which would provide greater insight.
    *   The descriptions of some implementation details could be more precise. For example, the specifics of API description rewriting might benefit from greater elaboration.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging more systematic research into environment design and scaling for agentic AI. The approach provides a clear framework for building more diverse and verifiable training data. It could also encourage further research into overcoming the challenges of long-horizon tool calling.

*   **Rationale for Score:** While the paper demonstrates a significant step forward in environment scaling for agentic AI, limitations in simulated environment realism and a performance gap with closed-source models temper the score. The systematic approach, emphasis on verifiability, and demonstrated performance improvements justify a solid evaluation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10
